Seminorm estimates and joint ergodicity for pairwise independent Hardy sequences Thanks: The first author was partially funded by Centro de Modelamiento Matemático (CMM) FB210005, BASAL funds for centers of excellence from ANID-Chile and ANID/Fondecyt/1241346. The second author was supported by the “Excellence in Research” program of the Special Account for Research Funds AUTh (Code 10316). The third author was supported by the NCN Polonez Bis 3 grant No. 2022/47/P/ST1/00854 (H2020 MSCA GA No. 945339); for the initial stages of the project, the third author was supported by the ELIDEK Grant No: 1684. The fourth author was partially supported by the NSF Grant DMS-2247331. The fifth author was initially funded by ELIDEK–Fellowship number 5367 (3rd Call for HFRI Ph.D. Fellowships) and currently supported by the Swiss National Science Foundation grant TMSGI2-211214. For the purpose of Open Access, the authors have applied a CC-BY public copyright licence to any Author Accepted Manuscript (AAM) version arising from this submission.
Sebastián Donoso, Andreas Koutsogiannis, Borys Kuca, Wenbo Sun, and Konstantinos Tsinas

Abstract
. We develop a robust structure theory for multiple ergodic averages of commuting transformations along Hardy sequences of polynomial growth. We then apply it to derive a number of novel results on joint ergodicity, recurrence and convergence. In particular, we prove joint ergodicity for (a) pairwise independent Hardy sequences and weakly mixing transformations, (b) strongly independent Hardy sequences and ergodic transformations, (c) strongly irrationally independent Hardy sequences and totally ergodic transformations. We use these joint ergodicity results to provide new recurrence results for multidimensional patterns along strongly independent Hardy sequences, showing for instance that all subsets of ℤ 2 {\mathbb{Z}}^{2} of positive upper density contain patterns of the form ( m 1 , m 2 ) , ( m 1 + ⌊ n 2 ⌋ , m 2 ) , ( m 1 , m 2 + ⌊ n 2 + n 1 / 2 ⌋ ) . (m_{1},m_{2}),\;(m_{1}+{\lfloor n^{\sqrt{2}}\rfloor},m_{2}),\;(m_{1},m_{2}+{\lfloor n^{\sqrt{2}}+n^{1/2}\rfloor}). Last but not least, we positively resolve the joint ergodicity classification problem for pairwise independent Hardy sequences, of which the aforementioned families are special cases. While building on recent technical advances (e.g. PET coefficient tracking schemes and joint ergodicity criteria), our work introduces a number of technical developments of its own. We construct a suitable generalization of Host-Kra and box seminorms that quantitatively control ergodic averages along Hardy sequences. We subsequently use them to obtain Host-Kra seminorm estimates for averages along all pairwise independent Hardy sequences. Furthermore, we develop an ergodic version of the quantitative concatenation argument that has recently found extensive use in combinatorics, number theory and harmonic analysis. Lastly, we obtain new simultaneous Taylor approximations for Hardy sequences, a crucial ingredient to deal with the aforementioned classes of Hardy sequences.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 37A44; Secondary: 11B30, 28D05

1.
Introduction

Furstenberg’s celebrated proof of the Szemerédi theorem
[
37
]
initiated the study of the limiting behavior of multiple ergodic averages, a rich field within ergodic theory with profound connections to combinatorics, number theory, Lie groups and harmonic analysis. Given a multiple ergodic average, one is interested in understanding whether the average converges (in norm or pointwise) and what recurrence properties it satisfies. For either purpose, one usually needs to have some structural description of the average; that is, one needs to identify a (useful) seminorm and/or a factor that controls its limiting behavior.

In this paper, we develop a robust machinery to examine the
L^{2}(\mu)
-limiting behavior of multiple ergodic averages of the form

(1)

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}\Bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{k}T_{i}^{{\left\lfloor a_{1i}(n)\right\rfloor}}\Bigr)f_{1}\cdots\Bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{k}T_{i}^{{\left\lfloor a_{\ell i}(n)\right\rfloor}}\Bigr)f_{\ell}

(here,
\E\limits_{n\in[N]}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{n=1}^{N}
as customary in the field),
where
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
is a system of commuting invertible measure-preserving transformations on a standard probability space
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
(henceforth referred to as
system
);
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}
are
L^{\infty}(\mu)
functions; and
a_{ji}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
belong to the broad class
{\mathcal{H}}
of
Hardy functions of polynomial growth
, defined in Section
2.2
, that includes real polynomials (e.g.
\sqrt{2}t^{2}+t
), fractional powers (e.g.
t^{3/2}
), and functions involving exponentials and logarithms (e.g.
t\log t
or
\exp(\sqrt{\log t})
). As a special subclass of interest, we study averages

(2)

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{{\left\lfloor a_{\ell}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{\ell}

exemplified by

(3)

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{{\lfloor n^{\sqrt{2}}\rfloor}}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{{\lfloor n^{\sqrt{2}}+n^{1/2}\rfloor}}f_{2};

for the latter average, no convergence or recurrence properties have been known so far except when
T_{1}=T_{2}
. For those broad classes of averages, we obtain novel seminorm estimates and use them to derive new ergodic identities and combinatorial applications that significantly generalize recent advances on multiple ergodic averages of commuting transformations along polynomials and Hardy sequences
[
18
,
20
,
21
,
27
,
36
,
50
,
66
]
.

As an example of our results, we not only show that (
3
) converges in
L^{2}(\mu)
, but we in fact find its limit to explicitly equal
\E(f_{1}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1}))\cdot\E(f_{2}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2}))
, where
\E(f_{j}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{j}))
is the conditional expectation of
f_{j}
onto the
\sigma
-algebra of
T_{j}
-invariant functions. When
T_{1},T_{2}
are ergodic, this simplifies to
\int f_{1}\;d\mu\cdot\int f_{2}\;d\mu
. We then use this limiting formula in conjunction with the Furstenberg correspondence principle to deduce that each subset of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
of positive upper density contains a configuration

\displaystyle(m_{1},m_{2}),\;(m_{1}+{\lfloor n^{\sqrt{2}}\rfloor},m_{2}),\;(m_{1},m_{2}+{\lfloor n^{\sqrt{2}}+n^{1/2}\rfloor})

for some
n\neq 0
(in fact, we can ensure that
n
is a prime number).

1.1.
Joint ergodicity

Our most exciting results concern
joint ergodicity
, whose definition we recall now.

Definition 1.1

(Joint ergodicity)
.

Sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
are
jointly ergodic
for a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
if

(4)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}-\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\int f_{j}\,d\mu\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

holds for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
.

Introduced in the 1980s by Berend and Bergelson
[
2
]
, joint ergodicity and its variants have been a subject of intense scrutiny in the last few years
[
3
,
4
,
8
,
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
,
17
,
18
,
20
,
21
,
22
,
24
,
25
,
26
,
27
,
31
,
34
,
35
,
36
,
44
,
47
,
49
,
50
,
66
]
. We derive a number of new joint ergodicity results on Hardy sequences, which vary in terms of how we balance the assumptions imposed on Hardy sequences and the system. First, we list various independence conditions on Hardy sequences, each of which gives rise to new joint ergodicity results under suitable assumptions on the system.

Definition 1.2

(Independence properties of Hardy sequences)
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
. We call them:

(i)

pairwise independent
if for any
i\neq j
and
c_{i},c_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}
not both zero, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{|c_{i}a_{i}(t)+c_{j}a_{j}(t)|}{\log t}=\infty;

(ii)

strongly independent
if every nontrivial linear combination of
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
stays logarithmically away from rational polynomials, i.e. for all
c_{1},\ldots,c_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}
not all zero and for all
p\in{\mathbb{Q}}[t]
, we have

(5)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{|c_{1}a_{1}(t)+\cdots+c_{\ell}a_{\ell}(t)-p(t)|}{\log t}=\infty;

(iii)

strongly irrationally independent
if (
5
) holds for all
c_{1},\ldots,c_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}
not all rational and for all
p\in{\mathbb{Q}}[t]
;

(iv)

irrationally independent
if (
5
) holds for all
c_{1},\ldots,c_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\mathbb{Q}_{\ast}:=(\mathbb{R}\backslash\mathbb{Q})\cup\{0\}
not all zero and for all
p\in{\mathbb{Q}}[t]
.

Clearly, strong independence implies strong irrational independence, which implies irrational independence, which in turn implies pairwise independence. For instance,

(i)

the family
t^{\sqrt{2}},\;t^{\sqrt{2}}+t^{1/2}
from (
3
) is strongly independent; same with
t^{3/2},\;t^{3/2}+t^{1/2}
or any other family of linearly independent
fractional polynomials

a_{j}(t)=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{d}\alpha_{ji}t^{b_{i}}
for
noninteger
positive real powers
b_{1},\ldots,b_{d}
.

(ii)

t^{\sqrt{2}},\;t^{\sqrt{2}}+t
is a strongly irrationally independent family that is not strongly independent; more generally, families of linearly independent fractional polynomials
a_{j}(t)=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{d}\alpha_{ji}t^{b_{i}}
for (possibly integer) positive reals
b_{1},\ldots,b_{d}
and rational coefficients
\alpha_{ji}
will be strongly irrationally independent, but not necessarily strongly independent;

(iii)

t^{3}+at^{2}+a^{2}t,

t^{2}+at,
for
a\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\mathbb{Q},
is an irrationally independent family that is not strongly irrationally independent;

(iv)

linearly independent real polynomials are pairwise independent, but they need not even be strongly irrationally independent (consider e.g.
\sqrt{2}t^{2},\;\sqrt{2}t^{2}+\sqrt{3}t
).

While technical, the properties from Definition
1.2
are natural for our purposes, as:

•

the ergodic averages along sequences satisfying condition (i) admit Host-Kra seminorm estimates (see Theorem
1.16
); that is because under condition (i), analytic maneuvers applied to obtain Theorem
1.16
allow us to distinguish between
f_{i}
and
f_{j}
for
i\neq j
;

•

condition (ii) characterizes those sequences whose nontrivial linear combinations are equidistributed modulo 1 (see Theorem
1.5
below) and are therefore predestined to be jointly ergodic in the light of the joint ergodicity criteria (see Theorem
12.3
);

•

condition (iii) is a sufficient condition for joint ergodicity in totally ergodic systems (see Theorem
1.4
, which extends an earlier result of Frantzikinakis
[
27
, Theorem 1.9 (i)]
); and

•

condition (iv) is a sufficient condition for (total) joint ergodicity in totally ergodic systems (see the second part of Theorem
1.4
, which extends
[
49
, Theorem E]
of the second and fourth authors).

Our first joint ergodicity result deals with pairwise independent Hardy sequences and weakly mixing transformations. We emphasize that our definition of
{\mathcal{H}}
from Section
2.2
includes the assumption of polynomial growth.

Theorem 1.3

(Joint ergodicity for pairwise independent Hardy sequences and weakly mixing transformations)
.

Let
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
be pairwise independent. Then
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are jointly ergodic for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
in which
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
are all weakly mixing.

While known for integer polynomials
[
3
,
36
]
, Theorem
1.3
is completely new even for pairwise independent fractional polynomials, let alone pairwise independent elements of
{\mathcal{H}}
in general.

The assumption that pairwise linear combinations of
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
“stay away” from
\log
is necessary because functions growing slower than
\log
tend to behave badly with regards to norm convergence.
For instance, the averages

\E_{n\in[N]}T^{{\left\lfloor\log_{2}n\right\rfloor}}f\quad\text{or }\quad\E_{n\in[N]}T^{{\left\lfloor n+\log_{2}n\right\rfloor}}f_{1}\cdot T^{n}f_{2}

fail to converge in general by considering eigenfunctions of
T
and breaking the average into dyadic intervals. Without the pairwise independence assumption, one cannot hope for a useful structural description of the average without making rather restrictive assumptions on the system.

For strongly independent families of Hardy sequences, we obtain the following joint ergodicity result. In contrast to Theorem
1.3
, it makes no mixing assumptions on the system.

Theorem 1.4

(Joint ergodicity for strongly independent Hardy sequences)
.

Let
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
be strongly independent. Then for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}-\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\E(f_{j}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{j}))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0,

where
\E(f_{j}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{j}))
is the orthogonal projection of
f_{j}
onto the invariant factor of
T_{j}
.

In particular,
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are jointly ergodic for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
being ergodic.

As a special case, Theorem
1.4
applied to (
3
) implies that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{{\lfloor n^{\sqrt{2}}\rfloor}}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{{\lfloor n^{\sqrt{2}}+n^{1/2}\rfloor}}f_{2}-\int f_{1}\;d\mu\cdot\int f_{2}\;d\mu\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

if
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are ergodic, a result not known before. More generally, it yields joint ergodicity for arbitrary linearly independent fractional polynomials with noninteger powers.

The relevance of strong independence comes from the equidistribution theorem of Boshernitzan, which implies that every nontrivial linear combination of strongly independent Hardy sequences is equidistributed.

Theorem 1.5

(
[
15
, Theorem 1.3]
)
.

Let
a\in\mathcal{H}
. The sequence
(a(n))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is equidistributed
\mod~\!1
if and only if for every
p\in{\mathbb{Q}}[t]
we have

\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{|a(t)-p(t)|}{\log t}=\infty.

Theorems
1.3
and
1.4
vastly generalize previously known joint ergodicity results that, broadly speaking, have dealt with
integer polynomials
[
3
,
17
,
18
,
31
,
36
]
, real polynomials
[
44
,
46
]
, and Hardy field functions
[
11
,
24
,
25
,
27
,
66
]
, under various distinctness assumptions (in some cases for a single transformation, in other for multiple ones).
In particular, they imply norm convergence for a large class of ergodic averages for which no convergence results have been known before, an example being again the average (
3
).

For strongly irrationally independent Hardy sequences, we obtain the following variant of Theorem
1.4
.

Theorem 1.6

(Rational Kronecker control for strongly irrationally independent Hardy sequences)
.

Let
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
be strongly irrationally independent. Then for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}-\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}\E(f_{j}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{j}))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

Here,
\E(f_{j}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{j}))
is the orthogonal projection of
f_{j}
onto the rational Kronecker factor
{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{j})
.

In particular,
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are jointly ergodic for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
totally ergodic. The latter conclusion also holds if
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are irrationally independent rather than strongly irrationally independent.
1
1

1

This last conclusion extends
[
49
, Theorem A]
to multiple commuting transformations.

The appearance of the rational Kronecker factors comes from the fact that while the exponential averages of
c_{1}a_{1}(n)+\cdots+c_{\ell}a_{\ell}(n)
with at least one
c_{j}
irrational will always vanish by Theorem
1.5
, the same may not be the case when all
c_{j}
’s are rational.

Theorem
1.6
is a multidimensional generalization of
[
27
, Theorem 1.9]
. Since the family of strongly irrationally independent Hardy sequences includes linearly independent polynomials
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t]
, Theorem
1.6
contains as special cases all previously known results for linearly independent integer polynomials
[
17
,
31
,
32
,
36
]
.

Before proceeding to state more results, we remark that the occurrences of floor functions in the previous statements can be individually replaced by other rounding functions, such as the ceiling function (denoted by
\lceil\cdot\rceil
) or the closest integer function (denoted by
[[\cdot]]
) since
\lceil t\rceil=-{\left\lfloor-t\right\rfloor}
and
[[t]]={\left\lfloor t+1/2\right\rfloor}
for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}.
This is also the case for various other results in this paper, a fact that we will not explicitly mention again.

So far, we have stated results for broad, naturally occurring classes of Hardy sequences under either no assumption on the system or some fixed assumption (weak mixing or total ergodicity of individual transformations). Now, we switch gears and move towards establishing necessary and sufficient conditions on the system under which pairwise independent Hardy sequences are jointly ergodic. The definition below will frequently appear in the following discussion.

Definition 1.7

(Ergodic sequences of transformations)
.

A sequence of measure-preserving transformations
(T_{n})_{n}
on a probability space
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
is
ergodic
if

\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{n}f-\int f\,d\mu\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

for all
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
.

There is a general heuristic that proposes a set of “natural” necessary and sufficient conditions for joint ergodicity. The problem below, stated before as
[
21
, Problem 1]
(for the general case) or
[
11
, Conjecture 6.1]
(for fractional polynomials), inquires about the extent to which this heuristic holds.

Problem 1.8

(Joint ergodicity classification problem)
.

Let
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Classify the sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
that are jointly ergodic for an (arbitrary) system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
if and only if the following two conditions hold:

(i)

(Difference ergodicity condition)

(T_{i}^{{\left\lfloor a_{i}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{j}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is ergodic on
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
for all distinct
i,j\in[\ell]
; and

(ii)

(Product ergodicity condition)

(T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}\times\cdots\times T_{\ell}^{{\left\lfloor a_{\ell}(n)\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is ergodic on the product system
(X^{\ell},{\mathcal{X}}^{\otimes\ell},\mu^{\ell})
.

The two conditions appearing in the problem are natural generalizations of the joint ergodicity conditions provided by Berend and Bergelson in their pioneer work
[
2
]
.

Despite ongoing progress in the recent years
[
8
,
18
,
20
,
21
,
34
,
36
]
, the joint ergodicity classification problem remains challenging and open. Our contribution to this question consists in the following.

Theorem 1.9

(Resolution of Problem
1.8
for pairwise independent Hardy sequences)
.

A family of pairwise independent Hardy sequences of polynomial growth is jointly ergodic for a system if and only if it satisfies the difference and product ergodicity conditions on this system.

Theorem
1.9
contains both Theorem
1.3
and the second parts of Theorem
1.4
and
1.6
as special cases, and it vastly extends previously known joint ergodicity results.

1.2.
Applications to recurrence and combinatorics

As an immediate consequence, Theorem
1.4
gives a multiple recurrence result with an explicit lower bound which is optimal in that it cannot be improved for weakly mixing systems.

Corollary 1.10

(Multiple recurrence for strongly independent Hardy sequences)
.

Let
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
be strongly independent. Then for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and sets
E\in{\mathcal{X}}
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\mu(E\cap T_{1}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}E\cap\cdots\cap T_{\ell}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{\ell}(n)\right\rfloor}}E)\geq(\mu(E))^{\ell+1}.

A standard application of the Furstenberg correspondence principle to Corollary
1.10
not only gives many strongly independent Hardy progressions in sets of positive upper density, but it implies the existence of so-called
popular common differences
, i.e. the differences
n
for which the upper density of the intersection

E\cap(E-{\mathbf{v}}_{1}{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor})\cap\cdots\cap(E-{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell}{\left\lfloor a_{\ell}(n)\right\rfloor})

is close to what it would be if the sets
E,\;E-{\mathbf{v}}_{1}{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor},\;\ldots,\;E-{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell}{\left\lfloor a_{\ell}(n)\right\rfloor}
were independent.

Corollary 1.11

(Hardy progressions in positive density sets)
.

Let
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
be strongly independent. Then for all sets
E\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
with positive upper density
2
2

2

The upper density of
E\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
is defined by
\overline{d}(E)=\limsup\limits_{N\to\infty}\frac{|E\cap\{-N,\ldots,N\}^{k}|}{(2N+1)^{k}}
.

and vectors
{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\overline{d}(E\cap(E-{\mathbf{v}}_{1}{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor})\cap\cdots\cap(E-{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell}{\left\lfloor a_{\ell}(n)\right\rfloor}))\geq(\overline{d}(E))^{\ell+1}.

In particular,
E
contains

{\mathbf{m}},\;{\mathbf{m}}+{\mathbf{v}}_{1}{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor},\;\ldots,\;{\mathbf{m}}+{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell}{\left\lfloor a_{\ell}(n)\right\rfloor}

for some
{\mathbf{m}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

In particular, Corollaries
1.10
and
1.11
give lower bounds on the number of patterns

\displaystyle(m_{1},m_{2}),\;(m_{1}+{\lfloor n^{\sqrt{2}}\rfloor},m_{2}),\;(m_{1},m_{2}+{\lfloor n^{\sqrt{2}}+n^{1/2}\rfloor})

in subsets of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
of positive upper density. The same conclusion follows for patterns involving strongly independent real polynomials such as

\displaystyle(m_{1},m_{2}),\;(m_{1}+{\lfloor\sqrt{2}n^{3}+n^{2}\rfloor},m_{2}),\;(m_{1},m_{2}+{\lfloor\sqrt{3}n^{3}-n\rfloor}).

1.3.
Results along primes

Our new joint ergodicity and recurrence results can be combined with a transference principle from
[
50
]
to deduce new ergodic and combinatorial results along primes. This transference principle itself is based on deep result on the Gowers uniformity of the modified von Mangoldt function on short intervals
[
56
]
.
Studying multiple ergodic averages along primes has rich history, see e.g.
[
5
,
16
,
26
,
28
,
29
,
44
,
45
,
48
,
50
,
51
,
57
,
58
,
62
,
69
,
70
]
for some earlier works.
We denote the set of primes by
{\mathbb{P}}
and the number of primes up to
N
by
\pi(N)
. We will also use the averaging notation

\E_{p\in{\mathbb{P}}\cap[N]}a_{p}=\frac{1}{\pi(N)}\sum_{p\in{\mathbb{P}}\colon p\leq N}a_{p}.

Our first theorem follows by combining Theorem
1.4
with
[
50
, Theorem 1.2]
.

Theorem 1.12

(Joint ergodicity for strongly independent Hardy sequences along primes)
.

Let
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
be pairwise independent and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},

\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system. Suppose that one of the following conditions holds:

(i)

a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are strongly independent; or

(ii)

T_{1},\dots,T_{\ell}
are weakly mixing.

Then

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{p\in{\mathbb{P}}\cap[N]}\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(p)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}-\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\E(f_{j}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{j}))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0.

In particular, for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
ergodic, we have

\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{p\in{\mathbb{P}}\cap[N]}\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(p)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}-\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\int f_{j}\ d\mu\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0.

Applying the previous theorem to functions
f_{1}=\cdots=f_{\ell}={1}_{E}
for a measurable set
E
, we deduce the following multiple recurrence result.

Corollary 1.13

(Multiple recurrence for strongly independent Hardy sequences along primes)
.

Let
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
be strongly independent. Then for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and sets
E\in{\mathcal{X}}
, we have

\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\E_{p\in{\mathbb{P}}\cap[N]}\mu(E\cap T_{1}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{1}(p)\right\rfloor}}E\cap\dots\cap T_{\ell}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{\ell}(p)\right\rfloor}}E)\geq(\mu(E))^{\ell+1}.

Once again, Furstenberg’s correspondence principle translates the previous multiple recurrence result to the combinatorial setting.

Corollary 1.14

(Prime configurations in positive density sets)
.

Let
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
be strongly independent. Then for all sets
E\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
with positive upper density and vectors
{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
, we have

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\E_{p\in{\mathbb{P}}\cap[N]}\overline{d}(E\cap(E-{\mathbf{v}}_{1}{\left\lfloor a_{1}(p)\right\rfloor})\cap\cdots\cap(E-{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell}{\left\lfloor a_{\ell}(p)\right\rfloor}))\geq(\overline{d}(E))^{\ell+1}.

In particular,
E
contains

{\mathbf{m}},\;{\mathbf{m}}+{\mathbf{v}}_{1}{\left\lfloor a_{1}(p)\right\rfloor},\;\ldots,\;{\mathbf{m}}+{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell}{\left\lfloor a_{\ell}(p)\right\rfloor}

for some
{\mathbf{m}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
and
p\in{\mathbb{P}}
.

1.4.
Seminorm estimates

The main new input that allows us to prove joint ergodicity results
and their corollaries
are novel seminorm estimates on averages (
1
) and (
2
), which we then combine with joint ergodicity criteria from
[
36
]
and equidistribution results for Hardy sequences
to derive Theorems
1.3
,
1.4
, and
1.9
. Specifically, we develop a generalization of box seminorms robust enough to control the averages (
1
) with polynomial bounds under some necessary nondegeneracy condition on the Hardy sequences. The gist of our main result is captured in the following rather informal statement: for its (somewhat complicated) precise version, we refer the reader to Theorem
10.1
.

Theorem 1.15

(Generalized box seminorm estimates)
.

Let
k,\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\mathbf{a}_{j}=(a_{j1},\dots,

a_{jk})\in{\mathcal{H}}^{k}
be
k
-tuples of functions in
{\mathcal{H}}
for
1\leq j\leq\ell
.
Suppose that for every
0\leq j\leq\ell
different from
1
, the tuple
\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{j}
has a coordinate that grows faster than
\log
.
Then for every system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
there exists a generalized box seminorm on
\left|\!\left|\!\left|\cdot\right|\!\right|\!\right|
on
L^{\infty}(\mu)
of degree
O_{\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{\ell}}(1)
such that

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\Bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{k}T_{i}^{{\left\lfloor a_{1i}(n)\right\rfloor}}\Bigr)f_{1}\cdots\Bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{k}T_{i}^{{\left\lfloor a_{\ell i}(n)\right\rfloor}}\Bigr)f_{\ell}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{O_{\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{\ell}}(1)}\ll_{\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{\ell}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{1}\right|\!\right|\!\right|

for all 1-bounded functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
.

Generalized box seminorms and their properties are defined and discussed at length in Section
3
. The important point is that the seminorm appearing in Theorem
1.15
has “bounded degree” and depends only on the (appropriately defined) “leading coefficients” of the sequences
\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{\ell}
. As such, Theorem
1.15
vastly generalizes previous results of similar flavor
[
18
,
20
,
21
]
. To hint at what these generalized box seminorms look like, we look at perhaps the simplest nontrivial special case of Theorem
1.15
. For a double ergodic average

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{{\lfloor\sqrt{2}n\rfloor}}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{{\lfloor\sqrt{3}n\rfloor}}f_{2},

Theorem
1.15
gives the estimate

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{{\lfloor\sqrt{2}n\rfloor}}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{{\lfloor\sqrt{3}n\rfloor}}f_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{8}\ll\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{2}\right|\!\right|\!\right|

for the seminorm

\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|=\left(\lim_{M_{2}\to\infty}\;\E_{m_{2},m_{2}^{\prime}\in[M_{2}]}\;\lim_{M_{1}\to\infty}\;\E_{m_{1},m_{1}^{\prime}\in[M_{1}]}\right.\\
\left|\int T_{1}^{{\lfloor-\sqrt{2}m_{1}\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\lfloor\sqrt{3}m_{1}\rfloor}+{\lfloor\sqrt{3}m_{2}\rfloor}}f\cdot T_{1}^{{\lfloor-\sqrt{2}m_{1}\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\lfloor\sqrt{3}m_{1}\rfloor}+{\lfloor\sqrt{3}m_{2}^{\prime}\rfloor}}\overline{f}\right.\\
\left.\left.T_{1}^{{\lfloor-\sqrt{2}m_{1}^{\prime}\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\lfloor\sqrt{3}m_{1}^{\prime}\rfloor}+{\lfloor\sqrt{3}m_{2}\rfloor}}\overline{f}\cdot T_{1}^{{\lfloor-\sqrt{2}m_{1}^{\prime}\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\lfloor\sqrt{3}m_{1}^{\prime}\rfloor}+{\lfloor\sqrt{3}m_{2}^{\prime}\rfloor}}f\;d\mu\right|^{2}\right)^{1/8}.

One should think of this seminorm as a box-type seminorm along real subgroups
\langle(0,\sqrt{3})\rangle
and
\langle(-\sqrt{2},\sqrt{3})\rangle
, the former of which corresponds to the action
(T_{2}^{{\lfloor\sqrt{3}n\rfloor}})_{n}
and the latter to the action of
(T_{1}^{{\lfloor-\sqrt{2}n\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\lfloor\sqrt{3}n\rfloor}})_{n}
.
The existence of the limits in the definition of the seminorm is derived from known results in Section
3
.

For averages (
2
) along pairwise independent Hardy sequences such as (
3
), we can improve the conclusion of Theorem
1.15
to a proper Host-Kra seminorm control.

Theorem 1.16

(Host-Kra seminorm estimates for pairwise independent Hardy sequences)
.

Let
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
be pairwise independent. Then there exists a positive integer
s=O_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}}(1)
such that for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and 1-bounded functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{{\left\lfloor a_{\ell}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{\ell}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{O_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}}(1)}\ll_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}}\min_{1\leq j\leq\ell}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{j}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{s,T_{j}}.

In contrast to earlier results for polynomials, Theorem
1.16
cannot be strengthened to averages along more general Følner sequences. See e.g.
[
10
]
for an explanation of issues arising in this case.

A reader may notice that unlike most seminorm estimates in ergodic theory, ours are not only quantitative, but they also come with good polynomial bounds. To the best of our knowledge, no such quantitative seminorm estimates exist in ergodic literature even for averages along integer polynomials.
3
3

3

For averages (
2
) of integer polynomials along a single transformation
T_{1}=\cdots=T_{\ell}
, a quantitative seminorm estimate could likely be proved by carefully quantifying the usual PET argument, and this would avoid the use of concatenation tools ubiquitous in this article. However, no avoidance of concatenation seems possible for quantitative estimates for averages involving several transformations.

We state Theorems
1.15
and
1.16
quantitatively not only because we can; the quantitative nature of Theorem
1.15
gives considerable advantage in the proof of Theorem
1.16
, even if one were only interested in the qualitative control implied by Theorem
1.16
. Indeed, the first proof of Host-Kra seminorm control for averages (
2
) along integer polynomials obtained in
[
36
]
necessitated a quantification of (qualitative) box seminorm control for averages (
1
) along integer polynomials from
[
18
]
. The quantification used in
[
36
]
was completely ineffective and relied on an abstract functional analytic argument that turned qualitative seminorm control of a multilinear operator into a soft quantitative one. By contrast, our proof of Theorem
1.16
is completely effective and therefore recovers one of the main results from
[
36
]
using arguably more elementary tools.

While proving seminorm estimates for ergodic averages along Hardy sequences (e.g.
[
11
,
24
,
25
,
66
]
, one splits the average over
[N]
into one over short intervals, and then Taylor expands the Hardy sequences on short intervals as polynomials (it is this step that requires Hardy sequences to have polynomial growth). Therefore, an important tool in all previous seminorm estimates (e.g.
[
11
,
24
,
25
,
66
]
) are simultaneous Taylor approximations for Hardy sequences that make it possible to control the degrees and leading coefficients of approximating polynomials. As it turns out, the existing approximations are far too weak to deal with commuting transformations. One of our key technical advances is Proposition
10.3
, a new result on simultaneous Taylor approximations. The content of this result and its role in the overall strategy is outlined in Section
1.6.8

If one quantitatively strengthened the joint ergodicity criteria from
[
36
]
, one could combine them with our Theorem
1.16
to give an alternative proof for the rational Kronecker control for linearly independent integer polynomials along commuting transformations from
[
36
]
that would avoid altogether the Host-Kra structure theorem. As our ambitions have been exhausted in different directions, we leave the details to the interested reader.

As an additional application, the quantitative seminorm estimates provided by Theorem
1.16
can be used to recover Gowers norm estimates for multidimensional integer polynomial progressions proved recently in
[
53
]
. Again, we do not provide the details of this derivation.

Last but not least, we emphasize that the proof of Theorem
1.16
only requires Walsh’s result from
[
68
]
for commuting transformations with mutlivariable linear iterates (see also
[
71
]
), its corresponding strengthening to averages along real linear polynomials which follows from
[
46
]
, and the Fubini-type principle for ergodic averages
[
7
]
(we need these ingredients to show that the limits in the definition of generalized box seminorms are well-defined and can be permuted). Hence it is highly possible that the only required norm convergence results are pre-Walsh norm convergence of ergodic averages along integer linear polynomials
[
1
,
42
,
64
]
. Combined with the Host-Kra structure theorem
[
43
]
and Leibman’s results on the convergence of polynomial averages on nilmanifolds
[
54
,
55
]
, Theorem
1.16
gives an alternative proof of the norm convergence of ergodic averages of commuting transformations along pairwise independent integer polynomials.
4
4

4

By contrast, the qualitative Host-Kra seminorm control for averages along pairwise independent integer polynomials proved in
[
36
]
, as well as the general convergence result for real polynomial iterates from
[
46
]
, crucially use more general implications of Walsh’s result.

1.5.
Further applications of our result

Since the release of this manuscript, Theorem
1.15
and its corollaries have played a key part in two important developments
[
19
,
33
]
. In both cases, Theorem
1.15
supplied initial seminorm estimates for the averages under consideration.
These original estimates were then improved to optimal ones using techniques that depended on the particular problem.

In
[
19
]
, we used Theorem
1.15
to resolve Problem
1.8
for all Hardy sequences of polynomial growth. In particular, we extended Theorem
1.9
to all families of Hardy sequences that are “nondegenerate” in some suitable sense. We also constructed a counterexample of “degenerate” Hardy sequences, which is jointly ergodic for some system but fails to satisfy the difference ergodicity condition.

In
[
33
]
, identities like

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{{\lfloor n^{3/2}\rfloor}}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{{\lfloor n^{3/2}\rfloor}}f_{2}-\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n}f_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

have been established by using the following special case of Theorem
1.15
in which all the iterates are multiples of the same sequence (this is the setting orthogonal to that of Theorem
1.16
).

Corollary 1.17

(Seminorm estimates in the pairwise dependent case)
.

Let
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and let
a\in{\mathcal{H}}
satisfy
a\succ\log
. Then there exists a positive integer
s=O_{a,\ell}(1)
such that for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and 1-bounded functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor a(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{{\left\lfloor a(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{\ell}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{O_{a,\ell}(1)}\ll_{a,\ell}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{1}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{{\mathbf{e}}_{1}^{\times s},({\mathbf{e}}_{1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2})^{\times s},\ldots,({\mathbf{e}}_{1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{\ell})^{\times s}}.

Corollary
1.17
has previously been known only when
a
is an integer polynomial
[
18
]
or a strongly nonpolynomial Hardy field function
[
21
]
. It is therefore new even for real polynomials like
\sqrt{2}t^{2}+t
.

More generally, if we replace each
T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor a(n)\right\rfloor}}
in the average by
\prod_{i=1}^{k}T_{i}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{ji}a(n)\right\rfloor}}
for some
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}=(\alpha_{j1},\ldots,\alpha_{jk})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
, then Theorem
1.16
gives control by a seminorm in which every
{\mathbf{e}}_{j}
is replaced by
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}
.

1.6.
Outline of the paper and new ideas

The paper is structured as follows. After presenting our notation and the basic properties of Hardy sequences in Section
2
, we move on to define generalized box seminorms and derive its various properties in Section
3
. We then dedicate Section
4
to a quantitative concatenation result for averages of generalized box seminorms that emulates similar results proved recently in the finitary setting
[
39
,
52
,
53
,
59
,
60
]
. Its culmination are Proposition
4.5
and Corollary
4.6
, which can be seen as the quantitative analogues of the Bessel-type inequality from
[
65
, Corollary 1.22]
, as well as Corollary
4.7
, tailor-made for subsequent applications. After some preliminary lemmas in Section
5
, the bulk of the paper (Sections
6
-
10
) is then devoted to proving Theorems
10.1
and
10.2
, precise versions of Theorem
1.15
, which assert that generalized box seminorms control averages (
1
) with polynomial bounds. We then upgrade, in Section
11
, the generalized box seminorm estimates to the Host-Kra seminorm estimates for pairwise independent Hardy sequences, in effect proving Theorem
1.16
. In Section
12
, we use these estimates to derive joint ergodicity results. Lastly, Appendix
A
list various elementary properties of Hardy sequences while Appendix
B
proves Proposition
10.3
, a new result on simultaneous Taylor approximations of Hardy sequences, which is of independent interest.

1.6.1.
Quantitative concatenation

Here, we outline the main arguments together with new ideas that come into the proofs, starting with quantitative concatenation. The basic idea behind concatenation is that averages of generalized box seminorms of the form

\displaystyle\E_{i\in I}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1i},\ldots,G_{si}}^{2^{s}},

where
I
is a finite indexing set and
\left|\!\left|\!\left|\cdot\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1i},\ldots,G_{si}}

is a generalized box seminorm along finitely generated subgroups
G_{1i},\ldots,G_{si}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
, are controlled by an average of generalized box seminorms along subgroups
G_{ji_{1}}+\cdots+G_{ji_{\ell}}
for
1\leq j\leq s
and indices
i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell}\in I
which typically will be pairwise distinct.

The utility of such control lies in the fact that the larger subgroups
G_{1i_{1}}+\cdots+G_{1i_{\ell}}
often admit a more explicit description than the original ones. As a model example, the arguments from Section
4
show that the average

(6)

\displaystyle\E_{h\in[\pm H]}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{\langle\sqrt{2}h\rangle}^{2}:=\E_{h\in[\pm H]}\;\lim_{M\to\infty}\;\E_{m,m^{\prime}\in[\pm M]}\int T^{{\left\lfloor\sqrt{2}hm\right\rfloor}}f\cdot T^{{\left\lfloor\sqrt{2}hm^{\prime}\right\rfloor}}\overline{f}\;d\mu

(where
[\pm H]:=\{-H,-H+1,\ldots,H-1,H\}
)
can be controlled with polynomial bounds by an average like

\displaystyle\E_{h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4}\in[\pm H]}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{\langle\sqrt{2}h_{1}\rangle+\cdots+\langle\sqrt{2}h_{4}\rangle}^{16}.

One can then show that for almost all tuples
(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{4}
, the group

\langle\sqrt{2}h_{1}\rangle+\cdots+\langle\sqrt{2}h_{4}\rangle:=\langle\sqrt{2}(m_{1}h_{1}+m_{2}h_{2}+m_{3}h_{3}+m_{4}h_{4}):\;m_{1},m_{2},m_{3},m_{4}\in{\mathbb{Z}}\rangle

is close (in an appropriate sense) to the group
\langle\sqrt{2}\rangle
. Hence, once
H
is large enough, the average (
6
) can be quantitatively bounded by a single seminorm along the group
\langle\sqrt{2}\rangle
.

Section
4
provides the first quantitative concatenation results in the ergodic setting, which can be seen as quantitative improvements on the original concatenation results from
[
65
]
. While our proofs largely follow the finite-field argument from
[
53
]
(and its integer adaptation from
[
52
]
), the transition to the ergodic setting requires nontrivial modifications of certain technical bits. The output of Section
4
is then crucially used in Section
8
to deliver Proposition
8.6
, the version of Theorem
1.15
for polynomials, in a way that will be explained shortly.

1.6.2.
Seminorm estimates: overview

As already mentioned, the lion’s share of our effort goes into deducing Theorem
10.2
which gives generalized box seminorm estimates on averages along Hardy sequences. Theorem
10.2
is then used to derive Theorem
10.1
, a precise formulation of Theorem
1.15
, as well as Theorem
1.16
, Host-Kra seminorm estimates for averages along pairwise independent Hardy sequences. The proof of Theorem
10.2
is modelled on existing works that obtain box seminorm control for polynomial averages of commuting transformations
[
18
]
and their finitary analogues
[
52
,
53
]
, as well as Host-Kra seminorm control for averages of a single transformation along Hardy sequences
[
66
]
and averages of commuting transformations along the same Hardy iterate
[
21
]
. Yet dealing with Hardy sequences
and
commuting transformations simultaneously raises the level of technicalities to the heights unseen in the aforementioned papers. To illustrate the point with one telling example, the arguments from
[
18
,
21
,
52
,
53
]
invoke concatenation results once while
[
66
]
does not use them at all; we need them at two separate occasions.

The proof of seminorm estimate for averages (
1
) (Theorems
1.15
,
10.1
and
10.2
) is split into five different cases depending on the shape of Hardy sequences, where the simpler cases are invoked in the proofs of the more difficult cases. Rewriting (
1
) as

(7)

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}T^{{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{1}\cdots T^{{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{\ell}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{\ell}

for
T^{{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}:=T_{1}^{{\lfloor a_{j1}(n)\rfloor}}\cdots T_{k}^{{\lfloor a_{jk}(n)\rfloor}}
, we can list these five cases as follows:

(i)

\mathbf{a}_{j}
’s are linear, i.e.
a_{ji}(t)=\alpha_{ji}t
for some
\alpha_{ji}\in{\mathbb{R}}
for all
j,i
;

(ii)

\mathbf{a}_{j}
’s are sublinear, i.e.
a_{ji}(t)\prec t
for all
j,i
, e.g.

(8)

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}_{1}(t)=(\sqrt{2}t^{1/2},(\log t)^{2}),\quad\mathbf{a}_{2}(t)=(\log t,\sqrt{3}t^{1/2});

(iii)

\mathbf{a}_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[t]
;

(iv)

\mathbf{a}_{j}
’s are sums of a sublinear and a polynomial term, e.g.

(9)

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}_{1}(t)=(\sqrt{2}t^{2},(\log t)^{2}),\quad\mathbf{a}_{2}(t)=(t^{2/3},\sqrt{3}t^{2});

(v)

\mathbf{a}_{j}
’s are arbitrary; this includes the iterates

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}_{1}(t)=(t^{\sqrt{2}},0),\quad\mathbf{a}_{2}(t)=(0,t^{\sqrt{2}}+t^{1/2})

from (
3
).

1.6.3.
Seminorm estimates for linear and sublinear sequences

When
\mathbf{a}_{j}
’s are linear, the proof consists of
\ell
applications of the van der Corput inequality, much like in the by now classical proof of Host-Kra seminorm control for Furstenberg averages

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}T^{n}f_{1}\cdots T^{\ell n}f_{\ell}

from
[
43
]
. They are followed by an extra application of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality needed to remove the errors accrued while comparing
{\left\lfloor\alpha(n+m)\right\rfloor}
and
{\left\lfloor\alpha n\right\rfloor}+{\left\lfloor\alpha m\right\rfloor}
. The linear case, handled in Lemma
6.1
and Corollary
6.2
, already showcases the need for a more robust notion of seminorms than the regular box seminorms.

When
\mathbf{a}_{j}
’s are sublinear, the proof of the generalized box seminorm estimates (isolated as Proposition
6.3
and Corollary
6.4
) is fairly classical and amounts to reducing the problem to the linear case. The starting point is to decompose each
\mathbf{a}_{j}
as a
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
-linear combination of some functions
g_{1},\ldots,g_{m}\in{\mathcal{H}}
satisfying the growth condition

\displaystyle 1\prec g_{1}(t)\prec\cdots\prec g_{m}(t)\prec t;

for (
8
), we would take

g_{1}(t)=\log t,\quad g_{2}(t)=(\log t)^{2},\quad g_{3}(t)=t^{1/2}.

We then split

(10)

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[R]}\approx\E_{N\in[R]}\E_{n\in(N,N+L(N)]}

for some
1\prec L(t)\prec t
. The function
L
is chosen in such a way as to make the contribution of
g_{1},\ldots,g_{m-1}
constant on each short interval; for (
8
), any
L\in{\mathcal{H}}
satisfying
t^{1/2}\prec L(t)\prec t^{3/4}
would do, giving roughly

\E_{n\in[R]}T_{1}^{{\lfloor\sqrt{2}n^{1/2}\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\lfloor(\log n)^{2}\rfloor}}f_{1}\cdot T_{1}^{{\lfloor\log n\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\lfloor\sqrt{3}n^{1/2}\rfloor}}f_{2}\\
\approx\E_{N\in[R]}\E_{n\in[L(N)]}T_{1}^{{\lfloor-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2N^{1/2}}n\rfloor}}\mathopen{}\left(T_{1}^{{\lfloor\sqrt{2}N^{2}\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\lfloor(\log N)^{2}\rfloor}}f_{1}\mathclose{}\right)\cdot T_{2}^{{\lfloor-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2N^{1/2}}n\rfloor}}\mathopen{}\left(T_{1}^{{\lfloor\log N\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\lfloor\sqrt{3}N^{2}\rfloor}}f_{2}\mathclose{}\right)

(for expository purposes, we ignore all the error terms that come from comparing an integer part of a sum with a sum of integer parts).
We then split each short interval into a union of arithmetic progressions and change variables so that on each progression, the coefficient in front of
n
becomes independent of
N
(for (
8
), the appropriate step of arithmetic progressions to make this happen would be
\sim 2N^{1/2}
). If the leading terms in
\mathbf{a}_{1}
and
\mathbf{a}_{j}
are different for all
j\neq 1
, then the claimed bound follows by invoking the linear case for the average over
n
. Otherwise we need to additionally analyze the average over
N
to disentangle from
\mathbf{a}_{1}
those
\mathbf{a}_{j}
that have the same leading term, much like this was done in
[
66
, Proposition 5.3]
.

1.6.4.
Averages with dual twists

From the sublinear case onwards, we frequently need to deal with averages (
7
) that have 1-bounded weights
c_{n}
and are twisted by
dual sequences
, i.e. sequences of the form
{\mathcal{D}}({\left\lfloor b(n)\right\rfloor})
for
b\in{\mathcal{H}}
and
{\mathcal{D}}(n)=T^{n}{\mathcal{D}}_{s,T}(f)
, where
{\mathcal{D}}_{s,T}(f)
is the level-
s
dual function of
f
with respect to
T
defined in Section
3.1
. In effect, we work with averages

(11)

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}),

where
c_{n}
are bounded complex weights,
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}
are dual sequences of bounded level and
b_{1},\ldots,b_{J}
are Hardy sequences of bounded growth.
The motivation for studying these more general expressions comes from the proof of Theorem
1.16
, in which we gradually replace arbitrary bounded functions
f_{j}
by dual functions
{\mathcal{D}}_{s,T}(g_{j})
. (The reason why the replacement of arbitrary bounded functions to dual functions gives us an advantage is that much like nilsequences, dual sequences can be removed by finitely many applications of the van der Corput inequality.) A key tool to get rid of dual sequences from our expression is Lemma
5.2
, a finitary version of
[
25
, Proposition 6.1]
.

Whenever we deal with averages twisted by dual sequences, needed for the proof of Theorem
1.16
, we typically have to make some maximum growth/degree assumption on the sequence
\mathbf{a}_{1}
acting on a function
f_{1}
for which we want to obtain a seminorm estimate. However, in the absence of dual sequences, we can usually compose the integral with an iterate of maximum growth/degree, ensuring that the now modified sequence
\mathbf{a}_{1}
has itself maximum growth/degree. For instance, in the sublinear average twisted by dual sequences for which we prove seminorm estimates in Proposition
6.3
, the sequence
\mathbf{a}_{1}
must have maximum growth among
\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{\ell}
. But in the sublinear average with no dual sequences considered in Corollary
6.4
, this assumption is no longer necessary. A similar disparity will later appear between Theorem
10.2
(which covers averages (
11
) twisted by dual functions for general Hardy sequences except it requires
\mathbf{a}_{1}
to have positive fractional degree) and Theorem
10.1
(which covers averages (
1
) with no dual sequences and therefore also works if
\mathbf{a}_{1}
is subfractional).

1.6.5.
Seminorm estimates for polynomial averages

Seminorm estimates for polynomial averages are split over two sections,
7
and
8
, and involve a number of intermediate results, some of purely auxiliary character and others fine-tuned for later applications.
In contrast with the sublinear case, the entire argument for polynomials has to be carried out finitarily, i.e. at most stages we work with (
11
) for fixed
N
; the reason is that the proof of Theorem
1.15
actually requires seminorm estimates for finite polynomial averages. Section
7
contains the PET argument; its main outcomes are Proposition
7.10
, which asserts that finite polynomial averages are controlled by finitary counterparts of generalized box seminorms, as well as Corollary
7.11
, its infinitary version. In Section
8
, we combine these PET estimates with a concatenation argument to derive Proposition
8.6
, (generalized box seminorm estimates for polynomials). Previously, Proposition
8.6
was known only for integer polynomials and in a purely qualitative form
[
18
, Theorem 2.5]
due to its reliance on the Tao-Ziegler concatenation
[
65
]
. By contrast, Proposition
8.6
holds for all real polynomials and is fully quantitative.

The proofs in Sections
7
and
8
combine the latest technical advances from the ergodic and combinatorial settings with our novel notion of generalized box seminorms. Throughout the PET argument, we need to carefully track the coefficients of the polynomials emerging at the intermediate stages of the argument. This is done using a sophisticated coefficient tracking scheme from
[
52
]
that itself refines an older scheme from
[
18
]
. The polynomial concatenation argument from Section
8
emulates an analogous argument recently carried out in the combinatorial setting
[
52
]
, and it combines the general concatenation argument from Section
4
with equidistribution results from
[
52
]
for massive systems of multilinear forms that arise during the PET procedure.

1.6.6.
Seminorm estimates in the polynomial + sublinear case

The arguments for the sublinear and polynomial cases come together in Section
9
, where we give seminorm estimates for sequences that are sums of sublinear and polynomial parts. Emulating the proof of
[
66
, Proposition 5.1]
, we decompose a single average into a double average as in (
10
), choosing the length
L
of the short intervals in such a way as to render the sublinear terms constant on each short interval. For the family (
9
), any
1\prec L(t)\prec t^{1/3}
would do, giving the approximation

\E_{n\in[R]}T_{1}^{{\lfloor\sqrt{2}n^{2}\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\lfloor(\log n)^{2}\rfloor}}f_{1}\cdot T_{1}^{{\lfloor n^{2/3}\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\lfloor\sqrt{3}n^{2}\rfloor}}f_{2}\\
\approx\E_{N\in[R]}\E_{n\in[L(N)]}T_{1}^{{\lfloor\sqrt{2}(N+n)^{2}\rfloor}}\mathopen{}\left(T_{2}^{{\lfloor(\log N)^{2}\rfloor}}f_{1}\mathclose{}\right)\cdot T_{2}^{{\lfloor\sqrt{3}(N+n)^{2}\rfloor}}\mathopen{}\left(T_{1}^{{\lfloor N^{2/3}\rfloor}}f_{2}\mathclose{}\right).

The average over
n
thus becomes a polynomial average, and we control it by an average of (finitary versions of) generalized box seminorms using the finitary results from Section
7
. Then we move to the average over
N
, which contains the sublinear part of our Hardy sequences, and we handle it using Proposition
6.3
. Once the sublinear part is dealt with, we concatenate the average of finitary generalized box seminorms obtained in the first step using results from Section
8
. This last step constitutes a marked departure from how the analogous argument in the single transformation case has been concluded in
[
66
]
. In that work, a simple PET argument sufficed to control the average of seminorms by a Host-Kra seminorm; in our case, we need the full power of the results from Sections
7
-
8
to finish the argument.

1.6.7.
Seminorm estimates in the general case

All these proofs, already formidably technical, fade in comparison with the proofs from Section
10
that complete the proof of Theorem
10.1
, the precise form of Theorem
1.15
. The bulk of the section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
10.2
, seminorm estimates for general averages (
11
) twisted by dual sequences. Theorem
10.2
is the main component in the proof of Theorem
10.1
with the caveat that the former requires
\mathbf{a}_{1}
(or more generally, at least one of
\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{\ell}
) to have positive fractional degree. The missing case of Theorem
10.1
in which all
\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{\ell}
are subfractional is supplied by Corollary
6.4
.

The starting point of the proof of Theorem
10.2
is to split the average into short intervals as in (
10
) and Taylor approximate the sequences
\mathbf{a}_{j}
by polynomials on each short interval. Having passed to short intervals, we use the PET results for polynomials from Section
7
to control the finite averages over
n
by a finite average (over integer tuples
{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{h}}
) of finitary generalized box seminorms. It then takes a tremendous amount of effort to massage these resulting expressions until the directions of these finitary seminorms get realized as Hardy sequences in
N
. As it turns out, these new Hardy sequences are sums of polynomial and sublinear terms. This clever reduction, originally performed for a single transformation in
[
66
]
, allows us to use Proposition
9.1
in order to bound the average over
N
. The result of these maneuvers is an average (over the tuples
{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{h}}
obtained at the previous step) of generalized box seminorms whose directions are polynomials with coefficients coming from the set of (appropriately defined) “leading coefficients” of
\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{\ell}
. A final application of the concatenation results from Section
8
gives us a single generalized box seminorm along the leading coefficients of
\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{\ell}
.

1.6.8.
Simultaneous Taylor approximations

Throughout Section
10
, even more so than in the previous sections, we go to considerable lengths to track the coefficients of polynomials and Hardy sequences that appear at intermediate stages. This is needed to ensure that the seminorm of
f_{1}
that ultimately controls the original average (
1
) has the “correct” directions, corresponding to the leading coefficients of
\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{\ell}
. As it transpires in the proof, we need a much finer understanding of the degrees and coefficients of simultaneous Taylor expansions than in previous works
[
11
,
24
,
25
,
50
,
61
,
66
,
67
]
. These new results on simultaneous Taylor expansions, presented in Proposition
10.3
and proved in Appendix
B
, are of independent interest and will likely play a significant role in further works on Hardy sequences.

The rough idea behind Proposition
10.3
is as follows. Throughout the proof, we split our (tuples of) Hardy sequences
\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{\ell}
into polynomial and nonpolynomial parts; we then take a basis
g_{1},\ldots,g_{m}
for the nonpolynomial part of our Hardy sequences subject to the growth condition
1\prec g_{1}(t)\prec\cdots\prec g_{m}(t)
. In order to track the coefficients of
\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{\ell}
and other intermediate sequences in terms of
g_{1}(t),\ldots,g_{m}(t),t,t^{2},\ldots
, we need to find functions
H,L
for which the Taylor approximants
g_{jN}
’s of
g_{j}
’s on the interval
(N,N+L(N)]
satisfy the following properties:

(i)

if
g_{j}
has positive fractional degree, i.e. it grows faster than
t^{\delta}
for some
\delta>0
, then the degree of
g_{jN}
exceeds some a priori chosen threshold (in particular,
g_{jN}
has higher degree than the polynomial parts of all our Hardy sequences, which is needed to separate the polynomial and nonpolynomial components of the Hardy sequences throughout the analysis);

(ii)

the Taylor approximants of any two
g_{i},g_{j}
have the same degree if and only if
g_{i},g_{j}
have the same fractional degree;

(iii)

after the change of variables
n\mapsto{\left\lfloor H(N)\right\rfloor}n+r
, the leading coefficients
\chi_{g_{j}}(N)
of
g_{jN}
are increasing, sublinear, and have distinct growth rates (without changing variables this way, they would go to 0). Only then can we invoke the polynomial + sublinear case to deal with the average over
N
.

The key difference between our Taylor approximations and those previously established for a single transformation
T
(e.g.
[
23
,
24
,
66
]
) is that in those previous works, it was sufficient for the iterates appearing in various intermediate averages to be pairwise distinct. This condition alone was enough to control the averages by some seminorm of
T
. Here, however, each iterate is a sum of a polynomial in
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[t]
and a
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
-linear combination of
g_{1},\ldots,g_{m}
; hence each coefficient could in principle correspond to a different power of
T_{1},\ldots,T_{k}
, giving rise to a different seminorm. To get seminorm estimates with correct directions, we thus need to be more delicate. It does not suffice for
sublinear Hardy sequences
\chi_{g_{1}},\ldots,\chi_{g_{m}}
to be pairwise distinct. Rather,
they need to have
distinct growth rates
. Only then can we keep track of the coefficients throughout the analysis in a way that will give us the desired seminorm estimates at the end.

While we refrain from discussing here the highly technical proof of Proposition
10.3
contained in Appendix
B
, we do point out that it considerably departs from proofs of earlier simultaneous Taylor approximation results, using ideas vaguely related to the theory of equidistribution and Diophantine approximations.

1.6.9.
Seminorm smoothing for pairwise independent polynomials

Having proved generalized box seminorm estimates for general averages of the form (
1
), we focus our attention on the somewhat more specialized case of averages (
2
) in which the Hardy sequences are pairwise independent. In Section
11
, we deduce that the latter family of averages is controlled by Host-Kra seminorms, proving Theorem
1.16
. The proof goes via a variant of the seminorm smoothing argument developed in
[
36
]
. As it turns out, the argument does not really use any particular properties of Hardy sequences other than the estimates from Theorem
10.2
and Proposition
6.3
(the latter supplies the case of all
\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{\ell}
being subfractional missing from Theorem
10.2
). We therefore write the proof of Theorem
1.16
down for an arbitrary family of sequences satisfying generalized seminorm estimates of the sort given by Theorem
10.2
and Proposition
6.3
. This more abstract formulation makes the proof cleaner and renders more general estimates (Proposition
11.3
) that may find applications beyond this paper.

While the general strategy behind the proofs in Section
11
closely resembles the strategy from
[
36
]
, quite a few of the technical tools used abundantly in
[
36
]
need to be adjusted. Perhaps the most important issue is that the degree-1 generalized box seminorms do not admit as clean inverse theorems as degree-1 Host-Kra seminorms. To see where this issue arises, consider the average

(12)

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor\sqrt{2}n^{3/2}+n\log n\right\rfloor}}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{{\left\lfloor\sqrt{3}n^{3/2}\right\rfloor}}f_{2}.

To bound it by Host-Kra seminorms, we need to invoke Host-Kra seminorm estimates for

(13)

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor\sqrt{2}n^{3/2}+n\log n\right\rfloor}}f_{1}\cdot T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor\sqrt{2}n^{3/2}\right\rfloor}}f_{2}.

If we copied blindly the argument from
[
36
]
, we would reduce from (
12
) to (
13
) by restricting to functions
f_{2}
that are
T_{1}^{-\sqrt{2}}T_{2}^{\sqrt{3}}
-invariant. However,
T_{1}^{-\sqrt{2}}T_{2}^{\sqrt{3}}
is not a well-defined transformation, and so to make this crucial reduction work, we need to find a meaningful way of talking about “
T_{1}^{-\sqrt{2}}T_{2}^{\sqrt{3}}
-invariance” in the context of our problem.

The following diagram illustrates the logical relationship between various seminorm estimates established in this paper.

2.
Notation, background on Hardy sequences, basic definitions

2.1.
Basic notation

We start with presenting basic notation used throughout the paper.

The symbols
{\mathbb{C}},{\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{Z}},{\mathbb{N}}_{0},{\mathbb{N}}
stand for the sets of complex numbers, real numbers, integers, nonnegative integers, and positive integers respectively. With
{\mathbb{T}}
, we denote the one dimensional torus, and we often identify it with
{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
or with
[0,1)
. For a ring
R
, we denote the collection of polynomials over
R
in variables
n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}
via
R[n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}]
. If
E\subseteq G
is a subset of a group
G
, then
\langle E\rangle
is the subgroup generated by
E
.

We frequently use the interval notation to mean either a real interval or its restriction to
{\mathbb{Z}}
:

(i)

for
M<N
, we set
[M,N]
to be either
\{{\left\lceil M\right\rceil},\ldots,{\left\lfloor N\right\rfloor}\}
or, occasionally, the real interval from
M
to
N
;

(ii)

for
N>0
, we set
[N]
to be either
\{1,\ldots,{\left\lfloor N\right\rfloor}\}
or, occasionally, the real interval from 0 to
N
;

(iii)

for
N>0
, we set
[\pm N]
to be either
[-{\left\lfloor N\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor N\right\rfloor}]
or, occasionally, the real interval from
-N
to
N
.

When we average over an interval
[M,N],[N],[\pm N]
or their product, then we always think of the intervals as restricted to integers; when however we look at expressions like
G\cap[\pm N]^{k}
for some real subgroup
G\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
, then
[\pm N]
always denotes a real interval (and similarly with
[N]
or
[M,N]
). In other cases, the meaning should be clear from the context.

For a finite set
E,
we define the average of a sequence
a:E\to{\mathbb{C}}
as

\E_{n\in E}a(n):=\frac{1}{|E|}\sum_{n\in E}a(n).

We will mainly use finite averages along subsets of integers of the form
[N]^{s}
or
[\pm N]^{s}
, also known as
Cesàro averages
.

We let
{\mathcal{C}}z:=\overline{z}
be the complex conjugate of
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
.

For a set
E
, we define its indicator function by
1_{E}
. Whenever convenient, we also use the notation
1(E)
for an event
E
.

Typically, we use underlined symbols
{\underline{h}}
to denote elements of
{\mathbb{R}}^{s}
(which we think as tuples of parameters), and we employ the bold notation
{\mathbf{x}}
to denote elements of
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
(which we think as points in a space); sometimes we also use bold notation to denote
s
-tuples of points in
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
, e.g.,
{\mathbf{m}}=({\mathbf{m}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{m}}_{s})\in({\mathbb{R}}^{s})^{k}
.

We often write
{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}
for a vector of 0s and 1s of length
s
. For
{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}
and
{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{s}
, we set
{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{h}}:=\epsilon_{1}h_{1}+\cdots+\epsilon_{s}h_{s}
,
\mathopen{}\left|{\underline{h}}\mathclose{}\right|:=|h_{1}|+\cdots+|h_{s}|
and

(14)

\displaystyle h_{j}^{\epsilon_{j}}=\begin{cases}h_{j},\;&\epsilon_{j}=0\\
h_{j}^{\prime},\;&\epsilon_{j}=1\end{cases}\quad\textrm{for}\quad j\in[s].

Given a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
and
{\mathbf{m}}=(m_{1},\dots,m_{k})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
, we set
{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}:=({\left\lfloor m_{1}\right\rfloor},\ldots,{\left\lfloor m_{k}\right\rfloor})
and

T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}:=T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor m_{1}\right\rfloor}}\cdots T_{k}^{{\left\lfloor m_{k}\right\rfloor}}.

For
j\in[k]
, we set
{\mathbf{e}}_{j}
to be the unit vector in
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
in the
j
-th direction, and we let
{\mathbf{e}}_{0}=\mathbf{0}
, so that
T^{{\mathbf{e}}_{j}}=T_{j}
for
j\in[k]
and
T^{{\mathbf{e}}_{0}}
is the identity transformation.

For
M>0
, we say that a function
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
is
M
-bounded
if
\left\|f\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}\leq M
.

For a
\sigma
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}
and
f\in L^{1}(\mu)
, we let
\E(f|{\mathcal{A}})
be the conditional expectation of
f
with respect to
{\mathcal{A}}
. By abuse of notation, we can identify the
\sigma
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
with the algebra of functions
L^{2}({\mathcal{A}})
. The algebras that we often work with include the
invariant factor

{\mathcal{I}}(T)=\{f\in L^{2}(\mu):\;Tf=f\},

and the
rational Kronecker factor

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T)=\bigvee_{i=1}^{\infty}{\mathcal{I}}(T^{i})=\overline{\{f\in L^{\infty}(\mu):\;T^{i}f=f\;\;\textrm{for\; some}\;\;i\in{\mathbb{N}}\}}.

Given two functions
a,b:(t_{0},\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
for some
t_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
we write

(i)

a\ll b
,
a=O(b)
,
b\gg a
, or
b=\Omega(a)
if there exists
C>0
and
t_{1}\geq t_{0}
such that
|a(t)|\leq C|b(t)|
for all
t\geq t_{1}
;

(ii)

a\asymp b
if
a\ll b
and
a\gg b
;

(iii)

a\prec b
,
b\succ a
or
a=o(b)
if
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\frac{a(t)}{b(t)}=0
;

(iv)

a\lll b
if there exists
\delta>0
such that
|a(t)|t^{\delta}\prec|b(t)|
.

If the absolute constant in (i) depends on some parameters
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}
, then we write
a\ll_{t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}}b
,
a=O_{t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}}(b)
, etc. If in (ii) we want to emphasize what parameter we are taking to infinity, we can also write
a=o_{t\to\infty;t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}}(b)
to emphasize that
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}
are kept fixed while
t
goes to infinity.

2.2.
Hardy field functions

Let
B
be the collection of equivalence classes of real valued functions defined on some halfline
(t_{0},\infty)
for
t_{0}\geq 0
, where two functions that eventually agree are identified. These equivalence classes are called
germs
of functions. A
Hardy field
is a subfield of the ring
(B,+,\cdot)
that is closed under differentiation.
5
5

5

By abuse of notation, we use the word
function
while referring to elements of
B
which in reality are germs of functions, understanding that all the operations defined and statements made for elements of
B
are considered only for sufficiently large values of
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

As in
[
66
]
, we will work with a Hardy field
\mathcal{H}_{0}
which is closed under composition and compositional
inversion of functions (whenever the latter is defined). That is, if
a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}
with
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}a_{2}(t)=\infty,
then
a_{1}\circ a_{2},

a_{2}^{-1}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}
. We say that
a\in{\mathcal{H}}
has
polynomial growth
if it satisfies
a(t)\ll t^{d}
for some
d>0
, and we then let

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}=\{a\in{\mathcal{H}}_{0}:\;a(t)\ll t^{d}\;\;\textrm{for\; some}\;\;d>0\}

be the restriction of
{\mathcal{H}}_{0}
to the functions of polynomial growth. The set
{\mathcal{H}}
is still a Hardy field closed under compositions, but it is no longer closed under inverses since, for instance,
\log^{-1}=\exp
is not in
{\mathcal{H}}
.

Usually, one considers Hardy fields
{\mathcal{H}}_{0}
that contain the class
\mathcal{LE}
of
logarithmico-exponential Hardy field functions
, i.e. functions defined on
(t_{0},\infty)
for some
t_{0}\geq 0
by a finite combination of symbols
+,-,\times,\div,\sqrt[n]{\cdot},\exp,\log
acting on the real variable
t
and on real constants. While
\mathcal{LE}
is not closed under compositional inverses, it is contained in the Hardy field of Pfaffian functions that is. See
[
24
,
25
,
41
]
for more on Hardy fields in general and
\mathcal{LE}
in particular.

We commonly refer to sequences of the form
(a(n))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
as
Hardy sequences
. Since ultimately we only care about their asymptotic behavior, we can modify their first finitely many values whenever convenient, or make them arbitrary if
a
is only defined on
[t_{0},\infty)
for some
t_{0}>0
. This is consistent with our viewpoint on Hardy functions as germs rather than individual functions.

For a Hardy function
a\in{\mathcal{H}}
, we define its
fractional degree
to be

\fracdeg a:=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{ta^{\prime}(t)}{a(t)};

the limit always exists since Hardy fields are closed under differentiation, multiplication and division, and it is a real number (see Lemma
A.3
).
If additionally
{\mathcal{H}}
contains
\log
and is closed under compositions, we can also express
\fracdeg a
slightly more intuitively as

\fracdeg a=\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\frac{\log a(t)}{\log t}

(the equivalence of the two formulations follows from the L’ Hôpital’s rule applied to the latter expression).
For instance, the functions
t^{3/2},t^{3/2}\log t,t^{3/2}/\log t,
while of different growths, all have fractional degree
3/2
. For tuples
\mathbf{a}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{k}
, we similarly define

\fracdeg\mathbf{a}:=\max_{i\in[k]}\fracdeg a_{i}.

The definition of fractional degree is quite natural as it allows us to succinctly rephrase various properties of Hardy functions. With this definition, we for instance have

\displaystyle a\lll b\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\fracdeg a<\fracdeg b,

and the condition of
a
having polynomial growth can be restated concisely as
\fracdeg a<\infty
. For a list of basic properties of fractional degrees, see Lemma
A.4
.

In order to facilitate future discussion, we name various naturally occuring properties of Hardy functions. Thus, we call
a\in{\mathcal{H}}
:

(i)

strongly nonpolynomial
if
t^{d}\prec a(t)\prec t^{d+1}
for some
d\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
;

(ii)

subfractional
if
\fracdeg a=0
, or equivalently if
a(t)\prec t^{\delta}
for every
\delta>0
;

(iii)

sublinear
if
a(t)\prec t
.

Thus,
t^{C}
is strongly nonpolynomial for any
C\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\setminus{{\mathbb{Z}}}
and sublinear for any
C<1
;
t\log t
is strongly nonpolynomial; and
(\log t)^{C}
is subfractional (and hence also sublinear) for any
C>0
. Occasionally, we also call
\mathbf{a}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{k}
subfractional (resp. sublinear) if these properties hold for each coordinate of
\mathbf{a}
.

In our PET argument, we will need to treat polynomial and strongly nonpolynomial functions separately, essentially because polynomial functions vanish after differentiating finitely many times whereas strongly nonpolynomial functions do not. Using the lemma below, we can always decompose a collection of functions from
{\mathcal{H}}^{k}
as a linear combination of polynomials, strongly nonpolynomial parts, and error terms.

Lemma 2.1

(
[
61
, Lemma A.3]
)
.

Let
k,\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\mathbf{a}_{1},...,\mathbf{a}_{\ell}\in\mathcal{H}^{k}
. Then there exist
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, functions
g_{1},...,g_{m}\in\mathcal{H}
, vectors
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j,i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
and polynomials
{\mathbf{p}}_{1},...,{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[t]
such that

(i)

g_{1}\prec g_{2}\prec...\prec g_{m}
,

(ii)

t^{d_{i}}\prec g_{i}(t)\prec t^{d_{i}+1}
for some
d_{i}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
(i.e. they are strongly nonpolynomial) and

(iii)

for all
j\in[\ell]
we have

\mathbf{a}_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j,i}g_{i}+{\mathbf{p}}_{j}+{\mathbf{r}}_{j},

where
{\mathbf{r}}_{j}(t)\to\mathbf{0}
as
t\to\infty
.

3.
Generalized box seminorms: basic theory

An important new tool in this paper is a generalization of box seminorms which is useful for studying the limiting behavior in
L^{2}(\mu)
of multiple ergodic averages of commuting transformations along Hardy sequences. Throughout the entire Section
3
, we fix a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
. Let
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
be finitely generated subgroups.
For functions
f_{\underline{\epsilon}}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
indexed by
{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}
, we define the
Gowers-Cauchy-Schwarz inner product
along
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}
to be

(15)

\displaystyle\langle(f_{\underline{\epsilon}})_{\underline{\epsilon}}\rangle_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime}\in G_{1}}\cdots\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime}\in G_{s}}\int\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}}\right\rfloor}}f_{\underline{\epsilon}}\,d\mu,

where

\E\limits_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G}=\lim\limits_{M\to\infty}\E\limits_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G\cap[\pm M]^{k}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\E\limits_{{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\in G}=\lim\limits_{M\to\infty}\E\limits_{{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\in G\cap[\pm M]^{k}}

(here and in the majority of this section,
[\pm M]
denotes the real interval from
-M
to
M
), and also

{\mathbf{m}}_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}=\begin{cases}{\mathbf{m}}_{i},\;&\epsilon_{i}=0\\
{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}_{i},\;&\epsilon_{i}=1\end{cases}

much like in (
14
). For instance, if
s=2
and
G_{i}=\langle\alpha_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}\rangle
, then

\displaystyle\langle f_{00},f_{01},f_{10},f_{11}\rangle_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1},G_{2}\end{subarray}}=\E_{m_{1},m_{1}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\E_{m_{2},m_{2}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\int

\displaystyle T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{2}m_{2}\right\rfloor}}f_{00}\cdot T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{2}m_{2}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}}\overline{f_{01}}

\displaystyle T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{2}m_{2}\right\rfloor}}\overline{f_{10}}\cdot T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{2}m_{2}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}}f_{11}\,d\mu.

It is not a priori clear that the iterated limit in (
15
) exists; we show now that this is indeed the case by proving the
L^{2}(\mu)
convergence of a much more general class of averages.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\ell,s\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
be finitely generated subgroups and
\gamma_{ji}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
for
j\in[\ell]
and
i\in[s]
. Then for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, the followings hold:

(i)

The iterated
L^{2}(\mu)
limit

(16)

\displaystyle\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{1}\in G_{1}}\cdots\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{s}\in G_{s}}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{\gamma_{j1}{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+\gamma_{js}{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}\right\rfloor}}f_{j}

exists; in fact, we can replace
G_{i}\cap[\pm M]^{k}
in the definition of
\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{i}\in G_{i}}
by any Følner sequence on
G_{i}
;
6
6

6

A
Følner sequence
on an abelian group
(G,+)
is a sequence
(I_{M})_{M}
of finite subsets of
G
so that
\lim\limits_{M\to\infty}\frac{|I_{M}\Delta(I_{M}+g)|}{|I_{M}|}=0
for all
g\in G.

(ii)

The order of taking limits does not matter, i.e. for any permutation
\sigma
on
[s]
, the limit (
16
) equals

\displaystyle\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{\sigma(1)}\in G_{\sigma(1)}}\cdots\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{\sigma(s)}\in G_{\sigma(s)}}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{\gamma_{j1}{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+\gamma_{js}{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}\right\rfloor}}f_{j}.

(iii)

The single
L^{2}(\mu)
limit

(17)

\displaystyle\E_{({\mathbf{m}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{m}}_{s})\in G_{1}\times\cdots\times G_{s}}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{\gamma_{j1}{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+\gamma_{js}{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}\right\rfloor}}f_{j}

exists along any Følner sequence on
G_{1}\times\cdots\times G_{s}
and equals (
16
).

Proof.

First, we deduce the existence of the limit in (
17
) from a straightforward multivariable generalization of
[
46
, Theorem 3.2]
,
7
7

7

More specifically,
[
46
, Theorem 3.2]
can immediately be adapted to multiple variables. Condition (i) in
[
46
, Theorem 3.2]
follows from a straightforward extension of
[
68
]
due to
[
71
]
, and Condition (ii) in
[
46
, Theorem 3.2]
can be derived in a similar way as in the proof of
[
46
, Theorem 1.4]
using classical equidistribution results on tori. We use here the fact that an expression with a linear iterate
T^{{\left\lfloor\alpha n+\beta\right\rfloor}}
can be seen as
S_{\alpha}^{n}\cdot S_{\beta},
where
(S_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
is a flow in a suitable extension of the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
(see
[
46
]
for details). Hence, as we highlighted already in the introduction, we only need to use the linear multivariable variants of the results from
[
68
,
71
]
.
and the goal of the forthcoming manipulations is to express (
17
) as an average along a Følner sequence on
{\mathbb{Z}}^{K}
for some
K\in{\mathbb{N}}
in order to apply the multivariable generalization of
[
46
, Theorem 3.2]
.

Fix a Følner sequence
(I_{M})_{M\in{\mathbb{N}}}
on

\displaystyle G=G_{1}\times\cdots\times G_{s}.

Fix also
j\in[s]
.
Each group
G_{j}
is finitely generated, and so by the fundamental theorem of finitely generated abelian groups, it is group-isomorphic to
{\mathbb{Z}}^{K_{j}}
for some
K_{j}\in{\mathbb{N}}
. We can therefore find
{\mathbf{b}}_{j1},\ldots,{\mathbf{b}}_{jK_{j}}\in G_{j}
and a group isomorphism

\displaystyle\phi_{j}:{\mathbb{Z}}^{K_{j}}

\displaystyle\to G_{j}

\displaystyle(m_{j1},\ldots,m_{jK_{j}})

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{i\in[K_{j}]}{\mathbf{b}}_{ji}m_{ji}.

Letting

\displaystyle G=G_{1}\times\cdots\times G_{s}\quad\textrm{and}\quad K=K_{1}+\cdots+K_{s},

it is then easy to see that
(\phi^{-1}(I_{M}))_{M\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is a Følner sequence on
{\mathbb{Z}}^{K}
.
Letting

A({\mathbf{m}})=\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{\gamma_{j1}{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+\gamma_{js}{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}\right\rfloor}}f_{j},

the average that we investigate equals

\displaystyle\E_{({\mathbf{m}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{m}}_{s})\in G\cap I_{M}}A({\mathbf{m}})=\E_{{\underline{m}}\in\phi^{-1}(I_{M})}A\mathopen{}\left(\phi({\underline{m}})\mathclose{}\right).

Inducting on
s
, we now prove (i), showing at each stage that the iterated limit (
16
) exists and equals (
17
). For
s=1
, the existence of the limit in (
17
