) and its independence of the choice of Følner sequence is a special case of (iii). Let
s>1
. By the induction hypothesis, we have

\displaystyle\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{2}\in G_{2}}\cdots\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{s}\in G_{s}}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{\gamma_{j1}{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+\gamma_{js}{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}\right\rfloor}}f_{j}=\E_{({\mathbf{m}}_{2},\ldots,{\mathbf{m}}_{s})\in G_{2}\times\cdots\times G_{s}}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{\gamma_{j1}{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+\gamma_{js}{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}\right\rfloor}}f_{j}

for every
{\mathbf{m}}_{1}\in G_{1}
,
where the limits can be taken along any Følner sequences. This fact together with (iii) and
[
7
, Lemma 1.1]
imply that the limit in (
16
) exists and equals the single limit in (iii). Hence (i) follows.

To see that (ii) holds, we note that the expression (
17
) does not depend on the ordering of
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}
, and hence the equality of the expressions (
17
) and (
16
) implies immediately that the indices
1,\ldots,s
can be permuted.
∎

For
{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{s}
and
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we set

\Delta_{({{\mathbf{m}}},{{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}})}f=T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}f\cdot T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}}\overline{f}.

This is a symmetric version of the classical
multiplicative derivative
. We define the
generalized box seminorm
of
f
along subgroups
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
by the formula

\displaystyle\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle:=\langle(f)_{\underline{\epsilon}}\rangle_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\end{subarray}}^{1/2^{s}}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\left(\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime}\in G_{1}}\cdots\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime}\in G_{s}}\int\Delta_{({{\mathbf{m}}_{1}},{{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}_{1}}),\ldots,({{\mathbf{m}}_{s}},{{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}_{s}})}f\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right)^{1/2^{s}}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\left(\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime}\in G_{1}}\cdots\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime}\in G_{s}}\int\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}}\right\rfloor}}f\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right)^{1/2^{s}}.

For instance, if
G_{i}=\langle\alpha_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}\rangle
, then

\displaystyle\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1}}^{2}=\E_{m_{1},m_{1}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\int T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor}}f\cdot T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}}\overline{f}\,d\mu

and

\displaystyle\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1},G_{2}\end{subarray}}^{4}=\E\limits_{m_{1},m^{\prime}_{1}\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\E\limits_{m_{2},m^{\prime}_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\int

\displaystyle T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{2}m_{2}\right\rfloor}}f\cdot T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{2}m^{\prime}_{2}\right\rfloor}}\overline{f}

\displaystyle\cdot T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{1}m^{\prime}_{1}\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{2}m_{2}\right\rfloor}}\overline{f}\cdot T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{2}m_{2}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}}f\,d\mu.

We note that the formula for
\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\end{subarray}}
is well-defined since we can rewrite it as

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime}\in G_{1}}\cdots\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{s-1},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}_{s-1}\in G_{s-1}}\int\mathopen{}\left|\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{s}\in G_{s}}\Delta_{({{\mathbf{m}}_{1}},{{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime}}),\ldots,({{\mathbf{m}}_{s-1}},{{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}_{s-1}})}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}\right\rfloor}}f\mathclose{}\right|^{2}\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right)^{1/2^{s}};

hence the expression under the root is nonnegative, which allows us to take the root in the first place.
8
8

8

From now on, because of the existence of limits and bounded convergence theorem, we will freely interchange summation with integration without mentioning it.
From the definition, we immediately get the inductive formula

(18)

\displaystyle\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\end{subarray}}^{2^{s}}=\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime}\in G_{1}}\cdots\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{s^{\prime}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}\in G_{s^{\prime}}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|\Delta_{({{\mathbf{m}}_{1}},{{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime}}),\ldots,({{\mathbf{m}}_{s^{\prime}}},{{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}})}f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{s^{\prime}+1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{2^{s-s^{\prime}}}

for each
s^{\prime}\in[s]
.

If
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
, then
\left|\!\left|\!\left|\cdot\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}
equals the usual box seminorm along
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}
as defined e.g., in
[
18
,
63
,
65
]
.

We also define another family of seminorms which will show up more naturally in the PET procedure:

\displaystyle\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle:=\mathopen{}\left(\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime}\in G_{1}}\cdots\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime}\in G_{s}}\mathopen{}\left|\int\Delta_{({{\mathbf{m}}_{1}},{{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime}}),\ldots,({{\mathbf{m}}_{s}},{{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime}})}f\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|^{2}\mathclose{}\right)^{1/2^{s+1}}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\left(\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime}\in G_{1}}\cdots\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime}\in G_{s}}\int\Delta_{({{\mathbf{m}}_{1}},{{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime}}),\ldots,({{\mathbf{m}}_{s}},{{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime}})}f\otimes\overline{f}\,d(\mu\times\mu)\mathclose{}\right)^{1/2^{s+1}}

\displaystyle=\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\otimes\overline{f}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{1/2}.

Most of the groups that we end up dealing with will have a single generator, in which case the notation can be somewhat simplified. Whenever
G_{i}=\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}\rangle
for some
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
, we set

\displaystyle\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{s}}:=\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\end{subarray}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{s}}:=\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\end{subarray}};

and if a vector
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
appears
s
times, then we abbreviate its
s
occurrences as
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{s}
, e.g.

\displaystyle\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2}}:=\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1}^{2},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2}}^{+}:=\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1}^{2},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2}}^{+}.

We also use further conventions for iterated multiplicative derivatives. Wherever convenient, we write

(19)

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{s};({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime})}=\Delta_{({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1}m_{1},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{s}m_{s},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{s}m_{s}^{\prime})}.

for
{\underline{m}}=(m_{1},\dots,m_{s}),{\underline{m}}^{\prime}=(m^{\prime}_{1},\dots,m^{\prime}_{s})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{s}
.

Because of Lemma
3.1
, we can replaced the iterated limits in the definition of generalized box seminorms by single limits, and we can permute the order of taking subgroups, obtaining the identities

(20)

\displaystyle\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{s}}=\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\sigma(s)}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{s}}=\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\sigma(s)}}

for any permutation
\sigma:[s]\to[s]
.

Like Host-Kra seminorms or box seminorms, generalized box seminorms satisfy the Gowers-Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, which we will derive from the standard finitary Gowers-Cauchy-Schwarz inequality (it can be proved e.g., by adapting
[
40
, Lemma B.2]
).

Lemma 3.2

(Finitary Gowers-Cauchy-Schwarz inequality)
.

Let
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
E_{1},\ldots,E_{s}
be finite nonempty sets,
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
be a probability space, and
A_{\underline{\epsilon}}(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s})\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
for every
(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s})\in E_{1}\times\cdots\times E_{s}
and
{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}
. Then

\mathopen{}\left|\E_{m_{1},m_{1}^{\prime}\in E_{1}}\cdots\E_{m_{s},m_{s}^{\prime}\in E_{s}}\int\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}A_{\underline{\epsilon}}(m_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}},\ldots,m_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}})\;d\mu\mathclose{}\right|^{2^{s}}\\
\leq\prod_{\underline{\omega}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}\E_{m_{1},m_{1}^{\prime}\in E_{1}}\cdots\E_{m_{s},m_{s}^{\prime}\in E_{s}}\int\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}A_{\underline{\omega}}(m_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}},\ldots,m_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}})\;d\mu.

Lemma 3.3

(Gowers-Cauchy-Schwarz inequality)
.

Let
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
be finitely generated subgroups. For all functions
f_{\underline{\epsilon}}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
indexed by
{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|\langle(f_{\underline{\epsilon}})_{\underline{\epsilon}}\rangle_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\end{subarray}}\mathclose{}\right|\leq\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{\underline{\epsilon}}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}.

Proof.

The proof follows easily from Lemma
3.2
; our ability to change the order of limits in a way that allows us to deduce the infinitary statement from the finitary one is a consequence of Lemma
3.1
.
∎

Much like it is the case for Host-Kra and box seminorms, the Gowers-Cauchy-Schwarz inequality is a crucial fact ensuring that the generalized box seminorms are indeed seminorms.

Lemma 3.4

(Triangle inequality)
.

Let
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
be finitely generated subgroups. For all
f,g\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

(21)

\displaystyle\left|\!\left|\!\left|f+g\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\end{subarray}}\leq\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\end{subarray}}+\left|\!\left|\!\left|g\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\end{subarray}}

(22)

and

\displaystyle\left|\!\left|\!\left|f+g\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\end{subarray}}^{+}\leq\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\end{subarray}}^{+}+\left|\!\left|\!\left|g\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\end{subarray}}^{+}.

Proof.

The lemma for (
21
) follows in the standard way by rewriting
\left|\!\left|\!\left|f+g\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\end{subarray}}^{2^{s}}
as a Gowers-Cauchy-Schwarz inner product along
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}
, then splitting it using multilinearity into terms involving
f
and
g
, and bounding each of them using Lemma
3.3
. For (
22
), we express
(\left|\!\left|\!\left|f+g\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}})^{2}=\left|\!\left|\!\left|(f+g)\otimes(f+g)\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}
and split

\displaystyle(f+g)\otimes\overline{(f+g)}=f\otimes\overline{f}+g\otimes\overline{f}+f\otimes\overline{g}+g\otimes\overline{g},

so that

\displaystyle(\left|\!\left|\!\left|f+g\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}})^{2}\leq\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\otimes\overline{f}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}+\left|\!\left|\!\left|g\otimes\overline{f}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}+\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\otimes\overline{g}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}+\left|\!\left|\!\left|g\otimes\overline{g}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}.

From the definition of seminorms and Lemma
3.1
, we have

\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\otimes\overline{g}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{2^{s}}=\E_{({\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,{\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime})\in G_{1}\times G_{1}\times\cdots\times G_{s}\times G_{s}}\\
\int\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}}\right\rfloor}}f\,d\mu\cdot\overline{\int\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}}\right\rfloor}}g\,d\mu},

and so by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we get

\displaystyle\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\otimes\overline{g}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}\leq\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}\cdot\left|\!\left|\!\left|g\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}.

Hence

\displaystyle(\left|\!\left|\!\left|f+g\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}})^{2}\leq(\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+})^{2}+2\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}\left|\!\left|\!\left|g\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}+(\left|\!\left|\!\left|g\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+})^{2},

and the result follows by taking square roots on both sides.
∎

Similarly to Host-Kra and box seminorms, generalized box seminorms satisfy an important monotonicity property.

Lemma 3.5

(Monotonicity property)
.

Let
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s+1}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
be finitely generated subgroups. For all
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}\leq\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s+1}}

and

\displaystyle\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}\leq\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s+1}}^{+}.

Proof.

The second inequality follows from the first and the fact that
\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}=\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\otimes\overline{f}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{1/2}
while the first one is a standard consequence of the Gowers-Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.
∎

The following lemma gives sufficient conditions for two generalized box seminorms to agree.

Lemma 3.6

(Invariance property)
.

Let
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
G_{1},G_{1}^{\prime}\ldots,G_{s},G_{s}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
be finitely generated subgroups with the property that

(23)

\displaystyle\E_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G_{i}}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}f=\E_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G_{i}^{\prime}}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}f

for every
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
and
i\in[s]
.
Then

\displaystyle\langle(f_{\underline{\epsilon}})_{\underline{\epsilon}}\rangle_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}=\langle(f_{\underline{\epsilon}})_{\underline{\epsilon}}\rangle_{G^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,G^{\prime}_{s}}

for all
f_{\underline{\epsilon}}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
indexed by
{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}
.

Proof.

We write
{\underline{\epsilon}}=(\tilde{{\underline{\epsilon}}},\epsilon_{s})
, so that
\langle(f_{\underline{\epsilon}})_{\underline{\epsilon}}\rangle_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}
equals

\displaystyle\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime}\in G_{1}}\cdots\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{s-1},{\mathbf{m}}_{s-1}^{\prime}\in G_{s-1}}\int

\displaystyle\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{s}\in G_{s}}\prod_{\tilde{{\underline{\epsilon}}}\in\{0,1\}^{s-1}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|\tilde{{\underline{\epsilon}}}|}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s-1}^{\epsilon_{s-1}}\right\rfloor}+{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}\right\rfloor}}f_{\tilde{{\underline{\epsilon}}}0}

\displaystyle\E_{{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}_{s}\in G_{s}}\prod_{\tilde{{\underline{\epsilon}}}\in\{0,1\}^{s-1}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|\tilde{{\underline{\epsilon}}}|}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s-1}^{\epsilon_{s-1}}\right\rfloor}+{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}}\overline{f_{\tilde{{\underline{\epsilon}}}1}}\,d\mu.

Using (
23
) for
G_{s}
, we rewrite the two averages inside the integral as averages over
G_{s}^{\prime}
instead. It follows that

\displaystyle\langle(f_{\underline{\epsilon}})_{\underline{\epsilon}}\rangle_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s-1},G_{s}}=\langle(f_{\underline{\epsilon}})_{\underline{\epsilon}}\rangle_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s-1},G^{\prime}_{s}}.

Using the fact that we can swap the order of summation in the formula for Gowers-Cauchy-Schwarz inner product thanks to Lemma
3.1
, we iterate the procedure over the indices
i=1,\ldots,s-1
, obtaining the claim.
∎

Much like for box seminorms, we can compare the generalized box seminorm along
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}
to a seminorm along its subgroups.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
G_{1},G_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,G_{s},G_{s}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
be finitely generated subgroups such that
G^{\prime}_{i}\subseteq G_{i}
for each
i\in[s]
. For every 1-bounded function
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}\ll_{k,s}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,G^{\prime}_{s}}^{+}.

Conversely, if the index of
G^{\prime}_{i}
in
G_{i}
is at most
D
for all
i\in[s]
, then

\displaystyle\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,G^{\prime}_{s}}^{+}\ll_{D,s}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}.

Proof.

We prove both inequalities for
s=1
(in which case we simply write
G^{\prime}=G^{\prime}_{1}
, and
G=G_{1}
) since the cases
s>1
follow by induction using the inductive formula (
18
) and the fact that we can permute the order of groups in the definition of box norms (Lemma
3.1
(ii)).

For the first inequality, we first note that if
G^{\prime}\subseteq G
, then

\displaystyle\E_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G}A({\mathbf{m}})=\E_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G}A({\mathbf{m}}+{\mathbf{k}})

for every
{\mathbf{k}}\in G^{\prime}
(as long as the first limit exists), and hence

(24)

\displaystyle\E_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G}A({\mathbf{m}})=\E_{{\mathbf{k}}\in G}\E_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G}A({\mathbf{m}}+{\mathbf{k}}).

Now, by definition of the degree 1 seminorm, we have

(25)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G}^{+}\mathclose{}\right)^{4}

\displaystyle=\int\E_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G}(T\times T)^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}f\otimes\overline{f}\cdot\E_{{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\in G}(T\times T)^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}}\overline{f}\otimes f\;d(\mu\times\mu).

The identity (
24
) and Lemma
3.1
give

\displaystyle\E_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G}(T\times T)^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}\overline{f}\otimes f=\E_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G}\E_{{\mathbf{k}}\in G^{\prime}}(T\times T)^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}+{\mathbf{k}}\right\rfloor}}\overline{f}\otimes f.

Inserting this identity into (
25
) and applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we get

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G}^{+}\mathclose{}\right)^{4}

\displaystyle\leq\E_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G}\int\mathopen{}\left|\E_{{\mathbf{k}}\in G^{\prime}}(T\times T)^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}+{\mathbf{k}}\right\rfloor}}\overline{f}\otimes f\mathclose{}\right|^{2}\;d(\mu\times\mu).

This looks almost like what we aim for except for the presence of
{\mathbf{m}}
. We can remove it by composing the integral with
(T\times T)^{-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}
, swapping the subtraction inside the fractional part and making the error term
{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}+{\mathbf{k}}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}
independent of
{\mathbf{m}}
and
{\mathbf{k}}
using the pigeonhole principle (this causes a loss in the multiplicative factor of
O_{k}(1)
since there are that many choices for the error term). This gives us a bound of the form

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G}^{+}\mathclose{}\right)^{4}

\displaystyle\ll_{k}\int\E_{{\mathbf{k}}\in G^{\prime}}(T\times T)^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{k}}\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}}\overline{f}\otimes f\cdot\E_{{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}\in G^{\prime}}(T\times T)^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}^{\prime}}\overline{f}\otimes f\;d(\mu\times\mu)

for some
{\mathbf{r}},{\mathbf{r}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
. These error terms can be removed using an application of the Gowers-Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, after which the claimed inequality follows.

For the converse direction, assume that
the index of
G^{\prime}
in
G
is at most
D
. If the averages of a function
A:G\to[0,\infty)
along
G,G^{\prime}
converge, then

\displaystyle\E_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G^{\prime}}A({\mathbf{m}})

\displaystyle=\lim_{M\to\infty}\frac{1}{|G^{\prime}\cap[\pm M]^{k}|}\sum_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G^{\prime}\cap[\pm M]^{k}}A({\mathbf{m}})

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{M\to\infty}\frac{D}{|G\cap[\pm M]^{k}|}\sum_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G\cap[\pm M]^{k}}A({\mathbf{m}})=D\cdot\E_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G}A({\mathbf{m}});

the point is that nonnegativity of
A
allows us to extend the summation from
G^{\prime}
to
G
. Applying this argument twice to averages over
{\mathbf{m}}
and
{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}
, we get that

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G^{\prime}}^{+}\mathclose{}\right)^{4}=\E_{{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\in G^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\left|\int\Delta_{({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})}f\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|^{2}\ll_{D}\E_{{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\in G}\mathopen{}\left|\int\Delta_{({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})}f\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|^{2}=\mathopen{}\left(\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G}^{+}\mathclose{}\right)^{4},

and the claim follows.
∎

Generalized box seminorms are also essentially invariant under rescalings of the groups, as indicated by the result below.

Lemma 3.8

(Rescaling property)
.

Let
k,K,s\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\alpha\geq 1
be a real number,
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
be finitely generated subgroups such that for every
i\in[s]
, we have
G_{i}=\langle{\mathbf{g}}_{i1},\ldots,{\mathbf{g}}_{iK_{i}}\rangle
with
K_{i}\leq K
. For
i\in[s],j\in[K_{i}]
, let
\alpha_{ij}\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}
satisfy
1/\alpha\leq\alpha_{ij}\leq\alpha
, and let

\displaystyle G^{\prime}_{i}=\langle\alpha_{i1}{\mathbf{g}}_{i1},\ldots,\alpha_{iK_{i}}{\mathbf{g}}_{iK_{i}}\rangle.

Then for any
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,G^{\prime}_{s}}\asymp_{\alpha,k,K,s}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}.

Whenever
s\geq 2
, we also have

\displaystyle\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,G^{\prime}_{s}}\asymp_{\alpha,k,K,s}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}.

Proof.

We only prove the first statement, as the second follows similarly.

We prove the forward inequality
\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,G^{\prime}_{s}}\ll_{\alpha,K,s}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}
for
s=1
, and the higher degree case will follow from the inductive formula (
18
) and the fact that we can permute the subgroups thanks to Lemma
3.1
. The converse inequality then follows by inverting the roles of subgroups
G_{i},G_{i}^{\prime}
. We let all the constants depend on
\alpha,K,s
.

Recall that

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G^{\prime}}^{+}\mathclose{}\right)^{4}

\displaystyle=\E_{{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\in\langle\alpha_{1}{\mathbf{g}}_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}{\mathbf{g}}_{K}\rangle}\mathopen{}\left|\int\Delta_{({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})}f\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|^{4}

\displaystyle=\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{K}\end{subarray}}\mathopen{}\left|\int\Delta_{(\alpha_{1}{\mathbf{g}}_{1}m_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{K}{\mathbf{g}}_{K}m_{K},\alpha_{1}{\mathbf{g}}_{1}m^{\prime}_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{K}{\mathbf{g}}_{K}m^{\prime}_{K})}f\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|^{4}

\displaystyle=\int\mathopen{}\left|\E_{{\underline{m}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{K}}(T\times T)^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{1}{\mathbf{g}}_{1}m_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{K}{\mathbf{g}}_{K}m_{K}\right\rfloor}}f\otimes\overline{f}\mathclose{}\right|^{2}\;d(\mu\times\mu),

where we are dropping the indices
i
for clarity (here,
[\pm M]
denotes the appropriate integer interval).
We observe first that

(26)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|{\left\lfloor\alpha_{1}{\mathbf{g}}_{1}m_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{K}{\mathbf{g}}_{K}m_{K}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{g}}_{1}{\left\lfloor\alpha_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\mathbf{g}}_{K}{\left\lfloor\alpha_{K}m_{K}\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}\mathclose{}\right|\ll 1,

and so by the pigeonhole principle, there exist
{\mathbf{r}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
and a set
E\subset{\mathbb{Z}}^{K}
such that

\mathopen{}\left(\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G^{\prime}}^{+}\mathclose{}\right)^{4}\ll\int\mathopen{}\left|\E_{{\underline{m}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{K}}1_{E}({\underline{m}})\cdot(T\times T)^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{g}}_{1}{\left\lfloor\alpha_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\mathbf{g}}_{K}{\left\lfloor\alpha_{K}m_{K}\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}}f\otimes\overline{f}\mathclose{}\right|^{2}\;d(\mu\times\mu)

(
E
consists of those tuples for which the difference between two tuples in (
26
) is
{\mathbf{r}}
).
By composing with
(T\times T)^{-{\mathbf{r}}}
, we can assume that
{\mathbf{r}}=\mathbf{0}
.
Each integer takes the form
{\left\lfloor\alpha_{i}m_{i}\right\rfloor}
for at most
O(1)
many
m_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and hence

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G^{\prime}}^{+}\mathclose{}\right)^{4}\ll\int\mathopen{}\left|\E_{{\underline{m}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{K}}c_{{\underline{m}}}\cdot(T\times T)^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{g}}_{1}m_{1}+\cdots+{\mathbf{g}}_{K}m_{K}\right\rfloor}}f\otimes\overline{f}\mathclose{}\right|^{2}\;d(\mu\times\mu)

for some nonnegative
O(1)
-bounded weight
c_{{\underline{m}}}
. The right-hand side can then be expanded as

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{K}\end{subarray}}c_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\cdot\mathopen{}\left|\int\Delta_{({\mathbf{g}}_{1}m_{1}+\cdots+{\mathbf{g}}_{K}m_{K},{\mathbf{g}}_{1}m^{\prime}_{1}+\cdots+{\mathbf{g}}_{K}m^{\prime}_{K})}f\mathclose{}\right|^{2}\;d\mu

for some nonnegative,
O(1)
-bounded weight
c_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime}}
. The weight can be removed by non-negativity at the cost of losing a factor
O(1)
, and the result follows.
∎

Last but not least, the two families of seminorms that we have defined, those with and without plus, are related to each other as follows.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s+1}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
be finitely generated subgroups. For every
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}\leq\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}\leq\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s+1}}.

Proof.

The first inequality follows from a straightforward application of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. For the second inequality, we prove the case
s=1
, and the case
s>1
will follow from the inductive formula (
18
). By definition, we have

\displaystyle(\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1}}^{+})^{4}=\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime}\in G_{1}}\mathopen{}\left|\int\Delta_{({\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime})}f\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|^{2}.

For every subgroup
G_{2}
, we can introduce an extra averaging over
G_{2}
so that

\displaystyle\int\Delta_{({\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime})}f\,d\mu=\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{2}\in G_{2}}\int T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{2}\right\rfloor}}\Delta_{({\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime})}f\,d\mu,

and hence

\displaystyle(\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1}}^{+})^{4}=\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime}\in G_{1}}\mathopen{}\left|\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{2}\in G_{2}}\int T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{2}\right\rfloor}}\Delta_{({\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime})}f\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|^{2}.

By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have

\displaystyle(\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1}}^{+})^{4}\leq\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime}\in G_{1}}\int\mathopen{}\left|\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{2}\in G_{2}}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{2}\right\rfloor}}\Delta_{({\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime})}f\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|^{2},

and since
\int\mathopen{}\left|\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{2}\in G_{2}}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{2}\right\rfloor}}\Delta_{({\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime})}f\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|^{2}=\left|\!\left|\!\left|\Delta_{({\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime})}f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{2}}^{2}
,
we get the claimed inequality from the inductive formula (
18
).
∎

3.1.
Dual functions and sequences

To generalized box seminorms we can assign a notion of dual functions that naturally extends dual functions for Host-Kra seminorms. Let
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\{0,1\}^{s}_{*}=\{0,1\}^{s}\setminus\{\underline{0}\}
. For a function
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
and a finitely generated subgroup
G\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
we define the
level-
s
dual function of
f
with respect to
G
to be

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{s,G}(f):=\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,{\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s^{\prime}}\in G_{1}}\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}_{*}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}\right\rfloor}}f;

by Lemma
3.1
, the iterated limit exists and equals a single limit. Thus, for instance,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{1,G}(f)

\displaystyle=\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime}\in G}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}_{1}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}\right\rfloor}}\overline{f}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{2,G}(f)

\displaystyle=\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime},{\mathbf{m}}_{2},{\mathbf{m}}_{2}^{\prime}\in G}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}_{1}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}\right\rfloor}}\overline{f}\cdot T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}_{2}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{2}\right\rfloor}}\overline{f}\cdot T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}_{1}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}\right\rfloor}+{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}_{2}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{2}\right\rfloor}}f.

Dual functions satisfy the identity

(27)

\displaystyle\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{s,G}^{2^{s}}=\int f\cdot{\mathcal{D}}_{s,G}(f)\,d\mu,

which justifies their name.

If
G=\langle{\mathbf{b}}\rangle
for some
{\mathbf{b}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
, we simply write
{\mathcal{D}}_{s,{\mathbf{b}}}(f)={\mathcal{D}}_{s,G}(f)
; similarly, if
{\mathbf{b}}={\mathbf{e}}_{j}
, then we may write
{\mathcal{D}}_{s,T_{j}}(f)={\mathcal{D}}_{s,{\mathbf{b}}}(f)
. In the latter case, the dual functions take the better known form

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{s,T_{j}}(f):=\lim_{M\to\infty}\E_{{\underline{m}}\in[\pm M]^{s}}\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}_{*}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T_{j}^{{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{m}}}f;

the average over
[\pm M]^{s}
can also be replaced by the one-sided average over
[M]^{s}
.

For
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we denote

(28)

{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}(T_{1},\dots,T_{k}):=\{(T_{j}^{n}{\mathcal{D}}_{s,T_{j}}(f))_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\colon\;\left\|f\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}\leq 1,\ j\in[k],\ s\in[d]\}

to be the set of sequences of 1-bounded functions coming from dual functions of degree up to
d
for the transformations
T_{1},\ldots,T_{k}
. We call each
(T_{j}^{n}{\mathcal{D}}_{s,T_{j}}(f))_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
a
level-
s
dual sequence
. When there is no confusion regarding the system
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T_{1},\dots,T_{k})
, we write
{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}:={\mathfrak{D}}_{d}(T_{1},\dots,T_{k})
for short.

4.
Quantitative concatenation of generalized box seminorms

Our goal now is to present concatenation results for averages of generalized box seminorms. Contrary to the original concatenation work of Tao-Ziegler
[
65
]
which introduced the notion of concatenation of seminorms for the first time, our result is fully quantitative. Its proof closely follows (and significantly borrows from) recent quantitative concatenation arguments in the discrete setting
[
52
,
53
,
59
,
60
]
. Once again, we fix a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
throughout this section.

One of the key tools in our argument is the following lemma which allows us to concatenate averages along sumsets.

Lemma 4.1

(Sumset lemma)
.

Let
G,G^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
be finitely generated groups. Then

\displaystyle\E_{{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\in G^{\prime}}\E_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}-{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}}f=\E_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G+G^{\prime}}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}f

for every
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
.

Proof.

Let
G_{0}=G\cap G^{\prime}
; we note that
G_{0}
is finitely generated itself since it is a subgroup of a finitely generated abelian group
G
. Our first goal is to decompose

(29)

\displaystyle G=G_{0}\oplus K\oplus E\quad\textrm{and}\quad G^{\prime}=G_{0}\oplus K^{\prime}\oplus E^{\prime}

for some subgroups
K\subseteq G,\;K^{\prime}\subseteq G^{\prime}
and finite sets
E\subseteq G,\;E^{\prime}\subseteq G^{\prime}
. If
G/G_{0}
is finite, then there exists a finite set
E\subseteq G
for which
G=G_{0}\oplus E
. Otherwise
G/G_{0}
is infinite, and since it is finitely generated,
9
9

9

A quotient
G/G_{0}
of a finitely generated group
G
is finitely generated since if
{\mathbf{g}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{g}}_{n}
generate
G
, then their cosets generate
G/G_{0}
.
there must exist
{\mathbf{g}}_{1}\in G
such that
\langle{\mathbf{g}}_{1}\rangle
intersects
G_{0}
trivially. Next, either
G/(G_{0}\oplus\langle{\mathbf{g}}_{1}\rangle)
is finite, and so
G=G_{0}\oplus\langle{\mathbf{g}}_{1}\rangle\oplus E
for some finite set
E
, or there exists
{\mathbf{g}}_{2}\in G
such that
\langle{\mathbf{g}}_{2}\rangle
intersects
G_{0}\oplus\langle{\mathbf{g}}_{1}\rangle
trivially. Iterating this way, we find a subgroup
K\subseteq G
and a set
E\subseteq G
satisfying the claim; similarly for
G^{\prime}
.

As a consequence of (
29
), we can decompose each
{\mathbf{m}}\in G+G^{\prime}
uniquely as

(30)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{m}}={\mathbf{g}}+{\mathbf{k}}+{\mathbf{e}}-{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}-{\mathbf{e}}^{\prime}

for
{\mathbf{g}}\in G_{0},

{\mathbf{k}}\in K
,
{\mathbf{e}}\in E
,
{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}\in K^{\prime}
,
{\mathbf{e}}^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
.

Given any
{\mathbf{g}}^{\prime}\in G_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle\E_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G+G^{\prime}}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}f=\E_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G+G^{\prime}}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}-{\mathbf{g}}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}}f=\E_{{\mathbf{g}}^{\prime}\in G_{0}}\E_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G+G^{\prime}}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}-{\mathbf{g}}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}}f,

which by the Fubini-type principle
[
7
, Lemma 1.1]
equals

(31)

\displaystyle\E_{({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{g}}^{\prime})\in(G+G^{\prime})\times G_{0}}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}-{\mathbf{g}}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}}f.

Given the uniqueness of the decomposition (
30
), for every Følner sequence
(I_{M})_{M}
on
(G+G^{\prime})\times G_{0}
, we define

\displaystyle\tilde{I}_{M}=\{({\mathbf{g}}+{\mathbf{k}}+{\mathbf{e}},{\mathbf{g}}^{\prime}+{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}+{\mathbf{e}}^{\prime})\in G\times G^{\prime}:\;({\mathbf{g}}+{\mathbf{k}}+{\mathbf{e}}-{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}-{\mathbf{e}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{g}}^{\prime})\in I_{M}\};

it is an easy exercise to see that
(\tilde{I}_{M})_{M}
is a Følner sequence on
G\times G^{\prime}
. Hence (
31
) can be rephrased

\displaystyle\E_{({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})\in G\times G^{\prime}}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}-{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}}f=\E_{{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\in G^{\prime}}\E_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}-{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}}f,

the latter equality being once again a consequence of
[
7
, Lemma 1.1]
.
∎

In order to illustrate better how concatenation works, we start with a simple concatenation result for degree 1 seminorms.

Lemma 4.2

(Concatenation of degree 1 norms)
.

Let
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
I
be a finite indexing set and
G_{i}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
be a finitely generated subgroup for each
i\in I
. Then for every 1-bounded function
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(\E_{i\in I}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{i}}\mathclose{}\right)^{16}\ll_{k}\E_{i,i^{\prime}\in I}(\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{i}+G_{i^{\prime}}})^{4}.

In this and subsequent results in this section, the implicit constants and
O(1)
terms do not depend on the system. Also, we frequently use Lemma
3.1
(without mentioning it explicitly) to swap the order of limits, replace an iterated limit by a single limit, or do the opposite.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon=\E_{i\in I}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{i}}
. Composing the emerging integral with
T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}}
and expanding the definition of the seminorm, we get

\displaystyle\E_{i\in I}\E_{{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\in G_{i}}\mathopen{}\left|\int f\cdot T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}\overline{f}\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|^{2}=\varepsilon^{4}.

Passing to the product system allows us to get rid of the absolute value, giving

\displaystyle\int f\otimes\overline{f}\cdot\E_{i\in I}\E_{{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\in G_{i}}(T\times T)^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}\overline{f}\otimes f\,d(\mu\times\mu)=\varepsilon^{4}.

An application of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality duplicates the group
G_{i}
, giving

\displaystyle\E_{i,i^{\prime}\in I}\E_{{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\in G_{i}}\E_{{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\in G_{i^{\prime}}}\int(T\times T)^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}f\otimes\overline{f}\cdot(T\times T)^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}}\overline{f}\otimes f\,d(\mu\times\mu)\geq\varepsilon^{8}.

We now compose the integral with
(T\times T)^{-({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor})}
and change the order of summations, so that

\displaystyle\E_{i,i^{\prime}\in I}\int\E_{{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\in G_{i}}\E_{{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\in G_{i^{\prime}}}f\otimes\overline{f}\cdot(T\times T)^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}+{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}\overline{f}\otimes f\,d(\mu\times\mu)\geq\varepsilon^{8}.

By going back from the product system to the original system, we can easily see that each integral above is real and nonnegative, and so we employ
the pigeonhole principle to choose
{\mathbf{r}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
with
|{\mathbf{r}}|\leq k
independently of
i,i^{\prime},{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}
so that

\displaystyle\E_{i,i^{\prime}\in I}\int f\otimes\overline{f}\cdot\E_{{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\in G_{i}}\E_{{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\in G_{i^{\prime}}}(T\times T)^{{\left\lfloor({\mathbf{m}}-{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}-{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime}+{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime})\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}}\overline{f}\otimes f\,d(\mu\times\mu)\gg_{k}\varepsilon^{8}.

Splitting the double average
\E_{{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\in G_{i}}
as
\E_{{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\in G_{i}}\E_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G_{i}}
(and similarly for the average over
G_{i^{\prime}}
) and changing variables
{\mathbf{m}}_{0}={\mathbf{m}}-{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}
as well as
{\mathbf{m}}_{1}={\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime}-{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}
(for each fixed
{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\in G_{i},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\in G_{i}
), we get

\displaystyle\E_{i,i^{\prime}\in I}\int f\otimes\overline{f}\cdot\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{0}\in G_{i}}\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{1}\in G_{i^{\prime}}}(T\times T)^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{0}-{\mathbf{m}}_{1}\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}}\overline{f}\otimes f\,d(\mu\times\mu)\gg_{k}\varepsilon^{8}.

By Lemma
4.1
, we can replace the iterated limit by a single limit over the sumset
G_{i}+G_{i^{\prime}}
, getting

\displaystyle\E_{i,i^{\prime}\in I}\int f\otimes\overline{f}\cdot\E_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G_{i}+G_{i^{\prime}}}(T\times T)^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}}\overline{f}\otimes f\,d(\mu\times\mu)\gg_{k}\varepsilon^{8}.

The claim follows from one more application of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality followed by composing the resulting integral with
(T\times T)^{-{\mathbf{r}}}
.
∎

Example 4.3
.

Let
G_{h_{1},h_{2}}=\langle\alpha{\mathbf{e}}_{1}h_{1}+\beta{\mathbf{e}}_{2}h_{2}\rangle
for all
(h_{1},h_{2})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
. Then Lemma
4.2
gives

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(\limsup_{H\to\infty}\E_{h_{1},h_{2}\in[\pm H]}{\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{h_{1},h_{2}}}}\mathclose{}\right)^{16}\ll_{k}\limsup_{H\to\infty}\E_{h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime},h_{2},h^{\prime}_{2}\in[\pm H]}{\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{h_{1},h_{2}}+G_{h_{1}^{\prime},h_{2}^{\prime}}}}.

For all
(h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime},h_{2},h_{2}^{\prime})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{4}
, the group
G_{h_{1},h_{2}}+G_{h_{1}^{\prime},h_{2}^{\prime}}
contains the subgroup

\displaystyle G^{\prime}_{h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime},h_{2},h_{2}^{\prime}}=\langle\alpha{\mathbf{e}}_{1}(h_{1}h_{2}^{\prime}-h_{1}^{\prime}h_{2}),\beta{\mathbf{e}}_{2}(h_{1}h_{2}^{\prime}-h_{1}^{\prime}h_{2})\rangle,

and hence

\displaystyle\limsup_{H\to\infty}\mathopen{}\left(\E_{h_{1},h_{2}\in[\pm H]}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{h_{1},h_{2}}}\mathclose{}\right)^{16}\ll_{k}\limsup_{H\to\infty}\E_{h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime},h_{2},h^{\prime}_{2}\in[\pm H]}{\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G^{\prime}_{h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime},h_{2},h_{2}^{\prime}}}}

by Lemma
3.7
. Importantly, the subgroups
G^{\prime}_{h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime},h_{2},h_{2}^{\prime}}
are finite index subgroups of
\langle\alpha{\mathbf{e}}_{1},\beta{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle
for all
(h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime},h_{2},h_{2}^{\prime})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{4}
except a zero density set of tuples satisfying
h_{1}h_{2}^{\prime}-h_{1}^{\prime}h_{2}=0
, and so by Lemma
3.7
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{H\to\infty}\E_{h_{1},h_{2}\in[\pm H]}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{h_{1},h_{2}}}=0\quad\textrm{whenever}\quad\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{\langle\alpha{\mathbf{e}}_{1},\beta{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}=0.

Later on, we will quantify this reasoning, showing that in fact

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(\limsup_{H\to\infty}\E_{h_{1},h_{2}\in[\pm H]}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{h_{1},h_{2}}}\mathclose{}\right)^{O(1)}\ll_{k}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{\langle\alpha{\mathbf{e}}_{1},\beta{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}.

The argument becomes more complicated when we deal with averages of generalized box seminorms of degree
s>1
. In handling this general case, we will iteratively use the lemma below, which is an ergodic version of
[
53
, Lemma 2.2]
and
[
52
, Lemma 6.1]
.
Its proof relies on rather elementary maneuvers that involve the Gowers-Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, multiple applications of the inductive formula (
18
) and a simple change of variables.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
I
be a finite indexing set and
G_{i},K_{1i},\ldots,K_{si}
be finitely generated subgroups of
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
for each
i\in I
. For each 1-bounded function
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left({\E_{i\in I}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{i},K_{1i},\ldots,K_{si}}^{+}}\mathclose{}\right)^{2^{3s+4}}\ll_{k,s}\E_{i,i^{\prime}\in I}\mathopen{}\left(\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{K_{1i},\ldots,K_{si},K_{1i^{\prime}},\ldots,K_{si^{\prime}},G_{i}+G_{i^{\prime}}}^{+}\mathclose{}\right)^{2^{2s+2}}.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon={\E_{i\in I}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{i},K_{1i},\ldots,K_{si}}^{+}}
and
K_{i}=K_{1i}\times\cdots\times K_{si}
.
Using the Hölder inequality, expanding the definition of the seminorm and passing to the product system, we obtain

\displaystyle\E_{i\in I}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{k}},{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}\in K_{i}\end{subarray}}\E_{{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\in G_{i}}\int\Delta_{({\mathbf{k}}_{1},{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}_{1}),\ldots,({\mathbf{k}}_{s},{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}_{s}),({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})}\tilde{f}\;d\tilde{\mu}\geq\varepsilon^{2^{s+2}},

where
\tilde{T}=T\times T,\;\tilde{f}=f\otimes\overline{f}
and
\tilde{\mu}=\mu\times\mu
. Composing the integral with
\tilde{T}^{-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}
and changing the order of summations, we obtain

\int\tilde{f}\cdot\E_{i\in I}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{k}},{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}\in K_{i}\end{subarray}}\E_{{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\in G_{i}}\tilde{T}^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}\overline{\tilde{f}}\\
\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}_{\ast}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{k}}_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{k}}_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}}\right\rfloor}}(\tilde{f}\cdot\tilde{T}^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}\overline{\tilde{f}})\;d\tilde{\mu}\geq\varepsilon^{2^{s+2}},

where we recall that
\{0,1\}^{s}_{*}=\{0,1\}^{s}\setminus\{\underline{0}\}
.
We then apply the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to remove
\tilde{f}
and change the order of summation back, so that

\E_{i,i^{\prime}\in I}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\in G_{i},\\
{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\in G_{i^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{k}},{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}\in K_{i},\\
{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime},{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\in K_{i^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}\int\tilde{T}^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}\overline{\tilde{f}}\cdot\tilde{T}^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}}{\tilde{f}}\\
\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}_{\ast}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{k}}_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{k}}_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}}\right\rfloor}}(\tilde{f}\cdot\tilde{T}^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}\overline{\tilde{f}})\\
\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{s}_{\ast}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}|}T^{{\left\lfloor{{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime}_{1}}^{\epsilon^{\prime}_{1}}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\left\lfloor{{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime}_{s}}^{\epsilon^{\prime}_{s}}\right\rfloor}}(\overline{\tilde{f}}\cdot\tilde{T}^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}}\tilde{f})\;d\tilde{\mu}\geq\varepsilon^{2^{s+3}},

where

\displaystyle{\mathbf{k}}_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}=\begin{cases}{\mathbf{k}}_{i},\;&\epsilon_{i}=0\\
{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}_{i},\;&\epsilon_{i}=1\end{cases},\quad\textrm{and}\quad{{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime}_{i}}^{\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}}=\begin{cases}{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime}_{i},\;&\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}=0\\
{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i},\;&\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}=1\end{cases}.

The crucial observation is that for each fixed
i,i^{\prime}\in I
,
{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\in G_{i},
and
{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\in G_{i^{\prime}}
, the average

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{k}},{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}\in K_{i},\\
{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime},{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\in K_{i^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}\int\tilde{T}^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}\overline{\tilde{f}}\cdot\tilde{T}^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}}{\tilde{f}}\cdot\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}_{\ast}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{k}}_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{k}}_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}}\right\rfloor}}(\tilde{f}\cdot\tilde{T}^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}\overline{\tilde{f}})\\
\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{s}_{\ast}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}|}T^{{\left\lfloor{{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime}_{1}}^{\epsilon^{\prime}_{1}}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\left\lfloor{{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime}_{s}}^{\epsilon^{\prime}_{s}}\right\rfloor}}(\overline{\tilde{f}}\cdot\tilde{T}^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}}\tilde{f})\;d\tilde{\mu}

is a Gowers-Cauchy-Schwarz inner product along
K_{1i},\ldots,K_{si},K_{1i^{\prime}},\ldots,K_{si^{\prime}}
; more specifically, it
can be written as

\begin{split}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{k}},{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}\in K_{i},\\
{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime},{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\in K_{i^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}\int\prod_{({\underline{\epsilon}},{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime})\in\{0,1\}^{2s}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{{\underline{\epsilon}}}|+|{{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}}|}\tilde{T}^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{k}}_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{k}}_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}}\right\rfloor}+{\left\lfloor{{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime}_{1}}^{\epsilon^{\prime}_{1}}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\left\lfloor{{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime}_{s}}^{\epsilon^{\prime}_{s}}\right\rfloor}}\tilde{g}_{{\underline{\epsilon}},{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}}\,d\tilde{\mu}\end{split}

for some functions
\tilde{g}_{{\underline{\epsilon}},{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}}=g_{{\underline{\epsilon}},{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}}\otimes\overline{g_{{\underline{\epsilon}},{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}}}
with

(32)

\displaystyle\tilde{g}_{\underline{{0}},\underline{{0}},{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{T}^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}\overline{\tilde{f}}\cdot\tilde{T}^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}}{\tilde{f}}

\displaystyle=({T}^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}\overline{{f}}\cdot\tilde{T}^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}}{{f}})\otimes({T}^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}{{f}}\cdot\tilde{T}^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}}\overline{{f}}).

By the Gowers-Cauchy-Schwarz inequality (Lemma
3.3
) and a change of the order of summations, we thus have

\displaystyle\E_{i,i^{\prime}\in I}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\in G_{i},\\
{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\in G_{i^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}\int\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{k}},{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}\in K_{i},\\
{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime},{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\in K_{i^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{({\mathbf{k}},{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}),({\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime},{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime\prime})}(\tilde{T}^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}\overline{\tilde{f}}\cdot\tilde{T}^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}}{\tilde{f}})\;d\tilde{\mu}\geq\varepsilon^{2^{3s+3}}.

The product structure of the function
\tilde{f}
implies that each integral above is real and nonnegative (since it is a square of an integral over the original system). Composing the integral with
\tilde{T}^{-({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor})}
and moving all the summation and subtraction inside the integer part, we argue as in the proof of Lemma
4.2
to find
{\mathbf{r}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
such that

\displaystyle\E_{i,i^{\prime}\in I}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{m}}_{0}\in G_{i},\\
{\mathbf{m}}_{1}\in G_{i^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{k}},{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}\in K_{i},\\
{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime},{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\in K_{i^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}\int\Delta_{({\mathbf{k}},{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}),({\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime},{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime\prime})}(\overline{\tilde{f}}\cdot\tilde{T}^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}-{\mathbf{m}}_{0}\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}}{\tilde{f}})\;d\tilde{\mu}\gg_{k,s}\varepsilon^{2^{3s+3}}.

Lemma
4.1
and a rearrangement gives

\displaystyle\E_{i,i^{\prime}\in I}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{k}},{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}\in K_{i},\\
{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime},{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\in K_{i^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}\int\Delta_{({\mathbf{k}},{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}),({\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime},{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime\prime})}\overline{\tilde{f}}\cdot\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{m}}\in G_{i}+G_{i^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{({\mathbf{k}},{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}),({\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime},{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime\prime})}\tilde{T}^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}}{\tilde{f}}\;d\tilde{\mu}\gg_{k,s}\varepsilon^{2^{3s+3}}.

A final application of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality gives

\displaystyle\E_{i,i^{\prime}\in I}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{k}},{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}\in K_{i},\\
{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime},{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\in K_{i^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\in G_{i}+G_{i^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}\int\Delta_{({\mathbf{k}},{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}),({\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime},{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime\prime}),({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})}{\tilde{f}}\;d\tilde{\mu}\gg_{k,s}\varepsilon^{2^{3s+4}},

and the result follows upon moving back to the original system.
∎

By repeatedly applying Lemma
4.4
, we derive the following concatenation result, which is an ergodic version of
[
53
, Proposition 2.3]
and
[
52
, Proposition 6.2]
.

Proposition 4.5

(Concatenation of box seminorms, version I)
.

Let
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
I
be a finite indexing set and
G_{1i},\ldots,G_{si}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
be finitely generated subgroups for each
i\in I
. For all 1-bounded functions
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(\E_{i\in I}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1i},\ldots,G_{si}}^{+}\mathclose{}\right)^{O_{s}(1)}\ll_{k,s}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{\underline{\epsilon}}\in I:\\
{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}\end{subarray}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{G_{ji_{\underline{\epsilon}}}+G_{ji_{{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}}}:\ j\in[s],\ {\underline{\epsilon}},{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{s}\\
\mathrm{with}\ (\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{s-j})=(\epsilon^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\epsilon^{\prime}_{s-j}),\ \epsilon_{s+1-j}=1,\ \epsilon^{\prime}_{s+1-j}=0\}\end{subarray}}.

Proof.

The proof of Proposition
4.5
relies on a gradual concatenation of the “unconcatenated” subgroups
G_{1i},\ldots,G_{si}
in the average using Lemma
4.4
. We repeatedly use the inductive formula (
18
) in order to reinterpret the average in such a way that successive applications of Lemma
4.4
concatenate the subgroups
G_{1i},\ldots,G_{si}
one by one.

In order to simplify the notation, we shall write in this proof
({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{k}})
for what we would typically write as
({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})
; we also assume that
{\mathbf{m}}_{j}^{{\underline{\epsilon}}},{\mathbf{k}}_{j}^{{\underline{\epsilon}}}
are always elements of
G_{ji_{\underline{\epsilon}}}
and write
\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{j}^{{\underline{\epsilon}}},{\mathbf{k}}_{j}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}=\E_{({\mathbf{m}}_{j}^{{\underline{\epsilon}}},{\mathbf{k}}_{j}^{\underline{\epsilon}})\in G_{ji_{\underline{\epsilon}}}\times G_{ji_{\underline{\epsilon}}}}
.
We let all the constants depend on
k,s
, noting however that the powers of
\varepsilon
will not depend on
k
.

The case
s=2
:

For illustrative purposes, we first prove the statement of Proposition
4.5
for degree 2 box seminorms. Let
\varepsilon=\E_{i\in I}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{1i},G_{2i}}
. We first want to concatenate the group
G_{2i}
. By Lemma
4.4
, we have

(33)

\displaystyle\E_{i_{0},i_{1}\in I}{(\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{1i_{0}},G_{1i_{1}},G_{2i_{0}}+G_{2i_{1}}})}^{2^{4}}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)},

and so the group
G_{2i}
indeed got concatenated. The price we paid for this is that while applying Lemma
4.4
, the unconcatenated group
G_{1i}
doubled into
G_{1i_{0}}
and
G_{1i_{1}}
. The next goal is therefore to concatenate these groups one by one. Using the inductive formula for box seminorms and the 1-boundedness of
f
, we deduce from (
33
) that
10
10

10

The only reason why we keep the seminorm
\left|\!\left|\!\left|\Delta_{({\mathbf{m}}^{1}_{1},{\mathbf{k}}^{1}_{1})}f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{1i_{0}},G_{2i_{0}}+G_{2i_{1}}}
raised to the power
2^{s}
is so that the averages
\E_{{\mathbf{m}}^{1}_{1},{\mathbf{k}}^{1}_{1}}\E_{i_{0}\in I}\mathopen{}\left(\left|\!\left|\!\left|\Delta_{({\mathbf{m}}^{1}_{1},{\mathbf{k}}^{1}_{1})}f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{1i_{0}},G_{2i_{0}}+G_{2i_{1}}}\mathclose{}\right)^{2^{3}}
are guaranteed to converge by Lemma
3.1
. Without the power of
2^{3}
being there, the convergence would not be guaranteed.

\displaystyle\E_{i_{1}\in I}\E_{{\mathbf{m}}^{1}_{1},{\mathbf{k}}^{1}_{1}}\E_{i_{0}\in I}\mathopen{}\left(\left|\!\left|\!\left|\Delta_{({\mathbf{m}}^{1}_{1},{\mathbf{k}}^{1}_{1})}f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{1i_{0}},G_{2i_{0}}+G_{2i_{1}}}\mathclose{}\right)^{2^{3}}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

Applying Lemma
4.4
to each
11
11

11

Technically, to use Lemma
4.4
as stated, we need first to use 1-boundedness to bound
\E_{i_{0}\in I}\mathopen{}\left(\left|\!\left|\!\left|\Delta_{({\mathbf{m}}^{1}_{1},{\mathbf{k}}^{1}_{1})}f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{1i_{0}},G_{2i_{0}}+G_{2i_{1}}}\mathclose{}\right)^{2^{3}}\leq\E_{i_{0}\in I}{\left|\!\left|\!\left|\Delta_{({\mathbf{m}}^{1}_{1},{\mathbf{k}}^{1}_{1})}f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{1i_{0}},G_{2i_{0}}+G_{2i_{1}}}}
.

\E_{i_{0}\in I}\mathopen{}\left(\left|\!\left|\!\left|\Delta_{({\mathbf{m}}^{1}_{1},{\mathbf{k}}^{1}_{1})}f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{1i_{0}},G_{2i_{0}}+G_{2i_{1}}}\mathclose{}\right)^{2^{3}}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\E_{i_{00},i_{01},i_{1}\in I}\E_{{\mathbf{m}}^{1}_{1},{\mathbf{k}}^{1}_{1}}\mathopen{}\left(\left|\!\left|\!\left|\Delta_{({\mathbf{m}}^{1}_{1},{\mathbf{k}}^{1}_{1})}f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{1i_{00}}+G_{1i_{01}},G_{2i_{00}}+G_{2i_{1}},G_{2i_{01}}+G_{2i_{1}}}\mathclose{}\right)^{2^{4}}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

The inductive formula for box seminorms and Lemma
4.1
once again give us

\displaystyle\E_{i_{00},i_{01}\in I}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{m}}^{00}_{1},{\mathbf{k}}^{00}_{1},\\
{\mathbf{m}}^{01}_{1},{\mathbf{k}}^{01}_{1}\end{subarray}}\E_{i_{1}\in I}\mathopen{}\left(\left|\!\left|\!\left|\Delta_{({\mathbf{m}}^{00}_{1}+{\mathbf{m}}^{01}_{1},{\mathbf{k}}^{00}_{1}+{\mathbf{k}}^{01}_{1})}f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{1i_{1}},G_{2i_{00}}+G_{2i_{1}},G_{2i_{01}}+G_{2i_{1}}}\mathclose{}\right)^{2^{4}}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

We then apply Lemma
4.4
for the last time, this time to each expression

\E_{i_{1}\in I}\mathopen{}\left(\left|\!\left|\!\left|\Delta_{({\mathbf{m}}^{00}_{1}+{\mathbf{m}}^{01}_{1},{\mathbf{k}}^{00}_{1}+{\mathbf{k}}^{01}_{1})}f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{1i_{1}},G_{2i_{00}}+G_{2i_{1}},G_{2i_{01}}+G_{2i_{1}}}\mathclose{}\right)^{2^{4}},

obtaining

\displaystyle\E_{i_{00},i_{01},i_{10},i_{11}\in I}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{m}}^{00}_{1},{\mathbf{k}}^{00}_{1},\\
{\mathbf{m}}^{01}_{1},{\mathbf{k}}^{01}_{1}\end{subarray}}\mathopen{}\left(\left|\!\left|\!\left|\Delta_{({\mathbf{m}}^{00}_{1}+{\mathbf{m}}^{01}_{1},{\mathbf{k}}^{00}_{1}+{\mathbf{k}}^{01}_{1})}f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1i_{10}}+G_{1i_{11}},G_{2i_{00}}+G_{2i_{10}},G_{2i_{00}}+G_{2i_{11}},\\
G_{2i_{01}}+G_{2i_{10}},G_{2i_{01}}+G_{2i_{11}}\end{subarray}}\mathclose{}\right)^{2^{6}}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

The inductive formula for box seminorms, 1-boundedness, and Lemma
4.1
then imply that

\displaystyle\E_{i_{00},i_{01},i_{10},i_{11}\in I}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1i_{00}}+G_{1i_{01}},G_{1i_{10}}+G_{1i_{11}},G_{2i_{00}}+G_{2i_{10}},\\
G_{2i_{00}}+G_{2i_{11}},G_{2i_{01}}+G_{2i_{10}},G_{2i_{01}}+G_{2i_{11}}\end{subarray}}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)},

finishing the
s=2
case.

We note that the proof of Proposition
4.5
for
s=2
relies on 3 applications of Lemma
4.4
. More generally, the proof for an arbitrary
s\geq 2
will require
1+2+\cdots+2^{s-1}=2^{s}-1
applications of Lemma
4.4
.

The general case:

We move on to prove the general case. Starting with
\varepsilon=\E_{i\in I}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G_{1i},\ldots,G_{si}}^{+}
, we apply Lemma
4.4
to bound

(34)

\displaystyle\E_{i_{0},i_{1}\in I}\mathopen{}\left(\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{1i_{0}},\ldots,G_{(s-1)i_{0}},G_{1i_{1}},\ldots,G_{(s-1)i_{1}},G_{si_{0}}+G_{si_{1}}}\mathclose{}\right)^{2^{2s}}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

We note that we passed from having
s
unconcatenated groups indexed by
i
to
s-1
unconcatenated groups indexed by
i_{0}
and another
s-1
unconcatenated groups indexed by
i_{1}
(in addition to the concatenated group
G_{si_{0}}+G_{si_{1}}
). Thus, the total number of groups almost doubled, but what matters is that for each index
i_{0},i_{1}
, the number of unconcatenated groups with this index went down by 1. At the next stage of the argument, we will apply Lemma
4.4
twice to concatenate
G_{(s-1)i_{0}}
first and then
G_{(s-1)i_{1}}
. As a consequence, the two indices
i_{0},i_{1}
will be replaced by four indices
i_{00},i_{01},i_{10},i_{11}
, and for each of them we will have exactly
s-2
unconcatenated groups.
We will continue in this manner: at each stage, the number of indices
i_{\underline{\epsilon}}
will double, but the number of unconcatenated groups with each index
i_{\underline{\epsilon}}
will decrease by 1. Eventually, on the
s
-th step, we will be left with
2^{s-1}
unconcatenated groups
G_{1i_{\underline{\epsilon}}}
, and
2^{s-1}
applications of Lemma
4.4
will allow us to concatenate them all without producing any new unconcatenated groups. This will finish the argument.

This is the general strategy; let us see in detail what happens at the second stage, i.e., after obtaining the bound (
34
). Using the inductive formula for box seminorms, we can rephrase (
34
) as

\displaystyle\E_{i_{1}\in I}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{m}}^{1}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{m}}^{1}_{s-1},\\
{\mathbf{k}}^{1}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{k}}^{1}_{s-1}\end{subarray}}\E_{i_{0}\in I}\mathopen{}\left(\left|\!\left|\!\left|\Delta_{({\mathbf{m}}^{1}_{1},{\mathbf{k}}^{1}_{1}),\ldots,({\mathbf{m}}^{1}_{s-1},{\mathbf{k}}^{1}_{s-1})}f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{1i_{0}},\ldots,G_{(s-1)i_{0}},G_{si_{0}}+G_{si_{1}}}\mathclose{}\right)^{2^{s+1}}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

For each fixed
i_{1},{\mathbf{m}}^{1}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{m}}^{1}_{s-1},{\mathbf{k}}^{1}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{k}}^{1}_{s-1}
, we apply Lemma
4.4
separately to each average over
i_{0}
, obtaining

\E_{i_{1}\in I}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{m}}^{1}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{m}}^{1}_{s-1},\\
{\mathbf{k}}^{1}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{k}}^{1}_{s-1}\end{subarray}}\E_{i_{00},i_{01}\in I}\\
\mathopen{}\left(\left|\!\left|\!\left|\Delta_{({\mathbf{m}}^{1}_{1},{\mathbf{k}}^{1}_{1}),\ldots,({\mathbf{m}}^{1}_{s-1},{\mathbf{k}}^{1}_{s-1})}f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1i_{00}},\ldots,G_{(s-2)i_{00}},G_{1i_{01}},\ldots,G_{(s-2)i_{01}},\\
G_{(s-1)i_{00}}+G_{(s-1)i_{01}},G_{si_{00}}+G_{si_{1}},G_{si_{01}}+G_{si_{1}}\end{subarray}}\mathclose{}\right)^{2^{2s}}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

We rearrange the inequality above using the inductive formula for box seminorms as

\E_{i_{00},i_{01}\in I}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{m}}^{00}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{m}}^{00}_{s-1},\\
{\mathbf{m}}^{01}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{m}}^{01}_{s-1}\end{subarray}}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{k}}^{00}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{k}}^{00}_{s-1},\\
{\mathbf{k}}^{01}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{k}}^{01}_{s-1}\end{subarray}}\E_{i_{1}\in I}\\
\mathopen{}\left(\Big|\!\Big|\!\Big|\Delta_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\mathbf{m}}^{00}_{1},{\mathbf{k}}^{00}_{1}),\ldots,({\mathbf{m}}^{00}_{s-2},{\mathbf{k}}^{00}_{s-2}),\\
({\mathbf{m}}^{01}_{1},{\mathbf{k}}^{01}_{1}),\ldots,({\mathbf{m}}^{01}_{s-2},{\mathbf{k}}^{01}_{s-2}),\\
({\mathbf{m}}^{00}_{s-1}+{\mathbf{m}}^{01}_{s-1},{\mathbf{k}}^{00}_{s-1}+{\mathbf{k}}^{01}_{s-1})\end{subarray}}f\Big|\!\Big|\!\Big|^{+}_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1i_{1}},\ldots,G_{(s-1)i_{1}},\\
G_{si_{00}}+G_{si_{1}},G_{si_{01}}+G_{si_{1}}\end{subarray}}\mathclose{}\right)^{2^{s+2}}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

in order to concatenate
G_{(s-1)i_{1}}
. By Lemma
4.4
applied separately to each average over
i_{1}\in I
, we have

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{00},i_{01},\\
i_{10},i_{11}\in I\end{subarray}}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{m}}^{00}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{m}}^{00}_{s-1},\\
{\mathbf{m}}^{01}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{m}}^{01}_{s-1}\end{subarray}}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{k}}^{00}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{k}}^{00}_{s-1},\\
{\mathbf{k}}^{01}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{k}}^{01}_{s-1}\end{subarray}}\\
\mathopen{}\left(\Big|\!\Big|\!\Big|\Delta_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\mathbf{m}}^{00}_{1},{\mathbf{k}}^{00}_{1}),\ldots,({\mathbf{m}}^{00}_{s-2},{\mathbf{k}}^{00}_{s-2}),\\
({\mathbf{m}}^{01}_{1},{\mathbf{k}}^{01}_{1}),\ldots,({\mathbf{m}}^{01}_{s-2},{\mathbf{k}}^{01}_{s-2}),\\
({\mathbf{m}}^{00}_{s-1}+{\mathbf{m}}^{01}_{s-1},{\mathbf{k}}^{00}_{s-1}+{\mathbf{k}}^{01}_{s-1})\end{subarray}}f\Big|\!\Big|\!\Big|^{+}_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1i_{10}},\ldots,G_{(s-2)i_{10}},G_{1i_{11}},\ldots,G_{(s-2)i_{11}},\\
G_{(s-1)i_{10}}+G_{(s-1)i_{11}},G_{si_{00}}+G_{si_{10}},G_{si_{00}}+G_{si_{11}},\\
G_{si_{01}}+G_{si_{10}},G_{si_{01}}+G_{si_{11}}\end{subarray}}\mathclose{}\right)^{2^{2s+2}}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

An application of the inductive formula for box seminorms and 1-boundedness then gives

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{00},i_{01},\\
i_{10},i_{11}\in I\end{subarray}}{\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1i_{00}},\ldots,G_{(s-2)i_{00}},G_{1i_{01}},\ldots,G_{(s-2)i_{01}},\\
G_{1i_{10}},\ldots,G_{(s-2)i_{10}},G_{1i_{11}},\ldots,G_{(s-2)i_{11}},\\
G_{(s-1)i_{00}}+G_{(s-1)i_{01}},G_{(s-1)i_{10}}+G_{(s-1)i_{11}},\\
G_{si_{00}}+G_{si_{10}},G_{si_{00}}+G_{si_{11}},\\
G_{si_{01}}+G_{si_{10}},G_{si_{01}}+G_{si_{11}}\end{subarray}}}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)},

which can be written more compactly as

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{00},i_{01},\\
i_{10},i_{11}\in I\end{subarray}}{\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{G_{ji_{\underline{\epsilon}}}:\ {\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{2},\ j=1,\ldots,s-2\},\\
\{G_{(s-1)i_{\underline{\epsilon}}}+G_{(s-1)i_{{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}}}:\ {\underline{\epsilon}},{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{2}\ \mathrm{with}\ \epsilon_{1}=\epsilon^{\prime}_{1},\ \epsilon_{2}=1,\epsilon^{\prime}_{2}=0\},\\
\{G_{si_{\underline{\epsilon}}}+G_{si_{{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}}}:\ {\underline{\epsilon}},{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{2}\ \mathrm{with}\ \epsilon_{1}=1,\epsilon^{\prime}_{1}=0\}\end{subarray}}}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

We have thus successfully concatenated all the groups
G_{(s-1)i_{\underline{\epsilon}}}
and
G_{si_{\underline{\epsilon}}}
.

At the next stage, we concatenate the groups
G_{(s-2)i_{\underline{\epsilon}}}
. Applying Lemma
4.4
and the inductive formula for box seminorms four times like before, each time to an average over
i_{00},i_{01},i_{10},i_{11}
respectively, we arrive at the inequality

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{{\underline{\epsilon}}}\in I,\\
{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{3}\end{subarray}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{G_{ji_{\underline{\epsilon}}}:\ {\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{3},\ j\in[s-3]\},\\
\{G_{ji_{\underline{\epsilon}}}+G_{ji_{{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}}}:\ j=s-2,s-1,s,\ {\underline{\epsilon}},{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{3}\\
\mathrm{with}\ (\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{s-j})=(\epsilon^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\epsilon^{\prime}_{s-j}),\ \epsilon_{s+1-j}=1,\;\epsilon^{\prime}_{s+1-j}=0\}\end{subarray}}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

This time, we have successfully concatenated the groups
G_{(s-2)i_{\underline{\epsilon}}}
. At the next step, 8 applications of Lemma
4.4
and the inductive formula for box seminorms allow us to concatenate groups
G_{(s-3)i_{\underline{\epsilon}}}
. Continuing the argument like this, we arrive, after a total of

\displaystyle 1+2+2^{2}+\cdots+2^{s-1}=2^{s}-1

applications of Lemma
4.4
and the inductive formula for box seminorms, at the claimed inequality.
∎

For applications, the following weaker but notationally lighter corollary of Proposition
4.5
is sufficient. It can be seen as an ergodic version of
[
53
, Corollary 2.4]
or
[
52
, Corollary 6.3]
.

Corollary 4.6

(Concatenation of box seminorms, version II)
.

Let
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
t=2^{s}
,
I
be a finite indexing set and
G_{1i},\ldots,G_{si}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
be finitely generated subgroups for each
i\in I
. For all 1-bounded functions
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

(35)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(\E_{i\in I}{\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{1i},\ldots,G_{si}}}\mathclose{}\right)^{O_{s}(1)}\ll_{k,s}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},\ldots,k_{t}\in I\end{subarray}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{\{G_{jk_{i_{1}}}+G_{jk_{i_{2}}}:\ j\in[s],\ 1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}\leq t\}}.

Corollary
4.6
plays the role of the Bessel-type inequality from
[
65
, Corollary 1.22]
. However, its advantage over
[
65
, Corollary 1.22]
is that we are quantitatively comparing averages of generalized box seminorms rather than the
L^{2}(\mu)
norms of projections onto appropriate Host-Kra factors. Since usually we have no quantitative information on the latter, the results from
[
65
]
are in effect of purely qualitative nature, and they were used as such in previous ergodic works on polynomial concatenation
[
18
,
20
]
. The most important difference is of course that we quantitatively compare two averages of box seminorms while an analogous comparison in
[
65
]
is fully qualitative. By contrast, the quantitative nature of Corollary
4.6
is a key improvement that allows us to obtain quantitative statements in Theorems
1.15
and
1.16
.

A further difference between Corollary
4.6
and
[
65
, Corollary 1.22]
concerns the nature of seminorms and groups that appear in the right-hand side of the inequality (
35
). In our result, we only sum subgroups with the same index
j
whereas results from
[
65
]
involve subgroups of the form
G_{jk}+G_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}
for all indices
j,j^{\prime}
. The price that we pay is that our argument necessitates the introduction of
t=2^{s}
indices
k_{1},\ldots,k_{t}
on the right hand side of (
35
) while the arguments in
[
65
]
allow to average on the right hand side over only two indices
k,k^{\prime}
, and also the degree of the generalized box seminorms in our output is much larger than the one in
[
65
]
.
At the end of the day, however, these inefficiencies are of little importance; what matters is that the number
t
of indices and the degree of the seminorms are both
O_{s}(1)
.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon=\E_{i\in I}{\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{1i},\ldots,G_{si}}}
. We let all the constants depend on
k,s
, noting however that powers of
\varepsilon
will not depend on
k
.
Proposition
4.5
immediately implies that

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{\underline{\epsilon}}\in I:\\
{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}\end{subarray}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{G_{ji_{\underline{\epsilon}}}+G_{ji_{{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}}}:\ j\in[s],\ {\underline{\epsilon}},{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{s}\\
\mathrm{with}\ (\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{s-j})=(\epsilon^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\epsilon^{\prime}_{s-j}),\ \epsilon_{s+1-j}=1,\ \epsilon^{\prime}_{s+1-j}=0\}\end{subarray}}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

Ordering
\{0,1\}^{s}
lexicographically and identifying it with
[2^{s}]=[t]
in an order-preserving way, we obtain a subset
{\mathcal{K}}_{0}
of
{\mathcal{K}}:=
\{(i_{1},i_{2})\in[t]\times[t]:i_{1}<i_{2}\}
such that

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},\ldots,k_{t}\in I\end{subarray}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{G_{jk_{i_{1}}}+G_{jk_{i_{2}}}:\;j\in[s],\ ({i_{1}},{i_{2}})\in{\mathcal{K}}_{0}\}\end{subarray}}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

By Lemma
3.5
, we can replace
{\mathcal{K}}_{0}
by the full set
{\mathcal{K}}
, giving the claimed result.
∎

We shall use the following consequence of Corollary
4.6
, which can be compared with
[
53
, Corollary 2.5]
and
[
52
, Corollary 6.4]
, and which is obtained from an iterated application of Corollary
4.6
. Its advantage is that it allows us to take the directions in the concatenated box seminorms to be arbitrarily long sums of the original directions rather than just double sums, as is the case in Corollary
4.6
.

Corollary 4.7

(Iterated concatenation of box seminorms)
.

Let
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\ell
be a nonnegative integer power of 2,
I
be a finite indexing set and
G_{1i},\ldots,G_{si}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
be finitely generated subgroups for each
i\in I
. There exists a positive integer
t=O_{\ell,s}(1)
such that for all 1-bounded functions
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

(36)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(\E_{i\in I}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{1i},\ldots,G_{si}}\mathclose{}\right)^{O_{\ell,s}(1)}\ll_{k,\ell,s}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},\ldots,k_{t}\in I\end{subarray}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{jk_{i_{1}}}+\cdots+G_{jk_{i_{\ell}}}:\;j\in[s],\;1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\ell}\leq t\end{subarray}}.

It will be important for our applications later on that in the sum
G_{jk_{i_{1}}}+\cdots+G_{jk_{i_{\ell}}}
inside (
36
),
the indices
i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell}
are all distinct since otherwise the group might not be larger than its summands.

Proof.

The proof proceeds by induction on
\ell
, which we recall is a power of 2. For
\ell=1
, the claim is trivial (with
t=1
), and for
\ell=2
, it follows from Corollary
4.6
(with
t=2^{s}
). We will just prove the cases
\ell=4
since this will make the strategy for the general case clear enough.

Let
\varepsilon=\E_{i\in I}{\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{1i},\ldots,G_{si}}}
. Then by Corollary
4.6
, we have

(37)

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},\ldots,k_{t_{1}}\in I\end{subarray}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{\{G_{jk_{i_{1}}}+G_{jk_{i_{2}}}:\ j\in[s],\ 1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}\leq t_{1}\}}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}

with
t_{1}=2^{s}

(once again, the constants are allowed to depend on
k,\ell,s
except that the powers of
\varepsilon
do not depend on
k
).
To prove the result for
\ell=4
, we apply Corollary
4.6
to (
37
), taking
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{t_{1}})\in I^{t_{1}}
in place of
i\in I
. This gives

(38)

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{il}\in I:\\
i\in[t_{1}],\;l\in[t_{1}^{\prime}]\end{subarray}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{G_{jk_{i_{1}l_{1}}}+G_{jk_{i_{2}l_{1}}}+G_{jk_{i_{1}l_{2}}}+G_{jk_{i_{2}l_{2}}}:\\
j\in[s],\;1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}\leq t_{1},\;1\leq l_{1}<l_{2}\leq t_{1}^{\prime}\}\end{subarray}}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}

for some positive integer
t_{1}^{\prime}=O(1)
. Reindexing
(i,l)\in[t_{1}]\times[t_{1}^{\prime}]
as
i\in[t_{2}]
and using the monotonicity property (Lemma
3.5
), we argue as in the proof of Corollary
4.6
that

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},\ldots,k_{t_{2}}\in I\end{subarray}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{\{G_{jk_{i_{1}}}+\cdots+G_{jk_{i_{4}}}:\ j\in[s],\ 1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{4}\leq t_{2}\}}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

This proves the case
\ell=4
. We continue in the same manner for larger
\ell
.
∎

5.
Standard techniques for averages along Hardy sequences

We have so far proved basic properties of generalized box seminorms and developed the theory of their quantitative concatenation. From now on, we will show how these seminorms can be used to control multiple ergodic averages along Hardy sequences. Before we embark on this, we list several standard technical lemmas that shall be profusely applied in the process.
We start with two variants of the classical van der Corput lemma. The derivation of van der Corput-type inequalities is standard, and we refer the reader to
[
9
]
for more background.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
v_{1},\ldots,v_{N}
be 1-bounded vectors in an inner product space. For every natural number
H\leq N
, we have
