\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}v_{n}\right\|^{2}\leq 6\E_{h\in[\pm H]}\mathopen{}\left|\E_{n\in[N]}\langle v_{n},v_{n+h}\rangle\mathclose{}\right|+\frac{6H}{N}

and

\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}v_{n}\right\|^{2}\leq 3\E_{h,h^{\prime}\in[\pm H]}\E_{n\in[N]}\langle v_{n+h},v_{n+h^{\prime}}\rangle+\frac{3H}{N}.

We then move on to prove the finitary version of
[
25
, Proposition 6.1]
which allows us to remove from our average dual functions evaluated at Hardy sequences. We state two versions (based on two different versions of the van der Corput trick from Lemma
5.1
), each of which will be useful at different stages of the PET argument. We recall for the convenience of the reader that the collection
{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}
of dual sequences of degree at most
d
is defined in Section
3.1
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
d,J,k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and suppose that
b_{1},\ldots,b_{J}\in\mathcal{H}
satisfy the growth condition
b_{j}(t)\ll t^{d}
. Then there exists a positive integer
s=O_{d,J}(1)
such that for all systems
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
, sequences of 1-bounded functions
A
and
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}
, and numbers
n_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and
K,N\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
K\leq N
, we have

\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}A(n_{0}+n)\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n_{0}+n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2^{s}}\\
\ll_{d,J,k}\E\limits_{{\underline{k}}\in[\pm K]^{s}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}\mathcal{C}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}A(n_{0}+n+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{k}})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\\
+\frac{K}{N}+o_{N\to\infty;d,J,k,b_{1},\ldots,b_{J}}(1).

Similarly, we have

\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}A(n_{0}+n)\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n_{0}+n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2^{s}}\\
\ll_{d,J,k}\E\limits_{{\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}\in[\pm K]^{s}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}\mathcal{C}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}A(n_{0}+n+({\underline{1}}-{\underline{\epsilon}})\cdot{\underline{k}}+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{k}}^{\prime})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\\
+\frac{K}{N}+o_{N\to\infty;d,J,k,b_{1},\ldots,b_{J}}(1).

If
b_{1},\ldots,b_{J}
are polynomials, then the term
o_{N\to\infty;d,J,k,b_{1},\ldots,b_{J}}(1)
can be taken to be 0.

Proof.

The proof proceeds by a bounded number of applications of the van der Corput inequality in a PET-like manner (much like in
[
25
, Proposition 6.1]
), where at each step we reduce to an average of smaller complexity.
To obtain the first claim, we will repeatedly use the first part of Lemma
5.1
in order to remove all the dual terms. The second claim will follow analogously from the second part of Lemma
5.1
.

Our first task is to rephrase the average that we study in a way that facilitates the induction. By the definition of the dual functions, we can find positive integers
l,\ell=O_{d,J}(1)
, tuples of Hardy field functions
{\mathbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{b}}_{\ell}\in\{0,b_{1},\ldots,b_{J}\}^{d}
, and 1-bounded sequences
d_{1},\ldots,d_{\ell}:{{\mathbb{Z}}}^{l}\to L^{\infty}(\mu)
for which

(39)

\displaystyle\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n_{0}+n)\right\rfloor})=\lim_{M\to\infty}\E_{{{\mathbf{m}}}\in[\pm M]^{l}}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}d_{j}({{\mathbf{m}}}+{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{b}}_{j}(n_{0}+n)\right\rfloor}).

For instance, we have
12
12

12

We note that the following representation is not unique: we could equally well set
{\mathbf{b}}_{3}(n)=(0,b_{1}(n),0)
if we wanted to “assign”
b_{1}(n)
to
m_{2}
rather than
m_{1}
.

\displaystyle T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor b_{1}(n_{0}+n)\right\rfloor}}{\mathcal{D}}_{2,T_{1}}(f_{1})\cdot T_{2}^{{\left\lfloor b_{2}(n_{0}+n)\right\rfloor}}{\mathcal{D}}_{1,T_{2}}(f_{2})=\lim_{M\to\infty}\E_{{{\mathbf{m}}}\in[\pm M]^{3}}\prod_{j\in[4]}d_{j}({\mathbf{m}}+{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{b}}_{j}(n_{0}+n)\right\rfloor})

for

\displaystyle d_{j}(m_{1},m_{2},m_{3})=\begin{cases}T_{1}^{m_{1}}\overline{f_{1}},\;&j=1\\
T_{1}^{m_{2}}\overline{f_{1}},\;&j=2\\
T_{1}^{m_{1}+m_{2}}{f_{1}},\;&j=3\\
T_{2}^{m_{3}}\overline{f_{2}},\;&j=4\end{cases},\quad\textrm{and}\quad{\mathbf{b}}_{j}(n)=\begin{cases}(b_{1}(n),0,0),\;&j=1\\
(0,b_{1}(n),0),\;&j=2\\
(b_{1}(n),0,0),\;&j=3\\
(0,0,b_{2}(n)),\;&j=4\end{cases}.

We remark that in (
39
), we have used Lemma
3.1
to combine limits coming from various dual functions.

We then set

\displaystyle\varepsilon:=\sup_{E\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}}\limsup\limits_{M\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}A(n_{0}+n)\cdot\E_{{{\mathbf{m}}}\in[\pm M]^{l}}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}d_{j}({{\mathbf{m}}}+{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{b}}_{j}(n_{0}+n)\right\rfloor})\cdot 1_{E}(n)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Let
W
be the type of the family of sequences
{\mathbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{b}}_{\ell}
defined as in
[
25
, Subsection 5.1]
.
13
13

13

Technically,
[
25
, Subsection 5.1]
defines the type for
l
-tuples
({\mathcal{B}}_{1},\ldots,

{\mathcal{B}}_{J}),
where
{\mathcal{B}}_{i}=(b_{1i},\ldots,b_{\ell i})
, but it is easy to move between the two formalisms.
We notice that since
d,l,\ell
are fixed, we have, by
[
25
, Lemma 5.3]
, only
O_{d,\ell,J}(1)
many
W
’s. Assuming that
b_{11}
is the function of the largest growth rate, we proceed by induction on
W.

Base case:
Assume that
b_{ji}
converge in
{\mathbb{R}}
for all
j,i
.
Consequently, the sequences
{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{b}}_{j}(n_{0}+n)\right\rfloor}
are constant for
n
large enough. Hence the average over
{\mathbf{m}}
becomes independent of
n
and can be removed using the Hölder inequality, giving

\sup_{E\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}A(n_{0}+n)\cdot 1_{E}(n)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}+o_{N\to\infty;{\mathbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{b}}_{\ell}}(1)\geq\varepsilon,

with the error term corresponding to the small values
n
. The result follows immediately since
1_{E}(n)
is a 1-bounded weight.

Inductive step:
Suppose now that
b_{11}(t)\to\infty
, and assume that for all families of
2\ell
sequences with type
W^{\prime}<W
, the statement holds with
s=s_{0}(W^{\prime},l,2\ell)
. We then set

s_{0}(W,l,\ell):=\max_{W^{\prime}<W}(s_{0}(W^{\prime},l,2\ell))+1.

Swapping the order of summation of
n
and
{\mathbf{m}}
, using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, and then the van der Corput inequality (the first part of Lemma
5.1
), we get

\E_{k_{1}\in[\pm K]}\limsup\limits_{M\to\infty}\Bigg\|\E_{n\in[N]}A(n_{0}+n+k_{1})\cdot\overline{A}(n_{0}+n)\cdot\E_{{{\mathbf{m}}}\in[\pm M]^{l}}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}d_{j}({{\mathbf{m}}}+{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{b}}_{j}(n_{0}+n+k_{1})\right\rfloor})\\
\overline{d}_{j}({{\mathbf{m}}}+{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{b}}_{j}(n_{0}+n)\right\rfloor})\cdot 1_{E}(n+k_{1})\cdot 1_{E}(n)\Bigg\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}+\frac{K}{N}\gg\varepsilon^{2}.

We now choose the vector
{\mathbf{b}}
that reduces the type of
W
and make the change of variables
{{\mathbf{m}}}\to{{\mathbf{m}}}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{b}}(n_{0}+n)\right\rfloor}
, so that

\E_{k_{1}\in[\pm K]}\limsup\limits_{M\to\infty}\Bigg\|\E_{n\in[N]}A(n_{0}+n+k_{1})\cdot\overline{A}(n_{0}+n)\\
\E_{{{\mathbf{m}}}\in[\pm M]^{l}}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}d_{j}({{\mathbf{m}}}+{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{b}}_{j}(n_{0}+n+k_{1})-{\mathbf{b}}(n_{0}+n)\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}_{j,k_{1}}(n))\\
\overline{d}_{j}({{\mathbf{m}}}+{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{b}}_{j}(n_{0}+n)-{\mathbf{b}}(n_{0}+n)\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}^{\prime}_{j}(n))\cdot 1_{E_{k_{1}}}(n)\Bigg\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}+\frac{K}{N}\gg\varepsilon^{2},

where the error terms
{\mathbf{r}},{\mathbf{r}}^{\prime}
take values in
\{0,1\}^{l},
and
E_{k_{1}}=E\cap(E-k_{1}).

Partitioning
E_{k_{1}}
into
O_{l,\ell}(1)
sets in which the
{\mathbf{r}},{\mathbf{r}}^{\prime}
are constant, we get

\sup_{E\subseteq[N]}\E_{k_{1}\in[\pm K]}\limsup\limits_{M\to\infty}\Bigg\|\E_{n\in[N]}A(n_{0}+n+k_{1})\cdot\overline{A}(n_{0}+n)\\
\E_{{{\mathbf{m}}}\in[\pm M]^{l}}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}d_{j}({{\mathbf{m}}}+{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{b}}_{j}(n_{0}+n+k_{1})-{\mathbf{b}}(n_{0}+n)\right\rfloor})\\
\overline{d}_{j}({{\mathbf{m}}}+{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{b}}_{j}(n_{0}+n)-{\mathbf{b}}(n_{0}+n)\right\rfloor})\cdot 1_{E}(n)\Bigg\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}+\frac{K}{N}\gg_{l,\ell}\varepsilon^{2}.

Since the new averages have smaller type, the claim follows by the induction hypothesis.
∎

We also need a lemma for removing error terms that take finitely many values. This lemma, which is a variation on
[
66
, Lemma 3.2]
, will be used to remove error terms that come from swapping the order of summing and taking integer parts. We omit the proof.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
k,\ell,N\in{\mathbb{N}}
and assume that for every
j\in[\ell]
, the functions
{\mathbf{r}}_{j}:{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
take values in a finite set
S
. Then for any family of sequences
\mathbf{a}_{j}:{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
there exist
{\mathbf{r}}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{r}}^{\prime}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
(where we can take
{\mathbf{r}}^{\prime}_{1}=\mathbf{0}
) such that for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
and 1-bounded functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{\mathbf{a}_{j}(n)+{\mathbf{r}}_{j}(n)}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\ll_{k,\ell,S}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{\mathbf{a}_{j}(n)+{\mathbf{r}}^{\prime}_{j}}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Lastly, we shall use a version of
[
66
, Lemma 3.3]
that allows us to pass to short intervals.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
d,\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
L\in{\mathcal{H}}
with
1\prec L(t)\prec t
. Let also
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
be a probability space and for each
n\in[N]
and
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
A_{nN}:L^{\infty}(\mu)^{\ell}\to L^{\infty}(\mu)
be a function such that
|A_{nN}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell})|\leq 1
for all 1-bounded functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
. Then

\mathopen{}\left(\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\left\|f_{1}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot A_{nN}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\mathclose{}\right)^{d}\\
\leq\limsup_{R\to\infty}\sup_{\left\|f_{1}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\E\limits_{N\in[R]}\left\|\E_{n\in(N,N+L(N)]}c_{n}\cdot A_{nR}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{d}.

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle\varepsilon=\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\left\|f_{1}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot A_{nN}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

We can find 1-bounded functions
f_{jN}
and constants
c_{nN}
approximating the suprema well enough so that

\displaystyle\varepsilon=\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{nN}\cdot A_{nN}(f_{1N},\ldots,f_{\ell N})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Setting
B_{nN}=c_{nN}\cdot A_{nN}(f_{1N},\ldots,f_{\ell N})
and using the same argument as in the proof of
[
66
, Lemma 3.3]
, we observe that

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[R]}B_{nR}-\E\limits_{N\in[R]}\E_{n\in(N,N+L(N)]}B_{nR}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0,

and so

\displaystyle\limsup_{R\to\infty}\E\limits_{N\in[R]}\left\|\E_{n\in(N,N+L(N)]}c_{nR}\cdot A_{nR}(f_{1R},\ldots,f_{\ell R})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\geq\varepsilon.

The bound then follows by bounding

\left\|\E_{n\in(N,N+L(N)]}c_{nR}\cdot A_{nR}(f_{1R},\ldots,f_{\ell R})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\\
\leq\sup_{\left\|f_{1}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\E\limits_{N\in[R]}\left\|\E_{n\in(N,N+L(N)]}c_{n}\cdot A_{nR}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)},

taking the limit
R\to\infty

and applying the Hölder inequality.
∎

6.
Seminorm estimates for linear and sublinear sequences

Having listed all the prerequisites, we are ready to give the first applications of our generalized box seminorms. In this section, we will prove that these seminorms quantitatively control multiple ergodic averages along linear and sublinear Hardy sequences. We start with a standard lemma that allows us to bound finitary multiple ergodic averages along linear functions.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},...,T_{k})
be a system and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
be nonzero. For any
M,N\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
M\leq N

and 1-bounded
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}n\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2^{\ell+1}}\\
\ll_{k,\ell}\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]^{\ell}}\mathopen{}\left|\int\Delta_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\ell},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2};({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime})}f_{1}\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|^{2}+\frac{M}{N}.

We recall here the convention (
19
) for the iterated multiplicative derivative.

The proof of Lemma
6.1
is standard and follows from several applications of Lemma
5.1
. Taking the supremum over 1-bounded
f_{2},\ldots,f_{\ell}
followed by the limits
N\to\infty
and
M\to\infty
(in this order), we obtain the following infinitary corollary.

Corollary 6.2
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},...,T_{k})
be a system and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
be nonzero. For any 1-bounded
f_{1}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}n\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\ll_{k,\ell}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{1}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\ell},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}}^{+}.

The seminorm control for linear sequences can then be used to prove a similar result for sublinear sequences, whose proof requires considerably more effort, though. We first prove a result for averages twisted by dual sequences and then use it to derive a stronger corollary for sublinear averages without dual twists.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
d,k,\ell,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
J\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
,
0\leq m_{1}\leq m
and
g_{1},...,g_{m},b_{1},\ldots,b_{J}\in\mathcal{H}
be Hardy functions satisfying the following growth conditions:

(i)

1\prec g_{1}(t)\prec\cdots\prec g_{m}(t)\prec t
;

(ii)

g_{i}(t)\ll\log t
iff
i\leq m_{1}
;

(iii)

b_{j}(t)\ll t^{d}
for every
j\in[J]
.

For
j\in[\ell]
, let

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}_{j}=\sum_{i\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{ji}g_{i}+{\mathbf{r}}_{j}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{k}\quad\textrm{for\; some}\quad{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{ji}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\lim_{t\to\infty}{\mathbf{r}}_{j}(t)=\mathbf{0},

and for distinct
j,j^{\prime}\in[0,\ell]
, let

\displaystyle d^{\prime}_{jj^{\prime}}=\max\{i\in[m]:\;{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{ji}\neq{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j^{\prime}i}\}

(where
\mathbf{a}_{0}=\mathbf{0}
and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{0i}=\mathbf{0}
).
Suppose that the following two conditions are satisfied:

(i)

for every
j\in[0,\ell]\setminus\{1\}
, we have
d^{\prime}_{1j}>m_{1}
, i.e., at least one coordinate of
\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{j}
grows faster than log;

(ii)

{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1m}\neq\textbf{0}
, i.e.,
\mathbf{a}_{1}
has maximum growth.

Then there exists a positive integer
s=O_{d,J,\ell,m}(1)
such that for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},

...,T_{k})
and 1-bounded
f_{1}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
,
we have

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1,\\
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{O_{d,J,\ell,m}(1)}\\
\ll_{d,J,k,\ell,m}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{1}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1d^{\prime}_{10}}^{s},({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1d^{\prime}_{12}}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2d^{\prime}_{12}})^{s},\ldots,({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1d^{\prime}_{1\ell}}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\ell d^{\prime}_{1\ell}})^{s}}^{+}.

Proof.

We assume that
m>m_{1}
(i.e.
g_{m}\succ\log
) since in the case
m=m_{1}
the proposition is vacuously true. The idea of the proof is to first apply Lemma
5.2
to remove the dual sequences, then pass to short intervals appropriately chosen so that on Taylor expanding
g_{1},\ldots,g_{m}
, all but the contribution of
g_{m}
to
\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{\ell}
becomes constant. Subsequently, we pass to arithmetic progressions on which our sequences become linear, and for those we can obtain the claimed seminorm bound using Corollary
6.2
.

We let all the quantities in this proof depend on
d,k,m,J,\ell
without mentioning the dependence explicitly, noting however that
s
(to be chosen later) and powers of
\varepsilon
do not depend on
k
.

Step 1: Passing to short intervals, removing the dual functions and changing variables.

Let

\displaystyle\varepsilon=\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1,\\
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\underset{n\in[N]}{\E}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Using Lemma
5.4
, we pass to short intervals of some length
1\prec L(t)\prec t
to be chosen later, obtaining

\limsup_{R\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1,\\
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\E\limits_{N\in[R]}\\
\left\|\E_{n\in[L(N)]}c_{N+n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{j}(N+n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(N+n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\geq\varepsilon.

Subsequently, we apply Lemma
5.2
in order to remove the functions
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}
, so that

\limsup_{R\to\infty}\;\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\;\E\limits_{N\in[R]}\;\E\limits_{{\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}\in[\pm K_{N}]^{s_{1}}}\;\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\\
\left\|\E_{n\in[L(N)]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{j}(N+n+({\underline{1}}-{\underline{\epsilon}})\cdot{\underline{k}}+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{k}}^{\prime})\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}+\frac{K_{N}}{L(N)}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}

for some integer
s_{1}=O(1)
(independent of
k
) and
K_{N}>0
that we choose shortly.

Recall that
\mathbf{a}_{j}=\sum_{i\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{ji}g_{i}
and
g_{1}\prec\cdots\prec g_{m}
. We want to choose the length
L(t)
in such a way that the functions
g_{1},\ldots,g_{m-1}
are approximately constant on each interval
(N,N+L(N)]
while
g_{m}
can be approximated by a linear polynomial. This can be accomplished by choosing any
L
in the range

g_{m}^{\prime}(t)^{-1}\prec L(t)\prec\min(g_{m}^{\prime\prime}(t)^{-1/2},g_{m-1}^{\prime}(t)^{-1}).

That this range is nonempty can be seen as follows: the relation
g_{m}^{\prime}(t)^{-1}\prec g_{m}^{\prime\prime}(t)^{-1/2}
is a consequence of Lemma
A.2
and the sublinearity of
g_{m}
while
g_{m}^{\prime}(t)^{-1}\prec g_{m-1}^{\prime}(t)^{-1}
follows from the L’Hôpital rule. Using Lemma
A.2
once more, we get
1\prec g_{m}^{\prime}(t)^{-1}
and
\min(g_{m}^{\prime\prime}(t)^{-1/2},g_{m-1}^{\prime}(t)^{-1})\prec t,
from which we have
1\prec L(t)\prec t,
and so the application of Lemma
5.4
above is justified.

We additionally let
K_{N}=K{\left\lfloor|g^{\prime}_{m}(N)^{-1}|\right\rfloor}
for some
K>0
; with this choice, the quantity
K_{N}
satisfies the upper bound
\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\frac{K_{N}}{KL(N)}=0
for each fixed
K
. If
n\in[L(N)]
and
{\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}\in[\pm K_{N}]^{s_{1}}
, then

n+({\underline{1}}-{\underline{\epsilon}})\cdot{\underline{k}}+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{k}}^{\prime}\in[-s_{1}K_{N},L(N)+s_{1}K_{N}]\subseteq[-o(KL(N)),L(N)(1+o(K))],

and so by Taylor expanding the functions
g_{i}
around
N
, we can approximate

g_{i}(N+n+({\underline{1}}-{\underline{\epsilon}})\cdot{\underline{k}}+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{k}}^{\prime})\\
=\begin{cases}g_{m}(N)+g^{\prime}_{m}(N)(n+({\underline{1}}-{\underline{\epsilon}})\cdot{\underline{k}}+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{k}}^{\prime})+r_{mNn{\underline{\epsilon}}{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}},\;&i=m\\
g_{i}(N)+r_{iNn{\underline{\epsilon}}{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}},\;&i\in[m-1]\end{cases}

for error terms
r_{iNn{\underline{\epsilon}}{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}}
satisfying
14
14

14

The bound on the error term follows since for every
i<m
, the error term
r_{iNn{\underline{\epsilon}}{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}}
coming from the Taylor expansion of
g_{i}(N+n)
at
N
equals
g^{\prime}_{i}(N)n_{0}
for some
n_{0}\in[-s_{1}K_{N},L(N)+s_{1}K_{N}]
and can be bounded by
r_{iNn{\underline{\epsilon}}{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}}\prec g_{i}^{\prime}(N)L(N)(1+o(K))\prec 1
for every fixed
K
. A similar argument works for
i=m
, this time however we use the second derivative of
g_{m}
.

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\;\max_{n\in[L(N)]}\;\max_{{\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}\in[\pm K_{N}]^{s_{1}}}|r_{iNn{\underline{\epsilon}}{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}}|={0}\quad\textrm{for\; all}\quad(i,{\underline{\epsilon}})\in[m]\times\{0,1\}^{s_{1}}.

Then

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}_{j}(N+n+({\underline{1}}-{\underline{\epsilon}})\cdot{\underline{k}}+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{k}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad={\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{jm}g^{\prime}_{m}(N)(n+({\underline{1}}-{\underline{\epsilon}})\cdot{\underline{k}}+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{k}}^{\prime})+\sum_{i\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{ji}g_{i}(N)+{\mathbf{r}}_{jNn{\underline{\epsilon}}{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad={\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{jm}g^{\prime}_{m}(N)(n+({\underline{1}}-{\underline{\epsilon}})\cdot{\underline{k}}+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{k}}^{\prime})+\mathbf{a}_{j}(N)+{\mathbf{r}}_{jNn{\underline{\epsilon}}{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}}

for some error terms
{\mathbf{r}}_{jNn{\underline{\epsilon}}{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
satisfying

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\max_{n\in[L(N)]}\max_{{\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}\in[\pm K_{N}]^{s_{1}}}|{\mathbf{r}}_{jNn{\underline{\epsilon}}{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}}|=\mathbf{0}\quad\textrm{for\; all}\quad(j,{\underline{\epsilon}})\in[\ell]\times\{0,1\}^{s_{1}}.

By Lemma
5.3
and the pigeonhole principle, we can find
{\mathbf{r}}_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
for which

\limsup_{R\to\infty}\;\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\;\E\limits_{N\in[R]}\;\E\limits_{{\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}\in[\pm K_{N}]^{s_{1}}}\;\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\\
\left\|\E_{n\in[L(N)]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{jm}g^{\prime}_{m}(N)(n+({\underline{1}}-{\underline{\epsilon}})\cdot{\underline{k}}+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{k}}^{\prime})\right\rfloor}+{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{j}(N)\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}}}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

The next step is to remove the coefficient
g^{\prime}_{m}(N)
so that all the sequences become linear. This is accomplished by splitting the range
[L(N)]
of
n
into arithmetic progressions of difference
{\left\lfloor|g^{\prime}_{m}(N)|^{-1}\right\rfloor}
, and similarly with the ranges of
{\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}
. Passing to arithmetic progressions, we in effect
substitute
n{\left\lfloor|g^{\prime}_{m}(N)|^{-1}\right\rfloor}+r
for
n
,
k_{i}{\left\lfloor|g^{\prime}_{m}(N)|^{-1}\right\rfloor}+r_{i}
for
k_{i}
and
k_{i}^{\prime}{\left\lfloor|g^{\prime}_{m}(N)|^{-1}\right\rfloor}+r_{i}^{\prime}
for
k_{i}^{\prime}
. Letting

\tilde{L}(N)=\frac{L(N)}{{\left\lfloor|g^{\prime}_{m}(N)|^{-1}\right\rfloor}}\succ 1

be the length of the new interval in
n
, we deduce that

\limsup_{R\to\infty}\;\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{2{\underline{\epsilon}}}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell{\underline{\epsilon}}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\;\E\limits_{N\in[R]}\;\E\limits_{{\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}\in[\pm K]^{s_{1}}}\;\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}r,r_{1},\ldots,r_{s_{1}},\\
r_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,r_{s_{1}}^{\prime}\in[g^{\prime}_{m}(N)^{-1}]\end{subarray}}\;\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\\
\left\|\underset{n\in[\tilde{L}(N)]}{\E}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{jm}(n+({\underline{1}}-{\underline{\epsilon}})\cdot{\underline{k}}+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{k}}^{\prime})+{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{jm}g_{m}^{\prime}(N)(r+({\underline{1}}-{\underline{\epsilon}})\cdot{\underline{r}}+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{r}}^{\prime}))\right\rfloor}+{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{j}(N)\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}}}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\\
\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

We then split the large integer part and handle the error terms using Lemma
5.3
(possibly modifying
{\mathbf{r}}_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}}
if needed); since
g_{m}^{\prime}(N)(r+({\underline{1}}-{\underline{\epsilon}})\cdot{\underline{r}}+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{r}}^{\prime})\ll 1
, this term can be ignored altogether at the cost of
O(1)
factor in the lower bound. Thus

\limsup_{R\to\infty}\;\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{2{\underline{\epsilon}}}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell{\underline{\epsilon}}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\;\E\limits_{N\in[R]}\;\E\limits_{{\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}\in[\pm K]^{s_{1}}}\;\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\\
\left\|\underset{n\in[\tilde{L}(N)]}{\E}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{jm}n\right\rfloor}+{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{jm}k_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{jm}k_{s_{1}}^{\epsilon_{s_{1}}}\right\rfloor}+{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{j}(N)\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}}}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

We choose functions
f_{jR}
for
j\geq 2
close enough to the supremum, while also setting
f_{1R}=f_{1}
, so that upon applying the property

\limsup\limits_{R\to\infty}(A_{R}+B_{R})\leq\limsup_{R\to\infty}A_{R}+\limsup_{R\to\infty}B_{R}

to exchange
\limsup\limits_{R\to\infty}
with
\E\limits_{{\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}\in[\pm K]^{s_{1}}}
, we get

\E\limits_{{\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}\in[\pm K]^{s_{1}}}\limsup_{R\to\infty}\E\limits_{N\in[R]}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\\
\left\|\underset{n\in[\tilde{L}(N)]}{\E}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{jm}n\right\rfloor}+{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{jm}k_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{jm}k_{s_{1}}^{\epsilon_{s_{1}}}\right\rfloor}+{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{j}(N)\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}}}f_{jR}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

At this point, we split into the cases
\ell=1
and
\ell>1
.

Step 2: The case
\ell=1
.

If
\ell=1
, then we can compose the integral defining the
L^{2}(\mu)
norm above with
T^{-{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{1}(N)\right\rfloor}}
, so that the expression above reduces to

\E_{{\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}\in[\pm K]^{s_{1}}}\limsup_{R\to\infty}\E\limits_{N\in[R]}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\\
\left\|\underset{n\in[\tilde{L}(N)]}{\E}c_{n}\cdot T^{{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1m}n\right\rfloor}}\mathopen{}\left(\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1m}k_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1m}k_{s_{1}}^{\epsilon_{s_{1}}}\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}_{1{\underline{\epsilon}}}}f_{1}\mathclose{}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

Corollary
6.2
then gives

\displaystyle\E_{{\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}\in[\pm K]^{s_{1}}}\Big|\!\Big|\!\Big|\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1m}k_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1m}k_{s_{1}}^{\epsilon_{s_{1}}}\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}_{1{\underline{\epsilon}}}}f_{1}\Big|\!\Big|\!\Big|_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1m}}^{+}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

Applying the Hölder inequality, taking
K\to\infty
, expanding the expression above (with the seminorm raised to an appropriate powers) as a Gowers-Cauchy-Schwarz inner product, and using the translation invariance of seminorms, we obtain
\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{1}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1m}^{s_{1}+1}}^{+}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}
from the Gowers-Cauchy-Schwarz inequality (Lemma
3.3
).

Step 3: The case
\ell>1
.

We move on to the case
\ell>1
. This case is much more notationally complicated than the previous one, yet the underlying goal is simple: we want to reduce to an average of length less than
\ell
and apply the induction hypothesis. Reordering if necessary, we can find
\ell_{0}\in[\ell]
such that
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1m}={\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{jm}
iff
j\in[\ell_{0}]
. Upon setting

\displaystyle F_{RN{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}}:=\prod_{j\in[\ell_{0}]}T^{{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{j}(N)\right\rfloor}}\mathopen{}\left(\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1m}k_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1m}k_{s_{1}}^{\epsilon_{s_{1}}}\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}}}f_{jR}\mathclose{}\right)

and

\displaystyle F_{jRN{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}}:=T^{{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{j}(N)\right\rfloor}}\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{jm}k_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{jm}k_{s_{1}}^{\epsilon_{s_{1}}}\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}}}f_{jR}\quad\textrm{for}\quad j\in[\ell_{0}+1,\ell],

we have

\E_{{\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}\in[\pm K]^{s_{1}}}\limsup_{R\to\infty}\E\limits_{N\in[R]}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\\
\left\|\underset{n\in[\tilde{L}(N)]}{\E}c_{n}\cdot T^{{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1m}n\right\rfloor}}F_{RN{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}}\cdot\prod_{j=\ell_{0}+1}^{\ell}T^{{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{jm}n\right\rfloor}}F_{jRN{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

By Lemma
6.1
(here we use the assumption that
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1m}\neq\mathbf{0}
), for any
H>0
we have

\E_{{\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}\in[\pm K]^{s_{1}}}\E_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[\pm H]^{\ell-\ell_{0}+1}}\limsup_{R\to\infty}\E\limits_{N\in[R]}\\
\mathopen{}\left|\int\Delta_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1m},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1m}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\ell m},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1m}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{(\ell_{0}+1)m};({\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime})}F_{RN{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}}\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|^{2}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

The definition of
F_{RN{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}}
gives us

\displaystyle\E_{{\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}\in[\pm K]^{s_{1}}}\E_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[\pm H]^{\ell-\ell_{0}+1}}\limsup_{R\to\infty}\E\limits_{N\in[R]}\mathopen{}\left|\int\prod_{j\in[\ell_{0}]}T^{{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{j}(N)\right\rfloor}}f_{jR{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|^{2}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)},

where

f_{jR{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}:=\Delta_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1m},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1m}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\ell m},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1m}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{(\ell_{0}+1)m};({\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime})}\\
\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1m}k_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1m}k_{s_{1}}^{\epsilon_{s_{1}}}\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}}}f_{jR}

for each
j\in[\ell_{0}]
,
{\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}\in[\pm K]^{s_{1}}
and
{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[\pm H]^{\ell-\ell_{0}+1}
. Composing with
T^{-{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{\ell_{0}}(N)\right\rfloor}}
, swapping subtraction inside integer parts, and using Lemma
5.3
to handle error terms, we find
{\mathbf{r}}_{j}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}

such that

\displaystyle\E_{{\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}\in[\pm K]^{s_{1}}}\E_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[\pm H]^{\ell-\ell_{0}+1}}\limsup_{R\to\infty}\E\limits_{N\in[R]}\mathopen{}\left|\int\prod_{j\in[\ell_{0}]}T^{{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{j}(N)-\mathbf{a}_{\ell_{0}}(N)\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}_{j}}f_{jR{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|^{2}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

Passing to the product system, taking
\E\limits_{N\in[R]}
inside the average and applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we deduce that

\E_{{\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}\in[\pm K]^{s_{1}}}\E_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[\pm H]^{\ell-\ell_{0}+1}}\limsup_{R\to\infty}\\
\left\|\E\limits_{N\in[R]}\prod_{j\in[\ell_{0}-1]}(T\times T)^{{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{j}(N)-\mathbf{a}_{\ell_{0}}(N)\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}_{j}}(f_{jR{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\otimes\overline{f_{jR{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu\times\mu)}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

Each average indexed by
{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}{\underline{h}}^{\prime}
has length
\ell_{0}-1\leq\ell-1
, and so applying the induction hypothesis and recalling that
f_{1{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}:=f_{1R{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}
does not depend on
R
, we deduce that there exists a positive integer
s_{2}=O(1)
(independent of
k
) such that

\displaystyle\E_{{\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}\in[\pm K]^{s_{1}}}\E_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[\pm H]^{\ell-\ell_{0}+1}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{1{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1m}^{s_{2}},({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1d^{\prime}_{12}}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2d^{\prime}_{12}})^{s_{2}},\ldots,({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1d^{\prime}_{12}}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\ell_{0}d^{\prime}_{1\ell_{0}}})^{s_{2}}}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

Note that
d^{\prime}_{1j}=m
for
j\in[\ell_{0}+1,\ell]
. The result follows on applying the Hölder inequality, taking limits (first
H\to\infty
and then
K\to\infty
), and using the definition of
f_{1{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}
together with the inductive formula (
18
) and Lemma
3.3
(for the average over
{\underline{k}}
) to remove the shifts
{\mathbf{r}}_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}}
.

While applying the induction hypothesis, we implicitly use the assumption that some coordinate of the sequences
\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{\ell_{0}}
and
(\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{\ell_{0}})-(\mathbf{a}_{j}-\mathbf{a}_{\ell_{0}})=\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{j}
grows faster than
\log
, which follows from the assumption that
d^{\prime}_{1j}>m_{1}
for all
j\neq 1
.
∎

If the average has no dual sequences, we can remove the assumption of
\mathbf{a}_{1}
having maximum growth.

Corollary 6.4
.

Let
d,k,\ell,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
0\leq m_{1}\leq m
and
g_{1},...,g_{m}\in\mathcal{H}
be Hardy functions satisfying the following growth conditions:

(i)

1\prec g_{1}(t)\prec\cdots\prec g_{m}(t)\prec t
;

(ii)

g_{i}(t)\ll\log t
iff
i\leq m_{1}
.

For
j\in[\ell]
, let

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}_{j}=\sum_{i\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{ji}g_{i}+{\mathbf{r}}_{j}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{k}\quad\textrm{for\; some}\quad{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{ji}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\lim_{t\to\infty}{\mathbf{r}}_{j}(t)=\mathbf{0},

and for distinct
j,j^{\prime}\in[0,\ell]
, let

\displaystyle d^{\prime}_{jj^{\prime}}=\max\{i\in[m]:\;{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{ji}\neq{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j^{\prime}i}\}

(where
\mathbf{a}_{0}=\mathbf{0}
and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{0i}=\mathbf{0}
).
Suppose that for all
j\in[0,\ell]\setminus\{1\}
, we have
d^{\prime}_{1j}>m_{1}
, i.e., at least one coordinate of
\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{j}
grows faster than log.
Then there exists a positive integer
s=O_{d,\ell,m}(1)
such that for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},...,T_{k})
and 1-bounded
f_{1}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{O_{d,\ell,m}(1)}\\
\ll_{d,k,\ell,m}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{1}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1d^{\prime}_{10}}^{s},({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1d^{\prime}_{12}}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2d^{\prime}_{12}})^{s},\ldots,({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1d^{\prime}_{1\ell}}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\ell d^{\prime}_{1\ell}})^{s}}^{+}.

Proof.

Once again, we assume that
m>m_{1}
since otherwise the result follows vacuously.
If
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1m}\neq\mathbf{0}
, then the estimate follows immediately from Proposition
6.3
. If
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1m}=\mathbf{0}
, then we perform the standard trick of composing the average with
T^{-{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{i}(n)\right\rfloor}}
for some index
i\in[\ell]
for which
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{im}\neq\mathbf{0}
(we can assume without loss of generality that such an index exists). This gives

\displaystyle\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=\int f_{i}\cdot\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot T^{-{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{i}(n)\right\rfloor}}F_{N}\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[\ell],\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}T^{{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{i}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\,d\mu

for some 1-bounded function
F_{N}
. By applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, swapping the order of subtraction and taking integer parts, and using Lemma
5.3
to handle the ensuing error terms, we can bound the expression above by
O_{d,k,\ell,m}(1)
times

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[\ell]\end{subarray}}T^{{\left\lfloor\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)},

where

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{j}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{a}_{j}-\mathbf{a}_{i},\;&j\neq i\\
-\mathbf{a}_{i},\;&j=i\end{cases}.

Crucially, the sequence
\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{1}=\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{i}
has maximum growth, and so the limsup as
N\to\infty
of the average above can be controlled using Proposition
6.3
. Moreover, the families

\displaystyle\{\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{j}:j\in[2,\ell]\}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\{\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{1},\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{1}-\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{j}:j\in[2,\ell]\}

are identical, and hence so are their leading coefficients. The claimed estimate follows from an application of Proposition
6.3
.
∎

We remark that the trick of composing the average with
T^{-{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{i}(n)\right\rfloor}}
would not work when applied to an average twisted by dual functions like the one considered in Proposition
6.3
; the issue is that the resulting terms
T^{-{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{i}(n)\right\rfloor}}{\mathcal{D}}({\left\lfloor b(n)\right\rfloor})
cannot be removed using Lemma
5.2
. Therefore here and in the results to come, averages twisted by dual functions will usually have some growth assumptions on the sequence
\mathbf{a}_{1}
with respect to which we obtain seminorm estimates.

7.
PET: seminorm estimates for polynomials

The purpose of this section is to take first steps towards proving generalized box seminorm bounds for another naturally occurring family of sequences: polynomials over
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
. Even though many seminorm estimates for multiple ergodic averages along polynomials have been obtained in the past, no work so far has done it in this generality, not least because the appropriate family of seminorms has not been previously identified. The purpose of this section is to show in Proposition
7.10
that finite averages along polynomials twisted by dual sequences are controlled by averages of (finite analogues of) generalized box seminorms. The argument whereby we achieve this is known in the literature as PET, and it consists of many applications of the van der Corput inequality combined with keeping careful track of coefficients of the polynomials that appear in the intermediate steps of the induction. From Proposition
7.10
we then deduce in Corollary
7.11
that infinite averages are controlled quantitatively by averages of generalized box seminorms. In the next section, we will combine these auxiliary results with the quantitative concatenation machinery to identify a single generalized box seminorm that controls a given polynomial average with polynomial bounds.

7.1.
Setting up the induction scheme

In order to carry out the PET argument in general, we first need to set up some notation that allows us to talk about general multidimensional polynomial families. The formalism that we employ is borrowed directly from
[
52
]
, which itself draws heavily on previous works performing the PET argument (e.g.
[
3
,
6
,
17
,
18
,
20
,
59
]
).

Let
d,k,\ell,s_{1},s_{2}\in{\mathbb{N}}
. In the rest of the section,
{\underline{h}}
is always a tuple in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{s_{1}}
unless explicitly stated otherwise. All the polynomial families that we shall consider will take the following form.

Definition 7.1

(Essentially distinct and normal polynomial families)
.

Let
{\mathcal{Q}}=({\mathbf{q}}_{1},\ldots,

{\mathbf{q}}_{s_{2}})
be a family of polynomials in
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[n,{\underline{h}}]
of degree at most
d
. We call
{\mathcal{Q}}

essentially distinct
if the polynomials
{\mathbf{q}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{q}}_{s_{2}}
and their pairwise differences are nonconstant as polynomials in
n
. In a similar spirit, we say that
{\mathbf{q}}_{1}
is
essentially distinct
from
{\mathbf{q}}_{2},\ldots,{\mathbf{q}}_{s}
if
{\mathbf{q}}_{1},{\mathbf{q}}_{1}-{\mathbf{q}}_{2},\ldots,{\mathbf{q}}_{1}-{\mathbf{q}}_{s}
are nonconstant as polynomials in
n
.
Lastly, we call
{\mathcal{Q}}

normal
if it is essentially distinct,
{\mathbf{q}}_{1}
has the highest degree with respect to the variable
n
, and
{\mathbf{q}}_{1}(0,{\underline{h}}),\ldots,{\mathbf{q}}_{s_{2}}(0,{\underline{h}})
are the zero polynomial for all
{\underline{h}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{s_{1}}
.

Let
{\mathcal{P}}=({\mathbf{p}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell})
be a normal family of polynomials in
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[n]
of degree at most
d
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
\deg{\mathbf{p}}_{1}=d
.
We denote the coefficients of the polynomials
{\mathbf{p}}_{j}
by
{\mathbf{p}}_{j}(n)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{ji}n^{i}
, and we also set
{\mathbf{p}}_{0}:=\mathbf{0}
and
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0i}:=\mathbf{0}
(applying the same convention for families of polynomials in
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[n,{\underline{h}}]
whenever convenient). During the PET argument, we will subject
{\mathcal{P}}
to an iterated application of the following operation.

Definition 7.2

(van der Corput operation)
.

Let
{\mathcal{Q}}=({\mathbf{q}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{q}}_{s_{2}})
be a normal family of polynomials in
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[n,{\underline{h}}]
. Given
m\in[s]
, we define the new polynomial family

\displaystyle\partial_{m}{\mathcal{Q}}:=

\displaystyle(\sigma_{h_{s_{1}+1}}{{\mathbf{q}}}_{1}-{{\mathbf{q}}}_{m},\ldots,\sigma_{h_{s_{1}+1}}{{\mathbf{q}}}_{s_{2}}-{{\mathbf{q}}}_{m},{{\mathbf{q}}}_{1}-{{\mathbf{q}}}_{m},\ldots,{{\mathbf{q}}}_{s_{2}}-{{\mathbf{q}}}_{m})^{*},

where
\sigma_{h_{s_{1}+1}}{\mathbf{q}}(n,{\underline{h}})
is the polynomial in
\mathbb{R}^{k}[n,{\underline{h}},h_{s_{1}+1}]
defined by

\displaystyle\sigma_{h_{s_{1}+1}}{\mathbf{q}}(n,{\underline{h}}):={\mathbf{q}}(n+h_{s_{1}+1},{\underline{h}})-{\mathbf{q}}(h_{s_{1}+1},{\underline{h}}),

and the
∗
operation removes all monomials independent of
n
and all but the first copy of each polynomial that appears multiple times.

We refer to the operation
{\mathcal{Q}}\mapsto\partial_{m}{\mathcal{Q}}
as a
van der Corput operation
on
{\mathcal{Q}}
since the new family
\partial_{m}{\mathcal{Q}}
will arise from
{\mathcal{Q}}
via an application of the van der Corput inequality. It is through this operation that we obtain a new polynomial family from
{\mathcal{Q}}
at each step of the PET induction scheme.

Our next goal is to describe the properties of
{\mathcal{P}}
that are preserved by the van der Corput operation. Let
{\mathcal{Q}}=({\mathbf{q}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{q}}_{s_{2}})
be a normal family of polynomials in
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[n,{\underline{h}}]
, and write

(40)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{q}}_{j}(n,{\underline{h}})=\sum_{i=1}^{d}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{ji}({\underline{h}})n^{i}

for some
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{ji}\in{\mathbb{R}}[{\underline{h}}]
. In other words, the
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{ji}
’s are the coefficients of
{\mathbf{q}}_{j}
as a polynomial in
n
, and they are themselves polynomials in
{\underline{h}}
. For
j,j^{\prime}\in[0,s_{2}]
, we also define

d_{jj^{\prime}}:=\max\{i\in[d]:\;{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{ji}({\underline{h}})\neq{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{j^{\prime}i}({\underline{h}})\}

to be the degree of
{\mathbf{q}}_{j}-{\mathbf{q}}_{j^{\prime}}
as a polynomial in
n
,
where we set
{\mathbf{q}}_{0}:=\mathbf{0}
and
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{0i}:=\mathbf{0}
for all
i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
.
We want to show that if
{\mathcal{Q}}
is a family obtained from
{\mathcal{P}}
at some stage of the PET induction scheme, then the polynomials
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{ji}
preserve several useful properties. The following definition is taken from
[
52
]
.

Definition 7.3

(Descendent family of polynomials)
.

A normal family
{\mathcal{Q}}=({\mathbf{q}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{q}}_{s_{2}})
of polynomials in
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[n,{\underline{h}}]

descends from

{\mathcal{P}}
if for each
j\in[s_{2}]
, the polynomials
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{ji}
in (
40
) can be expressed as
15
15

15

The multinomial coefficient

\displaystyle{{\binom{u_{1}+\cdots+u_{s_{1}}+i}{u_{1},\ldots,u_{s_{1}}}}}=\frac{(u_{1}+\cdots+u_{s_{1}}+i)!}{u_{1}!\cdots u_{s_{1}}!\cdot i!}

is the coefficient of
{\underline{h}}^{{\underline{u}}}n^{i}=h_{1}^{u_{1}}\cdots h_{s_{1}}^{u_{s_{1}}}n^{i}
in
(n+h_{1}+\cdots+h_{s_{1}})^{i+u_{1}+\cdots+u_{s_{1}}}
.

(41)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{ji}({\underline{h}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{s_{1}},\\
|{\underline{u}}|\leq d-i\end{subarray}}{{\binom{u_{1}+\cdots+u_{s_{1}}+i}{u_{1},\ldots,u_{s_{1}}}}}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+i)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{{\underline{u}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+i)}){\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}

for some indices
w_{{\underline{u}}},w_{j{\underline{u}}}\in[0,\ell]
satisfying the following properties:

(i)

(Coefficients of
{\mathbf{q}}_{1}
)
w_{1{\underline{u}}}=1
for all
{\underline{u}}
;

(ii)

(Multilinearity) for each
j
, the polynomial
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1d_{1j}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{jd_{1j}}
(the leading coefficient of
{\mathbf{q}}_{1}-{\mathbf{q}}_{j}
as a polynomial in
n
) is multilinear in
{\underline{h}}
;

(iii)

(Leading coefficients) for each
j
and
{\underline{u}}
, the vector
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|{\underline{u}}|+d_{1j})}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+d_{1j})}
is nonzero only if it is the leading coefficient of
{\mathbf{p}}_{1}-{\mathbf{p}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}}
.

The following lemma ascertains that the van der Corput operation preserves the abovementioned properties inherited by
{\mathcal{Q}}
from
{\mathcal{P}}
by virtue of descending from
{\mathcal{P}}
. While the following two results were proved for integer polynomials, the exact same arguments work for real polynomials.

Proposition 7.4

(
[
52
, Proposition 4.6]
)
.

Let
d,k,\ell,s_{1},s_{2}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and let
{\mathcal{P}}=({\mathbf{p}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell})
be a normal family of polynomials in
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[n]
with coefficients given by
{\mathbf{p}}_{j}(n)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{ji}n^{i}
.
Suppose that the family
{\mathcal{Q}}=({\mathbf{q}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{q}}_{s_{2}})
of polynomials in
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[n,{\underline{h}}]
descends from
{\mathcal{P}}
.
Then the family
\partial_{m}{\mathcal{Q}}
also descends from
{\mathcal{P}}
for each
m\in[s_{2}]
for which
\sigma_{h_{s_{1}+1}}{{\mathbf{q}}}_{1}-{{\mathbf{q}}}_{m}

has the highest degree as a polynomial in
n
among the elements of
\partial_{m}{\mathcal{Q}}
.

The next result ensures that we reach a family of linear polynomials after a bounded number of van der Corput operations; this fact is the foundation of all PET induction schemes.

Proposition 7.5

(
[
52
, Proposition 4.7]
)
.

Let
d,k,\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
{\mathcal{P}}
be a normal family of polynomials in
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[n]
of degree at most
d
. There exist positive integers
s=O_{d,\ell}(1)
and
m_{1},\ldots,m_{s}
such that for every
i\in[s]
the family
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}=\partial_{m_{i}}\cdots\partial_{m_{1}}{\mathcal{P}}
descends from
{\mathcal{P}}
, and moreover the elements of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{s}
are linear in
n
.

We remark that
s
is independent of the dimension
k
of the underlying space.

The following lemma shows how we pass from the family
{\mathcal{Q}}
to
\partial_{m}{\mathcal{Q}}
in applications. In the language of
[
18
,
20
]
, the lemma below says that the new family
\partial_{m}{\mathcal{Q}}
is
1-standard
.

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
d,k,\ell,s_{1},s_{2},H,N\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
H\leq N
and
{\mathcal{P}}
be a normal family of polynomials. Let
{\mathcal{Q}}=\{{\mathbf{q}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{q}}_{s_{2}}\}
be a family of polynomials in
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[n,{\underline{h}}]
with
\deg{\mathbf{q}}_{1}>1
, and suppose that
{\mathcal{Q}}
and
\partial_{m}{\mathcal{Q}}
both descend from
{\mathcal{P}}
for some
m\in[s_{2}]
. For every 1-bounded
f_{1}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
and
n_{0}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, we have

\sup_{\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{s}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{q}}_{1}(n_{0}+n,{\underline{h}})\right\rfloor}}f_{1}\cdots T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{q}}_{s_{2}}(n_{0}+n,{\underline{h}})\right\rfloor}}f_{s}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}\\
\ll_{d,k,\ell,s_{1},s_{2}}\E_{h_{s_{1}+1}\in[\pm H]}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{\mathbf{q}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1,\\
{\mathbf{q}}\in\partial_{m}{\mathcal{Q}}\setminus\{\sigma_{h_{s_{1}+1}}{\mathbf{q}}_{1}-{\mathbf{q}}_{m}\}\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\\
\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{{\mathbf{q}}\in\partial_{m}{\mathcal{Q}}}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{q}}(n_{0}+n,{\underline{h}},h_{s_{1}+1})\right\rfloor}}f_{{\mathbf{q}}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}+\frac{H}{N}

for
f_{\sigma_{h_{s_{1}+1}}{\mathbf{q}}_{1}-{\mathbf{q}}_{m}}=f_{1}
.

Proof.

First, we observe crucially that all the polynomials

(42)

\displaystyle\sigma_{h_{s_{1}+1}}{{\mathbf{q}}}_{2}-{{\mathbf{q}}}_{m},\ldots,\sigma_{h_{s_{1}+1}}{{\mathbf{q}}}_{s_{2}}-{{\mathbf{q}}}_{m},{{\mathbf{q}}}_{1}-{{\mathbf{q}}}_{m},\ldots,{{\mathbf{q}}}_{s_{2}}-{{\mathbf{q}}}_{m}

are essentially distinct from
\sigma_{h_{s_{1}+1}}{{\mathbf{q}}}_{1}-{{\mathbf{q}}}_{m}
since all the polynomials in
{\mathcal{Q}}
are essentially distinct from
{\mathbf{q}}_{1}
. In making this deduction, we crucially use the assumption that
{\mathbf{q}}_{1}
is nonlinear in
n
for otherwise
{{\mathbf{q}}}_{1}-{{\mathbf{q}}}_{m}
and
\sigma_{h_{s_{1}+1}}{{\mathbf{q}}}_{1}-{{\mathbf{q}}}_{m}
would differ by a polynomial in
({\underline{h}},h_{s_{1}+1})
. Consequently, after applying the
*
operation, no other polynomial from (
42
) gets identified with
\sigma_{h_{s_{1}+1}}{{\mathbf{q}}}_{1}-{{\mathbf{q}}}_{m}
. We then apply the van der Corput inequality (Lemma
5.1
), compose with
T^{-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{q}}_{m}(n)\right\rfloor}}
, and use Lemma
5.3
in order to make error terms independent of
n
. While applying the van der Corput inequality, we remove the terms that do not depend on
n
, and we also group together functions that correspond to classes of iterates that are not essentially distinct. The essential distinctness of
\sigma_{h_{s_{1}+1}}{{\mathbf{q}}}_{1}-{{\mathbf{q}}}_{m}
from other polynomials ensures that
f_{\sigma_{h_{s_{1}+1}}{\mathbf{q}}_{1}-{\mathbf{q}}_{m}}
is not grouped together with anything else. Finally, we compose the integral with some expression
T^{-\mathbf{a}({\underline{h}},h_{s_{1}+1})}
in such a way as to ensure that
f_{\sigma_{h_{s_{1}+1}}{\mathbf{q}}_{1}-{\mathbf{q}}_{m}}=f_{1}
.
∎

7.2.
Preliminary estimates for finite polynomial averages

Having set up the formalism for studying polynomial averages and described how they change after applying the van der Corput inequality, we move on to provide first seminorm estimates for finite polynomial averages. The starting point for all the results in this section is the following result that essentially follows from iterated applications of Lemma
7.6
.

Proposition 7.7

(PET bound for polynomials)
.

Let
d,k,\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
. There exist positive integers
s_{1},s_{2}=O_{d,\ell}(1)
with the following property: for all
H,M,N\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
H,M\leq N
,
n_{0}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
, 1-bounded functions
f_{1}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, and polynomials
{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[n]
with degrees at most
d
,
form
{\mathbf{p}}_{j}(n)=\sum_{i=0}^{d}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{ji}n^{i}
, and such that
{\mathbf{p}}_{1}
is essentially distinct from
{\mathbf{p}}_{2},\ldots,{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}
, we have the bound

(43)

\sup_{\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{p}}_{1}(n_{0}+n)\right\rfloor}}f_{1}\cdots T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}(n_{0}+n)\right\rfloor}}f_{\ell}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{O_{d,\ell}(1)}\\
\ll_{d,k,\ell}\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}}\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s_{2}}}\mathopen{}\left|\int\Delta_{{{\mathbf{c}}_{1}({\underline{h}})},\ldots,{{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}({\underline{h}})};({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime})}f_{1}\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|+\frac{H}{N}+\frac{M}{N},

for nonzero polynomials
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[{\underline{h}}]
that depend only on
{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}
, are multilinear and take the form

(44)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{c}}_{j}({\underline{h}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}},\\
|{\underline{u}}|\leq d-1\end{subarray}}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)!\cdot({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}){\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}

for some indices
w_{j{\underline{u}}}\in[0,\ell]
(with
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}:=\mathbf{0}
). Moreover, each nonzero vector
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}
is the leading coefficient of
{\mathbf{p}}_{1}-{\mathbf{p}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}}
.

Note that in the proposition above, the polynomials and functions are chosen for fixed
N
, meaning that they can vary with
N
. The absolute constants depend only on the degree and number of polynomials as well as the number of transformations; they can be chosen uniformly so that they do not depend on the coefficients of the polynomials.

Proof.

We allow all the quantities to depend on
d,k,\ell
, noting however that
s_{1},s_{2}
will be independent of
k
.

We assume that
n_{0}=0
and that
{\mathbf{p}}_{1}
has maximum degree among
{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}
, and in the end we will explain the necessary modifications in the general case. Under this assumption, the family
{\mathcal{P}}:=\{{\mathbf{p}}_{1}-{\mathbf{p}}_{1}(0),\ldots,{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}-{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}(0)\}
is normal, and so we can use Proposition
7.5
in order to find
s_{1}=O(1)
(independent of
k
) and
m_{1},\ldots,m_{s_{1}}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that for
i\in[s_{1}]
the families
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}:=\partial_{m_{i}}\cdots\partial_{m_{1}}{\mathcal{P}}
are normal and descend from
{\mathcal{P}}
, and the family
{\mathcal{Q}}_{s_{1}}
is linear in
n
and has length
s_{2}
. The van der Corput operation at most doubles the length of the polynomial family, giving us a crude estimate
s_{2}=|{\mathcal{Q}}_{s_{1}}|\leq 2^{s_{1}}|{\mathcal{P}}|\ll 1
. We note in particular that the upper bound on
s_{2}
is independent of
k
.

Let

{\mathbf{b}}_{11}({\underline{h}})n,\ldots,{\mathbf{b}}_{s_{2}1}({\underline{h}})n

be the elements of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{s_{1}}
. Applying Lemma
7.6
inductively to each
{\mathcal{Q}}_{0},\ldots,{\mathcal{Q}}_{s_{1}-1}
(with
{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}={\mathcal{P}}
), we deduce that

\sup_{\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{p}}_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{1}\cdots T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{\ell}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2^{s_{1}}}\\
\ll\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}}\sup_{\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[s_{2}]}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{b}}_{j1}({\underline{h}})n\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}+\frac{H}{N}.

Since the family
{\mathcal{Q}}_{s_{1}}
descends from
{\mathcal{P}}
, the polynomials
{\mathbf{b}}_{11},\ldots,{\mathbf{b}}_{s_{2}1}
are all distinct. The claimed inequality then follows from Lemma
6.1
upon letting
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}
be the polynomials
{\mathbf{b}}_{11},{\mathbf{b}}_{11}-{\mathbf{b}}_{21},\ldots,{\mathbf{b}}_{11}-{\mathbf{b}}_{s_{2}1}
. That the polynomials
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}
are nonzero is a consequence of the distinctness of
{\mathbf{b}}_{11},\ldots,{\mathbf{b}}_{s_{2}1}
. The formula (
44
) follows from patching together various properties that make up Definition
7.3
.

When
n_{0}\neq 0
, then we carry out the argument as above but with
n_{0}+n
in place of
n
at every step. Arriving at the linear polynomials

{\mathbf{b}}_{11}({\underline{h}})(n_{0}+n),\ldots,{\mathbf{b}}_{s_{2}1}({\underline{h}})(n_{0}+n),

we simply compose with
T^{-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{b}}_{11}({\underline{h}})n_{0}\right\rfloor}}
and dispose of the error terms using Lemma
5.3
, obtaining the exact same result as in the case
n_{0}=0
.

When
{\mathbf{p}}_{1}
does not have maximum degree, then we argue as in the proof of Corollary
6.4
.
∎

The following two results extend Proposition
7.7
to families of polynomials with cubic shifts that we will need to consider later. The statement and proof differ somewhat depending on whether
{\mathbf{p}}_{1}
is linear or not, and so we consider these two cases separately.

Proposition 7.8

(Finitary PET bound for polynomials with cubic shifts, nonlinear case)
.

Let
s\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and
d,k,\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
d\geq 2
. There exist positive integers
s_{1},s_{2}=O_{d,\ell,s}(1)
with the following property: for all
H,K,M,N\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
H,M\leq N
,
n_{0}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
, 1-bounded functions
f_{1\underline{1}}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, and polynomials
{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[n]
with degrees at most
d
,
form
{\mathbf{p}}_{j}(n)=\sum_{i=0}^{d}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{ji}n^{i}
, and such that
{\mathbf{p}}_{1}
is nonlinear and essentially distinct from
{\mathbf{p}}_{2},\ldots,{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}
, we have

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1,\\
(j,{\underline{\epsilon}})\neq(1,\underline{1})\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{p}}_{j}(n_{0}+n+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{k}})\right\rfloor}}f_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{O_{d,\ell,s}(1)}\\
\ll_{d,k,\ell,s}\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}}\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s_{2}}}\mathopen{}\left|\int\Delta_{{\mathbf{c}}_{1}({\underline{h}},{\underline{k}}),\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}({\underline{h}},{\underline{k}});({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime})}f_{1\underline{1}}\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|+\frac{H}{N}+\frac{M}{N}

for all but at most
O_{d,k,\ell,s}(K^{s-1})
values
{\underline{k}}\in[\pm K]^{s}
and nonzero polynomials
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[{\underline{h}},{\underline{k}}]
that depend only on
{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}
and
s
, are multilinear and take the form

(45)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{c}}_{j}({\underline{h}},{\underline{k}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\underline{u}},{\underline{v}})\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{s+s_{1}},\\
|{\underline{u}}|+|{\underline{v}}|\leq d-1\end{subarray}}(|{\underline{u}}|+|{\underline{v}}|+1)!\cdot({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|{\underline{u}}|+|{\underline{v}}|+1)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}{\underline{v}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+|{\underline{v}}|+1)}){\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}{\underline{k}}^{\underline{v}},

for some indices
w_{j{\underline{u}}{\underline{v}}}\in[0,\ell]
(with
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0(|{\underline{u}}|+|{\underline{v}}|+1)}:=\mathbf{0}
). Moreover, each nonzero vector
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|{\underline{u}}|+|{\underline{v}}|+1)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}{\underline{v}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+|{\underline{v}}|+1)}
is the leading coefficient of
{\mathbf{p}}_{1}-{\mathbf{p}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}{\underline{v}}}}
.

Proof.

As in the proof of Proposition
7.7
, we can assume without loss of generality that
n_{0}=0
.
Fix
{\underline{k}}\in[\pm K]^{s}
and let

\displaystyle\varepsilon=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1,\\
(j,{\underline{\epsilon}})\neq(1,\underline{1})\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{p}}_{j}(n+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{k}})\right\rfloor}}f_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

We allow all the quantities in the proof to depend on
d,k,\ell,s
but not on
H,K,M,N,n_{0}
(noting however that
s_{1},s_{2}
and the powers of
\varepsilon
will be independent of
k
). Regrouping the polynomials if necessary, we pick
\ell_{0}\in[\ell]
such that
{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell_{0}}
are nonlinear while
{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell_{0}+1},\ldots,{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}
are linear. By assumption,
\ell_{0}\geq 1
, i.e.
{\mathbf{p}}_{1}
and possibly several other polynomials are nonlinear. Splitting
{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{p}}_{j}(n+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{k}})\right\rfloor}
into
{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j1}n\right\rfloor}+{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j1}{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{k}}\right\rfloor}
and the error terms for
j>\ell_{0}
and subsequently removing the error terms using Lemma
5.3
, we obtain

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1,\\
(j,{\underline{\epsilon}})\neq(1,\underline{1})\end{subarray}}\;\sup_{\left\|f_{\ell_{0}+1}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1}\;\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\\
\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell_{0}]}\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{p}}_{j}(n+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{k}})\right\rfloor}}f_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}}\cdot\prod_{j=\ell_{0}+1}^{\ell}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{p}}_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\gg\varepsilon

(we note that
f_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}}
for
j>\ell_{0}
will depend on
{\underline{k}}
).
After this regrouping, all the polynomials in the average above are essentially distinct for all but
O(K^{s-1})
values of
{\underline{k}}\in[\pm K]^{s}
, which will make it amenable to an application of Proposition
7.7
later on.

We can rewrite

\displaystyle{\mathbf{p}}_{j}(n+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{k}})=\sum_{i=0}^{d}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{ji}(n+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{k}})^{i}=\sum_{i=0}^{d}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{ji}\sum_{l=0}^{i}{{\binom{i}{l}}}n^{l}({\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{k}})^{i-l}=\sum_{l=0}^{d}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}{\underline{k}}l}n^{l}

for
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}{\underline{k}}l}=\sum_{i=l}^{d}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{ji}{{\binom{i}{l}}}({\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{k}})^{i-l}
.
By Proposition
7.7
applied with
f_{1\underline{1}}
in place of
f_{1}
to all the averages over
n
except those corresponding to the
O(K^{s-1})
exceptional values of
{\underline{k}}
, there exist positive integers
s_{1},s_{2}=O(1)
(independent of
k
) such that

\displaystyle\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}}\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s_{2}}}\mathopen{}\left|\int\Delta_{{{\mathbf{c}}_{1}({\underline{h}},{\underline{k}})},\ldots,{{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}({\underline{h}},{\underline{k}})};({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime})}f_{1\underline{1}}\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|+\frac{H}{N}+\frac{M}{N}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}

for nonzero polynomials
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}:{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[{\underline{h}},{\underline{k}}]
. Moreover, the polynomials
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}
are multilinear, depend only on
{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}
and
{\underline{k}}
, and take the form

(46)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{c}}_{j}({\underline{h}},{\underline{k}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}},\\
|{\underline{u}}|\leq d-1\end{subarray}}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)!\cdot({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1\underline{1}{\underline{k}}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\tilde{w}_{j{\underline{u}}}{\underline{k}}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}){\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}},

for some
\tilde{w}_{j{\underline{u}}}=(w_{j{\underline{u}}},{\underline{\epsilon}})\in[0,\ell]\times\{0,1\}^{s}
, where
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1\underline{1}{\underline{k}}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}={\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}
, and
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1\underline{1}{\underline{k}}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\tilde{w}_{j{\underline{u}}}{\underline{k}}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}
is the leading coefficient of
{\mathbf{p}}_{1}(n+\underline{1}\cdot{\underline{k}})-{\mathbf{p}}_{{w}_{j{\underline{u}}}}(n+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{k}})
.

Our task is to massage the formula (
46
) for
{\mathbf{c}}_{j}({\underline{h}},{\underline{k}})
in order to reach the formula (
45
) from the statement of the proposition. Specifically, we want to show that the coefficient of
{\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}{\underline{k}}^{\underline{v}}
in (
46
) takes the form given by (
45
), and the way to proceed is to scrutinize the expressions
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1\underline{1}{\underline{k}}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\tilde{w}_{j{\underline{u}}}{\underline{k}}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}
for fixed
j
,
{\underline{u}}
and
{\underline{\epsilon}}
.
If
{\underline{\epsilon}}=\underline{1}
, or if the polynomials
{\mathbf{p}}_{1},{\mathbf{p}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}}
have distinct leading coefficients, then the leading coefficient of

{\mathbf{p}}_{1}(n+\underline{1}\cdot{\underline{k}})-{\mathbf{p}}_{{w}_{j{\underline{u}}}}(n+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{k}})

is the same as the leading coefficient of
{\mathbf{p}}_{1}(n)-{\mathbf{p}}_{{w}_{j{\underline{u}}}}(n)
and equals
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}
for
|{\underline{u}}|=\deg({\mathbf{p}}_{1}-{\mathbf{p}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}})-1
. Then, for each
{\underline{v}}
, the coefficient of
{\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}{\underline{k}}^{\underline{v}}
in (
46
) matches the formula from (
45
) with
w_{j{\underline{u}}{\underline{v}}}=w_{j{\underline{u}}}
if
|{\underline{v}}|=0
and
w_{j{\underline{u}}{\underline{v}}}=1
otherwise.

The remaining case is when
{\underline{\epsilon}}\neq\underline{1}
and
{\mathbf{p}}_{1},{\mathbf{p}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}}
have the same leading coefficients. Assuming without loss of generality that
\deg{\mathbf{p}}_{1}=\deg{\mathbf{p}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}}=d
(so that
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1d}={\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}d}
), the leading coefficient of
{\mathbf{p}}_{1}(n+\underline{1}\cdot{\underline{k}})-{\mathbf{p}}_{{w}_{j{\underline{u}}}}(n+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{k}})
becomes

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1\underline{1}{\underline{k}}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\tilde{w}_{j{\underline{u}}}{\underline{k}}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}=d{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1d}(\underline{1}-{\underline{\epsilon}})\cdot{\underline{k}}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(d-1)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}(d-1)}

for
|{\underline{u}}|=d-2
, and so

(|{\underline{u}}|+1)!\cdot({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1\underline{1}{\underline{k}}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\tilde{w}_{j{\underline{u}}}{\underline{k}}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}){\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}\\
=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{v}}\in\{0,1\}^{s},\\
|{\underline{v}}|\leq d-(|{\underline{u}}|+1)\end{subarray}}(|{\underline{u}}|+|{\underline{v}}|+1)!\cdot({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|{\underline{u}}|+|{\underline{v}}|+1)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{{w}_{j{\underline{u}}{\underline{v}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+|{\underline{v}}|+1)}){\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}{\underline{k}}^{{\underline{v}}}

with

\displaystyle w_{j{\underline{u}}{\underline{v}}}=\begin{cases}w_{j{\underline{u}}},\;&|{\underline{v}}|=0\\
0,\;&|{\underline{v}}|=1\\
1,\;&|{\underline{v}}|>1\end{cases}.

We implicitly use here the observations that
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|{\underline{u}}|+|{\underline{v}}|+1)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{{w}_{j{\underline{u}}{\underline{v}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+|{\underline{v}}|+1)}
vanishes when
|{\underline{v}}|\geq 2
and that
(|{\underline{u}}|+|{\underline{v}}|+1)!=(|{\underline{u}}|+1)!\cdot d
for
|{\underline{v}}|=1
since in this case we have
|{\underline{u}}|+|{\underline{v}}|+1=|{\underline{u}}|+2=d
. Hence for such
{\underline{u}}
, the coefficient of
{\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}{\underline{k}}^{\underline{v}}
in (
46
) once again takes the form prescribed by (
45
).
∎

We now prove a version of Proposition
7.8
in which
{\mathbf{p}}_{1}
is linear. We note that the average that we aim to bound slightly differs from the one in the previous proposition; this has to do with the way in which Proposition
7.9
is meant to be applied.

Proposition 7.9

(Finitary PET bound for polynomials with cubic shifts, linear case)
.

Let
d,k,\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
s\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
. There exist positive integers
s_{1},s_{2}=O_{d,\ell,s}(1)
with the following property: for all
H,K,M,N\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
H,M\leq N
,
n_{0}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,

T_{k})
, 1-bounded functions
f_{1{{\underline{\epsilon}}}}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, and polynomials
{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[n]
with degrees at most
d
,
form
{\mathbf{p}}_{j}(n)=\sum_{i=0}^{d}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{ji}n^{i}
, and such that
{\mathbf{p}}_{1}
is linear and essentially distinct from
{\mathbf{p}}_{2},\ldots,{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}
, we have

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{2{\underline{\epsilon}}}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell{\underline{\epsilon}}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{p}}_{j}(n_{0}+n+({\underline{1}}-{\underline{\epsilon}})\cdot{\underline{k}}+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{k}}^{\prime})\right\rfloor}}f_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{O_{d,\ell,s}(1)}\\
\ll_{d,k,\ell,s}\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}}\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s_{2}}}\\
\mathopen{}\left|\int\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}}}T^{{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}k_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}k_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}}\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}_{1{\underline{\epsilon}}}}\Delta_{{\mathbf{c}}_{1}({\underline{h}}),\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}({\underline{h}});({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime})}f_{1{\underline{\epsilon}}}\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|+\frac{H}{N}+\frac{M}{N}

for all but at most
O_{d,k,\ell,s}(K^{2s-1})
values
({\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime})\in[\pm K]^{2s}
,
some shifts
{\mathbf{r}}_{1{\underline{\epsilon}}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
and nonzero polynomials
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}:{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[{\underline{h}}]
that depend only on
{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell},s
and satisfy the same properties as those listed in Proposition
7.7
.

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle\varepsilon=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{2{\underline{\epsilon}}}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell{\underline{\epsilon}}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{p}}_{j}(n_{0}+n+(\underline{1}-{\underline{\epsilon}})\cdot{\underline{k}}+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{k}}^{\prime})\right\rfloor}}f_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

We allow all the quantities to depend on
d,J,k,\ell
, noting however that
s_{1},s_{2}
and powers of
\varepsilon
will be independent of
k
.
We regroup the polynomials so that
{\mathbf{p}}_{j}
is linear if and only if
j\in[\ell_{0}]
for some
\ell_{0}\in[\ell]
. By the pigeonhole principle, there exist
{\mathbf{r}}_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
for which

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{2{\underline{\epsilon}}}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell{\underline{\epsilon}}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left|\!\left|\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell_{0}]}T^{{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j1}n\right\rfloor}}\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}T^{{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j1}k_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j1}k_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}}\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}}}f_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}}\right.\right.\\
\left.\left.\prod_{j=\ell_{0}+1}^{\ell}\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}}}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{p}}_{j}(n+(\underline{1}-{\underline{\epsilon}})\cdot{\underline{k}}+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{k}}^{\prime})\right\rfloor}}f_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}}\right|\!\right|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\gg\varepsilon.

For all
({\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime})\in[\pm K]^{2s}
save an exceptional set of size
O(K^{2s-1})
, the polynomials in the expression above are essentially distinct as polynomials in
n
, and so we can apply Proposition
7.7
to these “good” values
({\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime})
to find positive integers
s_{1},s_{2}=O(1)
(independent of
k
) such that

\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}}\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s_{2}}}\left|\int\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}}}T^{{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}k_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}k_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}}\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}_{1{\underline{\epsilon}}{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}}}\right.\\
\left.\vphantom{\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}}}}\mathopen{}\left(\Delta_{{\mathbf{c}}_{1}({\underline{h}},{\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}),\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}({\underline{h}},{\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime});({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime})}f_{1{\underline{\epsilon}}}\mathclose{}\right)\,d\mu\right|+\frac{H}{N}+\frac{M}{N}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}

for nonzero polynomials
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[{\underline{h}},{\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}]
. Moreover, the polynomials
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}
are multilinear, depend only on
{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}
,
{\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}
and
s
, and take the form

\displaystyle{\mathbf{c}}_{j}({\underline{h}},{\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}},\\
|{\underline{u}}|\leq d-1\end{subarray}}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)!\cdot({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1\underline{1}{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\tilde{w}_{j{\underline{u}}}{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}){\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}},

for some

\tilde{w}_{j{\underline{u}}}=(w_{j{\underline{u}}},{\underline{\epsilon}})\in[0,\ell]\times\{0,1\}^{s},

where
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1\underline{1}{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}={\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}
and
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1\underline{1}{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\tilde{w}_{j{\underline{u}}}{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}
is the leading coefficient of

(47)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}n-{\mathbf{p}}_{{w}_{j{\underline{u}}}}(n+(\underline{1}-{\underline{\epsilon}})\cdot{\underline{k}}+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{k}}^{\prime}).

The leading coefficient of (
47
) is either
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}
(if
w_{j{\underline{u}}}=0
), or
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j1}
(if
w_{j{\underline{u}}}\in[\ell_{0}]
), or it is minus times the leading coefficient of
{\mathbf{p}}_{{w}_{j{\underline{u}}}}
whenever
j\in[\ell_{0}+1,\ell]
since then
\deg{\mathbf{p}}_{{w}_{j{\underline{u}}}}\geq 2
. It follows from the observation that the collection of possible leading coefficients of (
47
) is the same as the collection of possible leading coefficients of
{\mathbf{p}}_{1},{\mathbf{p}}_{1}-{\mathbf{p}}_{2},\ldots,{\mathbf{p}}_{1}-{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}
. In particular, the leading coefficients are independent of
{\underline{k}}
