, and so are the polynomials
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}
.
∎

7.3.
Estimates for polynomial averages with dual twists

In later applications, we need to consider polynomial averages twisted by dual sequences. The next two results allow us to bound such averages by an average of generalized box seminorms (and their finitary versions), building on Propositions
7.8
and
7.9
.

Proposition 7.10

(Finitary PET bound for polynomials with dual twists)
.

Let
d,k,\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
J\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
. There exist positive integers
s_{1},s_{2}=O_{d,J,\ell}(1)
with the following property: for all
H,M,N\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
H,M\leq N
,
n_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
, 1-bounded functions
f_{1}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, Hardy sequences
b_{1},\ldots,b_{J}\in{\mathcal{H}}
of growth
b_{j}(t)\ll t^{d}
, and polynomials
{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[n]
with degrees at most
d
,
form
{\mathbf{p}}_{j}(n)=\sum_{i=0}^{d}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{ji}n^{i}
, and such that
{\mathbf{p}}_{1}
is essentially distinct from
{\mathbf{p}}_{2},\ldots,{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}
, we have

\sup_{n_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1,\\
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{p}}_{j}(n_{0}+n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n_{0}+n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{O_{d,J,\ell}(1)}\\
\ll_{d,J,k,\ell}\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}}\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s_{2}}}\mathopen{}\left|\int\Delta_{{\mathbf{c}}_{1}({\underline{h}}),\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}({\underline{h}});({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime})}f_{1}\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|\\
+\frac{1}{H}+\frac{1}{M}+\frac{H}{N}+\frac{M}{N}+o_{N\to\infty;d,J,k,\ell,b_{1},\ldots,b_{J}}(1)

for nonzero polynomials
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[{\underline{h}}]
that depend only on
{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}
,
d
and
L
and satisfy the same properties as those listed in Proposition
7.7
.

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle\varepsilon=\sup_{n_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1,\\
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{p}}_{j}(n_{0}+n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n_{0}+n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

We allow all the quantities to depend on
d,J,k,\ell
, noting however that
s_{0},s_{1},s_{2}
and powers of
\varepsilon
will not depend on
k
. We split into two cases based on the degree of
{\mathbf{p}}_{1}
.

Case 1:
\deg{\mathbf{p}}_{1}>1
.

Suppose first that
\deg{\mathbf{p}}_{1}>1
.
By Lemma
5.2
applied with
K=H
, there exists a positive integer
s_{0}=O(1)
(independent of
k
) such that

\E_{{\underline{k}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{0}}}\sup_{n_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1,\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{p}}_{j}(n_{0}+n+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{k}})\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\\
+\frac{H}{N}+o_{N\to\infty}(1)\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}

(the
o_{N\to\infty}(1)
term is allowed to depend on
b_{1},\ldots,b_{J}
in addition to
d,k,\ell,L
).
Proposition
7.8
gives us positive integers
s_{1},s_{2}=O(1)
independent of
k
and polynomials
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[{\underline{h}},{\underline{k}}]
satisfying the properties listed in Proposition
7.7
for which

\E_{({\underline{h}},{\underline{k}})\in[\pm H]^{s_{0}+s_{1}}}\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s_{2}}}\mathopen{}\left|\int\Delta_{{\mathbf{c}}_{1}({\underline{h}},{\underline{k}}),\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}({\underline{h}},{\underline{k}});({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime})}f_{1}\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|\\
+\frac{1}{H}+\frac{H}{N}+\frac{M}{N}+o_{N\to\infty}(1)\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}

(the error
1/H
comes from the exceptional set of
{\underline{k}}
’s mentioned in Proposition
7.8
).
The result follows upon renaming
({\underline{h}},{\underline{k}})
as
{\underline{h}}
and
s_{0}+s_{1}
as
s_{1}
.

Case 2:
\deg{\mathbf{p}}_{1}=1
.

If
\deg{\mathbf{p}}=1
, then Lemma
5.2
applied with
K=M
gives a positive integer
s_{0}=O(1)
(independent of
k
) such that

\E_{{\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s_{0}}}\sup_{n_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\\
\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{p}}_{j}(n_{0}+n+({\underline{1}}-{\underline{\epsilon}})\cdot{\underline{k}}+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{k}}^{\prime})\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}+\frac{M}{N}+o_{N\to\infty}(1)\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}

(again, the
o_{N\to\infty}(1)
term is allowed to depend on
b_{1},\ldots,b_{J}
in addition to
d,k,\ell,L
).
Proposition
7.9
then gives positive integers
s_{1},s_{2}=O(1)
(also independent of
k
), polynomials
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[{\underline{h}}]
satisfying the properties listed in Proposition
7.7
, and shifts
{\mathbf{r}}_{1{\underline{\epsilon}}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
such that

\E_{{\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s_{0}}}\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}}\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s_{2}}}\\
\mathopen{}\left|\int\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{0}}}T^{{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}k_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\left\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}k_{s_{0}}^{\epsilon_{s_{0}}}\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}_{1{\underline{\epsilon}}}}\Delta_{{\mathbf{c}}_{1}({\underline{h}}),\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}({\underline{h}});({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime})}f_{1}\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|\\
+\frac{1}{M}+\frac{H}{N}+\frac{M}{N}+o_{N\to\infty}(1)\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

This is almost what we want except for the shifts
{\mathbf{r}}_{1{\underline{\epsilon}}}
. After squaring the absolute value using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and passing to the product system, the shifts
{\mathbf{r}}_{1{\underline{\epsilon}}}
can however be removed using Lemma
3.2
(the finitary Gowers-Cauchy-Schwarz inequality) applied to the average over
{\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}
as well as translation invariance. This gives

\E_{{\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s_{0}}}\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}}\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s_{2}}}\\
\mathopen{}\left|\int\Delta_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}^{s_{0}};({\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime})}\Delta_{{\mathbf{c}}_{1}({\underline{h}}),\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}({\underline{h}});({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime})}f_{1}\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{M}+\frac{H}{N}+\frac{M}{N}+o_{N\to\infty}(1)\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

The result follows upon including
s_{0}
copies of
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}
into the family
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}
(this new family of polynomials still satisfies the conditions set in Proposition
7.7
) and renaming
s_{0}+s_{2}
as
s_{2}
,
({\underline{k}},{\underline{m}})
as
{\underline{m}}
and
({\underline{k}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime})
as
{\underline{m}}^{\prime}
.
∎

The result below is a straightforward infinitary corollary of Proposition
7.10
.

Corollary 7.11

(Infinitary PET bound for polynomials with dual twists)
.

Let
d,k,\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
J\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
. There exist positive integers
s_{1},s_{2}=O_{d,J,\ell}(1)
with the following property: for all
H\in{\mathbb{N}}
, systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
, 1-bounded functions
f_{1}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, Hardy sequences
b_{1},\ldots,b_{J}\in{\mathcal{H}}
of growth
b_{j}(t)\ll t^{d}
, and polynomials
{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[n]
with degrees at most
d
,
form
{\mathbf{p}}_{j}(n)=\sum_{i=0}^{d}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{ji}n^{i}
, and such that
{\mathbf{p}}_{1}
is essentially distinct from
{\mathbf{p}}_{2},\ldots,{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}
, we have

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{n_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1,\\
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\\
\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{p}}_{j}(n_{0}+n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n_{0}+n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{O_{d,J,\ell}(1)}\\
\ll_{d,J,k,\ell}\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{1}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{{\mathbf{c}}_{1}({\underline{h}}),\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}({\underline{h}})}^{+}+\frac{1}{H}

for nonzero polynomials
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[{\underline{h}}]
that depend only on
{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}
,
d
and
L
and satisfy the same properties as those listed in Proposition
7.7
.

8.
Quantitative concatenation for seminorms with polynomial parameters

The purpose of this section is to show that the averages

(48)

\displaystyle\limsup_{H\to\infty}\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{{\mathbf{c}}_{1}({\underline{h}}),\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}({\underline{h}})}

of box seminorms along multilinear polynomials
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}\in{\mathbb{R}}[{\underline{h}}]
that have appeared in the previous section can be quantitatively controlled by a single box seminorm in an appropriate choice of directions. The way in which we “concatenate” the box seminorms appearing in (
48
) is closely modeled on the finitary argument recently developed in
[
52
]
, with both the general strategy and the main inputs being directly borrowed from that paper.

The quantitative concatenation of (
48
) into a single box seminorm relies on three main ingredients. The first one is a consequence of Corollary
4.7
that allows us to replace the polynomials
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}
by ones in more variables that are consequently better distributed in their ranges than the original polynomials. The second ingredient is an equidistribution result for a system of multilinear forms that appear while concatenating (
48
). The last component is a technical lemma that allows us to eliminate all the “nongeneric” tuples
{\underline{h}}
that might potentially bring trouble. The last two components are taken verbatim from
[
52
]
, while the first one is an ergodic adaptation of the arguments from that paper.

Below we state and prove these results; we illustrate the main ideas with the following example.

Example 8.1
.

Suppose that
s=1
and consider

(49)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{c}}(h_{1},h_{2})={\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}h_{1}h_{2}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{10}h_{1}.

By passing to the product system, we can deal with
\left|\!\left|\!\left|\cdot\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{{\mathbf{c}}({\underline{h}})}
rather than
\left|\!\left|\!\left|\cdot\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{{\mathbf{c}}({\underline{h}})}^{+}
.
For the sake of simplicity, suppose that
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{10}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
so that we do not have to deal with integer parts.
Let

\displaystyle\varepsilon=\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{2}}{\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{2}_{{\mathbf{c}}({\underline{h}})}}

\displaystyle=\E_{h_{1},h_{2}\in[\pm H]}\lim_{M\to\infty}\E_{m,m^{\prime}\in[\pm M]}{\int T^{{\mathbf{c}}(h_{1},h_{2})m^{\prime}}f\cdot T^{{\mathbf{c}}(h_{1},h_{2})m}\overline{f}\,d\mu}

\displaystyle=\lim_{M\to\infty}{\int f\cdot\E_{h_{1},h_{2}\in[\pm H]}\E_{m\in[\pm M]}T^{{\mathbf{c}}(h_{1},h_{2})m}\overline{f}\,d\mu};

the second line follows by composing with
T^{-{\mathbf{c}}(h_{1},h_{2})m^{\prime}}
and recalling from Lemma
4.1
that the limits
\lim\limits_{M\to\infty}\E_{m,m^{\prime}\in[\pm M]}S^{m-m^{\prime}}f
and
\lim\limits_{M\to\infty}\E_{m\in[\pm M]}S^{m}f
are the same for any transformation
S
. Our point is to apply the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality several times to increase the number of variables so that the expression
{\mathbf{c}}(h_{1},h_{2})m
is replaced by one better distributed inside
\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{10}\rangle
. Applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and invoking the 1-boundedness of
f
, we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{M\to\infty}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{11},h_{12},\\
h_{21},h_{22}\in[\pm H]\end{subarray}}\E_{m_{1},m_{2}\in[\pm M]}\int T^{{\mathbf{c}}(h_{11},h_{21})m_{1}}f\cdot T^{{\mathbf{c}}(h_{12},h_{22})m_{2}}\overline{f}\,d\mu

\displaystyle=\lim_{M\to\infty}\int f\cdot\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{11},h_{12},\\
h_{21},h_{22}\in[\pm H]\end{subarray}}\E_{m_{1},m_{2}\in[\pm M]}T^{{\mathbf{c}}(h_{12},h_{22})m_{2}-{\mathbf{c}}(h_{11},h_{21})m_{1}}\overline{f}\,d\mu.

We note that after this and the next applications of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, the limit as
M\to\infty
exists because the expression that we are arriving at is a finite average of box seminorms.

The polynomial
{\mathbf{c}}(h_{12},h_{22})m_{2}-{\mathbf{c}}(h_{11},h_{21})m_{1}
is much better equidistributed inside its range than
{\mathbf{c}}(h_{1},h_{2})m
was. However, it is still not quite as well distributed as necessary. We therefore apply the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality one more time, getting

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{4}

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{M\to\infty}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1k},h_{2k}\in[\pm H]:\\
k\in[4]\end{subarray}}\;\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{k}\in[\pm M]:\\
k\in[4]\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\int T^{{\mathbf{c}}(h_{12},h_{22})m_{2}-{\mathbf{c}}(h_{11},h_{21})m_{1}}f\cdot T^{{\mathbf{c}}(h_{13},h_{23})m_{3}-{\mathbf{c}}(h_{14},h_{24})m_{4}}\overline{f}\,d\mu

\displaystyle=\lim_{M\to\infty}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1k},h_{2k}\in[\pm H]:\\
k\in[4]\end{subarray}}\;\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{k}\in[\pm M]:\\
k\in[4]\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\int f\cdot T^{{\mathbf{c}}(h_{11},h_{21})m_{1}-{\mathbf{c}}(h_{12},h_{22})m_{2}+{\mathbf{c}}(h_{13},h_{23})m_{3}-{\mathbf{c}}(h_{14},h_{24})m_{4}}\overline{f}\,d\mu.

By definition, we have

{\mathbf{c}}(h_{11},h_{21})m_{1}-{\mathbf{c}}(h_{12},h_{22})m_{2}+{\mathbf{c}}(h_{13},h_{23})m_{3}-{\mathbf{c}}(h_{14},h_{24})m_{4}\\
={\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}(h_{11}h_{21}m_{1}-h_{12}h_{22}m_{2}+h_{13}h_{23}m_{3}-h_{14}h_{24}m_{4})\\
+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{10}(h_{11}m_{1}-h_{12}m_{2}+h_{13}m_{3}-h_{14}m_{4})

As
h_{1k},m_{k}
range over
[\pm H],[\pm M]
respectively, the bilinear form
h_{11}m_{1}-h_{12}m_{2}+h_{13}m_{3}-h_{14}m_{4}
will range fairly uniformly over
[\pm 4HM]
; however, this is not the case for the trilinear form
h_{11}h_{21}m_{1}-h_{12}h_{22}m_{2}+h_{13}h_{23}m_{3}-h_{14}h_{24}m_{4}
. To uniformize the coefficient of
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}
, we apply the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality two more times, each time only doubling the variables
h_{2k},m_{k}
. This gives us

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{16}

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{M\to\infty}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1k_{1}},h_{2k_{1}k_{2}}\in[\pm H]:\\
k_{1},k_{2}\in[4]\end{subarray}}\;\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{k_{1}k_{2}}\in[\pm M]:\\
k_{1},k_{2}\in[4]\end{subarray}}\int f\cdot T^{\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\in[4]}(-1)^{k_{1}+k_{2}}{\mathbf{c}}(h_{1k_{1}},h_{2k_{1}k_{2}})m_{k_{1}k_{2}}}\overline{f}\,d\mu.

Since the range of all the variables
h_{1k_{1}},h_{2k_{1}k_{2}},m_{k_{1}k_{2}}
is symmetric around the origin, we can make suitable choices of variables to remove the
(-1)^{k_{1}+k_{2}}
factor, obtaining

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{16}

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{M\to\infty}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1k_{1}},h_{2k_{1}k_{2}}\in[\pm H]:\\
k_{1},k_{2}\in[4]\end{subarray}}\;\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{k_{1}k_{2}}\in[\pm M]:\\
k_{1},k_{2}\in[4]\end{subarray}}\int f\cdot T^{\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\in[4]}{\mathbf{c}}(h_{1k_{1}},h_{2k_{1}k_{2}})m_{k_{1}k_{2}}}\overline{f}\,d\mu.

The polynomial appearing in the average above takes the form

(50)

\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\in[4]}{\mathbf{c}}(h_{1k_{1}},h_{2k_{1}k_{2}})m_{k_{1}k_{2}}\\
={\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}(h_{11}(h_{211}m_{11}+\cdots+h_{214}m_{14})+\cdots+h_{14}(h_{241}m_{41}+\cdots+h_{244}m_{44}))\\
+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{10}(h_{11}(m_{11}+\cdots+m_{14})+\cdots+h_{14}(m_{41}+\cdots+m_{44})).

Fix
k_{1}\in[4]
. As
h_{2k_{1}k_{2}},m_{k_{1}k_{2}}
range over
[\pm H],[\pm M]
respectively, it turns out that the expression

(51)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}h_{2k_{1}1}m_{k_{1}1}+h_{2k_{1}2}m_{k_{1}2}+h_{2k_{1}3}m_{k_{1}3}+h_{2k_{1}4}m_{k_{1}4}\\
m_{k_{1}1}+m_{k_{1}2}+m_{k_{1}3}+m_{k_{1}4}\end{pmatrix}

is fairly uniformly distributed inside
[\pm 4HM]\times[\pm 4M]
in that any element of this set takes the form (
51
) for no more than
O(H^{3}M^{2})
values
(h_{2k_{1}k_{2}},m_{k_{1}k_{2}})_{k_{2}\in[4]}
. Morally, this allows us to replace (
51
) by
\begin{pmatrix}t_{k_{1}1}\\
t_{k_{1}0}\end{pmatrix}

ranging in
[\pm 4HM]\times[\pm 4M]
at the cost of a loss of a factor
O(1)
in the bound, yielding

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{16}

\displaystyle\ll\limsup_{M\to\infty}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1k_{1}}\in[\pm H]:\\
k_{1}\in[4]\end{subarray}}\;\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}t_{k_{1}1}\in[\pm 4HM]:\\
k_{1}\in[4]\end{subarray}}\;\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}t_{k_{1}0}\in[\pm 4M]:\\
k_{1}\in[4]\end{subarray}}\mathopen{}\left|\int f\cdot T^{\sum_{k_{1}\in[4]}\tilde{{\mathbf{c}}}(h_{1k_{1}},t_{k_{1}1},t_{k_{1}0})}\overline{f}\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|

for

\displaystyle\tilde{{\mathbf{c}}}(h_{1k_{1}},t_{k_{1}1},t_{k_{1}0})={\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}h_{1k_{1}}t_{k_{1}1}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{10}h_{1k_{1}}t_{k_{1}0}.

We have thus reduced the trilinear polynomial
{\mathbf{c}}(h_{1k_{1}},h_{2k_{1}k_{2}})m_{k_{1}k_{2}}
to the bilinear polynomial
\tilde{{\mathbf{c}}}(h_{1k_{1}},t_{k_{1}1},t_{k_{1}0})
. The iterate then takes the form

\displaystyle\sum_{k_{1}\in[4]}\tilde{{\mathbf{c}}}(h_{1k_{1}},t_{k_{1}1},t_{k_{1}0})={\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}(h_{11}t_{11}+\cdots+h_{14}t_{41})+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{10}(h_{11}t_{10}+\cdots+h_{14}t_{40}).

A similar argument as before shows that as
h_{1k_{1}},t_{k_{1}1},t_{k_{1}0}
range over
[\pm H],[\pm 4HM],[\pm 4M]
respectively, the vectors

(52)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}h_{11}t_{11}+h_{12}t_{21}+h_{13}t_{31}+h_{14}t_{41}\\
h_{11}t_{10}+h_{12}t_{20}+h_{13}t_{30}+h_{14}t_{40}\end{pmatrix}

are uniformly distributed in their range, which now takes the form
[\pm 16H^{2}M]\times[\pm 16HM]
in that no element of this box equals (
52
) more than
O(1)
times the expected number. This allows us to replace each vector (
52
) by a vector
\begin{pmatrix}t_{1}\\
t_{0}\end{pmatrix}
ranging in
[\pm 16H^{2}M]\times[\pm 16HM]
, giving

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{16}

\displaystyle\ll\limsup_{M\to\infty}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}t_{1}\in[\pm 16H^{2}M]\end{subarray}}\;\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}t_{0}\in[\pm 16HM]\end{subarray}}\mathopen{}\left|\int f\cdot T^{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}t_{1}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{10}t_{0}}\overline{f}\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|.

Squaring both sides and passing to the product system, we get

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{32}

\displaystyle\ll\lim_{M\to\infty}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}t_{1}\in[\pm 16H^{2}M]\end{subarray}}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}t_{0}\in[\pm 16HM]\end{subarray}}\int f\otimes\overline{f}\cdot(T\times T)^{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}t_{1}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{10}t_{0}}\overline{f}\otimes f\,d(\mu\times\mu)

\displaystyle=\lim_{M\to\infty}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}t_{0},t_{1}\in[\pm M]\end{subarray}}\int f\otimes\overline{f}\cdot(T\times T)^{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}t_{1}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{10}t_{0}}\overline{f}\otimes f\,d(\mu\times\mu);

the fact that the limit along
[\pm 16H^{2}M]\times[\pm 16HM]
exists and can be replaced by a limit along
[\pm M]^{\times}[\pm M]
follows from Lemma
3.1
. It follows that

\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G}=\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\otimes\overline{f}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G}^{1/2}\gg\varepsilon^{8},

where
G=\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{10}\rangle
, completing the concatenation step.

For longer averages involving higher degree polynomials, there will naturally be additional complications. Replacing the polynomials
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s}
by better distributed ones will be the subject of Proposition
8.2
. The relevant equidistribution results also become more complicated and are covered in Proposition
8.3
. Lastly, we will need to restrict the tuples of
{\underline{h}}
to those which are not too small and whose elements have pairwise small greatest common divisor. That such tuples are generic is guaranteed by Lemma
8.4
. However, the underlying idea behind dealing with the general case of (
48
) is similar: we first dramatically increase the number of variables to make the polynomials better distributed, and then we use this well-distribution to replace all multilinear polynomials by single variables.

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
d,k,s_{1},s_{2}\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\ell
be a nonnegative integer power of 2. There exists a positive integer
t=O_{\ell,s_{1},s_{2}}(1)
such that for all polynomials
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[{\underline{h}}]
of degree at most
d
and coefficients
O(1)
(where
{\underline{h}}
are vectors in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{s_{1}}
), numbers
H\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and 1-bounded functions
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

(53)

\mathopen{}\left(\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{{\mathbf{c}}_{1}({\underline{h}}),\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}({\underline{h}})}\mathclose{}\right)^{O_{d,\ell,s_{1},s_{2}}(1)}\\
\ll_{d,k,\ell,s_{1},s_{2}}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}}\in[\pm H]:\\
l\in[s_{1}],\;k_{1},\ldots,k_{s_{1}}\in[t]\end{subarray}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\langle{\mathbf{c}}_{j}(h_{1k_{1i_{1}}},\ldots,h_{s_{1}k_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{s_{1}i_{s_{1}}}}):\;i_{1},\ldots,i_{s_{1}}\in[\ell]\rangle:\\
j\in[s_{2}],\;1\leq k_{l1}<\cdots<k_{l\ell}\leq t,\;l\in[s_{1}]\end{subarray}}.

Proposition
8.2
generalizes the first step of Example
8.1
in which we increase the number of variables. In Example
8.1
, the role of
{\mathbf{c}}_{1}
in the left-hand side of (
53
) is played by (
49
), and the subgroup in the right-hand side of (
53
) consists of expressions (
50
) with
m
’s ranging over
{\mathbb{Z}}
;
the bound (
53
) then holds with
s_{1}=2
(since
{\mathbf{c}}_{1}
involves two parameters
h_{1},h_{2}
),
s_{2}=1
(corresponding to the degrees of the input box seminorms),
t=4
(corresponding to the range of the parameters
k_{1},k_{2}
) and
\ell=4
(corresponding to adding four different
k_{2}
’s in (
51
) and four different
k_{1}
’s in (
52
)).

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle\varepsilon=\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{{\mathbf{c}}_{1}({\underline{h}}),\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}({\underline{h}})}.

The proof consists of multiple applications of Corollary
4.7
. First, we apply it with the indexing set
{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}
to find a positive integer
t_{1}=O_{\ell,s_{2}}(1)
such that

\displaystyle\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}_{1},\ldots,{\underline{h}}_{t_{1}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\langle{\mathbf{c}}_{j}({\underline{h}}_{k_{11}}),\;\ldots,\;{\mathbf{c}}_{j}({\underline{h}}_{k_{1\ell}})\rangle:\\
j\in[s_{2}],\;1\leq k_{11}<\cdots<k_{1\ell}\leq t_{1}\end{subarray}}\gg_{k,\ell,s_{2}}\varepsilon^{O_{\ell,s_{2}}(1)}.

In the second application of Corollary
4.7
, we rewrite
{\underline{h}}_{k}=(h_{1k},\tilde{{\underline{h}}}_{1k})
, and we take the indexing set to be
(\tilde{{\underline{h}}}_{11},\ldots,\tilde{{\underline{h}}}_{1t_{1}})\in[\pm H]^{(s_{1}-1)t_{1}}
. Then Corollary
4.7
, applied separately to each
(h_{11},\ldots,h_{1t_{1}})
, gives a positive integer
t_{2}=O_{\ell,s_{2}}(1)
for which

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1k_{1}}\in[\pm H]:\\
k_{1}\in[t_{1}]\end{subarray}}\;\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{{\underline{h}}}_{1k_{1}k_{2}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}-1}:\\
k_{1}\in[t_{1}],\;k_{2}\in[t_{2}]\end{subarray}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\langle{\mathbf{c}}_{j}(h_{1k_{11}},\tilde{{\underline{h}}}_{1k_{11}k_{21}}),\;\ldots,\;{\mathbf{c}}_{j}(h_{1k_{11}},\tilde{{\underline{h}}}_{1k_{11}k_{2\ell}}),\\
\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{j}(h_{1k_{1\ell}},\tilde{{\underline{h}}}_{1k_{1\ell}k_{21}}),\;\ldots,\;{\mathbf{c}}_{j}(h_{1k_{1\ell}},\tilde{{\underline{h}}}_{1k_{1\ell}k_{2\ell}})\rangle:\\
j\in[s],\;1\leq k_{11}<\cdots<k_{1\ell}\leq t_{1},\;1\leq k_{21}<\cdots<k_{2\ell}\leq t_{2}\end{subarray}}\gg_{k,\ell,s_{2}}\varepsilon^{O_{\ell,s_{2}}(1)}.

At the subsequent step, we similarly write
\tilde{{\underline{h}}}_{1k_{1}k_{2}}=(h_{2k_{1}k_{2}},\tilde{h}_{2k_{1}k_{2}}).
Taking

(\tilde{h}_{2k_{1}k_{2}})_{k_{1}\in[t_{1}],k_{2}\in[t_{2}]}\in[\pm H]^{(s_{1}-2)t_{1}t_{2}}

as the indexing set, we apply Corollary
4.7
separately to each
(h_{1k_{1}},h_{2k_{1}k_{2}})_{k_{1},k_{2}}
, obtaining a positive integer
t_{3}=O_{\ell,s_{2}}(1)
for which

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1k_{1}}\in[\pm H]:\\
k_{1}\in[t_{1}]\end{subarray}}\;\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1k_{1}k_{2}}\in[\pm H]:\\
k_{1}\in[t_{1}],\;k_{2}\in[t_{2}]\end{subarray}}\;\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{{\underline{h}}}_{2k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}-2}:\\
k_{1}\in[t_{1}],\;k_{2}\in[t_{2}],\;k_{3}\in[t_{3}]\end{subarray}}\\
\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\langle{\mathbf{c}}_{j}(h_{1k_{1i_{1}}},h_{2k_{1i_{1}}k_{2i_{2}}},\tilde{{\underline{h}}}_{2k_{1i_{1}}k_{2i_{2}}k_{3i_{3}}}):\;i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\in[\ell]\rangle:\\
j\in[s_{2}],\;1\leq k_{l1}<\cdots<k_{l\ell}\leq t_{l},\;l\in[3]\end{subarray}}\gg_{k,\ell,s_{2}}\varepsilon^{O_{\ell,s_{2}}(1)}.

Continuing in this fashion
s_{1}-3
more times, we find positive integers
t_{4},\ldots,t_{s_{1}}=O_{\ell,s_{1},s_{2}}(1)
such that

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}}\in[\pm H]:\\
l\in[s_{1}],\;k_{1}\in[t_{1}],\;\ldots,\;k_{s_{1}}\in[t_{s_{1}}]\end{subarray}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\langle{\mathbf{c}}_{j}(h_{1k_{1i_{1}}},\ldots,h_{s_{1}k_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{s_{1}i_{s_{1}}}}):\;i_{1},\ldots,i_{s_{1}}\in[\ell]\rangle:\\
j\in[s_{2}],\;1\leq k_{l1}<\cdots<k_{l\ell}\leq t_{l},\;l\in[s_{1}]\end{subarray}}\gg_{k,\ell,s_{1},s_{2}}\varepsilon^{O_{\ell,s_{1},s_{2}}(1)}.

We could just end here, however, it is notationally convenient if we can deal with only one
t_{l}
. Setting
t=\max(t_{1},\ldots,t_{s_{1}})
, we deduce the result from the monotonicity property of the seminorms.
∎

Each group
\langle{\mathbf{c}}_{j}(h_{1k_{1i_{1}}},\ldots,h_{s_{1}k_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{s_{1}i_{s_{1}}}})\colon\;i_{1},\ldots,i_{s_{1}}\in[\ell]\rangle
in the left-hand side of (
53
) consists of multilinear forms like (
50
). Importantly, as long as
\ell
is sufficiently large (
\ell\geq 3
will do), the system of multilinear forms that comes from taking all the groups in the right hand side of (
53
) is equidistributed in its range. This statement is made precise by the following proposition.

Proposition 8.3

(Equidistribution of systems of multilinear forms,
[
52
, Proposition 7.5]
)
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
,
\ell,s_{1},s_{2},t\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
3\leq\ell\leq t
, and let
H,M\in{\mathbb{N}}
with

\varepsilon^{-O_{\ell,s_{1},s_{2},t}(1)}\ll_{\ell,s_{1},s_{2},t}H\leq M.

Let

(54)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}=\{(k_{li})_{\begin{subarray}{c}(l,i)\in[s_{1}]\times[\ell]\end{subarray}}\in[t]^{s_{1}\ell}:\;1\leq k_{li}<\cdots<k_{l\ell}\leq t\;\;\textrm{for\; all}\;\;l\in[s_{1}]\},

and for each
l\in[s_{1}]
, let also

(55)

{\mathcal{H}}_{l,\varepsilon}=\{(h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}})_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{l}\in[t]}\in[\pm H]^{t^{l}}:\;|h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}}-h_{lk^{\prime\prime}_{1}\cdots k^{\prime\prime}_{l}}|\geq\varepsilon H,\\
\gcd(h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}}-h_{lk^{\prime\prime}_{1}\cdots k^{\prime\prime}_{l}},h_{lk^{\prime}_{1}\cdots k^{\prime}_{l}}-h_{lk^{\prime\prime}_{1}\cdots k^{\prime\prime}_{l}})\leq\varepsilon^{-1}\\
\textrm{for\; distinct}\;(k_{1},\ldots,k_{l}),\;(k^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,k^{\prime}_{l}),\;(k^{\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,k^{\prime\prime}_{l})\in[t]^{l}\}.

Then

\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}:\;j\in[s],\\
{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}},\;{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}}\end{subarray}}\;\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}\cdots i_{s_{1}}}\in[\pm M]:\\
j\in[s_{2}],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}},\;i_{1},\ldots,i_{s_{1}}\in[\ell]\end{subarray}}\;\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}}\in[\pm H]:\\
k_{1},\ldots,k_{s_{1}}\in[t],\;l\in[s_{1}]\end{subarray}}\;\mathopen{}\left(\prod_{l\in[s_{1}]}1_{{\mathcal{H}}_{l,\varepsilon}}((h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}})_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{l}})\mathclose{}\right)\\
\prod_{j\in[s_{2}]}\;\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}}\end{subarray}}1\Bigl(\sum\limits_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{s_{1}}\in[\ell]}h_{1k_{1i_{1}}}^{u_{1}}\cdots h_{s_{1}k_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{s_{1}i_{s_{1}}}}^{u_{s_{1}}}m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}\cdots i_{s_{1}}}=n_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}\Bigr)\\
\ll_{\ell,s_{1},s_{2},t}\varepsilon^{-O_{\ell,s_{1},s_{2},t}(1)}M^{-2^{s_{1}}s_{2}|{\mathcal{K}}|}H^{-s_{1}2^{s_{1}-1}s_{2}|{\mathcal{K}}|},

where we recall our convention for the indicator function from Section
2
.

On the first read, we advise the reader to assume that
t=\ell
, so that the indexing set
{\mathcal{K}}
consists of only one element,
k_{li_{l}}
can be replaced by
k_{l}
, and the product
\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\end{subarray}}
disappears.

The point of restricting the range of
h
’s to (
55
) is that multilinear forms like
(h-h^{\prime\prime})(h^{\prime}-h^{\prime\prime})
that appear in the proof of Proposition
8.3
are only well distributed in its range if
h-h^{\prime\prime},h^{\prime}-h^{\prime\prime}
are not too small and do not have large gcd. The next lemma shows that the set of such well-behaved
h
’s is generic.

Lemma 8.4

(
[
52
, Lemma 7.8]
)
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
,
H,l,t\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
{\mathcal{H}}_{l,\varepsilon}
be as in (
55
). Then all but a
O_{l,t}(\varepsilon)
proportion of elements of
[\pm H]^{t^{l}}
lie in
{\mathcal{H}}_{l,\varepsilon}
.

In what follows, we let
A_{d,s_{1}}:=\left\{{\underline{u}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{s_{1}}:\ |{\underline{u}}|\leq d\right\}
. The next result establishes that averages of generalized box seminorms in which boxes are generated by multilinear polynomials
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}
can be controlled with polynomial losses by a single generalized box seminorm of bounded degree along subgroups spanned by the coefficients of the polynomials
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}
.

Proposition 8.5

(Quantitative concatenation of box seminorms along polynomials)
.

Let
d,k,s_{1},s_{2}\in{\mathbb{N}}
. There exists a positive integer
s_{3}=O_{d,s_{1},s_{2}}(1)
with the following property: for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
, 1-bounded functions
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, and polynomials
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[{\underline{h}}]

(where
{\underline{h}}
are vectors in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{s_{1}}
)
of the form

\displaystyle{\mathbf{c}}_{j}({\underline{h}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\in A_{d,s_{1}}\end{subarray}}{\mathbf{v}}_{j{\underline{u}}}{\underline{h}}^{{\underline{u}}}

for some
{\mathbf{v}}_{j{\underline{u}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
,
we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{H\to\infty}\mathopen{}\left(\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{{{\mathbf{c}}_{1}({\underline{h}})},\ldots,{{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}({\underline{h}})}}\mathclose{}\right)^{O_{s_{1},s_{2}}(1)}\ll_{k,s_{1},s_{2}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{1}^{s_{3}},\ldots,G_{s_{2}}^{s_{3}}},

where
G_{j}=\langle{\mathbf{v}}_{j{\underline{u}}}:\ {\underline{u}}\in A_{d,s_{1}}\rangle
for
j\in[s_{2}]
.

Proof.

Set

\displaystyle\varepsilon=\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{{{\mathbf{c}}_{1}({\underline{h}})},\ldots,{{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}({\underline{h}})}}^{+}.

We let all the constants depend on
k,s_{1},s_{2}
, noting however that powers of
\varepsilon
do not depend on
k
.
By Proposition
8.2
applied with
\ell=4
,
16
16

16

The reason for taking
\ell=4
is as follows: 4 is the smallest power of 2 greater than or equal to 3, and we have a lower bound
\ell\geq 3
in Proposition
8.3
that we will apply shortly.
there exists a positive integer
t=O(1)
(independent of
k
) for which

(56)

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}}\in[\pm H]:\\
l\in[s_{1}],\;k_{1},\ldots,k_{s_{1}}\in[t]\end{subarray}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\langle{\mathbf{c}}_{j}(h_{1k_{1i_{1}}},\ldots,h_{s_{1}k_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{s_{1}i_{s_{1}}}}):\;i_{1},\ldots,i_{s_{1}}\in[4]\rangle:\\
j\in[s_{2}],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\end{subarray}}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)};

we have also applied here Lemma
3.9
to replace
\left|\!\left|\!\left|\cdot\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}
with
\left|\!\left|\!\left|\cdot\right|\!\right|\!\right|
, which we can by increasing
t
by 1, say.
For each choice of
j\in[s_{2}],{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}
and
h
’s, the group

\langle{\mathbf{c}}_{j}(h_{1k_{1i_{1}}},\ldots,h_{s_{1}k_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{s_{1}i_{s_{1}}}}):\;i_{1},\ldots,i_{s_{1}}\in[4]\rangle

consists of elements

(57)

\displaystyle\sum_{{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}}}{\mathbf{v}}_{j{\underline{u}}}\sum\limits_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{s_{1}}\in[4]}h_{1k_{1i_{1}}}^{u_{1}}\cdots h_{s_{1}k_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{s_{1}i_{s_{1}}}}^{u_{s_{1}}}m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}\cdots i_{s_{1}}}

with
m
’s ranging over
{\mathbb{Z}}
, and so applying the Hölder inequality to (
56
) and expanding the definition of the seminorm, we get

\lim_{M\to\infty}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}}\in[\pm H]:\\
l\in[s_{1}],\;k_{1},\ldots,k_{s_{1}}\in[t]\end{subarray}}\;\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}\cdots i_{s_{1}}},m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}\cdots i_{s_{1}}}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]:\\
j\in[s_{2}],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}},\;i_{1},\ldots,i_{s_{1}}\in[4]\end{subarray}}\\
\int\Delta_{\begin{subarray}{c}\big(\sum\limits_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{s_{1}}\in[4]}{\mathbf{c}}_{j}(h_{1k_{1i_{1}}},\ldots,h_{s_{1}k_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{s_{1}i_{s_{1}}}})m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}\cdots i_{s_{1}}},\\
\sum\limits_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{s_{1}}\in[4]}{\mathbf{c}}_{j}(h_{1k_{1i_{1}}},\ldots,h_{s_{1}k_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{s_{1}i_{s_{1}}}})m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}\cdots i_{s_{1}}}^{\prime}\big):\;j\in[s_{2}],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\end{subarray}}\;f\;d\mu\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

To simplify the expression above, we compose the integral with

\displaystyle T^{-\sum_{j\in[s_{2}]}\sum_{{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}}{\left\lfloor\sum\limits_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{s_{1}}\in[4]}{\mathbf{c}}_{j}(h_{1k_{1i_{1}}},\ldots,h_{s_{1}k_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{s_{1}i_{s_{1}}}})m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}\cdots i_{s_{1}}}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}}.

Swapping the order of subtraction and integer parts, replacing
m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}\cdots i_{s_{1}}}-m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}\cdots i_{s_{1}}}^{\prime}
by
m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}\cdots i_{s_{1}}}
, dealing with the error terms using Lemma
5.3
, and using the triangle inequality to get rid of resulting weights, we get

(58)

\lim_{M\to\infty}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}}\in[\pm H]:\\
l\in[s_{1}],\;k_{1},\ldots,k_{s_{1}}\in[t]\end{subarray}}\;\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}\cdots i_{s_{1}}}\in[\pm 2M]:\\
j\in[s_{2}],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}},\;i_{1},\ldots,i_{s_{1}}\in[4]\end{subarray}}\\
\mathopen{}\left|\int\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{2}|{\mathcal{K}}|}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{\epsilon_{j{\underline{k}}}\cdot{\left\lfloor\sum\limits_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{s_{1}}\in[4]}{\mathbf{c}}_{j}(h_{1k_{1i_{1}}},\ldots,h_{s_{1}k_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{s_{1}i_{s_{1}}}})m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}\cdots i_{s_{1}}}\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}_{{\underline{\epsilon}}}}f\;d\mu\mathclose{}\right|\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}

for some
{\mathbf{r}}_{\underline{\epsilon}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
.

Our goal is to use Proposition
8.3
to show that the gigantic system of multilinear forms

(59)

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{s_{1}}\in[4]}h_{1k_{1i_{1}}}^{u_{1}}\cdots h_{s_{1}k_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{s_{1}i_{s_{1}}}}^{u_{s_{1}}}m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}\cdots i_{s_{1}}}

indexed by
j\in[s_{2}],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}},\;{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}}
that appears in (
58
) after substituting
{\mathbf{c}}_{j}(h_{1k_{1i_{1}}},\ldots,h_{s_{1}k_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{s_{1}i_{s_{1}}}})
by an element of the form (
57
)
is equidistributed in its range, in that we can replace each (
59
) by a separate variable
n_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}
ranging over
[\pm C_{{\underline{u}}}H^{|{\underline{u}}|}M]
for some
C_{\underline{u}}>0
. The first step to achieve this is to throw out those tuples of
h
’s whose coordinates are either close to each other or have a large greatest common divisor.
For some small
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
to be chosen later, let
{\mathcal{H}}_{l}={\mathcal{H}}_{l,\varepsilon^{\prime}}
be the set defined in (
55
). By Lemma
8.4
, the set
{\mathcal{H}}_{l,\varepsilon^{\prime}}
contains all but a
O(\varepsilon^{\prime})
proportion of elements of
[\pm H]^{t^{l}}
; that means that generically, the differences of the numbers
h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}}
are far from 0 and have small greatest common divisor. Taking
\varepsilon^{\prime}=c\varepsilon^{1/c}
for a sufficiently small
c>0
, we can ignore in (
58
) all the tuples
(h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}})_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{l}}
not in
{\mathcal{H}}_{l}
. Thus, we obtain

\limsup_{M\to\infty}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}}\in[\pm H]:\\
l\in[s_{1}],\;k_{1},\ldots,k_{s_{1}}\in[t]\end{subarray}}\;{{\prod_{l\in[s_{1}]}1_{{\mathcal{H}}_{l}}((h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}})_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{l}})}}\cdot\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}\cdots i_{s_{1}}}\in[\pm M]:\\
j\in[s_{2}],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}},\;i_{1},\ldots,i_{s_{1}}\in[4]\end{subarray}}\\
\mathopen{}\left|\int\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{2}|{\mathcal{K}}|}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{\epsilon_{j{\underline{k}}}\cdot{\left\lfloor\sum\limits_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{s_{1}}\in[4]}{\mathbf{c}}_{j}(h_{1k_{1i_{1}}},\ldots,h_{s_{1}k_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{s_{1}i_{s_{1}}}})m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}\cdots i_{s_{1}}}\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}_{{\underline{\epsilon}}}}f\;d\mu\mathclose{}\right|\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

The introduction of extra summations over
n_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}
for
j\in[s_{2}],{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}},{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}}
allows us to bound

\limsup_{M\to\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}\ll H^{|{\underline{u}}|}M:\\
j\in[s_{2}],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}},\;{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}}\end{subarray}}{\mathcal{N}}({\underline{n}})\\
\mathopen{}\left|\int\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{2}|{\mathcal{K}}|}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{\epsilon_{j{\underline{k}}}\cdot{\left\lfloor\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}}\end{subarray}}{\mathbf{v}}_{j{\underline{u}}}n_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}_{{\underline{\epsilon}}}}f\;d\mu\mathclose{}\right|\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)},

for

{\mathcal{N}}({\underline{n}})=\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}\cdots i_{s_{1}}}\in[\pm M]:\\
j\in[s_{2}],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}},\;i_{1},\ldots,i_{s_{1}}\in[\ell]\end{subarray}}\;\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}}\in[\pm H]:\\
k_{1},\ldots,k_{l}\in[t],\;l\in[s_{1}]\end{subarray}}\;{\prod_{l\in[s_{1}]}1_{{\mathcal{H}}_{l}}((h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}})_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{l}})}\\
\prod_{j\in[s]}\;\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}}\end{subarray}}1\Bigl(\sum\limits_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{s_{1}}\in[\ell]}h_{1k_{1i_{1}}}^{u_{1}}\cdots h_{s_{1}k_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{s_{1}i_{s_{1}}}}^{u_{s_{1}}}m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}\cdots i_{s_{1}}}=n_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}\Bigr).

The kernel
{\mathcal{N}}
is supported on the tuples
(n_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}})_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[s_{2}],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}},\\
{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}}\end{subarray}}
satisfying
n_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}\ll H^{|{\underline{u}}|}M
, of which there are

\displaystyle\ll\prod_{j\in[s_{2}]}\prod_{{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}}\prod_{{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}}}H^{|{\underline{u}}|}M\ll M^{2^{s_{1}}s_{2}|{\mathcal{K}}|}H^{s_{1}2^{s_{1}-1}s_{2}|{\mathcal{K}}|}

due to the identity

\displaystyle\sum_{u_{1}=0}^{1}\cdots\sum_{u_{s_{1}}=0}^{1}(u_{1}+\cdots+u_{s_{1}})=\sum_{i=0}^{s_{1}}i{\binom{s_{1}}{i}}=s_{1}2^{s_{1}-1}

that can easily be proved by induction.

Crucially, Proposition
8.3
allows us to bound

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}({\underline{n}})\ll\varepsilon^{-O(1)}M^{-2^{s_{1}}s_{2}|{\mathcal{K}}|}H^{-s_{1}2^{s_{1}-1}s_{2}|{\mathcal{K}}|}

as long as
M\geq H\gg\varepsilon^{-O(1)}
,
and so we get the estimate

\displaystyle\limsup_{M\to\infty}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}\ll H^{|{\underline{u}}|}M:\\
j\in[s_{2}],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}},\;{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}}\end{subarray}}\mathopen{}\left|\int\Delta_{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}}\end{subarray}}{\mathbf{v}}_{j{\underline{u}}}n_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}:\;j\in[s_{2}],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}}\;f\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

The claimed inequality promptly follows upon taking
M\to\infty
, followed by taking
H\to\infty
.
17
17

17

We note here that taking
H\to\infty
is not strictly necessary; a close analysis of the argument shows that if

\displaystyle\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{{{\mathbf{c}}_{1}({\underline{h}})},\ldots,{{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}({\underline{h}})}}\geq\varepsilon,

then

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{{{\mathbf{c}}_{1}({\underline{h}})},\ldots,{{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}({\underline{h}})}}\mathclose{}\right)^{O_{s_{1},s_{2}}(1)}\ll_{k,s_{1},s_{2}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{G_{1}^{s_{3}},\ldots,G_{s_{2}}^{s_{3}}},

as long as
H\gg_{k,s_{1},s_{2}}\varepsilon^{-O_{s_{1},s_{2}}(1)}.

∎

Propositions
7.7
and
8.5
together give the quantitative control of polynomial averages by a bounded-degree generalized box seminorm that depends only on the leading coefficients of the polynomials and their differences.

Proposition 8.6

(Box seminorm control for polynomial with dual twists)
.

Let
d,k,\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
J\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
. There exists a positive integer
s=O_{d,J,\ell}(1)
with the following property: for all
n_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
, 1-bounded functions
f_{1}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, Hardy sequences
b_{1},\ldots,b_{J}\in{\mathcal{H}}
of growth
b_{j}(t)\ll t^{d}
, and polynomials
{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[n]
of degrees at most
d
, form
{\mathbf{p}}_{j}(n)=\sum_{i=0}^{d}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{ji}n^{i}
, and such that
{\mathbf{p}}_{1}
is essentially distinct from
{\mathbf{p}}_{2},\ldots,{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}
, we have

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{n_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1,\\
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{p}}_{j}(n_{0}+n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n_{0}+n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{O_{d,\ell,J}(1)}\\
\ll_{d,J,k,\ell}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{1}\right|\!\right|\!\right|^{+}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1d_{10}}^{s},({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1d_{12}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2d_{12}})^{s},\ldots,({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1d_{1\ell}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\ell d_{1\ell}})^{s}},

where
d_{1j}=\deg({\mathbf{p}}_{1}-{\mathbf{p}}_{j})
for
j\in[0,\ell]
and
{\mathbf{p}}_{0}={\bf 0}
.

Proof.

Let

\varepsilon=\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{n_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1,\\
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\\
\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{p}}_{j}(n_{0}+n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n_{0}+n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Corollary
7.11
implies that

\displaystyle\limsup_{H\to\infty}\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{1}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{{\mathbf{c}}_{1}({\underline{h}}),\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}({\underline{h}})}^{+}\gg_{d,J,k,\ell}\varepsilon^{O_{d,\ell,J}(1)}

for some
s_{1},s_{2}=O_{d,\ell,J}(1)
(independent of
k
) and nonzero polynomials
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[{\underline{h}}]
that depend only on
{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}
,
d,J,\ell
and take the form

\displaystyle{\mathbf{c}}_{j}({\underline{h}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{1}},\\
|{\underline{u}}|\leq d-1\end{subarray}}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)!\cdot({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}){\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}

for some indices
w_{j{\underline{u}}}\in[0,\ell]
, where all nonzero vectors
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}
are the leading coefficients of the polynomials
{\mathbf{p}}_{1}-{\mathbf{p}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}}
, and so they belong to the family

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1d_{10}},\;{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1d_{12}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2d_{12}},\;\ldots,\;{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1d_{1\ell}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\ell d_{1\ell}}.

The claim follows from Proposition
8.5
as well as Lemma
3.7
; the latter allows us to ignore the scaling factors
(|{\underline{u}}|+1)!
.
∎

9.
Seminorms estimates in the polynomial + sublinear case

The next case that we need to deal with is when our Hardy sequences are sums of sublinear and polynomial functions.

Proposition 9.1
.

Let
d,k,\ell,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
0\leq m_{1}\leq m
be an integer,
g_{1},...,g_{m}\in\mathcal{H}
be Hardy functions satisfying the growth conditions

(i)

1\prec g_{1}(t)\prec\cdots\prec g_{m}(t)\prec t
;

(ii)

g_{i}(t)\ll\log t
iff
i\in[m_{1}]
.

For
j\in[\ell]
, let

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}_{j}(n)={\mathbf{u}}_{j}(n)+{\mathbf{p}}_{j}(n)+{\mathbf{r}}_{j}(n)\in{\mathcal{H}}^{k}

\displaystyle\textrm{for}\quad{\mathbf{u}}_{j}(n)=\sum_{i\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{ji}g_{i},\quad{\mathbf{p}}_{j}(n)=\sum_{i=0}^{d}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{ji}n^{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[n]\quad\textrm{and}\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}{\mathbf{r}}_{j}(n)\to\mathbf{0}

for some
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{ji},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{ji}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
.
Lastly, let

\displaystyle d_{jj^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\max\{i\in[d]:\;{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{ji}\neq{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j^{\prime}i}\}

\displaystyle d^{\prime}_{jj^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\max\{i\in[m]:\;{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{ji}\neq{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j^{\prime}i}\},

and assume that
d_{1j}>0
or
d^{\prime}_{1j}>m_{1}
for all
j\in[0,\ell]\setminus\{1\}
, where
\mathbf{a}_{0}={\mathbf{u}}_{0}={\mathbf{p}}_{0}=\mathbf{0}
(in other words, some coordinate of
\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{j}
grows faster than log).
Then there exist a positive integer
s=O_{d,\ell,m}(1)
and vectors

(60)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{s}\in

\displaystyle\{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1d_{1j}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{jd_{1j}}:\;j\in[0,\ell]\;\textrm{with}\;d_{1j}>0\}

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle\{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1d^{\prime}_{1j}}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{jd^{\prime}_{1j}}:\;j\in[0,\ell]\;\textrm{with}\;d_{1j}=0\;\textrm{and}\;d^{\prime}_{1j}>m_{1}\}

such that for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
and 1-bounded functions
f_{1}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{O_{d,\ell,m}(1)}\ll_{d,k,\ell,m}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{1}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{s}}^{+}.

The vectors in (
60
) are the leading coefficients of
\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{\ell}
with regards to the ordering of the generators of
\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{\ell}
as

\displaystyle g_{1}(t),\;\ldots,\;g_{m}(t),\;t,\;t^{2},\;\ldots,\;t^{d}

which matches the growth rate of these generators.

The proof of Proposition
9.1
follows a similar strategy as the proof of the analogous result in the single transformation case
[
66
, Proposition 5.1]
. That is, we pass to short intervals in such a way that the sublinear terms are constant on each short interval. We apply the arguments for polynomials from Section
7
to handle the polynomial average over
n
running over short intervals of length
L(N)
; then we use the results for sublinear functions from Section
6
to handle the average over
N
that contains the sublinear parts of the original sequences. At the end, we apply the concatenation results from Section
8
to concatenate the average of generalized box seminorms that arose at the earlier steps of the argument. This last step differs from the conclusion of the proof of
[
66
, Proposition 5.1]
in which no concatenation was required.

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle\varepsilon=\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

We allow all the quantities below to depend on
d,k,\ell,m
(however, powers of
\varepsilon
and the integers
s_{i}
will stay independent of
k
).

If all the polynomials
{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}
are constant, then the result follows from Proposition
6.3
. Suppose therefore that at least one of the polynomials is nonconstant. We assume that
{\mathbf{p}}_{1}
is nonconstant; if this is not the case and, say,
{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}
is nonconstant, then we can reduce to the previous case by composing the integral with
T^{-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}(n)\right\rfloor}}
and arguing as at the end of Proposition
7.7
.

Fix functions
f_{jN}
(with
f_{1N}=f_{1}
) that realize the limsup above. We then use Lemma
5.4
to subdivide
{\mathbb{N}}
into short intervals, obtaining

\displaystyle\limsup_{R\to\infty}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\E\limits_{N\in[R]}\left\|\E_{n\in[L(N)]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{j}(N+n)\right\rfloor}}f_{jR}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\geq\varepsilon.

We choose the length
L
of the short intervals in such a way that the sublinear parts
{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{u}}_{\ell}
are approximately constant on each short interval. To achieve this, we pick
L(N)\prec{g^{\prime}_{m}(N)}^{-1}
; with that choice, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\max_{n\in[L(N)]}|\mathbf{a}_{j}(N+n)-({\mathbf{u}}_{j}(N)+{\mathbf{p}}_{j}(N+n))|=0

for every
j\in[\ell].

By Lemma
5.3
and the pigeonhole principle, there exist
{\mathbf{r}}_{j}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
such that

\displaystyle\limsup_{R\to\infty}\E\limits_{N\in[R]}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[L(N)]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{p}}_{j}(N+n)\right\rfloor}}\mathopen{}\left(T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{j}(N)\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}_{j}}f_{jR}\mathclose{}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\gg\varepsilon.

Rearranging if necessary, we can assume that
{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell_{0}}
are identical and distinct from
{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell_{0}+1},\ldots,{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}
for some
\ell_{0}\in[\ell]
.

Applying Proposition
7.7
to the average over
n
, we obtain positive integers
s_{1},s_{2}=O(1)
(independent of
k
) such that

\displaystyle\limsup_{R\to\infty}\E\limits_{N\in[R]}\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}}\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s_{2}}}\mathopen{}\left|\int\Delta_{{\mathbf{c}}_{1}({\underline{h}}),\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}({\underline{h}});({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime})}F_{RN}\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}

for some polynomials
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[{\underline{h}}]
satisfying the conditions of Proposition
7.7
and

\displaystyle F_{RN}=\prod_{j\in[\ell_{0}]}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{j}(N)\right\rfloor}}f_{jR}.

We note also that
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}
do not depend on
N
.
Letting

\displaystyle G_{jR{\underline{h}}{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}}=\Delta_{{\mathbf{c}}_{1}({\underline{h}}),\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}({\underline{h}});({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime})}f_{jR}

for every
j\in[\ell_{0}]
,
we can rewrite the inequality above as

\displaystyle\limsup_{R\to\infty}\E\limits_{N\in[R]}\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}}\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s_{2}}}\mathopen{}\left|\int\prod_{j\in[\ell_{0}]}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{j}(N)\right\rfloor}}G_{jR{\underline{h}}{\underline{k}}{\underline{k}}^{\prime}{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}}\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

Composing the integral with
T^{-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{\ell_{0}}(N)\right\rfloor}}
, swapping the minus sign inside the integer part, handling the error terms using Lemma
5.3
, passing to the product system, and using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and properties of limsups, we get

\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}}\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s_{2}}}\limsup_{R\to\infty}\\
\left\|\E\limits_{N\in[R]}\prod_{j\in[\ell_{0}-1]}(T\times T)^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{j}(N)-{\mathbf{u}}_{\ell_{0}}(N)\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}_{j}}G_{jR{\underline{h}}{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}}\otimes\overline{G_{jR{\underline{h}}{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu\times\mu)}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}

for some
{\mathbf{r}}_{j}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
.
By assumption, for any
j\in[0,\ell_{0}]\setminus\{1\}
, we have
d^{\prime}_{1j}>m_{1}
, i.e., some coordinate of
{\mathbf{u}}_{1}-{\mathbf{u}}_{j}
grows faster than log.
Since
{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{u}}_{\ell_{0}}
are sublinear, we can apply Proposition
6.3
to find a positive integer
s_{3}=O(1)
(independent of
k
) for which

\displaystyle\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}}\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s_{2}}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|G_{1R{\underline{h}}{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1d^{\prime}_{12}}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2d^{\prime}_{12}})^{s_{3}},\ldots,({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1d^{\prime}_{1\ell_{0}}}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\ell_{0}d^{\prime}_{1\ell_{0}}})^{s_{3}}\end{subarray}}^{+}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

Recalling the definition of
G_{1R{\underline{h}}{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}}
, taking
M\to\infty
, and using the inductive formula, we get that

\displaystyle\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{1}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{c}}_{1}({\underline{h}}),\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}({\underline{h}}),({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1d^{\prime}_{12}}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2d^{\prime}_{12}})^{s_{3}},\ldots,({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1d^{\prime}_{1\ell_{0}}}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\ell_{0}d^{\prime}_{1\ell_{0}}})^{s_{3}}\end{subarray}}^{+}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

We then take the limsup as
H\to\infty
and conclude by applying Proposition
8.5
together with the previously observed fact that the coefficients of
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}}
satisfy the conditions of Proposition
7.7
.
∎

10.
Seminorm estimates for arbitrary Hardy sequences

Finally, we are ready to state and prove the general case of Theorem
1.15
.

Theorem 10.1

(Box seminorm bound)
.

Let
d,k,\ell,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
0\leq m_{1}\leq m_{2}\leq m
be integers and
{\mathcal{Q}}=\{g_{1},...,g_{m}\}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
be a collection of Hardy functions satisfying the following growth conditions:

(i)

1\prec g_{1}(t)\prec\cdots\prec g_{m}(t)\ll t^{d}
are strongly nonpolynomial;

(ii)

g_{i}(t)\ll\log t
iff
i\in[m_{1}]
;

(iii)

g_{i}(t)\prec t^{\delta}
for every
\delta>0
(i.e.,
\fracdeg g_{i}=0
) iff
i\in[m_{2}]
.

For
j\in[\ell]
, let

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}_{j}={\mathbf{u}}_{j}+{\mathbf{p}}_{j}+{\mathbf{r}}_{j}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{k}

\displaystyle\textrm{for}\quad{\mathbf{u}}_{j}=\sum_{i\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{ji}g_{i},\quad{\mathbf{p}}_{j}(t)=\sum_{i=0}^{d}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{ji}t^{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[t]\quad\textrm{and}\quad\lim_{t\to\infty}{\mathbf{r}}_{j}(t)\to\mathbf{0}

for some
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{ji},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{ji}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
.
Lastly, let

\displaystyle d_{jj^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\max\{i\in[d]:\;{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{ji}\neq{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j^{\prime}i}\}

\displaystyle d^{\prime}_{jj^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\max\{i\in[m]:\;{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{ji}\neq{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j^{\prime}i}\},

and assume that
d_{1j}>0
or
d^{\prime}_{1j}>m_{1}
for all
j\in[0,\ell]\setminus\{1\}
, where
\mathbf{a}_{0}={\mathbf{u}}_{0}={\mathbf{p}}_{0}=\mathbf{0}
(thus,
\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{j}
has a coordinate that grows faster than log).
Then there exist a positive integer
s=O_{d,\ell,{\mathcal{Q}}}(1)
and vectors

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{s}\in

\displaystyle\{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1d^{\prime}_{1j}}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{jd^{\prime}_{1j}}:\;j\in[0,\ell]\setminus\{1\},\;d^{\prime}_{1j}>m_{2}\}

(61)

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle\{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1d_{1j}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{jd_{1j}}:\;j\in[0,\ell]\setminus\{1\},\;d^{\prime}_{1j}\leq m_{2},\;d_{1j}>0\}

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle\{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1d^{\prime}_{1j}}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{jd^{\prime}_{1j}}:\;j\in[0,\ell]\setminus\{1\},\;m_{1}<d^{\prime}_{1j}\leq m_{2},\;d_{1j}=0\}

such that for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
and 1-bounded functions
f_{1}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\mathopen{}\left(\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\mathclose{}\right)^{O_{d,\ell,{\mathcal{Q}}}(1)}\\
\ll_{d,k,\ell,{\mathcal{Q}}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{1}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{s}}^{+}.

Before we prove Theorem
10.1
, we make several remarks regarding its content.
First, it is a priori far from obvious where the set of directions (
61
) comes from. We can think of
\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{\ell}
as
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
-linear combinations of
g_{1}(t),\ldots,g_{m}(t),t,t^{2},\ldots,t^{d}
, and we want to think of these generators as being ordered in an ascending way as

(62)

\displaystyle g_{1}(t),\;\ldots,\;g_{m_{2}}(t),\;t,\;t^{2},\;\ldots,\;t^{d},\;g_{m_{2}+1}(t),\;\ldots,\;g_{m}(t).

In other words, all strongly nonpolynomial functions of positive fractional degree take precedence over polynomials. This ordering naturally comes up in our argument, and it has to do with how we Taylor expand
g_{1},\ldots,g_{m}
on short intervals. Basically, the functions
g_{1},\ldots,g_{m_{2}}
will be constant on short intervals; by contrast, the functions
g_{m_{2}+1}(t),\;\ldots,\;g_{m}(t)
will be approximated by polynomials whose degree in
n
far surpasses
d
.
The collection (
61
) can then be interpreted as the set of leading coefficients of
\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{\ell}
with respect to (
62
).

Interestingly, Theorem
10.1
does not generalize Proposition
9.1
; rather, it invokes Proposition
9.1
in its proof. The reason why Proposition
9.1
is not merely a subcase of Theorem
10.1
is that the set of directions appearing in the conclusion of the former is different from (
61
), as it corresponds to the ordering

(63)

\displaystyle g_{1}(t),\;\ldots,\;g_{m}(t),\;t,\;t^{2},\;\ldots,\;t^{d},

which is arguably more natural than (
62
) in that
g_{1}(t)\prec\cdots\prec g_{m}(t)\prec t\prec\cdots\prec t^{d}
under the assumption of Proposition
9.1
. It is an interesting open question whether a version of Theorem
10.1
might hold with a set of direction vectors corresponding to the ordering (
63
) for arbitrary strongly nonpolynomial
g_{1},\ldots,g_{m}
.

Theorem
10.1
will be derived from the following more general result for averages twisted by dual sequences. We note that in Theorem
10.2
below,
\mathbf{a}_{1}
cannot be subfractional. The missing case of Theorem
10.1
in which
\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{\ell}
are all subfractional is covered by Corollary
6.4
.

Theorem 10.2

(Box seminorm bound with dual twists)
.

Let
d,k,\ell,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
J\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
,
0\leq m_{1}\leq m_{2}\leq m
be integers and
{\mathcal{Q}}=\{g_{1},...,g_{m},b_{1},\ldots,b_{J}\}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
be a collection of Hardy functions satisfying the following growth conditions:

(i)

1\prec g_{1}(t)\prec\cdots\prec g_{m}(t)\ll t^{d}
are strongly nonpolynomial;

(ii)

g_{i}(t)\ll\log t
iff
i\in[m_{1}]
;

(iii)

g_{i}(t)\prec t^{\delta}
for every
\delta>0
(i.e.
\fracdeg g_{i}=0
) iff
i\in[m_{2}]
;

(iv)

b_{j}(t)\ll t^{d}
for every
j\in[J]
.

For
j,j^{\prime}\in[\ell]
, let
\mathbf{a}_{j},{\mathbf{u}}_{j},{\mathbf{p}}_{j},{\mathbf{r}}_{j},d_{jj^{\prime}}
and
d^{\prime}_{jj^{\prime}}
be as in Theorem
10.1
. Suppose that the following two conditions hold:

(i)

for all
j\in[0,\ell]\setminus\{1\}
, we have
d_{1j}>0
or
d^{\prime}_{1j}>m_{1}
, i.e.,
\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{j}
has a coordinate that grows faster than log;

(ii)

d_{10}>0
or
d^{\prime}_{10}>m_{2}
, i.e.,
\mathbf{a}_{1}
has positive fractional degree.

Then there exist a positive integer
s=O_{d,\ell,{\mathcal{Q}}}(1)
and vectors
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{s}
in (
61
) such that for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
and 1-bounded functions
f_{1}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\mathopen{}\left(\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1,\\
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\mathclose{}\right)^{O_{d,\ell,{\mathcal{Q}}}(1)}\\
\ll_{d,k,\ell,{\mathcal{Q}}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{1}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{s}}^{+}.

We remark that in Theorems
10.1
and
10.2
, we have recorded the dependence of several parameters on
{\mathcal{Q}}
, the collection of generators
g_{1},\ldots,g_{m}
and the sequences
b_{1},\ldots,b_{J}
. Chasing through the argument, one can however establish that the parameters dependent on
{\mathcal{Q}}
really depend on
J,m
(the numbers of
g_{i}
’s and
b_{i}
’s respectively), the maximum growth of
b_{1},\ldots,b_{J}
(which is controlled by a single parameter
d
), and the collection
\fracdeg g_{1},\ldots,\fracdeg g_{m}
of the fractional degrees of the generators. Importantly, the parameters will depend on
all
fractional degrees, not just the maximum one.

Proof of Theorem
10.1
assuming Theorem
10.2
and Corollary
6.4
.

If
\mathbf{a}_{1}
has positive fractional degree, then the desired estimate in Theorem
10.1
follows immediately from Theorem
10.2
. If
\mathbf{a}_{1}
is subfractional but some
\mathbf{a}_{i}
with
i\in[2,\ell]
has positive fractional degree, then the result can be deduced from Theorem
10.2
by composing the integral with
T^{-{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{i}(n)\right\rfloor}}
in the same way in which Corollary
6.4
is deduced from Proposition
6.3
. Lastly, if all of
\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{\ell}
are subfractional, then the result follows from Corollary
6.4
(we note that the second and third case have a nonempty overlap).
∎

10.1.
Proof of Theorem
10.2

We are now ready to start the proof of Theorem
10.2
.
Let

(64)

\varepsilon=\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1,\\
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

We let all constants depend on
d,k,\ell,{\mathcal{Q}}
(and hence also on
J,m
), noting however that the positive integers
s_{i}\in{\mathbb{N}}
and powers of
\varepsilon
obtained throughout the argument will not depend on
k
.

The argument through which we control the average (
64
) by a box seminorm consists of five main steps:

(i)

approximating the sequences
\mathbf{a}_{j}
by polynomials on short intervals;

(ii)

applying finitary PET bounds for polynomials;

(iii)

massaging the resulting expression until it becomes an average along Hardy sequences that are sums of polynomial and sublinear terms;

(iv)

invoking seminorm estimates for such averages (Proposition
9.1
);

(v)

applying concatenation to an average of seminorms with polynomial parameters.

Because of the need to change the order of summations on a number of occasions, much of the argument has to be done with respect to finite averages. We also need much more delicate understanding of the Taylor expansions of nonpolynomial Hardy functions of distinct growth than in previous works on the subject.

Step 1: Passing to short intervals and Taylor expanding
g_{i}
’s.

Our first step is to split the intervals
[N]
into short intervals
[N+1,N+L(N)]
using Lemma
5.4
and approximate the functions
g_{i}
on short intervals by polynomials. The function
L\in{\mathcal{H}}
will be specified later; for now, its only important property is that
\fracdeg L\in(0,1)
.
Taylor expanding the functions
g_{i}
, we define

(65)

\displaystyle g_{iN}(n)=\sum_{l=0}^{d_{g_{i}}}\frac{g_{i}^{(l)}(N)}{l!}n^{l}

for all
n\in[L(N)]
. The degree
d_{g_{i}}
of the expansion satisfies

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}|g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}})}(N)|\cdot N^{d_{g_{i}}}=\infty\quad\textrm{and}\quad\lim_{N\to\infty}\max\limits_{n\in[L(N)]}|g_{i}(N+n)-g_{iN}(n)|=0,

and this happens whenever

(66)

\displaystyle\bigl|g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}})}(N)\bigr|^{-\frac{1}{d_{g_{i}}}}\prec L(N)\prec\bigl|g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}}+1)}(N)\bigr|^{-\frac{1}{d_{g_{i}}+1}}.

In particular, for
i\in[m_{2}]
, we have
d_{g_{i}}=0
,
g_{iN}(n)=g_{i}(N)
and

\displaystyle\max_{n\in[L(N)]}|g_{i}(N+n)-g_{i}(N)|\leq L(N)\cdot|g^{\prime}_{i}(N)|\to 0\quad\textrm{as}\quad N\to\infty.

Indeed, as
i\in[m_{2}],
we have
g_{i}(N)\prec N^{\delta}
for all
\delta>0,
so by Lemma
A.1
, we get

g^{\prime}_{i}(N)\ll N^{-1}g_{i}(N)\prec N^{\delta-1}

for all
\delta>0
; the claimed convergence to 0 then follows from the assumption that
L(N)\ll N^{1-\delta}
for some
\delta>0
.
Informally, this asserts that the functions
g_{1},\ldots,g_{m_{2}}
are essentially constant on the interval
(N,N+L(N)]
.

Thus, we have

(67)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\max\limits_{n\in[L(N)]}|\mathbf{a}_{j}(N+n)-\mathbf{a}_{jN}(n)|=0

for

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}_{jN}(n)=\sum_{i\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{ji}g_{iN}(n)+{\mathbf{p}}_{j}(N+n)\quad\textrm{and}\quad n\in[L(N)].

The following result on simultaneous Taylor approximations of
g_{1},\ldots,g_{m}
is of utmost importance for us, as it tells us how to choose the function
L
, and it gives useful properties of the degrees
d_{g_{i}}
of
g_{iN}
.

Proposition 10.3

(Common Taylor expansion)
.

Let
q\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Then there exist positive functions
H,L\in{\mathcal{H}}
with fractional degrees in
(0,1)
such that the degrees
d_{g_{i}}
of the Taylor approximants (
65
) satisfy the following conditions:

(i)

if
i>m_{2}
(i.e.
\fracdeg g_{i}>0
), then
d_{g_{i}}\geq q
, otherwise
d_{g_{i}}=0
;

(ii)

for all
i,j\in[m]
, we have
d_{g_{i}}=d_{g_{j}}
iff
\fracdeg g_{i}=\fracdeg g_{j}
;

(iii)

for all
i,j\in[m_{2}+1,m]
, we have
18
18

18

We recall that
a\lll b
iff
\fracdeg a<\fracdeg b
iff there exists
\delta>0
such that
a(N)N^{\delta}\ll b(N)
.

(68)

\bigl|g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}})}(N)\bigr|^{-\frac{1}{d_{g_{i}}}}\lll H(N)\lll L(N)\lll\bigl|g_{j}^{(d_{g_{j}}+1)}(N)\bigr|^{-\frac{1}{d_{g_{j}}+1}};

(iv)

for all
i\in[m_{2}+1,m]
, we have

\displaystyle\fracdeg\bigl|g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}})}(N)\bigr|^{-\frac{1}{d_{g_{i}}}}\leq\fracdeg\bigl|g_{m}^{(d_{g_{m}})}(N)\bigr|^{-\frac{1}{d_{g_{m}}}},

with equality if and only if
\fracdeg g_{i}=\fracdeg g_{m}
.

(v)

for all distinct
i,j\in[m]
, we have

g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}})}(N)H(N)^{d_{g_{i}}}\not\asymp g_{j}^{(d_{g_{j}})}(N)H(N)^{d_{g_{j}}};

in other words, these two functions have distinct growth rates.

Moreover, the function
H
takes the form

\displaystyle H(N)=\begin{cases}|g_{m}^{(d_{g_{m}})}(N)|^{-1/d_{g_{m}}}N^{\eta},\quad&\textrm{if}\quad d_{g_{m}}>0\\
N^{\eta},\quad&\textrm{if}\quad d_{g_{m}}=0\end{cases},

where
\eta
can be any number from an interval
(0,\eta_{0})
for some
\eta_{0}>0
depending only on the numbers
\fracdeg g_{i},d_{g_{i}}
, and
L
is any function satisfying
L\succ H
and (
68
).

When applying Proposition
10.3
, we will choose

(69)

\displaystyle q=2d\geq 2\max(\{\deg{\mathbf{p}}_{j}:\;j\in[\ell]\}\cup\{\fracdeg g_{i}:\;i\in[m]\}),

remarking that any higher
q
would do as well.

The first condition ensures that after passing to short intervals, those
g_{i}
that are not sublinear can be expanded as polynomials of arbitrarily high degree (in particular, the degrees of their Taylor expansions are higher than the degrees of the polynomials
{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\ldots{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}
). The second condition guarantees that strongly nonpolynomial functions of distinct fractional degrees can be expanded as polynomials of distinct degrees. The inequalities (
68
) provide the common Taylor expansion (as they imply (
66
)). They are also instrumental in showing that after the change of variables, the lengths of the intervals will go to
\infty
(as
H\prec L
), and that the new coefficients obtained after changes of variables will grow faster than log. The property (
iv
) will be used to show later on that these new coefficients are sublinear, and in fact, their fractional degrees are bounded away from 1.
The last condition will ensure that these new coefficients have distinct growth rates.
We will use this proposition as a black box and postpone its proof until Appendix
B
.

Splitting
{\mathbb{N}}
into short intervals using Lemma
5.4
, approximating
\mathbf{a}_{j}(N+n)
by
\mathbf{a}_{jN}
for each
j\in[\ell]
as in (
67
) and handling the error terms using Lemma
5.3
, we obtain

\limsup_{R\to\infty}\;\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1,\\
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\;\E\limits_{N\in[R]}\;\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\\
\left\|\E_{n\in[L(N)]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T^{{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{jN}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(N+n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\gg\varepsilon.

It is at this point that we use the assumption that
d_{10}>0
or
d^{\prime}_{10}>m_{2}
, i.e.,
\mathbf{a}_{1}
has positive fractional degree: this assumption is required for the polynomial
\mathbf{a}_{1N}
to be nonconstant (which is necessary for otherwise
f_{1}
would vanish from the average above). If
d_{10}>0
, then the polynomial part of
\mathbf{a}_{1N}
is nonconstant whereas if
d^{\prime}_{10}>m_{2}
, then the nonpolynomial part of
\mathbf{a}_{1N}
is noncostant by Proposition
10.3
(
i
).

Rearranging
\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{\ell}
if necessary, we may assume that there exists
\ell_{0}\in[\ell]
such that
j\in[\ell_{0}]
if and only if
\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{j}
is subfractional, so that

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}_{j}=\mathbf{a}_{1}+\sum_{i\in[m_{2}]}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{ji}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1i})g_{i}\quad\textrm{for}\quad j\in[\ell_{0}],

and similarly for
\mathbf{a}_{jN}
. Then

\limsup_{R\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{\ell_{0}+1}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1,\\
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\E\limits_{N\in[R]}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\\
\left\|\E_{n\in[L(N)]}c_{n}\cdot T^{{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{1N}(n)\right\rfloor}}F_{RN}\cdot\prod_{j=\ell_{0}+1}^{\ell}T^{{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{jN}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(N+n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\gg\varepsilon

for

(70)

\displaystyle F_{RN}=\prod_{j\in[\ell_{0}]}T^{{\left\lfloor\sum_{i\in[m_{2}]}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{ji}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1i})g_{i}(N)\right\rfloor}}f_{jR},

where
f_{1R}=f_{1}
and
f_{jR}
for
j\geq 2
are some 1-bounded functions that bring the
L^{2}(\mu)
norms above close to the supremum, and the error terms are absorbed by the sup over
c_{n}
.

We set

\displaystyle d^{\prime\prime}_{jj^{\prime}}:=\deg\mathopen{}\left(\sum_{i\in[m]}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{ji}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j^{\prime}i})g_{iN}\mathclose{}\right)

to be the degree of the strongly nonpolynomial part of
\mathbf{a}_{jN}-\mathbf{a}_{j^{\prime}N}
. We note from Proposition
10.3
(
i
) that

(71)

\displaystyle d^{\prime\prime}_{jj^{\prime}}=0\iff d^{\prime}_{jj^{\prime}}\leq m_{2},

and we also observe from the definition of
d^{\prime\prime}_{jj^{\prime}}
above that
d^{\prime\prime}_{jj^{\prime}}=d_{g_{i}}
for
i=d^{\prime}_{jj^{\prime}}
. We also subdivide
[m]={\mathcal{B}}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup{\mathcal{B}}_{m_{3}}
into classes of indices

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{j}=\{i\in[m]:\;d_{g_{i}}=j\}

corresponding to the sublinear functions whose Taylor expansions have the same degree.
By Proposition
10.3
(
ii
), all
g_{i}
belonging to a fixed class
{\mathcal{B}}_{j}
have the same fractional degree. Moreover, if
i,i^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{j}
for the same
j
and
i<i^{\prime}
, then
g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}})}\prec g_{i^{\prime}}^{(d_{g_{i^{\prime}}})}
; consequently, the functions
\{g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}})}:\;i\in{\mathcal{B}}_{j}\}
are linearly independent. Because of this and the assumption (
69
), the leading coefficient of
\mathbf{a}_{jN}-\mathbf{a}_{j^{\prime}N}
is

(72)

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{B}}_{d^{\prime\prime}_{jj^{\prime}}}}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{ji}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j^{\prime}i})\frac{g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}})}(N)}{d_{g_{i}}!}=\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{B}}_{d^{\prime\prime}_{jj^{\prime}}}}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{ji}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j^{\prime}i})\frac{g_{i}^{(d^{\prime\prime}_{jj^{\prime}})}(N)}{d^{\prime\prime}_{jj^{\prime}}!}

if
d^{\prime\prime}_{jj^{\prime}}>0
(or equivalently if
d^{\prime}_{jj^{\prime}}>m_{2}
) and

(73)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{jd_{jj^{\prime}}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j^{\prime}d_{jj^{\prime}}}

otherwise.

Before we apply our PET results, we want to change variables in order to ensure that the leading coefficients of the polynomials
g_{iN}
grow faster than 1. Substituting
n\mapsto{\left\lfloor H(N)\right\rfloor}n+r
and setting
\tilde{L}(N)=L(N)/H(N)
(which satisfies
\tilde{L}\succ 1
by Proposition
10.3
), we have

(74)

\limsup_{R\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f_{\ell_{0}+1}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1,\\
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\E\limits_{N\in[R]}\E_{r\in[H(N)]}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left|\!\left|\E_{n\in[\tilde{L}(N)]}c_{n}\cdot T^{{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{1N}({\left\lfloor H(N)\right\rfloor}n+r)\right\rfloor}}F_{RN}\right.\right.\\
\left.\left.\prod_{j=\ell_{0}+1}^{\ell}T^{{\left\lfloor\mathbf{a}_{jN}({\left\lfloor H(N)\right\rfloor}n+r)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(N+{\left\lfloor H(N)\right\rfloor}n+r)\right\rfloor})\right|\!\right|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\gg\varepsilon.

Step 2: PET in
n
on short intervals.

We move on now to apply our PET bounds from Section
7
.
Since
\mathbf{a}_{1N},\mathbf{a}_{(\ell_{0}+1)N},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{\ell N}
are polynomials in
n
, and
\mathbf{a}_{1N}
is nonconstant, (
74
) is amenable to Proposition
7.10
. Indeed,
by Proposition
7.10
, there exist positive integers
s_{1},s_{2}=O(1)
(independent of
k
) and nonzero multilinear polynomials
{\mathbf{c}}_{1N},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}N}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[{\underline{h}}]
satisfying

(75)

\limsup_{R\to\infty}\E\limits_{N\in[R]}\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}}\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s_{2}}}\\
\mathopen{}\left|\int\Delta_{{\mathbf{c}}_{1N}({\underline{h}}),\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}N}({\underline{h}});({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime})}F_{RN}\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|+\frac{1}{H}+\frac{1}{M}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

The coefficients of the polynomials
{\mathbf{c}}_{1N},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{s_{2}N}
come from the leading coefficients of the polynomials

(76)

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}_{1N}(N+{\left\lfloor H(N)\right\rfloor}n+r)-\mathbf{a}_{jN}(N+{\left\lfloor H(N)\right\rfloor}n+r)

for
j\in\{0\}\cup[\ell_{0}+1,\ldots,\ell]
.
An analysis of the leading coefficients of (
76
) shows that we can split
{\mathbf{c}}_{lN}={\mathbf{c}}^{\pol}_{lN}+{\mathbf{c}}^{\nonpol}_{lN}
into its “polynomial” and “nonpolynomial” parts as

(77)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{c}}^{\pol}_{lN}({\underline{h}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\underline{u}}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{l}}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)!({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{l{\underline{u}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}){\left\lfloor H(N)\right\rfloor}^{|{\underline{u}}|+1}{\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}},

(78)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{c}}^{\nonpol}_{lN}({\underline{h}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\underline{u}}\notin{\mathcal{A}}_{l}}\mathopen{}\left(\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{B}}_{|{\underline{u}}|+1}}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1i}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{w^{\prime}_{l{\underline{u}}}i})g_{i}^{(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}(N)\mathclose{}\right){\left\lfloor H(N)\right\rfloor}^{|{\underline{u}}|+1}{\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}},

for some set of multiindices
{\mathcal{A}}_{l}\subseteq\{0,1\}^{s_{1}}
and indices
w_{l{\underline{u}}},w^{\prime}_{l{\underline{u}}}\in\{0\}\cup\{\ell_{0}+1,\ldots,\ell\}
satisfying

(79)
