\displaystyle d_{1w_{l{\underline{u}}}}=\deg(\mathbf{a}_{1N}-\mathbf{a}_{w_{l{\underline{u}}}N})=\deg({\mathbf{p}}_{1}-{\mathbf{p}}_{w_{l{\underline{u}}}})=|{\underline{u}}|+1\quad\textrm{for}\quad{\underline{u}}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{l},

and

(80)

\displaystyle d^{\prime\prime}_{1w^{\prime}_{l{\underline{u}}}}=\deg(\mathbf{a}_{1N}-\mathbf{a}_{w^{\prime}_{l{\underline{u}}}N})=d_{g_{i}}=|{\underline{u}}|+1

for
{\underline{u}}\notin{\mathcal{A}}_{l}
and
i\in{\mathcal{B}}_{|{\underline{u}}|+1}
. By comparing the degrees of
g_{iN}
and
{\mathbf{p}}_{j}
, we make the following important observation.

(81)

\displaystyle{\underline{u}}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{l}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{w_{l{\underline{u}}}}\quad\textrm{has\; no\; contribution\; from}\quad g_{i}\quad\textrm{for}\quad i\in[m_{2}+1,m].

Step 3: Removing
{\left\lfloor H(N)\right\rfloor}
.

The formulas (
77
) and (
78
) for
{\mathbf{c}}_{lN}
somewhat inconveniently contain the expressions
{\left\lfloor H(N)\right\rfloor}
, and our next goal is to replace all their instances by
N
using the lemma below.

Lemma 10.4

(
[
24
, Lemma 5.1]
)
.

Let
(A_{R}(N))_{N,R\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be a 1-bounded, two-parameter sequence of vectors in a normed space and let
H\in\mathcal{H}
satisfy the growth condition
t^{\delta}\prec H(t)\prec t
for some
\delta>0
. Then, we have

\limsup\limits_{R\to\infty}\left\|\E\limits_{N\in[R]}A_{R}({\left\lfloor H(N)\right\rfloor})\right\|\ll_{H}\limsup\limits_{R\to\infty}\left\|\E\limits_{N\in[R]}A_{R}(N)\right\|.

In order to apply Lemma
10.4
, we need to rephrase the integral in (
75
) so that it only depends on
{\left\lfloor H(N)\right\rfloor}
, not
N
. This requires us to deal with the term
g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}})}(N)
appearing in the definition of
{\mathbf{c}}^{\nonpol}_{lN}
(for
i\in[m_{2}+1,m]
) and in the formula (
70
) for
F_{RN}
(for
i\in[m_{2}]
).
To this end,
we set

\displaystyle\chi_{g_{i}}(t)=g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}})}\circ H^{-1}(t)\cdot t^{d_{g_{i}}}\quad\textrm{so that}\quad\chi_{g_{i}}(H(N))=g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}})}(N)H(N)^{d_{g_{i}}}

are the expressions appearing in the formula for strongly nonpolynomial functions. In particular, Proposition
10.3
gives the following corollary.

Corollary 10.5

(Properties of the new coefficients)
.

The functions
\chi_{g_{i}}
enjoy the following properties:

(i)

if
i\in[m_{2}]
, then
\chi_{g_{i}}=g_{i}\circ H^{-1}
; in particular,
\fracdeg\chi_{g_{i}}=0
;

(ii)

for
i\in[m_{2}+1,m]
, we have
\fracdeg\chi_{g_{i}}\in(0,1)
; in particular, they are sublinear and have positive fractional degrees;

(iii)

if
i,j\in[m]
are distinct, then
\chi_{g_{i}}\not\asymp\chi_{g_{j}}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
10.3
(
i
),
d_{g_{i}}=0
whenever
i\in[m_{2}]
, and so
\chi_{g_{i}}=g_{i}\circ H^{-1}
by definition. Then
\fracdeg\chi_{g_{i}}=\fracdeg g_{i}/\fracdeg{H}=0
by Lemma
A.4
(
ii
), and the first property follows. The last property follows immediately from Proposition
10.3
(
v
). The second property is vacuously true whenever
m_{2}=m
, i.e., all the
g_{i}
’s are subfractional. Otherwise we have
H(N)=|g_{m}^{(d_{g_{m}})}(N)|^{-1/d_{g_{m}}}N^{\eta}
for some
\eta>0
, and hence by Lemma
A.4
, we have

\displaystyle\fracdeg\chi_{g_{i}}=\frac{\fracdeg g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}})}}{\fracdeg H}+d_{g_{i}}=\frac{c_{i}-d_{g_{i}}}{1-\frac{c_{m}}{d_{g_{m}}}+\eta}+d_{g_{i}}=\frac{c_{i}d_{g_{m}}-d_{g_{i}}c_{m}+\eta d_{g_{i}}d_{g_{m}}}{d_{g_{m}}-c_{m}+\eta d_{g_{m}}}

where
c_{i}=\fracdeg g_{i}>0
. Because of Proposition
10.3
(
i
) and our choice of
q
, we have

(82)

\displaystyle d_{g_{m}}\geq q\geq 2d\geq 2c_{m}

(recall that
d
upper bounds the fractional degrees of
g_{i}
’s while
q
lower bounds the degrees of Taylor approximations), and hence the denominator in the formula for
\fracdeg\chi_{g_{i}}
above is positive.
The positivity of the denominator in turn implies that
\fracdeg\chi_{g_{i}}>0
iff

(83)

\displaystyle\frac{c_{i}}{d_{g_{i}}}-\frac{c_{m}}{d_{g_{m}}}+\eta>0,

which in turn follows from the positivity of
\eta
and Proposition
10.3
(
iv
). Thus,
\fracdeg\chi_{g_{i}}>0
. To prove that
\fracdeg\chi_{g_{i}}<1
, it suffices to show in the light of the formula for
\fracdeg\chi_{g_{i}}
that

\displaystyle c_{i}d_{g_{m}}-d_{g_{i}}c_{m}+\eta d_{g_{i}}d_{g_{m}}<d_{g_{m}}-c_{m}+\eta d_{g_{m}}.

This in turn will follow from

(84)

\displaystyle(c_{i}-1)d_{g_{m}}<c_{m}(d_{g_{i}}-1)

and taking sufficiently small
\eta>0
. In order to show (
84
), we first use the bound

\displaystyle\bigl|g_{m}^{(d_{g_{m}})}(N)\bigr|^{-\frac{1}{d_{g_{m}}}}\lll\bigl|g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}}+1)}(N)\bigr|^{-\frac{1}{d_{g_{i}}+1}}

from (
68
), which can be restated as
c_{i}d_{g_{m}}<c_{m}(d_{g_{i}}+1)
. It then follows that

\displaystyle(c_{i}-1)d_{g_{m}}<c_{m}(d_{g_{i}}+1)-d_{g_{m}}=c_{m}(d_{g_{i}}-1)+2c_{m}-d_{g_{m}}\leq c_{m}(d_{g_{i}}-1)

as long as
d_{g_{m}}\geq 2c_{m}
, which holds by (
82
).
∎

The next lemma is crucial in rephrasing the integral in (
75
) in terms of
{\left\lfloor H(N)\right\rfloor}
.

Lemma 10.6
.

For every
i\in[m]
, we have

\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\bigl|g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}})}\circ H^{-1}(t)\cdot{\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor}^{d_{g_{i}}}-g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}})}\circ H^{-1}({\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor})\cdot{\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor}^{d_{g_{i}}}\bigr|=0.

Proof.

By the mean value theorem, Lemma
A.1
, and the fact that
\mathopen{}\left(g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}})}\circ H^{-1}\mathclose{}\right)^{\prime}
is decreasing, we have

\displaystyle\bigl|g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}})}\circ H^{-1}(t)-g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}})}\circ H^{-1}({\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor})\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\{t\}\cdot\mathopen{}\left(g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}})}\circ H^{-1}\mathclose{}\right)^{\prime}({\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor})

\displaystyle\ll\frac{g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}})}\circ H^{-1}({\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor})}{{\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor}}.

Replacing
{\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor}
by
t
for simplicity, it therefore suffices to show that

g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}})}\circ H^{-1}(t)\cdot t^{d_{g_{i}}}\prec t.

But the left hand side is just
\chi_{g_{i}}(t)
, and so the property follows immediately from Corollary
10.5
(ii), and specifically from the sublinearity of
\chi
.
∎

Recall the formula for
{\mathbf{c}}^{\nonpol}_{lN}
from (
78
). By Lemma
10.6
applied with
t={H(N)}
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathopen{}\left|{\mathbf{c}}^{\nonpol}_{lN}({\underline{h}})-\sum_{{\underline{u}}\notin{\mathcal{A}}_{l}}\mathopen{}\left(\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{B}}_{|{\underline{u}}|+1}}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1i}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{w^{\prime}_{l{\underline{u}}}i})\chi_{g_{i}}({\left\lfloor H(N)\right\rfloor})\mathclose{}\right){\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}\mathclose{}\right|=0

for every
l
and
{\underline{h}}
, and so we can replace
{\mathbf{c}}^{\nonpol}_{lN}({\underline{h}})
in (
75
) with the second expression above. We also set

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{RN}=\prod_{j\in[\ell_{0}]}T^{{\left\lfloor\sum_{i\in[m_{2}]}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{ji}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1i})g_{i}\circ H^{-1}(N)\right\rfloor}}f_{jR}=\prod_{j\in[\ell_{0}]}T^{{\left\lfloor\sum_{i\in[m_{2}]}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{ji}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1i})\chi_{g_{i}}(N)\right\rfloor}}f_{jR},

so that
\tilde{F}_{RH(N)}=F_{RN}
. Lemma
10.6
applied with
t={H(N)}
similarly gives

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathopen{}\left|\sum_{i\in[m_{2}]}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{ji}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1i})g_{i}(N)-\sum_{i\in[m_{2}]}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{ji}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1i})g_{i}\circ H^{-1}({\left\lfloor H(N)\right\rfloor})\mathclose{}\right|=0,

which allows us to replace
F_{RN}
with
\tilde{F}_{R{\left\lfloor H(N)\right\rfloor}}
. After all this massaging, (
75
) becomes

(85)

\limsup_{R\to\infty}\E\limits_{N\in[R]}\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}}\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s_{2}}}\\
\mathopen{}\left|\int\Delta_{\tilde{{\mathbf{c}}}_{1{\left\lfloor H(N)\right\rfloor}}({\underline{h}}),\ldots,\tilde{{\mathbf{c}}}_{s_{2}{\left\lfloor H(N)\right\rfloor}}({\underline{h}});({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime})}\tilde{F}_{R{\left\lfloor H(N)\right\rfloor}}\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|+\frac{1}{H}+\frac{1}{M}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}

for
\tilde{{\mathbf{c}}}_{lN}=\tilde{{\mathbf{c}}}^{\pol}_{lN}+\tilde{{\mathbf{c}}}^{\nonpol}_{lN}
given by

\displaystyle\tilde{{\mathbf{c}}}^{\pol}_{lN}({\underline{h}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\underline{u}}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{l}}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)!({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{l{\underline{u}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)})N^{|{\underline{u}}|+1}{\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}

\displaystyle\tilde{{\mathbf{c}}}^{\nonpol}_{lN}({\underline{h}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\underline{u}}\notin{\mathcal{A}}_{l}}\mathopen{}\left(\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{B}}_{|{\underline{u}}|+1}}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1i}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{w^{\prime}_{l{\underline{u}}}i})\chi_{g_{i}}(N)\mathclose{}\right){\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}.

The new polynomials
\tilde{{\mathbf{c}}}_{lN}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}[{\underline{h}}]
are also nonzero and multilinear.

The integral in (
85
) is now phrased purely in terms of
{\left\lfloor H(N)\right\rfloor}
rather than
N
. By Lemma
10.4
, we get
19
19

19

In reality, to apply Lemma
10.4
, we need the average over
N
to be inside the integral. This minor technicality can be dealt with in an entirely standard way: we apply the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to (
85
), pass to the product system, incorporate the average over
N
inside the integral, apply the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, and only then apply Lemma
10.4
. The desired conclusion will follow once we pass back to the original system.

(86)

\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}}\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s_{2}}}\limsup_{R\to\infty}\E\limits_{N\in[R]}\mathopen{}\left|\int\Delta_{\tilde{{\mathbf{c}}}_{1N}({\underline{h}}),\ldots,\tilde{{\mathbf{c}}}_{s_{2}N}({\underline{h}});({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime})}\tilde{F}_{RN}\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|+\frac{1}{H}+\frac{1}{M}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

Step 4: Applying seminorm estimates for the polynomial + sublinear case.

Our next goal is to analyze the average over
N
. The key observation, already made in
[
66
]
, is that the iterates in (
86
) are sums of polynomial and sublinear terms when reinterpreted as sequences in
N
; hence (
86
) is amenable to Proposition
9.1
.
In order to construe the polynomials
\tilde{{\mathbf{c}}}_{lN}
as functions of
N
rather than
{\underline{h}}
, we define

\displaystyle{\mathbf{b}}_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}}(N)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[m_{2}]}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{ji}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1i})\chi_{g_{i}}(N)+\sum_{l\in[s_{2}]}m_{l}^{\epsilon_{l}}\tilde{{\mathbf{c}}}_{lN}({\underline{h}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in[s_{2}]}m_{l}^{\epsilon_{l}}\tilde{{\mathbf{c}}}^{\pol}_{lN}({\underline{h}})+\sum_{i\in[m_{2}]}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{ji}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1i})\chi_{g_{i}}(N)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i\in[m_{2}+1,m]}\Bigl(\sum_{l\in[s_{2}]}\;\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\notin{\mathcal{A}}_{l}:\\
|{\underline{u}}|+1=d_{g_{i}}\end{subarray}}\;({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1i}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{w^{\prime}_{l{\underline{u}}}i})m_{l}^{\epsilon_{l}}{\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}\Bigr)\chi_{g_{i}}(N),

recalling the convention (
14
),
so that

\displaystyle\Delta_{\tilde{{\mathbf{c}}}_{1N}({\underline{h}}),\ldots,\tilde{{\mathbf{c}}}_{s_{2}N}({\underline{h}});({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime})}\tilde{F}_{RN}=\prod_{j\in[\ell_{0}]}\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{2}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{b}}_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}}(N)\right\rfloor}+{\mathbf{r}}_{j\varepsilon N{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}}}f_{jR}

for some
O(1)
error terms
{\mathbf{r}}_{j\varepsilon N{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}

that come from comparing a sum of integer parts to the integer part of the sum.
Combining this identity with (
86
) and removing the error terms using Lemma
5.3
, we deduce that

\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}}\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s_{2}}}\sup_{\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell_{0}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1}\\
\limsup_{R\to\infty}\E\limits_{N\in[R]}\sup_{|c_{N}|\leq 1}\mathopen{}\left|\int c_{N}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell_{0}]}\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{2}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{b}}_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}}(N)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|+\frac{1}{H}+\frac{1}{M}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

Passing to the product system and applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we get

\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}}\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s_{2}}}\sup_{\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{\infty},\ldots,\left\|f_{\ell_{0}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1}\\
\limsup_{R\to\infty}\left\|\E\limits_{N\in[R]}\prod_{j\in[\ell_{0}]}\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{2}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}(T\times T)^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{b}}_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}}(N)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\otimes\overline{f_{j}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu\times\mu)}+\frac{1}{H}+\frac{1}{M}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

In order to apply Proposition
9.1
, we need to understand the differences
{\mathbf{b}}_{1\underline{1}{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}}-{\mathbf{b}}_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}}
for
(j,{\underline{\epsilon}})\in([0,\ell_{0}]\times\{0,1\}^{s_{2}})\setminus(1,\underline{1})
which take the form

{\mathbf{b}}_{1\underline{1}{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}}(N)-{\mathbf{b}}_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}}(N)=\sum_{l\in[s_{2}]}(m_{l}^{\prime}-m_{l}^{\epsilon_{l}})\tilde{{\mathbf{c}}}^{\pol}_{lN}({\underline{h}})+\sum_{i\in[m_{2}]}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1i}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{ji})\chi_{g_{i}}(N)\\
+\sum_{i\in[m_{2}+1,m]}\Bigl(\sum_{l\in[s_{2}]}\;\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\notin{\mathcal{A}}_{l}:\\
|{\underline{u}}|+1=d_{g_{i}}\end{subarray}}\;({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1i}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{w^{\prime}_{l{\underline{u}}}i})(m_{l}^{\prime}-m_{l}^{\epsilon_{l}}){\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}\Bigr)\chi_{g_{i}}(N);

specifically, we need to show the following.

Lemma 10.7
.

For each
(j,{\underline{\epsilon}})\in([0,\ell_{0}]\times\{0,1\}^{s})\setminus(1,\underline{1})
, the difference function
{\mathbf{b}}_{1\underline{1}{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}}-{\mathbf{b}}_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}}:(t_{0},\infty)\to{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
has a coordinate function growing faster than
\log
for all but
O(M^{2s_{1}-1}H^{s_{1}-1})
elements

({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{h}})\in[\pm M]^{s_{2}}\times[\pm M]^{s_{2}}\times[\pm H]^{s_{1}}.

Proof.

First, we observe that the term
\sum_{l\in[s_{2}]}m_{l}^{\epsilon_{l}}\tilde{{\mathbf{c}}}^{\pol}_{lN}({\underline{h}})
is a polynomial in
N
while the other terms in
{\mathbf{b}}_{1\underline{1}{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}}-{\mathbf{b}}_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}}
are sublinear in
N
due to Corollary
10.5
(
ii
). Moreover, thanks to Corollary
10.5
(
iii
), for all distinct
i,i^{\prime}\in[m]
, the functions
\chi_{g_{i}},\chi_{g_{i^{\prime}}}
have distinct growth rates.

If
j\neq 1
, then
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1i}\neq{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{ji}
for some
i\in[m_{1}+1,m_{2}]
due to the assumption that
\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{j}
differs by a subfractional term faster than log, and since the coefficients
\chi_{g_{i}}
all have distinct growth and grow sublinearly (so more slowly than the polynomial
\tilde{{\mathbf{c}}}^{\pol}_{lN}({\underline{h}})
for generic
{\underline{h}}
), it follows that
{\mathbf{b}}_{1\underline{1}{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}}-{\mathbf{b}}_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}}
has a coordinate growing faster than log. If
{\underline{\epsilon}}=\underline{1}
, then necessarily
j\neq 1
, and so we land in the previous case.

If
j=1
,
{\underline{\epsilon}}\neq\underline{1}
, then
pick
l\in[s_{2}]
for which
\epsilon_{l}=0
. Then
m_{l}\neq m_{l}^{\prime}
for all but
O(M)
choices
(m_{l},m^{\prime}_{l})\in[\pm M]^{2}
.
Since
{\mathbf{c}}_{lN}
is nonzero, one of the two things has to happen:

•

{{\mathbf{c}}}^{\pol}_{lN}
is nonzero, in which case
\tilde{{\mathbf{c}}}^{\pol}_{lN}({\underline{h}})
is a nonconstant polynomial in
N
for all but
O(H^{s_{1}-1})
values
{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}
;

•

or
{{\mathbf{c}}}^{\nonpol}_{lN}
is nonzero, so that
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1i}\neq{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{w^{\prime}_{l{\underline{u}}}i}
for some
i\in[m_{2}+1,m]
. Then
\tilde{{\mathbf{c}}}^{\nonpol}_{lN}({\underline{h}})
has a coordinate growing faster than
\log
for all but
O(H^{s_{1}-1})
values
{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}
because the functions
\chi_{g_{i}}
have distinct growth rates and satisfy
\chi_{g_{i}}\succ\log
by Corollary
10.5
(
ii
).

∎

By Lemma
10.7
, the function
{\mathbf{b}}_{1\underline{1}{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}}-{\mathbf{b}}_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}}
is nonconstant for a generic choice of
({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{h}})
. Therefore, it follows from Proposition
9.1
that for all
({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{h}})
but the exceptional set from Lemma
10.7
, we have that
20
20

20

This is the point of the argument where we need the bounds in Proposition
9.1
to be quantitative, or at least uniform in
({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{h}})
, since otherwise we cannot guarantee the positivity of the average in
({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{h}})
as we take
M,H
to infinity.

(87)

\displaystyle\E\limits_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{s_{1}}}\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s_{2}}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{1}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{h}}),\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{s_{3}}({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{h}})}^{+}+\frac{1}{H}+\frac{1}{M}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}

for a positive integer
s_{3}=O(1)
and nonzero vectors
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{h}}),\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{s_{3}}({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{h}})
belonging to the set

\displaystyle\Bigl\{D!\cdot\sum_{l\in[s_{2}]}(m_{l}^{\prime}-m_{l}^{\epsilon_{l}})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{l}:\\
|{\underline{u}}|+1=D\end{subarray}}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1D}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{l{\underline{u}}}D}){\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}:\;D\in[d],\;{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{2}}\setminus\{\underline{1}\}\Bigr\}

\displaystyle\cup\;\Bigl\{\sum_{l\in[s_{2}]}\;\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\notin{\mathcal{A}}_{l}:\\
|{\underline{u}}|+1=d_{g_{i}}\end{subarray}}\;({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1i}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{w^{\prime}_{l{\underline{u}}}i})(m_{l}^{\prime}-m_{l}^{\epsilon_{l}}){\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}:\;i\in[m_{2}+1,m],\;{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s_{2}}\setminus\{\underline{1}\}\Bigr\}

\displaystyle\cup\;\{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1i}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{ji}:\;i\in[m_{1}+1,m_{2}],\;j\in[0,\ell],\;d_{1j}=0,\;d^{\prime}_{1j}=i\}.

The hierarchy of the three sets above is then as follows. If
{\mathbf{b}}_{1\underline{1}{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}}(N)-{\mathbf{b}}_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}}(N)
contains a nonzero element from the first set, then the vectors
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{h}})
that it contributes will come from the first set. Otherwise, if it contains a nonzero element from the second set, then it will contribute vectors from the second set. If the contributions of the first two sets to
{\mathbf{b}}_{1\underline{1}{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}}(N)-{\mathbf{b}}_{j{\underline{\epsilon}}{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{h}}}(N)
are trivial, then only it contributes vectors from the third set.

It is important to emphasize that nowhere above can one find vectors
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1i}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{ji}
with
i\in[m_{1}]
, i.e., those corresponding to
g_{i}\ll\log
; this is a direct consequence of our assumption that the differences
\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{i}
for
i\in[0,\ell]\setminus\{j\}
have a coordinate growing faster than
\log
.

Step 5: A final concatenation argument.

Recalling (
79
) and (
80
), we deduce that the coefficients of
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{s_{3}}
interpreted as polynomials in
{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{h}}
belong to the set of nonzero vectors

\displaystyle\{d_{1j}!\cdot({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1d_{1j}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{jd_{1j}}):\;j\in[0,\ell]\setminus\{1\},\;d^{\prime}_{1j}\leq m_{2},\;d_{1j}>0\}

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle\;\{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1d^{\prime}_{1j}}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{jd^{\prime}_{1j}}:\;j\in[0,\ell]\setminus\{1\},\;m_{1}<d^{\prime}_{1j}\leq m_{2},\;d_{1j}=0\}

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle\;\{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1d^{\prime}_{1j}}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{jd^{\prime}_{1j}}:\;j\in[0,\ell]\setminus\{1\},\;d^{\prime}_{1j}>m_{2}\},

which is precisely the set (
61
) with the first two components swapped. The first subset of the union above comes from the leading coefficients of
{\mathbf{p}}_{1}-{\mathbf{p}}_{j}
for those
j
for which
\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{j}
is a sum of a polynomial and a sublinear term; that these are the only coefficients that arise follows from the observation (
81
). The second subset corresponds to the case when
\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{j}
has a nonpolynomial term of positive fractional degree, in which case
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1d^{\prime}_{1j}}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{jd^{\prime}_{1j}}
is the leading coefficient of the nonpolynomial part of
\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{j}
. Lastly, the third subset consists of the leading coefficients of
\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{j}
when
\mathbf{a}_{1}-\mathbf{a}_{j}
is sublinear. Taking the limsup as
M\to\infty
in (
87
) followed by limsup as
H\to\infty
, and applying Proposition
8.5
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{1}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{s_{4}}}^{+}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}

for some
s_{4}=O(1)
and nonzero
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{s_{4}}
in (
61
). Using Lemma
3.7
, we can replace
d_{1j}!\cdot({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1d_{1j}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{jd_{1j}})
with simply
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1d_{1j}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{jd_{1j}}
. Theorem
10.2
follows by taking
s=s_{4}
.

11.
Upgrading to Host-Kra seminorm estimates

The next objective is to upgrade Theorem
10.2
to Theorem
1.16
, i.e. replace generalized box seminorm estimates by proper Host-Kra seminorm estimates for averages (
2
) along pairwise independent Hardy sequences. We will prove this by a variant of the seminorm smoothing argument developed in
[
36
]
. The argument does not really use any particular properties of Hardy sequences other than the estimates provided by Theorem
10.2
, so we will carry it out in a more abstract form that makes the computations cleaner and may find further applications in the future. The setting in which we end up working is captured by the definition below.

Definition 11.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{Q}}=\{q_{1},\ldots,q_{m}\}
be an ordered collection of sequences
q_{i}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
whose germs are linearly independent,
21
21

21

That means that for every
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the restrictions of
q_{1},\ldots,q_{m}
to
[N,\infty)
are
{\mathbb{R}}
-independent.
and fix an integer
0\leq m_{1}<m
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in\Span_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{Q}}
be given by
a_{j}(n)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\alpha_{ji}q_{i}(n)
. We define the
degree
of
a_{j}
via

\deg a_{j}:=\max\{i\in[m]:\;\alpha_{ji}\neq 0\},

and its
complexity
by
\max\limits_{i\in[m]}\{|\alpha_{ji}|,\;1/|\alpha_{ji}|:\;\alpha_{ji}\neq 0\}.
We call the sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}

pairwise independent
if
(\alpha_{j(m_{1}+1)},\ldots,\alpha_{jm})
and
(\alpha_{j^{\prime}(m_{1}+1)},\ldots,\alpha_{j^{\prime}m})
are
{\mathbb{R}}
-linearly independent for all distinct
j,j^{\prime}\in[0,\ell]
. We also denote
d_{jj^{\prime}}=\max\{i\in[m]:\;\alpha_{ji}\neq\alpha_{j^{\prime}i}\}
to be the
degree
of
a_{j}-a_{j^{\prime}}
, setting
d_{jj^{\prime}}=0
if
a_{j}=a_{j^{\prime}}
.

We call
{\mathcal{Q}}

good for smoothing
if for all
d,\ell,M\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
J\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
,
b_{1},\ldots,b_{J}\in\Span_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{Q}}
with
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
being pairwise independent and of complexity at most
M
, and
a_{1}
having maximum degree among
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
, systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
, indexing tuples
\eta\in[k]^{\ell}
, and 1-bounded functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

(88)

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{{O_{d,J,\ell,{\mathcal{Q}}}(1)}}\\
\ll_{d,J,k,\ell,M,{\mathcal{Q}}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{1}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{(\alpha_{1d_{10}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{1}})^{s},\;(\alpha_{1d_{12}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{1}}-\alpha_{2d_{12}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{2}})^{s},\;\ldots,\;(\alpha_{1d_{1\ell}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{1}}-\alpha_{\ell d_{1\ell}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}})^{s}}^{+}

for some
s=O_{d,J,\ell,{\mathcal{Q}}}(1)
.

Whilst it is not necessary for the seminorm estimates above to be of polynomial shape (any uniform bound would do), this is what we have in our case, and so there is no harm in specifying the shape of seminorm control in our definition.

Note that the definition above is sensitive to the ordering of
q_{1},\ldots,q_{m}
and the choice of
m_{1}.
In our case, the ordered basis
q_{1},\ldots,q_{m}
of sequences will consist of

(89)

\displaystyle g_{1}(n),\ldots,g_{m_{2}}(n),n,n^{2},\ldots,n^{d},g_{m_{2}+1}(n),\ldots,g_{m}(n)

satisfying the assumptions of Theorem
10.2
,
and pairwise independence of two sequences in the real span of (
89
) amounts to saying that any nontrivial real combination of
g_{m_{1}+1}(n),\ldots,g_{m}(n),n,\ldots,n^{d}
grows faster than log.

Lastly, we remark that in contrast to the arguments from the previous sections, here we do not allow
f_{2},\ldots,f_{\ell}
to depend on
N
. This will be important later on, as in the proof of Theorem
1.16
, we will transfer the control over the average between the functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}
, and for this to make sense, we need them to be fixed.

Theorem
10.2
and Proposition
6.3
then imply the following.

Theorem 11.2

(Hardy sequences are good for smoothing)
.

Let
d,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
0\leq m_{1}\leq m_{2}\leq m
, and
g_{1},...,g_{m}\in\mathcal{H}
be Hardy functions satisfying the following growth conditions:

(i)

1\prec g_{1}(t)\cdots\prec g_{m}(t)\ll t^{d};

(ii)

g_{i}(t)\ll\log t
iff
i\in[m_{1}]
;

(iii)

g_{i}(t)\prec t^{\delta}
for every
\delta>0
(i.e.
\fracdeg g_{i}=0
) iff
i\in[m_{2}]
.

Then the ordered family (
89
) is good for smoothing.

Proof.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
be linear combinations of sequences in (
89
), and suppose that
a_{1}
has maximum degree among
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
with respect to the ordering of (
89
). We want to establish that the estimate (
88
) always holds. If
\fracdeg a_{1}>0
, then (
88
) holds by Theorem
10.2
. Otherwise all of
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are subfractional by the assumption of the maximum degree of
a_{1}
, and so the desired estimate follows from Proposition
6.3
.
∎

The purpose of this section is to prove the following Host-Kra seminorm control that implies Theorem
1.16
as a special case.

Proposition 11.3
.

Let
d,k,\ell,m,M\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
J\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and
\eta\in[k]^{\ell}
. Suppose that the family of sequences
{\mathcal{Q}}

is good for smoothing, and the sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in\Span_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{Q}}
are pairwise independent and of complexity at most
M
. Then there exists a positive integer
s=O_{d,J,\ell,{\mathcal{Q}}}(1)
such that for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
, 1-bounded functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
and sequences
b_{1},\ldots,b_{J}\in\Span_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{Q}}
, we have

(90)

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{{O_{d,J,\ell,{\mathcal{Q}}}(1)}}\\
\ll_{d,J,k,\ell,M,{\mathcal{Q}}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{1}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{s,T_{\eta_{1}}}.

In proving Proposition
11.3
, we will use the formalism developed in
[
36
]
. Consider the average

(91)

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}).

We let
\ell
be its
length
,
m^{*}=\max_{j}\deg a_{j}
be its
degree
, and
\eta=(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{\ell})\in[k]^{\ell}
be its
indexing tuple
.
Furthermore, we define

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{L}}:=\{j\in[\ell]\colon\ \deg a_{j}=m^{*}\}

to be the set of indices corresponding to the sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}
of maximum degree.
The
type
of (
91
) is the tuple
w:=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{k})\in[0,\ell]^{k}
in which

\displaystyle w_{l}:=|\{j\in{\mathfrak{L}}\colon\ \eta_{j}=l\}|=|\{j\in[\ell]\colon\ \eta_{j}=l,\ \deg a_{j}=m^{*}\}|,

and it represents the number of times the transformation
T_{l}
appears in the average with a sequence of maximum degree. For instance, given the ordered family
{\mathcal{Q}}=\{n,n^{1/2},n^{3/2},n^{3/2}\log n\}
(which respects the ordering that we have in our applications since we consider nonpolynomial functions of positive fractional degree before polynomials), the average

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}\,T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor\sqrt{2}n^{3/2}\right\rfloor}}f_{1}\cdot T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor\sqrt{3}n^{1/2}\right\rfloor}}f_{2}\cdot T_{2}^{{\left\lfloor n^{3/2}+n\right\rfloor}}f_{3}\cdot T_{1}^{n}f_{4}\cdot T_{2}^{{\left\lfloor n^{3/2}+\sqrt{5}n\right\rfloor}}f_{5}\cdot{\mathcal{D}}_{1}\Bigl({\left\lfloor n^{3/2}\log n\right\rfloor}\Bigr)

has length 5, degree 3, indexing tuple (1, 1, 2, 1, 2) and type
(1,2)
because the transformation
T_{1}
appears only once with the sequence
n^{3/2}
of maximum degree (at the index
j=1
) while
T_{2}
has an iterate of maximum degree twice, at
j=3,5
. It does not matter that the sequence
n^{3/2}\log n
in the dual term has higher degree since our notion of type completely ignores how many dual terms we have and what sequences they involve.

The complexity of an average is measured by its length and type. Types
w
with a fixed length
|w|:=w_{1}+\cdots+w_{k}
(which coincides with the length of an average of type
w
) are ordered by their variance: we set
w^{\prime}<w
if
w^{\prime}
has strictly higher variance than
w
. We remark here that comparing variances is the same as comparing second moments since we only compare tuples of the same lengths; hence equivalently
w^{\prime}<w
if

{w^{\prime}_{1}}^{2}+\cdots+{w^{\prime}_{k}}^{2}>w_{1}^{2}+\cdots+w_{k}^{2}.

However, it is the variance rather than second moment that carries the intuition for why we take this particular ordering. Indeed, we think of the simplest averages as those in which all the leading terms correspond to the same transformation because for those averages, we can obtain a Host-Kra seminorm control immediately from the definition of being good for smoothing (see Proposition
11.4
below). It is an easy exercise to show that the types of such averages maximize variance. By contrast, the most difficult averages to deal with are those with minimum variance, i.e. those in which each highest degree term corresponds to a different transformation.

In the induction procedure, the type of an average will be lowered through the following
type operation
. For distinct integers
i_{1},i_{2}\in[\ell]
with
i_{1}\in\textrm{supp}(w)
(which we define to be
\textrm{supp}(w)=\{l\in[\ell]:\ w_{l}>0\}
), we define

\displaystyle(\sigma_{i_{1}i_{2}}w)_{l}:=\begin{cases}w_{l},\;&l\neq i_{1},i_{2}\\
w_{i_{1}}-1,\;&l=i_{1}\\
w_{i_{2}}+1,\;&l=i_{2}.\end{cases}.

For instance,
\sigma_{12}(2,3,7)=(1,4,7)
. A simple computation shows that if
w_{i_{1}}\leq w_{i_{2}}
, then
\sigma_{i_{1}i_{2}}w>w
, in which case
\sigma_{i_{1}i_{2}}

lowers the type
of
w
. Using this operation, we can for instance obtain the following chains of types:

\displaystyle(4,0,0)<(3,1,0)<(2,2,0)<(2,1,1)\quad\textrm{and}\quad(0,4,0)<(1,3,0)<(2,2,0)<(2,1,1).

We remark that this is not how the ordering on types of fixed length is defined in
[
36
]
, but the notion of order from
[
36
]
is a suborder of the current one in that
w^{\prime}<w
whenever
w^{\prime}\prec w
, where
\prec
is the order from
[
36
]
. Taking the (slightly more complicated) notion of type from
[
36
]
would thus work equally well.

The ordering
<
on types organizes the way in which we pass from averages of indexing tuple
\eta
to averages of indexing tuple
\eta^{\prime}
. After applying the type operations finitely many types to a type
w^{\prime}
, we arrive at a type
w
with the property that
w_{l}=0
for all but one value
l\in{\mathfrak{L}}
; we call these types
basic
. For instance,
(4,0,0)
and
(0,4,0)
in the example above are basic types but
(3,1,0),(1,3,0),(2,2,0),(2,1,1)
are not. Basic types are named so because they will serve as the basis for our induction procedure in that whenever the average has a basic type and
a_{1}
is a sequence of maximum degree, we immediately obtain the claimed result.

Proposition 11.4
.

Let
d,k,\ell,m,M\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
J\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
. Suppose that the family of sequences
{\mathcal{Q}}
is good for smoothing, and the sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in\Span_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{Q}}
are pairwise independent and of complexity at most
M
. Suppose that
a_{1}
is a sequence of maximum degree and the tuple
\eta\in[k]^{\ell}
is basic.
22
22

22

By abuse of notation, we say that the indexing tuple of an average is basic if the average itself is also basic. This is an abuse of notation because the type depends on
\eta|_{\mathfrak{L}}
rather than
\eta
, and to know
{\mathfrak{L}}
, we also need to know the sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
.
Then there exists a positive integer
s=O_{d,J,\ell,{\mathcal{Q}}}(1)
such that for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
, 1-bounded functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
and sequences
b_{1},\ldots,b_{J}\in\Span_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{Q}}
, the bound (
90
) holds.

Proof.

Let
a_{j}(n)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\alpha_{ji}q_{i}(n)
for each
j\in[\ell]
.
From the property of being good for smoothing and the assumption of maximum degree of
a_{1}
, we immediately get the bound (
88
). It remains to show that each vector

(92)

\displaystyle\alpha_{1d_{10}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{1}},\;\alpha_{1d_{12}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{1}}-\alpha_{2d_{12}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{2}},\;\ldots,\;\alpha_{1d_{1\ell}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{1}}-\alpha_{\ell d_{1\ell}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}

appearing in the generalized box seminorm from (
88
) is a nonzero real multiple of
{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{1}}
; if that is the case, then the result will follow from Lemma
3.8
. Since the average is of basic type and
a_{1}
has maximum degree, one of the two things can happen for each
j\in[0,\ell]\setminus\{1\}
: either
\deg a_{j}<\deg a_{1}
, in which case

\alpha_{1d_{1j}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{1}}-\alpha_{jd_{1j}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{j}}=\alpha_{1d_{1j}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{1}},

or
\deg a_{j}=\deg a_{1}
, in which case
\eta_{j}=\eta_{1}
, so that

\alpha_{1d_{1j}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{1}}-\alpha_{jd_{1j}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{j}}=(\alpha_{1d_{1j}}-\alpha_{jd_{1j}}){\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{1}}.

One way or another, we end up with a nonzero real multiple of
{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{1}}
.
∎

When the type of an average is not basic, we will prove Proposition
11.3
by inducting on the type and length of the average, reducing both until we reach an average of basic type. The length of the average in the induction process will be reduced using the proposition below.

Proposition 11.5
.

Let
d,k,\ell,m,M\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
J\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
. Suppose that the family of sequences
{\mathcal{Q}}
is good for smoothing, and the sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in\Span_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{Q}}
are pairwise independent and of complexity at most
M
. Suppose that
a_{1}
is a sequence of maximum degree,
\eta\in[k]^{\ell}
is a tuple of type
w
, and
w_{\eta_{1}}
is minimal among the nonzero coordinates of
w
. Then there exists a positive integer
s=O_{d,J,\ell,{\mathcal{Q}}}(1)
such that for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
, 1-bounded functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
and sequences
b_{1},\ldots,b_{J}\in\Span_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{Q}}
, the bound (
90
) holds.

Proposition
11.5
will be derived from an iterated application of the result below.

Proposition 11.6
.

Let
d,k,\ell,m,M\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
J\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
. Suppose that the family of sequences
{\mathcal{Q}}=\{q_{1},\ldots,q_{m}\}
is good for smoothing, and the sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in\Span_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{Q}}
given by
a_{j}(n)=\sum_{i\in[m]}\alpha_{ji}q_{i}(n)

are pairwise independent and of complexity at most
M
. Suppose that
a_{1}
is a sequence of maximum degree,
\eta\in[k]^{\ell}
is a tuple of type
w
, and
w_{\eta_{1}}
is minimal among the nonzero coordinates of
w
. Then for all vectors
{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s+1}
in (
92
) there exists a positive integer
s^{\prime}=O_{d,J,\ell,s,{\mathcal{Q}}}(1)
with the following property: if
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
is a system and

(93)

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{{O_{d,J,\ell,s,{\mathcal{Q}}}(1)}}\\
\ll_{d,J,k,\ell,M,s,{\mathcal{Q}}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{1}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s+1}}

holds for all 1-bounded functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
and sequences
b_{1},\ldots,b_{J}\in\Span_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{Q}}
, then

(94)

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{{O_{d,J,\ell,s,{\mathcal{Q}}}(1)}}\\
\ll_{d,J,k,\ell,M,s,{\mathcal{Q}}}\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{1}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s},{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{1}}^{s^{\prime}}}

also holds for all 1-bounded functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
and sequences
b_{1},\ldots,b_{J}\in\Span_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{Q}}
.

Proof of Proposition
11.5
using Proposition
11.6
.

By Lemma
3.9
and the definition of being good for smoothing, we have the bound (
88
) (Lemma
3.9
allows us to use “unplused” seminorms rather than the “plused” ones at the cost of increasing the degree by 1). The bound (
90
) then follows by iteratively applying Proposition
11.6
, at each step replacing one vector in the seminorm in (
88
) different from
{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{1}}
by a bounded number of copies of
{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{1}}
.
∎

We are now in the position to set out the main steps in the proof of Proposition
11.3
for an average of length
\ell
and type
w
:

(i)

We find an index
i\in[\ell]
so that
a_{i}
is a sequence of maximal degree and
w_{\eta_{i}}
minimizes the nonzero coordinates of
w
. Applying Proposition
11.5
with the role of 1 played by
i
, we obtain Host-Kra seminorm control on index
i
. By standard maneuvers, this allows us to replace
f_{i}
by a bounded degree dual function of
T_{\eta_{i}}
, and thus we reduce to an average of length
\ell-1
. Then Proposition
11.3
for the average of length
\ell
and type
w
follows by invoking Proposition
11.3
for averages of length
\ell-1
.

(ii)

To prove Proposition
11.6
(from which Proposition
11.5
follows by the argument above), we argue in two steps, which in
[
36
]
have been called
ping
and
pong
. In the
ping
step, we invoke Proposition
11.3
for an average of length
\ell
and type
w^{\prime}<w
; in the
pong
step, we invoke Proposition
11.3
for an average of length
\ell-1
.

Thus, in the inductive step we invoke Proposition
11.5
for averages of either smaller length or the same length but lower type. After finitely many iterations, we therefore land in the case of averages of length 1 or basic type. Since averages of length 1 are automatically of basic type, the base case is fully covered by Proposition
11.4
.

The proofs of Propositions
11.3
and
11.6
resemble closely the proofs from
[
36
]
at the level of both the general setup and specific maneuvers. The main challenge in adapting the arguments from
[
36
]
is the inadequacy of certain technical tools from
[
36
]
for our setting. Specifically, the proofs from
[
36
]
utilize crucially the fact that for
\alpha_{j},\alpha_{j^{\prime}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, a function has a large box seminorm
\left|\!\left|\!\left|\cdot\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{\alpha_{j}{\mathbf{e}}_{j}-\alpha_{j^{\prime}}{\mathbf{e}}_{j^{\prime}}}
if and only if it correlates with a
T_{j}^{\alpha_{j}}T_{j^{\prime}}^{-\alpha_{j^{\prime}}}
-invariant function. This statement does not make sense if
\alpha_{j}
or
\alpha_{j^{\prime}}
are not integers, so we need instead to develop an approximate version of the inverse theorem for degree-1 generalized box seminorms (Lemma
11.9
) and a way of applying it to the problem (Lemma
11.10
).

11.1.
Preliminary lemmas

Before we prove Propositions
11.3
and
11.6
, we gather several lemmas needed in the proofs. The first one is a straightforward modification of
[
27
, Proposition 4.2]
, phrased in a way that allows an immediate application. It says that at the cost of a small loss in the lower bound, we can replace an arbitrary function
f_{1}
by one that is structured in an appropriate sense.

Lemma 11.7
.

Let
d,\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
J\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
be numbers,
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell},b_{1},\ldots,b_{J}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be sequences,
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
be a system,
\eta\in[k]^{\ell}
be an indexing tuple and
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
be
1
-bounded functions. For every
\delta>0
there exists an increasing sequence of integers
(N_{i})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, 1-bounded weights
(c^{\prime}_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
,
1
-bounded functions
(g_{i})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subseteq L^{\infty}(\mu)
, and
{\mathcal{D}}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}
such that

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{4}-\delta\\
\leq\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot T_{\eta_{1}}^{{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}\tilde{f}_{1}\cdot\prod_{j\in[2,\ell]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)},

where
\tilde{f}_{1}
is the weak limit

(95)

\tilde{f}_{1}=\lim_{i\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N_{i}]}\overline{c^{\prime}_{n}}\cdot T_{\eta_{1}}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}g_{i}\\
\prod_{j\in[2,\ell]}T_{\eta_{1}}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{\eta_{j}}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}\overline{f_{j}}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}T_{\eta_{1}}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}\overline{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})}.

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle\varepsilon=\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)},

and fix
\delta>0
. There exist
{\mathcal{D}}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\prime},(c^{\prime}_{n})
such that

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}^{\prime}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}\geq\varepsilon^{2}-\delta.

Defining

\displaystyle F_{N}=\E_{n\in[N]}c^{\prime}_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}^{\prime}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}),

expanding the inner product and composing the integral with
T_{\eta_{1}}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}
, we get that

\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}^{\prime}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}\\
=\int\overline{f_{1}}\cdot\E_{n\in[N]}\overline{c^{\prime}_{n}}\cdot T_{\eta_{1}}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}F_{N}\cdot\prod_{j\in[2,\ell]}T_{\eta_{1}}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{\eta_{j}}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}\overline{f_{j}}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}T_{\eta_{1}}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}\overline{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})}\;d\mu.

By weak compactness, there exists a sequence
N_{i}\to\infty
along which the average over
n\in[N_{i}]
converges weakly; we call the weak limit
\tilde{f}_{1}
and set
g_{i}=F_{N_{i}}
. Then
\int f_{1}\cdot\tilde{f}_{1}\;d\mu\geq\varepsilon^{2}-\delta
.
Applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, using the 1-boundedness of
f_{1}
, and redefining
\delta
appropriately, we get
\int\tilde{f}_{1}\cdot\overline{\tilde{f}_{1}}\;d\mu\geq\varepsilon^{4}-\delta
. The result follows on expanding the second
\tilde{f}_{1}
, composing back with
T_{\eta_{1}}^{{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}
, applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and taking the sups and limsup.
∎

Definition 11.8
.

Given a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
and a finitely generated subgroup
G\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
, we call a function
u\in L^{\infty}(\mu)

approximately
G
-invariant
if it takes the form

\displaystyle u=\E_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}f.

for some
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
. If
G=\langle{\mathbf{g}}\rangle
, then we also call
u

approximately
{\mathbf{g}}
-invariant
.

Such functions naturally appear in inverse theorems for degree-1 generalized box seminorms.

Lemma 11.9

(Degree-1 inverse theorem)
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
be a system and
G\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
be a finitely generated subgroup. For every
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
there exists an approximately
G
-invariant function
u\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
satisfying

(96)

\displaystyle\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G}^{2}=\int_{X}f\cdot u\,d\mu.

Proof.

By the definition of degree 1-box norms, we have

\displaystyle\left|\!\left|\!\left|f\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{G}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\E_{{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\in G}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}f\cdot T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}}\overline{f}\,d\mu

\displaystyle=\int_{X}f\cdot\E_{{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\in G}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}}\Bigl(\E_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G}T^{-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}\overline{f}\Bigr)\,d\mu,

where in passing to the second line, we compose the integral with
T^{-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}
and split the limit using Lemma
3.1
. The result follows immediately.
∎

The following lemma explains the origin of the name for approximately invariant functions and illustrates the way in which such functions will be useful for us.

Lemma 11.10

(Approximate invariance)
.

Let
k,s\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
be a system,
j,j^{\prime}\in[0,k]
be indices, and
\alpha_{j},\alpha_{j^{\prime}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
. If
u\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
is a product of
s
1-bounded approximately
(\alpha_{j}{\mathbf{e}}_{j}-\alpha_{j^{\prime}}{\mathbf{e}}_{j^{\prime}})
-invariant functions, then there exists 1-bounded
u^{\prime}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
such that for every
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, functions
A_{1},\ldots,A_{N}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
and sequence
a:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot A_{n}\cdot T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{j}a(n)\right\rfloor}}u\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\ll_{s}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot A_{n}\cdot T_{j^{\prime}}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{j^{\prime}}a(n)\right\rfloor}}u^{\prime}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle u=\prod_{i\in[s]}\lim_{M\to\infty}\E_{m\in[\pm M]}T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{j}m\right\rfloor}}T_{j^{\prime}}^{{\left\lfloor-\alpha_{j^{\prime}}m\right\rfloor}}f_{i}.

Then

\displaystyle T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{j}a(n)\right\rfloor}}u

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\in[s]}T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{j}a(n)\right\rfloor}}\lim_{M\to\infty}\E_{m\in[\pm M]}T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{j}m\right\rfloor}}T_{j^{\prime}}^{{\left\lfloor-\alpha_{j^{\prime}}m\right\rfloor}}f_{i}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\in[s]}\lim_{M\to\infty}\E_{m\in[\pm M]}T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{j}(m+{\left\lfloor a(n)\right\rfloor})\right\rfloor}+r_{m,n}}T_{j^{\prime}}^{{\left\lfloor-\alpha_{j^{\prime}}m\right\rfloor}}f_{i}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\in[s]}\lim_{M\to\infty}\E_{m\in[\pm M]}T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{j}m\right\rfloor}+r^{\prime}_{m,n}}T_{j^{\prime}}^{{\left\lfloor-\alpha_{j^{\prime}}(m-{\left\lfloor a(n)\right\rfloor})\right\rfloor}}f_{i}

\displaystyle=T_{j^{\prime}}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{j^{\prime}}a(n)\right\rfloor}}\mathopen{}\left(\prod_{i\in[s]}\lim_{M\to\infty}\E_{m\in[\pm M]}T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{j}m\right\rfloor}+r^{\prime}_{m,n}}T_{j^{\prime}}^{{\left\lfloor-\alpha_{j^{\prime}}m\right\rfloor}+r^{\prime\prime}_{m,n}}f_{i}\mathclose{}\right)

for some integers
r_{m,n},r^{\prime}_{m,n},r^{\prime\prime}_{m,n}=O(1)
that come from commuting sums and integer parts. Plugging this formula into the original
L^{2}(\mu)
norm, applying the triangle inequality and using Lemma
5.3
to make
r_{m,n},r^{\prime}_{m,n},r^{\prime\prime}_{m,n}
independent of
n
, we get

\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot A_{n}\cdot T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{j}a(n)\right\rfloor}}u\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\ll_{s}\limsup_{M\to\infty}\E_{m_{1},\ldots,m_{s}\in[\pm M]}\\
\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot A_{n}\cdot T_{j^{\prime}}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{j^{\prime}}a(n)\right\rfloor}}\mathopen{}\left(\prod_{i\in[s]}T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{j}m_{i}\right\rfloor}+r^{\prime}_{m_{i}}}T_{j^{\prime}}^{{\left\lfloor-\alpha_{j^{\prime}}m_{i}\right\rfloor}+r^{\prime\prime}_{m_{i}}}f_{i}\mathclose{}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

for some
r^{\prime}_{m_{i}},r^{\prime\prime}_{m_{i}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Upon pigeonholing in
m_{1},\ldots,m_{s}
, the result follows with

u^{\prime}:=\prod_{i\in[s]}T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor\alpha_{j}m_{i}\right\rfloor}+r^{\prime}_{m_{i}}}T_{j^{\prime}}^{{\left\lfloor-\alpha_{j^{\prime}}m_{i}\right\rfloor}+r^{\prime\prime}_{m_{i}}}f_{i}.

∎

Lemma 11.11

(Dual-difference interchange)
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
be a system,
s,

s^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s+1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
be vectors,

(f_{n,i})_{n,i\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subseteq L^{\infty}(\mu)
be 1-bounded, and
\tilde{f}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
be defined by

\displaystyle\tilde{f}:=\,\lim_{i\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N_{i}]}\,f_{n,i},

for some
N_{i}\to\infty
, where the average is assumed to converge weakly. Then

|\!|\!|\tilde{f}|\!|\!|_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s},{\mathbf{v}}_{s+1}^{s^{\prime}}}^{O_{s,s^{\prime}}(1)}\leq\liminf_{M\to\infty}\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s}}\limsup_{i\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N_{i}]}\\
\int\Delta_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}),\ldots,\\
({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor})\end{subarray}}\;f_{n,i}\cdot u_{{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\,d\mu

for 1-bounded functions
u_{{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
which can be either of the following:

(i)

if
s^{\prime}\geq 1
, then
u_{{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}
is a product of
2^{s}
1-bounded dual functions of level
s^{\prime}
with respect to
{{\mathbf{v}}_{s+1}}
;

(ii)

if
s^{\prime}=1
, then we can alternatively take
u_{{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}
to be a product of
2^{s}
1-bounded approximately
{{\mathbf{v}}_{s+1}}
-invariant functions.

We note two alternative conclusions in the case
s^{\prime}=1
.

In proving Lemma
11.11
, we will use the following version of the Gowers-Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.

Lemma 11.12

(Twisted Gowers-Cauchy-Schwarz inequality)
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
be a system,
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
be vectors, and
(f_{\underline{\epsilon}})_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}},

(u_{{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}})_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s}}
be tuples of 1-bounded functions in
L^{\infty}(\mu)
. Then for every
M\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s}}\,\int\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}}\right\rfloor}}f_{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot u_{{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}}\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|^{2^{s}}

\displaystyle\leq\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s}}\,\int\Delta_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}),\ldots,\\
({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor})\end{subarray}}\;f_{\underline{1}}\cdot u_{{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\,d\mu,

where
u_{{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}
is a product of
2^{s}
functions from the set

\{T^{\mathbf{n}}{\mathcal{C}}^{\epsilon}u_{{\underline{k}},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}}:\;k_{i}\in\{m_{i},m_{i}^{\prime\prime}\},\;k^{\prime}_{i}\in\{m_{i}^{\prime},m_{i}^{\prime\prime\prime}\},\;\epsilon\in\{0,1\},\;{\mathbf{n}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}\}.

Proof.

We prove the case
s=2
, and the general case follows similarly. Upon composing with
T^{-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor}}
, the expression inside the absolute value equals

\E_{m_{2},m_{2}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]}\int T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{2}m_{2}\right\rfloor}}f_{00}\cdot T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{2}m_{2}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}}f_{01}\\
\E_{m_{1},m_{1}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor}}\mathopen{}\left(T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{2}m_{2}\right\rfloor}}f_{10}\cdot T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{2}m_{2}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}}f_{11}\mathclose{}\right)\cdot T^{-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor}}u_{m_{1}m_{2}m_{1}^{\prime}m_{2}^{\prime}}\;d\mu.

By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, its square is bounded by

\E_{m_{2},m_{2}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]}\int\E_{m_{1},m_{1}^{\prime},m_{1}^{\prime\prime},m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\in[\pm M]}\\
\Delta_{({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor})}\mathopen{}\left(T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{2}m_{2}\right\rfloor}}f_{10}\cdot T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{2}m_{2}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}}f_{11}\mathclose{}\right)\\
T^{-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor}}u_{m_{1}m_{2}m_{1}^{\prime}m_{2}^{\prime}}\cdot T^{-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}}\overline{u_{m_{1}^{\prime\prime}m_{2}m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}m_{2}^{\prime}}}\;d\mu.

Composing with
T^{-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{2}m_{2}\right\rfloor}}
and changing the order of summations, this equals

\E_{m_{1},m_{1}^{\prime},m_{1}^{\prime\prime},m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\in[\pm M]}\int\Delta_{({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor})}f_{10}\\
\E_{m_{2},m_{2}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]}\Delta_{({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor})}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{2}m_{2}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{2}m_{2}\right\rfloor}}f_{11}\\
T^{-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{2}m_{2}\right\rfloor}}u_{m_{1}m_{2}m_{1}^{\prime}m_{2}^{\prime}}\cdot T^{-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{2}m_{2}\right\rfloor}}\overline{u_{m_{1}^{\prime\prime}m_{2}m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}m_{2}^{\prime}}}\;d\mu.

An application of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality allows us to bound the square of the above by

\E_{m_{1},m_{1}^{\prime},m_{1}^{\prime\prime},m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\in[\pm M]}\int\left|\E_{m_{2},m_{2}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]}\Delta_{({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor})}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{2}m_{2}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{2}m_{2}\right\rfloor}}f_{11}\right.\\
\left.T^{-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{2}m_{2}\right\rfloor}}u_{m_{1}m_{2}m_{1}^{\prime}m_{2}^{\prime}}\cdot T^{-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{2}m_{2}\right\rfloor}}\overline{u_{m_{1}^{\prime\prime}m_{2}m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}m_{2}^{\prime}}}\vphantom{\E_{m_{2},m_{2}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]}}\right|^{2}\;d\mu.

The result follows with

u_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\\
m_{2}m_{2}^{\prime}m_{2}^{\prime\prime}m_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}\end{subarray}}=T^{-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{2}m_{2}\right\rfloor}}u_{m_{1}m_{2}m_{1}^{\prime}m_{2}^{\prime}}\cdot T^{-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{2}m_{2}\right\rfloor}}\overline{u_{m_{1}^{\prime\prime}m_{2}m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}m_{2}^{\prime}}}\\
T^{-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{2}m_{2}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}}\overline{u_{m_{1}m_{2}^{\prime\prime}m_{1}^{\prime}m_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}}}\cdot T^{-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{2}m_{2}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}}{u_{m_{1}^{\prime\prime}m_{2}^{\prime\prime}m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}m_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}}}

upon expanding the square.
∎

Proof of Lemma
11.11
.

Let

\displaystyle\varepsilon=|\!|\!|\tilde{f}|\!|\!|_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s},{\mathbf{v}}_{s+1}^{s^{\prime}}}.

Using the inductive formula for generalized box norms (
18
), we get that

\displaystyle\lim_{M\to\infty}\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s}}\int\Delta_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s};({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime})}\tilde{f}\cdot u_{{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}}\;d\mu=\varepsilon^{2^{s+s^{\prime}}},

where we can take
u_{{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}}
to be any of the following depending on which conclusion we want to obtain:

(i)

if
s^{\prime}\geq 1
: a dual function of level
s^{\prime}
with respect to
{\mathbf{v}}_{s+1}
(follows from the definition of dual functions);

(ii)

if
s^{\prime}=1
: an approximately
{\mathbf{v}}_{s+1}
-invariant function (follows from Lemma
11.9
).

We expand the expression above further as

\lim_{M\to\infty}\lim_{i\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N_{i}]}\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s}}\\
\int\mathopen{}\left(\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}_{*}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}}\right\rfloor}}\tilde{f}\mathclose{}\right)\cdot T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}}f_{n,i}\cdot u_{{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}}\;d\mu;

the reason why we expand only one copy of
\tilde{f}
is to take the average over
n
and the limit
N\to\infty
out of the integral using weak convergence. The result follows from an application of Lemma
11.12
with
f_{\underline{1}}=f_{n,i}
.
∎

11.2.
Proof of Proposition
11.6

We now prove Proposition
11.6
for averages of length
\ell
and type
w
assuming Proposition
11.3
for averages of length
\ell-1
as well as length
\ell
and types
w^{\prime}<w
. If the type
w
is basic, then the claim follows immediately from Proposition
11.4
, so we assume otherwise.

Let
{\mathbf{v}}_{s+1}=\alpha_{1d_{1l}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{1}}-\alpha_{ld_{1l}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{l}}
for some
l\in[0,\ell]\setminus\{1\}
; it is nonzero since it is the leading coefficient of
a_{1}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{1}}-a_{l}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{l}}
, and
a_{1},a_{l}
are essentially distinct (which follows from them being pairwise independent). We first sort out the easy cases. If
\eta_{l}=\eta_{1}
, then
{\mathbf{v}}_{s+1}
is a nonzero multiple of
{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{1}}
, and the claim follows from Lemmas
3.8
and
3.9
.
23
23

23

Specifically, we can use Lemma
3.9
to add
+
to our seminorm along
{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s+1}
, then apply Lemma
3.8
to replace
{\mathbf{v}}_{s+1}
with
{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{1}}
, and then apply the other part of Lemma
3.9
to “unplus” the seminorm at the cost of adding one more
{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{1}}
. This gives the result with
s^{\prime}=2
.
If
\deg a_{l}<\deg a_{1}
, then
\alpha_{ld_{1l}}=0
, and the claim once again follows from Lemmas
3.8
and
3.9
.

Suppose therefore that
\eta_{l}\neq\eta_{1}
and
\deg a_{l}=\deg a_{1}
(
\deg a_{l}>\deg a_{1}
is not allowed since
a_{1}
has the maximal degree by assumption), in which case
\alpha_{ld_{1l}}\neq 0
. The proof of Proposition
11.6
in this case follows the same two-step strategy as in the analogous result in
[
36
]
. First, we establish auxiliary control of our average by
\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{l}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{{{\mathbf{v}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\mathbf{v}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{l}}^{s_{1}}}
for some bounded
s_{1}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and then we use this ancillary bound to get the claimed result. Like in
[
36
]
, we refer to these two steps as
ping
and
pong
since we are transferring the control from
f_{1}
to
f_{l}
and then back to
f_{1}
much like a table tennis player passes the ball to his opponent, only to receive it back a second later.

Throughout the argument, we let all the constant depend on
d,J,k,\ell,M,s,{\mathcal{Q}}
, noting however that
s_{1},s^{\prime}
as well as the powers of
\varepsilon
will not depend on
k,M
.

Step 1 (
ping
): Obtaining auxiliary control by a seminorm of
f_{l}
.

Let

\displaystyle\varepsilon=\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Then Proposition
11.7
gives us a 1-bounded function
\tilde{f}_{1}
as in (
95
) for which

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\\
\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot T_{\eta_{1}}^{{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}\tilde{f}_{1}\cdot\prod_{j\in[2,\ell]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

By our assumption, we have

|\!|\!|\tilde{f}_{1}|\!|\!|_{{{\mathbf{v}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\mathbf{v}}_{s+1}}}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

We then unpack this seminorm using Lemma
11.11
and the formula (
95
) for
\tilde{f}_{1}
; after composing the resulting expression with
T_{\eta_{1}}^{{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}
, and applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we obtain

\liminf_{M\to\infty}\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s}}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left|\!\left|\vphantom{\prod_{j\in[J]}}\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot T_{\eta_{1}}^{{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}u_{{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\right.\right.\\
\left.\left.\prod_{j\in[2,\ell]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\right|\!\right|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)},

where

\displaystyle f_{j{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}),\ldots,\\
({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor})\end{subarray}}\overline{f_{j}}

\displaystyle\textrm{and}\quad{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}),\ldots,\\
({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor})\end{subarray}}\overline{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j}}

for some
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}
given by Lemma
11.7
, and each
u_{{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}
is a product of
2^{s}
1-bounded approximately
(\alpha_{1d_{1l}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{1}}-\alpha_{ld_{1l}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{l}})
-invariant functions.

Thus, at the cost of having a family of averages to deal with rather than a single one, we have replaced the arbitrary function
f_{1}
by one which is “structured” in the sense of being a product of approximately invariant functions. We now use this structure to lower the type of the average. By Lemma
11.10
, we can find 1-bounded functions
u^{\prime}_{{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}
such that

\liminf_{M\to\infty}\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s}}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left|\!\left|\vphantom{\prod_{j\in[J]}}\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot T_{\eta^{\prime}_{1}}^{{\left\lfloor a^{\prime}_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}u^{\prime}_{{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\right.\right.\\
\left.\left.\prod_{j\in[2,\ell]}T_{\eta^{\prime}_{j}}^{{\left\lfloor a^{\prime}_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\right|\!\right|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)},

where

\displaystyle a^{\prime}_{j}(n):=\begin{cases}\frac{\alpha_{ld_{1l}}}{\alpha_{1d_{1l}}}a_{1}(n),\;&j=1\\
a_{j},\;&j\in[2,\ell]\end{cases},\quad\textrm{and}\quad\eta^{\prime}_{j}:=\begin{cases}\eta_{l},\;&j=1\\
\eta_{j},\;&j\in[2,\ell]\end{cases}.

Since
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are pairwise independent,
24
24

24

All we need is that the sequences that we are dealing with at any given step of the argument are essentially distinct. However, the fact that we rescale sequences at subsequent steps means that essential distinctness need not be preserved from one step to another. By contrast, pairwise independence is preserved under scaling, and therefore it is the right assumption to make on our sequences.

a^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,a^{\prime}_{\ell}
are as well. Moreover, the complexity of
a^{\prime}_{j}
’s is at most the square of the complexity of
a_{j}
’s.

By assumption,
w_{\eta_{1}}
minimizes the nonzero coordinates of
w
. Since
\eta_{l}\neq\eta_{1}
, we deduce that the type
w^{\prime}=\sigma_{\eta_{1}\eta_{l}}w
of the new averages indexed by
({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{4s}
is strictly lower than
w
. We therefore apply inductively Proposition
11.3
for averages of length
\ell
and type
w^{\prime}<w
to find a positive integer
s_{1}=O(1)
such that for a set
B
of
({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{4s}
of lower density
\Omega(\varepsilon^{O(1)})
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{l,{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{s_{1},T_{\eta_{l}}}=\left|\!\left|\!\left|\Delta_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}),\ldots,\\
({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor})\end{subarray}}\overline{f_{l}}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{s_{1},T_{\eta_{l}}}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

Averaging over
B
, extending the average to all of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{4s}
by nonnegativity, raising it to the power
2^{s_{1}}
using the Hölder inequality and applying the inductive formula for generalized box seminorms (
18
), we get the auxiliary norm control
25
25

25

We are glossing over the fact that the inductive formula works for multiplicative derivatives of the form
\Delta_{({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor})}
rather than
\Delta_{({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor})}
. One way to fix it is to pigeonhole in
(m_{1},m_{1}^{\prime\prime})
to find a single pair for which the appropriate fiber in
B
has a lower density
\Omega(\varepsilon^{O(1)})
in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2s}
, and then use the Gowers-Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to remove the shifts by these fixed pairs.

\displaystyle\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{l}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s},{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{l}}^{s_{1}}}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

Step 2 (pong): Obtaining control by a seminorm of
f_{1}
.

To get the claim that
\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{1}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{{{\mathbf{v}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\mathbf{v}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{1}}^{s^{\prime}}}
controls the average for some bounded
s^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we repeat the procedure once more with
f_{l}
in place of
f_{1}
. From Proposition
11.7

it follows that

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\\
\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot T_{\eta_{l}}^{{\left\lfloor a_{l}(n)\right\rfloor}}\tilde{f}_{l}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell],j\neq l}T_{\eta_{j}}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

for some function

\tilde{f}_{l}=\lim_{i\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N_{i}]}\overline{c^{\prime}_{n}}\cdot T_{\eta_{l}}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{l}(n)\right\rfloor}}g_{i}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell],j\neq l}T_{\eta_{l}}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{l}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{\eta_{j}}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}\overline{f_{j}}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}T_{\eta_{l}}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{l}(n)\right\rfloor}}\overline{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})}.

where
g_{i}
’s are 1-bounded functions,
c^{\prime}_{n}
’s are 1-bounded weights,
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}
are dual sequences, and the limit is a weak limit. Then the auxiliary control obtained in the
ping
step gives

|\!|\!|\tilde{f}_{l}|\!|\!|_{{{\mathbf{v}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\mathbf{v}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{l}}^{s_{1}}}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

Lemma
11.11
implies that

\liminf_{H\to\infty}\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s}}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\\
\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell],j\neq l}T_{\eta_{j}}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J+1]}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)},

where

\displaystyle f_{j{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}),\ldots,\\
({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor})\end{subarray}}\overline{f_{j}}

and

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}(n)=\begin{cases}\Delta_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}),\ldots,\\
({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor})\end{subarray}}\overline{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j}}(n),\;&j\in[J]\\
T_{\eta_{l}}^{n}u_{{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}},\;&j=J+1\end{cases},

for
b_{J+1}=a_{l}
, some
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}
given by Lemma
11.7
, and 1-bounded functions
u_{{\underline{m}}{\underline{m}}^{\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime}{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}
, each of which is a product of
2^{s}
level-
s_{1}
dual functions of
T_{\eta_{l}}
.

Each of the new averages indexed by
({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{4s}
has length
\ell-1
. Hence, applying Proposition
11.3
for averages of length
\ell-1
, we obtain a positive integer
s^{\prime}=O(1)
such that for a set
B^{\prime}
of
