({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{4s}
of lower density
\Omega(\varepsilon^{O(1)})
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\!\left|\!\left|\Delta_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}),\ldots,\\
({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{s}m_{s}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor})\end{subarray}}f_{1}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{s^{\prime},T_{\eta_{1}}}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

Averaging over
B^{\prime}
, extending the average to all of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{4}
by nonnegativity, raising it to the power
2^{s^{\prime}}
using the Hölder inequality and applying the inductive formula for generalized box seminorms (
18
), we get

\displaystyle\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{1}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s},{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{1}}^{s^{\prime}}}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)},

as claimed.

11.3.
Proof of Proposition
11.3

Finally, we prove Proposition
11.3
for averages of length
\ell
and type
w
by assuming Proposition
11.5
for averages of length
\ell
and type
w
as well as Proposition
11.3
for averages of length
\ell-1
. In the base case
\ell=1
, Proposition
11.3
follows directly from Proposition
11.4
. We assume therefore that
\ell>1
.

We let all the constants depend on
d,J,k,\ell,M,s,{\mathcal{Q}}
, noting however that
s^{\prime}
as well as the powers of
\varepsilon
will not depend on
k,M
.

Let

\displaystyle\varepsilon=\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Fix
i\in[\ell]
for which
a_{i}
is a sequence of maximum degree, and
w_{\eta_{i}}
is minimal among the nonzero coordinates of
w
. By Proposition
11.5
with the role of 1 played by
i
, there exists a positive integer
s^{\prime}=O(1)
for which

\displaystyle\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{i}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{s^{\prime},T_{\eta_{i}}}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

This gives the claimed Host-Kra seminorm control on
f_{i}
. If
i=1
, then we are done. Otherwise we also need to obtain a Host-Kra seminorm estimate in terms of other functions. To this end, we use Lemma
11.7
to replace
f_{i}
with
\tilde{f}_{i}
(constructed analogously to
\tilde{f}_{1}
from (
95
)), and then apply Proposition
11.5
to get

\displaystyle|\!|\!|\tilde{f}_{i}|\!|\!|_{s^{\prime},T_{\eta_{i}}}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)}.

Using the identity (
27
) and expanding the definition of
\tilde{f}_{i}
, we get

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell],j\neq i}T_{\eta_{j}}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J+1]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\gg\varepsilon^{O(1)},

where
b_{J+1}=a_{i}
and

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{J+1}(n)=T_{\eta_{i}}^{n}{\mathcal{D}}_{s^{\prime},T_{\eta_{i}}}\tilde{f}_{i}.

This average has length
\ell-1
, and so the claimed Host-Kra seminorm control of functions
f_{j}
with
j\neq i
follows by invoking Proposition
11.3
for averages of length
\ell-1
.

12.
Joint ergodicity for pairwise independent Hardy sequences

The purpose of this section is to derive the joint ergodicity results from Section
1.1
.
We start with a few relevant definitions.

Definition 12.1

(Nonergodic eigenfunction)
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
be a system. A function
\chi\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
is a
nonergodic eigenfunction
of
T
if it satisfies the following two conditions:

(i)

there exist a
T
-invariant set
E\in{\mathcal{X}}
and a
T
-invariant function
\phi:X\to{\mathbb{T}}
s.t.

\displaystyle T\chi(x)=1_{E}(x)e(\phi(x))\chi(x)\quad\textrm{for}\quad\mu\textrm{-a.e.}\quad x\in X;

(ii)

|\chi(x)|\in\{0,1\}
for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
.

Similarly, we call
\chi\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
an
eigenfunction
of
T
with
eigenvalue

\lambda\in[0,1)
if
T\chi(x)=e(\lambda)\chi(x)
holds for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
, and
|\chi|=1

\mu
-a.e.

Classically, eigenfunctions of
T
generate the Kronecker factor
{\mathcal{K}}(T)
whereas eigenfunctions with rational eigenvalues span the rational Kronecker factor
{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T)
. Moreover, nonergodic eigenfunctions are known to generate the Host-Kra factor
{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}(T)
(see
[
30
, Theorem 5.2]
or
[
36
, Proposition 4.3]
). If
T
is ergodic, then
{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}(T)
equals
{\mathcal{K}}(T)
, but this fails for nonergodic systems in general (e.g., consider
T(x,y)=(x,y+x)
on
X={\mathbb{T}}^{2}
).

Definition 12.2

(Good properties)
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be sequences and let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system. The sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are:

(i)

good for seminorm control
for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
if there exists
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that for all 1-bounded
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

(97)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{{\left\lfloor a_{\ell}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{\ell}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

whenever
\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{j}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{s,T_{j}}=0
for some
j\in[\ell]
;

(ii)

controlled by the Kronecker factor
for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
if (
97
) holds whenever
\E(f_{j}|{\mathcal{K}}(T_{j}))=0
for some
j\in[\ell]
;

(iii)

controlled by the rational Kronecker factor
on
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
if (
97
) holds whenever
\E(f_{j}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{j}))=0
for some
j\in[\ell]
;

(iv)

good for equidistribution
for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
if for eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{\ell}
of
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
respectively such that
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{\ell}
are not all 0, we have

(98)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}e(\lambda_{1}{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}+\cdots+\lambda_{\ell}{\left\lfloor a_{\ell}(n)\right\rfloor})=0;

(v)

good for strong irrational equidistribution
for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
if (
98
) holds for eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{\ell}
of
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
respectively such that
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{\ell}
are not all rational;

(vi)

good for irrational equidistribution
for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
if (
98
) holds for eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{\ell}
of
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
respectively such that
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}_{\ast}
not all
0
;

(vii)

good for equidistribution
if (
98
) holds for all
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{\ell}\in[0,1)
not all 0.

The following characterizations have been proved in
[
36
]
.

Theorem 12.3
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be sequences and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system. Then

(99)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}-\prod_{j\in[\ell]}\E(f_{j}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{j}))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

holds for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)

if and only if

(i)

the sequences are good for seminorm control for this system, and

(ii)

the limiting formula (
99
) holds whenever
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}
are nonergodic eigenfunctions of
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
respectively.

Similarly,

(100)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}-\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}\E(f_{j}|{{\mathcal{K}}_{\text{\rm rat}}}(T_{j}))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

holds for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)

if and only if

(i)

the sequences are good for seminorm control for this system, and

(ii)

the limiting formula (
100
) holds whenever
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}
are nonergodic eigenfunctions of
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
respectively.

When
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
are ergodic, then in the first part of Theorem
12.3
, the second product in (
99
) simplifies to
\prod_{j\in[\ell]}\int f_{j}\;d\mu
, while in the second conditions of both statements we just need to consider eigenfunctions.

12.1.
Implications for general sequences

We start with several rudimentary lemmas that show logical relationships between various properties from Definitions
12.2
and conditions from Problem
1.8
. A standard result that we use many times concerns the equivalent conditions for the good equidistribution property.

Lemma 12.4
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be sequences and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

the sequences are good for equidistribution for the system;

(ii)

the identity (
4
) holds for all eigenfunctions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}
of
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
respectively;

(iii)

the identity

(101)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\bigotimes_{j\in[\ell]}T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}-\prod_{j\in[\ell]}\int f_{j}\,d\mu\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu^{\ell})}=0

holds for all eigenfunctions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}
of
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
respectively.

Proof.

We prove the equivalence of (i) and (iii); the equivalence of (i) and (ii) follows similarly. Let
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{\ell}
be the eigenvalues of
f_{1},\dots,f_{\ell}
with respect to
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
respectively. If
\lambda_{1}=\cdots=\lambda_{\ell}=0
, then there is nothing to check regarding equidistribution, and the identity (
101
) holds with both expressions on the left hand side being 1. Suppose therefore that
\lambda_{j}\neq 0
for some
j\in[\ell]
. Then
\int f_{j}\;d\mu=0
, and so the Cartesian product in (
101
) vanishes. The tensor product in (
101
) equals

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}e(\lambda_{1}{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}+\cdots+\lambda_{\ell}{\left\lfloor a_{\ell}(n)\right\rfloor})\cdot\bigotimes_{j\in[\ell]}f_{j},

and since
\bigotimes_{j\in[\ell]}f_{j}\neq 0

\mu
-a.e., it vanishes if and only if (
98
) holds. Hence being good for equidistribution is equivalent to condition (iii).
∎

Lemma
12.4
gives the following immediate corollary.

Corollary 12.5
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be sequences and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system. Suppose that one of the conditions holds:

(i)

the identity (
99
) holds for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
; or

(ii)

(T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}\times\cdots\times T_{\ell}^{{\left\lfloor a_{\ell}(n)\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is ergodic on the product space
(X^{\ell},{\mathcal{X}}^{\otimes\ell},\mu^{\ell})
.

Then the sequences are good for equidistribution for the system.

Proof.

If the condition (i) (resp. (ii)) holds, then the condition (ii) (resp. (iii)) of Lemma
12.4
holds, and so the sequences are good for equidistribution for the system.
∎

The next result gives conditions under which joint ergodicity implies the product ergodicity condition from Problem
1.8
.

Lemma 12.6
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be sequences and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system. Suppose that the following two conditions hold:

(i)

a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are jointly ergodic for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
,

(ii)

each
a_{j}
is controlled by the Kronecker factor on the system
(X\times X,{\mathcal{X}}\otimes{\mathcal{X}},\mu\times\mu,

T_{j}\times T_{j})
.

Then
(T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}\times\cdots\times T_{\ell}^{{\left\lfloor a_{\ell}(n)\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is ergodic on the product space
(X^{\ell},{\mathcal{X}}^{\otimes\ell},\mu^{\ell})
.

Corollary
12.5
and Lemma
12.6
jointly imply that under the additional property (ii) from Lemma
12.6
, the difference and product ergodicity conditions from Problem
1.8
are necessary for joint ergodicity on a given system.

Proof.

We want to show that

(102)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}\times\cdots\times T_{\ell}^{{\left\lfloor a_{\ell}(n)\right\rfloor}}f-\int f\;d\mu^{\ell}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu^{\ell})}=0

for all
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu^{\ell})
. By an
L^{2}(\mu^{\ell})
-approximation argument, it suffices to consider 1-bounded functions of the form
f_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{\ell}
. For such functions, we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}\times\cdots\times T_{\ell}^{{\left\lfloor a_{\ell}(n)\right\rfloor}}(f_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{\ell})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu^{\ell})}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad=\limsup_{N\to\infty}\E_{n,m\in[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}\int T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(m)\right\rfloor}}\overline{f_{j}}\;d\mu

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq\limsup_{N\to\infty}\E_{n,m\in[N]}\mathopen{}\left|\int T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(m)\right\rfloor}}\overline{f_{j}}\;d\mu\mathclose{}\right|

for each fixed
j\in[\ell]
. Applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and passing to the product system, we get

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}\times\cdots\times T_{\ell}^{{\left\lfloor a_{\ell}(n)\right\rfloor}}(f_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{\ell})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu^{\ell})}^{4}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}(T_{j}\times T_{j})^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}(f_{j}\otimes\overline{f_{j}})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu\times\mu)}^{2},

and by the second condition, the latter vanishes whenever
\E(f_{j}\otimes\overline{f_{j}}|{\mathcal{K}}(T_{j}\times T_{j}))=0
. Since
{\mathcal{K}}(T_{j}\times T_{j})={\mathcal{K}}(T_{j})\otimes{\mathcal{K}}(T_{j})
, this last condition holds whenever
\E(f_{j}|{\mathcal{K}}(T_{j}))=0
.

Using the well-known fact that
{\mathcal{K}}(T_{1}\times\cdots\times T_{\ell})={\mathcal{K}}(T_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes{\mathcal{K}}(T_{\ell})

[
38
, Lemma 4.18]
and an
L^{2}(\mu^{\ell})
approximation argument, it therefore
suffices to show that (
102
) holds if
f_{1},\dots,f_{\ell}
are eigenfunctions of
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
respectively. Since joint ergodicity implies being good for equidistribution by Corollary
12.5
, the veracity of (
102
) for eigenfunctions follows from Lemma
12.4
.
∎

Being good for equidistribution for a specific system does not imply that (
99
) holds for all
nonergodic
eigenfunctions, as illustrated by the following example.

Example 12.7
.

Let
Y=\{y_{0},y_{1},y_{2}\}
,
Z=\{z_{0},z_{1}\}
,
X
be the disjoint union of
Y
and
Z
, and
T:X\to X
be defined by setting
T|_{Y},T|_{Z}
to be nontrivial permutations of
Y,Z
respectively. Then
T
admits no nontrivial eigenvalues, and so any sequence
a:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is vacuously good for equidistribution. At the same time, the identity (
99
) fails for
a(n)=6n
since then

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}T^{6n}f=f

is different from
\E(f|{\mathcal{I}}(T))
whenever
f|_{Y}
or
f|_{Z}
is not constant. Since every
f:X\to{\mathbb{C}}
is a linear combination of nonergodic eigenfunctions, this implies that the identity (
99
) fails for some nonergodic eigenfunctions.

However, one can check that if a family of sequences is good for equidistribution, then (
99
) holds for all systems and all nonergodic eigenfunctions.

Lastly, we show how seminorm estimates aid in obtaining the sufficient direction in Problem
1.8
.

Lemma 12.8

(Sufficient direction for joint ergodicity)
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be sequences and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
ergodic. Suppose that:

(i)

a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are good for seminorm control for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
; and

(ii)

(T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}\times\cdots\times T_{\ell}^{{\left\lfloor a_{\ell}(n)\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is ergodic on the product space
(X^{\ell},{\mathcal{X}}^{\otimes\ell},\mu^{\ell})
.

Then
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are jointly ergodic for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
.

Proof.

In the light of Theorem
12.3
, it suffices to show that (
4
) holds when
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}
are eigenfunctions of
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
respectively. The second condition and Corollary
12.5
imply that
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are good for equidistribution for the system, and then Lemma
12.4
implies that (
4
) holds for eigenfunctions.
∎

12.2.
Specializing to pairwise independent Hardy sequences

We now specialize to the case of pairwise independent Hardy sequences, and use the general results from the previous section in conjunction with specific properties of Hardy sequences to prove results from Section
1.1
. We start with the easy proofs.

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

If
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
are weakly mixing, then
\left|\!\left|\!\left|f_{j}\right|\!\right|\!\right|_{s,T_{j}}=\mathopen{}\left|\int f_{j}\;d\mu\mathclose{}\right|
for every
j\in[\ell]
and
f_{j}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, and so the result follows immediately from Theorem
1.16
and a telescoping identity.
∎

For the proof of Theorem
1.6
, we need the following lemma:

Lemma 12.9
.

Let
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
be irrationally independent. Then the sequences
{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor},\dots,{\left\lfloor a_{\ell}(n)\right\rfloor}
are good for irrational equidistribution for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}),
where the
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
are totally ergodic.

Proof.

The proof is similar to
[
49
, Proposition 2.8]
and so it is omitted.
∎

Proofs of Theorems
1.4
and
1.6
.

If
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are strongly independent (resp. strongly irrationally independent), then by Theorem
1.5
as well as
[
27
, Lemma 6.2]
(resp.
[
27
, Lemma 6.4]
), the sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are good for equidistribution (resp. strong irrational equidistribution). This, the seminorm estimates from Theorem
1.16
, and Theorem
12.3
give the results, where for the irrationally independent case and a system with totally ergodic transformations, we use Lemma
12.9
to get the corresponding equidistribution property.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.12
.

The result follows from
[
50
, Theorem 1.2]
, Theorem
1.3
(resp. Theorem
1.4
), and the fact that if
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are strongly independent (resp. pairwise independent), then the same holds for
a_{1}(W\cdot+b),\ldots,a_{\ell}(W\cdot+b)
for all
W,b\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
∎

To prove Theorem
1.9
, we need a few more auxiliary results. The first one concerns the nonvanishing of exponential sums along pairwise independent Hardy sequences.

Lemma 12.10
.

Let
a_{1},a_{2}\in{\mathcal{H}}
be pairwise independent. Then there exists at most countably many
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
for which

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}e(\lambda_{1}a_{1}(n)+\lambda_{2}a_{2}(n))

does not converge to 0 as
N\to\infty
.

Lemma
12.10
clearly fails without the pairwise independence assumption. For instance, if
a_{1}=a_{2}
, then the conclusion fails for any
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=(\lambda,-\lambda)
with
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
1.5
, a sequence
a\in{\mathcal{H}}
is equidistributed if and only if stays logarithmically away from rational polynomials.
26
26

26

We recall that this means that
a-p\succ\log
for any
p\in{\mathbb{Q}}[t]
; otherwise we say that
a
is
logarithmically close to a rational polynomial
.
Our objective therefore is to show that
\lambda_{1}a_{1}+\lambda_{2}a_{2}
is logarithmically away from rational polynomials for all but countably many bad pairs
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

If
a_{1},a_{2}
are strongly independent, then the only bad pair is
(0,0)
, and we are done. Otherwise there exists some
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\neq(0,0)
for which
\lambda_{1}a_{1}+\lambda_{2}a_{2}
is logarithmically close to a rational polynomial. A rescaling
c\lambda_{1}a_{1}+c\lambda_{2}a_{2}
is also logarithmically close to a rational polynomial if and only if
c\in{\mathbb{Q}}
. If
\{(c\lambda_{1},c\lambda_{2}):\;c\in{\mathbb{Q}}\}
are the only bad pairs, then this gives us countably many bad pairs, and the claim holds.

Otherwise there exists
(\lambda_{1}^{\prime},\lambda_{2}^{\prime})\neq(0,0)
such that
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),(\lambda_{1}^{\prime},\lambda_{2}^{\prime})
are
{\mathbb{R}}
-linearly independent, and
\lambda^{\prime}_{1}a_{1}+\lambda^{\prime}_{2}a_{2}
is also logarithmically close to a rational polynomial.
Then

\displaystyle(\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}^{\prime}-\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\lambda_{2})a_{1}=\lambda_{2}^{\prime}(\lambda_{1}a_{1}+\lambda_{2}a_{2})-\lambda_{2}(\lambda^{\prime}_{1}a_{1}+\lambda^{\prime}_{2}a_{2})

is also logarithmically close to a rational polynomial, and similarly for
(\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}^{\prime}-\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\lambda_{2})a_{2}
. The
{\mathbb{R}}
-linear independence of
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),(\lambda_{1}^{\prime},\lambda_{2}^{\prime})
implies that
\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}^{\prime}-\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\lambda_{2}\neq 0
, and hence

\displaystyle a_{1}=p_{1}+g_{1}\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad a_{2}=p_{2}+g_{2},

where
p_{1},p_{2}
are real multiples of some rational polynomials and
g_{1},g_{2}\ll\log
. In other words, the abundance of bad pairs
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
imposes a special structure on the functions
a_{1},a_{2}
.

So far, we have made no use of the pairwise independence of
a_{1},a_{2}
; this is the time to utilize it. The pairwise independence of
a_{1},a_{2}
implies that
p_{1},p_{2}
are pairwise independent, and we claim that
\lambda^{\prime\prime}_{1}p_{1}+\lambda^{\prime\prime}_{2}p_{2}
can be a rational polynomial for only countably many pairs
(\lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime},\lambda_{2}^{\prime\prime})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
. Letting
p_{1}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\beta_{1i}t^{i}
,
p_{2}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\beta_{2i}t^{i}
(we can absorb constant terms into
g_{1},g_{2}
), we can then find indices
1\leq i<j\leq d
such that
(\beta_{1i},\beta_{1j})
and
(\beta_{2i},\beta_{2j})
are
{\mathbb{R}}
-linearly independent. If
\lambda^{\prime\prime}_{1}p_{1}+\lambda^{\prime\prime}_{2}p_{2}\in{\mathbb{Q}}[t]
, then

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}\beta_{1i}+\lambda_{2}^{\prime\prime}\beta_{2i}

\displaystyle=q_{i}

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}\beta_{1j}+\lambda_{2}^{\prime\prime}\beta_{2j}

\displaystyle=q_{j}

for some
q_{1},q_{j}\in{\mathbb{Q}}
. The
{\mathbb{R}}
-linear independence of
(\beta_{1i},\beta_{1j})
and
(\beta_{2i},\beta_{2j})
then implies that
(q_{i},q_{j})
uniquely determines
(\lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime},\lambda_{2}^{\prime\prime})
. Since there are countably many choices of
(q_{i},q_{j})\in{\mathbb{Q}}^{2}
, we also have countably many choices of
(\lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime},\lambda_{2}^{\prime\prime})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
that make
\lambda^{\prime\prime}_{1}p_{1}+\lambda^{\prime\prime}_{2}p_{2}
into a rational polynomial, and we are done.
∎

The next corollary shows that the conclusion of Lemma
12.10
is upheld when we take integer parts of
a_{1},a_{2}
.

Corollary 12.11
.

Let
a_{1},a_{2}\in{\mathcal{H}}
be independent. Then there exists at most countably many
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
for which

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}e(\lambda_{1}{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}+\lambda_{2}{\left\lfloor a_{2}(n)\right\rfloor})

does not converge to 0 as
N\to\infty
.

Proof.

We follow the strategy of
[
27
, Lemma 6.2]
.
We begin by rephrasing our exponential sum as

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}e(\lambda_{1}{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}+\lambda_{2}{\left\lfloor a_{2}(n)\right\rfloor})=\E_{n\in[N]}e(\lambda_{1}{a_{1}(n)}+\lambda_{2}{a_{2}(n)}-\lambda_{1}\left\{a_{1}(n)\right\}-\lambda_{2}\left\{a_{2}(n)\right\}).

The function
G(x,y)=e(-\lambda_{1}x-\lambda_{2}y)
on
[0,1)^{2}
is Riemann integrable, and so we can split it into real and imaginary parts and approximate each from below and above by trigonometric polynomials. It therefore suffices to show that

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}e((\lambda_{1}+k_{1}){a_{1}(n)}+(\lambda_{2}+k_{2}){a_{2}(n)})

fails to converge to 0 as
N\to\infty
for countably many pairs
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},k_{1},k_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
. This follows from Lemma
12.10
.
∎

The next lemma shows that joint ergodicity for pairwise independent Hardy sequences implies the difference ergodicity condition from Problem
1.8
.

Lemma 12.12
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
be pairwise independent, and suppose that they are jointly ergodic for the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
. Then
(T_{i}^{{\left\lfloor a_{i}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{j}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is ergodic for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
for all distinct
i,j\in[\ell]
.

For general sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, a much simpler argument shows that joint ergodicity implies the average along
(T_{i}^{{\left\lfloor a_{i}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{j}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}})_{n}
to converge to an integral
weakly
(see e.g.
[
20
, Proposition 5.3]
). However, for independent Hardy sequences
a_{i},a_{j}
, the
L^{2}(\mu)
limit along
(T_{i}^{{\left\lfloor a_{i}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{j}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}})_{n}
may a priori not exist, and so we need a different approach than the one used in previous works on joint ergodicity
[
18
,
21
,
20
,
36
,
34
]
in which the
L^{2}(\mu)
convergence was already known.

Proof.

Let
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, and suppose that
\int f\;d\mu=0
, so that the claim reduces to showing that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{i}^{{\left\lfloor a_{i}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{j}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0.

By the classical Herglotz-Bochner theorem, there exists a finite Borel measure
\nu
on
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
such that

\displaystyle\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{i}^{{\left\lfloor a_{i}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{j}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}e\mathopen{}\left(\lambda_{i}{\left\lfloor a_{i}(n)\right\rfloor}-\lambda_{j}{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}\mathclose{}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\nu(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}))}

for every
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
; since
\int f\;d\mu=0
, we have no point mass on the origin. By Corollary
12.11
, the exponential sum vanishes in the limit for all but countably many
(\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j})\in{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
. By the joint ergodicity, the exponential sum also converges to 0 whenever
(\lambda_{i},-\lambda_{j})
is an eigenvalue of the joint action of
(T_{i},T_{j})
. Hence the set
(\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j})\in{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
for which the exponential sum does not vanish has no point masses, and since it is countable, it is
\nu
-measure must be 0. The claim follows from the bounded convergence theorem.
∎

Lemma 12.13
.

Let
a\in{\mathcal{H}}
with
a\succ\log
. Then
a
is controlled by the Kronecker factor on any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
.

Lemma
12.13
is fairly classical, and we refrain from providing a proof since it would descend into a tedious analysis of several cases. If
a
is a polynomial of degree
d
, then it can be proved by
d-1
applications of the van der Corput inequality and the known fact that weighted averages along
{\left\lfloor\alpha n\right\rfloor}
are controlled by the Kronecker factor. If
a=p+g
for
p\in{\mathbb{R}}[t]
and strongly nonpolynomial
g\prec p
, then after
d-1
applications of the van der Corput inequality, we additionally need to pass to short intervals on which
g
is constant and then apply the results for the linear case. If
a=p+g
for
p\in{\mathbb{R}}[t]
and strongly nonpolynomial
g\succ\log
(which overlaps with the previous case), then we can pass to short intervals, Taylor expand
g
to a polynomial of degree exceeding the degree of
p
, apply the van der Corput inequality to kill
p
, and then apply the usual machinery for polynomials.

We can now deduce Theorem
1.9
from the following more precise statement that illustrates exactly how the implications in the joint ergodicity classification problem for pairwise independent Hardy sequences go.

Theorem 12.14
.

Let
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},

\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system, and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
be pairwise independent. Then the following implications hold:

(i)

the joint ergodicity of
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
is equivalent to the ergodicity of
(T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}\times\cdots\times T_{\ell}^{{\left\lfloor a_{\ell}(n)\right\rfloor}})_{n}
on the product system
(X^{\ell},{\mathcal{X}}^{\otimes\ell},\mu^{\ell})
;

(ii)

the joint ergodicity of
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
implies that the sequence
(T_{i}^{{\left\lfloor a_{i}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{j}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is ergodic on
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
for all distinct
i,j\in[\ell]
.

Proof.

In the first statement, the forward implication follows from Lemmas
12.6
and
12.13
, and the converse implication is covered by Lemma
12.8
and Theorem
1.16
. The second statement follows from Lemma
12.12
.
∎

Appendix A
Properties of Hardy functions

In this appendix, we gather properties of Hardy sequences that are relevant in our proofs. Most of the lemmas exist in the literature already, so we omit their proofs.

The first lemma appears in
[
23
]
and allows us to compare the growth rate of a Hardy field function to the growth rate of its derivative.

Lemma A.1

(
[
23
, Lemma 2.1]
)
.

Suppose that
a\in\mathcal{H}
satisfies
t^{-m}\prec a(t)\prec t^{m}
for some
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
a(t)
does not converge to a nonzero constant as
t\to\infty
. Then

\frac{a(t)}{t\log^{2}t}\prec a^{\prime}(t)\ll\frac{a(t)}{t}.

In addition, if
a(t)\gg t^{\varepsilon}
or
a(t)\ll t^{-\varepsilon}
for some
\varepsilon>0
, we also have

a^{\prime}(t)\sim\frac{a(t)}{t}.

In the case of strongly nonpolynomial functions that dominate
\log
, we can show that the derivatives always satisfy the assumptions of the hypothesis, so that this lemma can be iterated arbitrarily many times. The proof of this assertion appears in
[
66
, Lemma A.2]
.

Lemma A.2

(
[
66
, Lemma A.2]
)
.

Suppose that
a\in\mathcal{H}
satisfies
a(t)\succ\log t
and
t^{d}\prec a(t)\prec t^{d+1}
for some
d\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
. Then, for any
m\geq d+1,
we have

1\prec|a^{(m)}(t)|^{-\frac{1}{m}}\prec|a^{(m+1)}(t)|^{-\frac{1}{m+1}}\prec t.

Additionally, the leftmost relation holds under the weaker assumption
a(t)\succ 1
.

By considering
a(t)=\log t
and its derivatives, it is immediate that the assumption
a(t)\succ\log t
is necessary for the second and third relations to hold.

We turn to presenting various properties of our novel notion of fractional degree. First, we show that the fractional degree of a function in
{\mathcal{H}}
is always a real number.

Lemma A.3
.

Let
a\in{\mathcal{H}}
. We have

\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{ta^{\prime}(t)}{a(t)}\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Proof.

Since Hardy fields are closed under multiplication and differentiation, and Hardy field functions are eventually monotone,
27
27

27

The closure of the Hardy field under compositional inverses implies that each Hardy field function has eventually constant sign, for otherwise it would not be possible to define an inverse. Applying this observation to
a^{\prime}
, we deduce that
a
is eventually monotone.
the limit
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\frac{ta^{\prime}(t)}{a(t)}
exists in the extended real line.

We will show that it cannot equal to
+\infty
(the case
-\infty
is completely analogous).

Indeed, assuming the contrary, for every
M>0
there exists
N>0
so that for all
x>N,
we have
\frac{ta^{\prime}(t)}{a(t)}>M.
Then

\log\left(\left(\frac{x}{N}\right)^{M}\right)=\int_{N}^{x}\frac{M}{t}\;dt\leq\int_{N}^{x}\frac{a^{\prime}(t)}{a(t)}\;dt\leq\log\frac{|a(x)|}{|a(N)|},

from where we get

|a(N)|(x/N)^{M}\leq|a(x)|,

a contradiction to the fact that
a
is of polynomial growth.
∎

The next lemma lists various straightforward properties of the notion of fractional degree.

Lemma A.4

(Properties of fractional degrees)
.

Let
a,b\in{\mathcal{H}}
. Then

(i)

\fracdeg{a^{(k)}}=\fracdeg{a}-k
for any
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
whenever
a
is strongly nonpolynomial;

(ii)

\fracdeg(ab)=\fracdeg a+\fracdeg b
;

(iii)

\fracdeg{a\circ b}=\fracdeg{a}\cdot\fracdeg{b}
whenever
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}b(t)=\infty
; and

(iv)

\fracdeg{a^{k}}=k\cdot\fracdeg{a}
for any
k\in{\mathbb{R}}
for which
a^{k}\in{\mathcal{H}}
.

We note that the first property fails for polynomials since all sufficiently large derivatives of a particular polynomial are zero.

Proof.

To prove the first property, we first note from Lemma
A.3
that there exists
c\in{\mathbb{R}}
for which

\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{ta^{\prime}(t)}{a(t)}=c.

By a repeated application of L’Hôpital’s rule, we deduce that for every
k\in{\mathbb{N}},
we have

\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{ta^{(k+1)}(t)}{a^{(k)}(t)}=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{ta^{(k)}(t)}{a^{(k-1)}(t)}-1=\ldots=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{ta^{\prime}(t)}{a(t)}-k=c-k.

The second property is a simple application of Leibniz’ rule. For the third property, we compute that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{t(a\circ b)^{\prime}(t)}{a\circ b(t)}=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{ta^{\prime}(b(t))b^{\prime}(t)}{a(b(t))}=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{tb^{\prime}(t)}{b(t)}\cdot\frac{b(t)a^{\prime}(b(t))}{a(b(t))}.

Since
b(t)\to\infty
as
t\to\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{b(t)a^{\prime}(b(t))}{a(b(t))}=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{ta^{\prime}(t)}{a(t)}=\fracdeg a,

and so the result follows from the product rule for limits.

Lastly, since

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{t(a^{k})^{\prime}(t)}{a^{k}(t)}=k\cdot\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{ta(t)^{k-1}a^{\prime}(t)}{a^{k}(t)}=k\cdot\fracdeg a,

we have property (
iv
).
∎

Appendix B
Simultaneous approximations in short intervals

In this part, we provide a proof for Proposition
10.3
. This necessitates the study of the growth rates of Hardy field functions and their interaction with differentiation. A large part of this analysis has been carried out originally in
[
66
]
. However, the information provided by the work in
[
66
]
is insufficient for our purposes.

Firstly, we establish a combinatorial lemma, which virtually covers the case of Proposition
10.3
when all the implicit Hardy field functions are distinct fractional powers.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\dots,\theta_{\ell}
be distinct positive real numbers, all smaller than a real number
u
. Then for any
q\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exist integers
d_{\theta_{1}},\dots,d_{\theta_{\ell}},d_{u}\geq q
such that the following conditions are satisfied:

(i)

we have

(103)

\Big(\frac{u}{d_{u}+1},\frac{u}{d_{u}}\Big]\cap\mathopen{}\left(\bigcap_{i=1}^{\ell}\Big(\frac{\theta_{i}}{d_{\theta_{i}}+1},\frac{\theta_{i}}{d_{\theta_{i}}}\Big]\mathclose{}\right)\neq\emptyset;

(ii)

for any
i\in[\ell]
, we have

(104)

\frac{u}{d_{u}}<\frac{\theta_{i}}{d_{\theta_{i}}}.

In particular,
d_{u}>d_{\theta_{i}}
;

(iii)

for any
i,j\in[\ell]
with
i\neq j
, we have

(105)

\frac{u}{d_{u}}<\frac{\theta_{i}-\theta_{j}}{d_{\theta_{i}}-d_{\theta_{j}}}.

The reader will observe that no Hardy sequences appear in our statement, so we make some remarks on the connection between this lemma and Proposition
10.3
.
Suppose we are given the collection
g_{i}(t)=t^{\theta_{i}}
and
g(t)=t^{u}
with the notation of Lemma
B.1
.
The computation of the derivatives of fractional powers is straightforward, so that
unpacking the complicated conditions of Proposition
10.3
leads us to some inequalities between the integers
\theta_{i},u
and
d_{\theta_{i}},d_{u}
, which will correspond to the degrees of the Taylor expansions.
For instance, the fact that the intersection (
103
) is nonempty corresponds to condition (
iii
) of Proposition
10.3
, the inequality (
104
) corresponds to condition (
iv
) while the inequality (
105
) corresponds to condition (
v
). Then, our main task is to demonstrate that this system of inequalities possesses infinitely many solutions
(d_{\theta_{1}},\dots,d_{\theta_{\ell}},d_{u})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\ell+1}
. We will establish this by constructing such a family of solutions, which will be produced through the set of the return times of a (multidimensional) irrational rotation.

We will use the following lemma, which is a simple consequence of Weyl’s equidistribution theorem.

Lemma B.2
.

Let
k,Q\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\varepsilon,x_{1},\dots,x_{k}>0
be such that
1,\;x_{1},\;x_{1}x_{2},\;\ldots,\;x_{1}\cdots x_{k}
are
\mathbb{Q}
-independent. Then there exist infinitely many
\theta\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
Q
dividing
\theta
such that the following hold:

(i)

we have the inequality

(106)

{\left\lfloor x_{k}{\left\lfloor x_{k-1}{\left\lfloor\dots{\left\lfloor x_{2}{\left\lfloor x_{1}\theta\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}\dots\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}\geq x_{k}\ldots x_{1}\theta-\varepsilon;

(ii)

for every
1\leq i\leq k
,
Q
divides
{\left\lfloor x_{i}{\left\lfloor x_{i-1}{\left\lfloor\dots{\left\lfloor x_{2}{\left\lfloor x_{1}\theta\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}\dots\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}
.

Proof.

Since
1,x_{1},x_{1}x_{2},\ldots,x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{k}
are
\mathbb{Q}
-independent, there exist infinitely many
\theta\in{\mathbb{N}}
, such that the following collection of inequalities is satisfied

\begin{cases}\frac{\varepsilon}{Q}\leq\frac{\{x_{1}\theta/Q\}}{x_{1}}\leq\frac{2\varepsilon}{Q}\\
\frac{2\varepsilon}{Q}\leq\frac{\{x_{1}x_{2}\theta/Q\}}{x_{1}x_{2}}\leq\frac{4\varepsilon}{Q}\\
\vdots\\
\frac{2^{k-2}\varepsilon}{Q}\leq\frac{\{x_{1}\cdots x_{k-1}\theta/Q\}}{x_{1}\cdots x_{k-1}}\leq\frac{2^{k-1}\varepsilon}{Q}\\
\frac{2^{k-1}\varepsilon}{Q}\leq\frac{\{x_{1}\cdots x_{k}\theta/Q\}}{x_{1}\cdots x_{k}}\leq\frac{2^{k}\varepsilon}{Q}\end{cases},

where we assume (as we may) that
\varepsilon
is very small in terms of
Q/(2^{k}x_{1}\cdots x_{k})
. Furthermore, we can choose
\theta
to be a multiple of
Q
(by applying the previous argument to
Qx_{1},\;Qx_{1}x_{2},\;\ldots,\;Qx_{1}\cdots x_{k}
).

Observe that the previous inequalities immediately imply the following weaker inequalities

(107)

2^{i-1}\varepsilon\leq\frac{\{x_{1}\cdots x_{i}\theta\}}{x_{1}\cdots x_{i}}\leq{2^{i}\varepsilon}.

for every
1\leq i\leq k
. This is an immediate consequence of the observation that if

\{a\}
is very small in terms of
1/Q
, then
\{Qa\}=Q\{a\}
(which does not hold, in general, if
Q
is not an integer).

We use induction to show that for all
1\leq i\leq k
and any
\theta
satisfying the previous inequalities, we have

x_{i}\cdots x_{1}\theta\geq{\left\lfloor x_{i}{\left\lfloor x_{i-1}{\left\lfloor\dots{\left\lfloor x_{2}{\left\lfloor x_{1}\theta\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}\dots\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}\geq x_{i}\cdots x_{1}\theta-2^{i}\varepsilon x_{i}\dots x_{1}

For
i=1
, the inequality follows from (
107
). Now, assume that the claim has been proven for
i
. Then, we want to show that

x_{i+1}\cdots x_{1}\theta\geq{\left\lfloor x_{i+1}{\left\lfloor x_{i}{\left\lfloor\dots{\left\lfloor x_{2}{\left\lfloor x_{1}\theta\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}\dots\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}\geq x_{i+1}\cdots x_{1}\theta-2^{i+1}\varepsilon x_{i+1}\cdots x_{1}.

The left inequality is always valid, as dropping the integer parts increases the quantity in the middle. In addition, the induction hypothesis implies that

(108)

x_{i+1}\cdots x_{1}\theta\geq x_{i+1}{\left\lfloor x_{i}{\left\lfloor\dots{\left\lfloor x_{2}{\left\lfloor x_{1}\theta\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}\dots\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}\geq x_{i+1}\cdots x_{1}\theta-2^{i}\varepsilon x_{i+1}\cdots x_{1}

Since

2^{i}\varepsilon x_{i+1}\cdots x_{1}\leq\{x_{i+1}\cdots x_{1}\theta\}\leq 2^{i+1}\varepsilon x_{i+1}\cdots x_{1}

by (
107
), we infer that

(109)

{\left\lfloor x_{i+1}\cdots x_{1}\theta\right\rfloor}={\left\lfloor x_{i+1}{\left\lfloor x_{i}{\left\lfloor\dots{\left\lfloor x_{2}{\left\lfloor x_{1}\theta\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}\dots\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}={\left\lfloor x_{i+1}\cdots x_{1}\theta-2^{i}\varepsilon x_{i+1}\cdots x_{1}\right\rfloor}.

Consequently, using the fact that the fractional part of
x_{i+1}\cdots x_{1}\theta
is smaller than
2^{i+1}\varepsilon x_{i+1}\cdots x_{1}
, we infer that

{\left\lfloor x_{i+1}{\left\lfloor x_{i}{\left\lfloor\dots{\left\lfloor x_{2}{\left\lfloor x_{1}\theta\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}\dots\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}\geq x_{i+1}\cdots x_{1}\theta-2^{i+1}\varepsilon x_{i+1}\cdots x_{1}

and the induction is complete.

Now, we show that
Q
divides each one of the integers
{\left\lfloor x_{i}{\left\lfloor\dots{\left\lfloor x_{2}{\left\lfloor x_{1}\theta\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}\dots\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}
.

We recall (
109
) which readily implies that

{\left\lfloor x_{i}{\left\lfloor x_{i-1}{\left\lfloor\dots{\left\lfloor x_{2}{\left\lfloor x_{1}\theta\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}\dots\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}={\left\lfloor x_{i}\cdots x_{1}\theta\right\rfloor}.

Therefore, it suffices to show that
Q|{\left\lfloor x_{i}\cdots x_{1}\theta\right\rfloor}
, which follows easily from the fact that

\left\{\frac{x_{i}\cdots x_{1}\theta}{Q}\right\}\leq\frac{2^{i}\varepsilon x_{i}\cdots x_{1}}{Q}<\frac{1}{Q},

where the last inequality holds if we pick
\varepsilon
to be sufficiently small.
∎

We are ready to prove Lemma
B.1
. We will apply Lemma
B.2
to a
{\mathbb{Q}}
-independent subset of
\theta_{i}
’s. The
d_{\theta_{i}}
’s will be constructed inductively using iterated integer parts for the subset of the
\theta_{i}
and then the other
d_{\theta_{i}}
will be defined using linearity.
The assumption that
Q
divides the expressions with the integer parts in the previous lemma is necessary to ensure that certain fractions in the definition of the
d_{i}
below will be integer numbers.

Proof of Lemma
B.1
.

We choose a subset
\{c_{0},\ldots,c_{k}\}
of
\{\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{\ell}\}
with maximal cardinality, and such that
c_{0},\ldots,c_{k}
are
\mathbb{Q}
-independent. Reordering if necessary, we will assume that
c_{0}<c_{1}<\ldots<c_{k}
. Observe that for any
\theta_{i}
, we can write

(110)

\theta_{i}=p_{i0}c_{0}+\dots+p_{ik}c_{k}

for some
p_{ij}\in\mathbb{Q}
.
Let
M\in{\mathbb{N}}
be larger than the height
28
28

28

The height of a rational number
p/q
written in lowest terms is the quantity
\max\{|p|,|q|\}.
of all
p_{ij}
. In particular, this implies
|p_{ij}|<M
for all admissible indices
i,j
.

We consider the set

\mathcal{A}=\Bigl\{\beta\in(0,u)\colon\beta=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\frac{p_{i}}{q_{i}}c_{i},\ \text{where }p_{i},q_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}},|q_{i}|<M^{2},|p_{i}|<M^{3}\Bigr\}

Namely,
\mathcal{A}
contains those linear combinations of
c_{0},\ldots,c_{k}
with bounded rational coefficients, whose denominators are also bounded, and which also lie in the open interval
(0,u)
.

Observe that all of the initial numbers
\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{\ell}
belong to
\mathcal{A}
. Also, we observe that
\mathcal{A}
is finite and therefore bounded away from zero and
u
. Namely, there exists
\delta>0
, such that

(111)

\delta<s<u

for all
s\in\mathcal{A}
.

We define
d_{0},\dots,d_{k}
recursively by

(112)

d_{0}=\theta,\ \ d_{i+1}={\left\lfloor\frac{c_{i+1}}{c_{i}}d_{i}\right\rfloor},\ \text{ for every }0\leq i\leq k-1.

where
\theta\in{\mathbb{N}}
is chosen (arbitrarily large) so that the following relations hold:

(i)

we have that

(113)

M!\big|d_{0}\ \text{and }M!\big|d_{i}={\left\lfloor\frac{c_{i}}{c_{i-1}}{\left\lfloor\dots{\left\lfloor\frac{c_{2}}{c_{1}}{\left\lfloor\frac{c_{1}}{c_{0}}d_{0}\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}\dots\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}

for every
1\leq i\leq k
;

(ii)

we have the inequality

(114)

{\left\lfloor\frac{c_{k}}{c_{k-1}}{\left\lfloor\dots{\left\lfloor\frac{c_{2}}{c_{1}}{\left\lfloor\frac{c_{1}}{c_{0}}d_{0}\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}\dots\right\rfloor}\right\rfloor}\geq\frac{d_{0}c_{k}}{c_{0}}-c_{k}\varepsilon,

where
\varepsilon
is very small in terms of the fixed numbers
\delta,M,u
and
c_{0}
.

We can choose
d_{0}
in such a way by applying Lemma
B.2
(with
\theta=d_{0},Q=M!,x_{1}=\frac{c_{1}}{c_{0}},\ldots,x_{k}=\frac{c_{k}}{c_{k-1}}
), since the numbers
1,\frac{c_{1}}{c_{0}}.\ldots,\frac{c_{k}}{c_{0}}
are
\mathbb{Q}
-independent by assumption. By the definition of
d_{0},\ldots,d_{k}
, we have the following chain of inequalities

(115)

\frac{d_{0}}{c_{0}}\geq\frac{d_{1}}{c_{1}}\geq\ldots\geq\frac{d_{k}}{c_{k}}\geq\frac{d_{0}}{c_{0}}-\varepsilon.

This implies that

(116)

\Bigl|\frac{d_{i}}{c_{i}}-\frac{d_{j}}{c_{j}}\Bigr|\leq\varepsilon\implies|d_{i}c_{j}-d_{j}c_{i}|\leq\varepsilon|c_{i}||c_{j}|<\varepsilon u^{2}.

Now, for any
s\in\mathcal{A}
of the form
s=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\frac{p_{i}}{q_{i}}c_{i},
we define

D_{s}=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\frac{p_{i}}{q_{i}}d_{i}.

In particular, if
s
is one of the original numbers
\theta_{i}
, we will ultimately pick
d_{\theta_{i}}=D_{\theta_{i}}
. Observe that there is no ambiguity in this definition, since the numbers
1,\frac{c_{1}}{c_{0}},\ldots,\frac{c_{k}}{c_{0}}
are
\mathbb{Q}
-independent, so that every
s\in A
has a unique representation as a rational combination of these numbers.

Firstly, we prove that
D_{\theta_{i}}
are integers.
We observe that is true since the denominators of the rationals
{p_{i0}},\dots,p_{ik}
in (
110
) are all smaller than
M
(by the initial choice of
M
) and that
M!
divides
d_{0},\dots,d_{k}
by (
113
).

Next we establish a bound for the difference

(117)

\Bigl|\frac{D_{s}}{s}-\frac{d_{0}}{c_{0}}\Bigr|

for any
s\in\mathcal{A}
.
Indeed, this may be rewritten as

\mathopen{}\left|\dfrac{\sum_{i=0}^{k}\frac{p_{i}}{q_{i}}d_{i}}{\sum_{i=0}^{k}\frac{p_{i}}{q_{i}}c_{i}}-\frac{d_{0}}{c_{0}}\mathclose{}\right|=\mathopen{}\left|\dfrac{\sum_{i=1}^{k}\dfrac{p_{i}}{q_{i}}\big(d_{i}c_{0}-d_{0}c_{i}\big)}{c_{0}\big(\sum_{i=0}^{k}\frac{p_{i}}{q_{i}}c_{i}\big)}\mathclose{}\right|\leq\dfrac{\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathopen{}\left|\frac{p_{i}}{q_{i}}\mathclose{}\right|\big|d_{i}c_{0}-d_{0}c_{i}\big|}{|c_{0}||s|}<\varepsilon\frac{M^{3}ku^{2}}{|c_{0}||\delta|},

where we used (
111
), (
116
), and the fact that
|p_{i}|<M^{3}
by the definition of
\mathcal{A}
.
This also implies that

(118)

\mathopen{}\left|\frac{D_{s}}{s}-\frac{D_{t}}{t}\mathclose{}\right|<2\varepsilon\frac{M^{3}ku^{2}}{|c_{0}||\delta|}

for any
s,t\in\mathcal{A}
.

Claim 1
.

If
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small, then the following hold:

(i)

for each
s\in\mathcal{A}
, we have
D_{s}>0
;

(ii)

the intersection

\bigcap_{s\in\mathcal{A}}\Big(\frac{s}{D_{s}+1},\frac{s}{D_{s}}\Big]

is non-empty.

For the first part of the claim, note that
s
is positive by the definition of the set
\mathcal{A}
. So

(119)

\frac{D_{s}}{s}>\frac{d_{0}}{c_{0}}-\varepsilon\frac{M^{3}ku^{2}}{|c_{0}||\delta|}\geq\frac{1}{c_{0}}-\varepsilon\frac{M^{3}ku^{2}}{|c_{0}||\delta|}>0

if we pick
\varepsilon
sufficiently small.

The second part of the claim amounts to showing that

\frac{s}{D_{s}}>\frac{t}{D_{t}+1}

for any
s,t\in\mathcal{A}
, which can be rewritten
29
29

29

Recall that
D_{s}>0
for all
s\in\mathcal{A}
.
as

\frac{D_{s}}{s}-\frac{D_{t}}{t}<\frac{1}{t}.

If the left-hand side is negative, then the claim is obvious. Otherwise, our claim will follow if we prove that

\mathopen{}\left|\frac{D_{s}}{s}-\frac{D_{t}}{t}\mathclose{}\right|<\frac{1}{u},

since all elements
t\in\mathcal{A}
are smaller than
u
. However, we have already established that the left-hand side is smaller than

\frac{2\varepsilon M^{3}ku^{2}}{|c_{0}||\delta|}

which can be made smaller than
1/u
if we pick
\varepsilon
sufficiently small.

Claim 2
.

If
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small, then there exists
d_{u}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, such that the following two conditions are satisfied:

(i)

We have

(120)

\Big(\frac{u}{d_{u}+1},\frac{u}{d_{u}}\Big]\cap\mathopen{}\left(\bigcap_{s\in\mathcal{A}}\Big(\frac{s}{D_{s}+1},\frac{s}{D_{s}}\Big]\mathclose{}\right)\neq\emptyset.

(ii)

For all
s\in\mathcal{A}
, we have

(121)

\frac{u}{d_{u}}<\frac{s}{D_{s}}.

If Claim
2
holds, then the first two conditions of the statement will be proven (with
d_{\theta_{i}}=D_{\theta_{i}}
)
30
30

30

We will also need to show that
D_{\theta_{i}}
are integers.
by restricting (
120
) and (
121
) from the more general set
\mathcal{A}
to its subset
\{\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{\ell}\}
.

To prove Claim
2
, it suffices to show that there exists
d_{u}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that

(122)

\frac{D_{s}}{s}<\frac{d_{u}}{u}<\frac{D_{s}+1}{s}

for all
s\in\mathcal{A}
.
By multiplying both sides with
u
, we can rephrase these inequalities as

\max_{s\in\mathcal{A}}\Bigl\{u\cdot\frac{D_{s}}{s}\Bigr\}<d_{u}<\min_{s\in\mathcal{A}}\Bigl\{u\cdot\frac{D_{s}}{s}+\frac{u}{s}\Bigr\}.

So it suffices to show that

(123)

\min_{s\in\mathcal{A}}\Bigl\{u\cdot\frac{D_{s}}{s}+\frac{u}{s}\Bigr\}-\max_{s\in\mathcal{A}}\Bigl\{u\cdot\frac{D_{s}}{s}\Bigr\}>1.

We remark that the desired
d_{u}
guaranteed by (
123
) is not equal to either
u\cdot\frac{D_{t}}{s}
and
u\cdot\frac{D_{s}}{s}+\frac{u}{s}
for any
s\in\mathcal{A}
, because the inequality in (
123
) is strict.

Note that (
123
) holds if we can show that

u\cdot\frac{D_{s}}{s}+\frac{u}{s}-u\cdot\frac{D_{t}}{t}>1

for all
s,t\in\mathcal{A}
, which can be rewritten
in the form

\frac{D_{t}}{t}-\frac{D_{s}}{s}<\frac{1}{s}-\frac{1}{u}.

Observe that the right-hand side is positive, since all elements of
\mathcal{A}

are smaller than
u
. On the other hand, the left-hand side is bounded in magnitude by the quantity in (
118
). If we pick
\varepsilon
sufficiently small, we can ensure that

\frac{2\varepsilon M^{3}ku^{2}}{|c_{0}||\delta|}<\min_{s\in\mathcal{A}}\Bigl|{\frac{1}{s}-\frac{1}{u}}\Bigr|,

which implies the desired result.

In addition to Claim
2
, we shall consider the positive integers
D_{\theta_{1}},\ldots,D_{\theta_{\ell}}
provided by the previous construction and which correspond to our original real numbers
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{\ell}
.
We have already proven that for this choice of
d_{u}
and
d_{\theta_{i}}=D_{\theta_{i}}
, conditions (
i
) and (
ii
) of our statement hold and we will show that the same extends to condition (
iii
).
We also remind the reader that we have already established that
D_{\theta_{i}}
are positive integers, so the choice of
d_{\theta_{i}}=D_{\theta_{i}}
is indeed sensible.

Now, we show that condition (
iii
) in our statement is satisfied.
We have to restrict to the set
\{\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{\ell}\}
instead of the whole set
\mathcal{A}
.
The main point is that the difference
\theta_{i}-\theta_{j}
belongs to the set
\mathcal{A}
(when it is a positive number), while the difference
s-t
might not. Indeed, if
p_{i}/q_{i}
and
p^{\prime}_{i}/q^{\prime}_{i}
are the coefficients of
s,t
in their linear combinations with respect to
c_{0},\dots,c_{k}
, then the difference
s-t
is a linear combination of
c_{0},\dots,c_{k}
. However, the rational number
p_{i}/q_{i}-p^{\prime}_{i}/q^{\prime}_{i}
can have a denominator comparable to
q_{i}q^{\prime}_{i}
, which can be as large as
M^{4}
.

To conclude the proof, we show that

(124)

\frac{u}{d_{u}}<\frac{\theta_{i}-\theta_{j}}{D_{\theta_{i}}-D_{\theta_{j}}}

for all distinct
i,j\in[\ell]
. Assume without loss of generality that
\theta_{i}>\theta_{j}
. If
\theta_{i}-\theta_{j}\in\mathcal{A}
, then
D_{\theta_{i}-\theta_{j}}=D_{\theta_{i}}-D_{\theta_{j}}
by the linearity in the definition of
D_{s}
, and so (
121
) implies (
124
). So it suffices to show that
\theta_{i}-\theta_{j}\in\mathcal{A}
.

We remark the obvious bounds

0<\theta_{i}-\theta_{j}<u.

In addition, if we write

\theta_{i}=\sum_{h=0}^{k}p_{ih}c_{h}

and similarly for
\theta_{j}
, then the rational numbers
p_{ih}
are bounded by
M
and have height at most
M
, by the initial choice of the positive integer
M
. Therefore, we have

\theta_{i}-\theta_{j}=\sum_{h=0}^{k}(p_{ih}-p_{jh})c_{h},

and the rational number
p_{ih}-p_{jh}
is bounded in magnitude by
2M<M^{3}
(so that its numerator is bounded by
M^{3}
as well) and has denominator at most
2M^{2}
, which are both acceptable.

This shows that
\theta_{i}-\theta_{j}
is an element of
\mathcal{A}
and we are done.
∎

We are now ready to prove Proposition
10.3
, which is a consequence of the following proposition.

Proposition B.3
.

Let
m,q\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
g_{1}\prec\dots\prec g_{m}\in\mathcal{H}
be strongly nonpolynomial functions satisfying
c_{i}:=\fracdeg g_{i}>0
.
Then there exist
d_{g_{1}},\dots,d_{g_{m}}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that the following hold:

(i)

for all
i\in[m]
,
d_{g_{i}}\geq q
;

(ii)

for
i,j\in[m]
, we have
d_{g_{i}}=d_{g_{j}}
iff
c_{i}=c_{j}
;

(iii)

for all
i,j\in[m]
, we have

(125)

\bigl|g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}})}(N)\bigr|^{-\frac{1}{d_{g_{i}}}}\lll\bigl|g_{j}^{(d_{g_{j}}+1)}(N)\bigr|^{-\frac{1}{d_{g_{j}}+1}};

(iv)

for all
i\in[m]
, we have

(126)

\fracdeg\bigl|g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}})}(N)\bigr|^{-\frac{1}{d_{g_{i}}}}\leq\fracdeg\bigl|g_{m}^{(d_{g_{m}})}(N)\bigr|^{-\frac{1}{d_{g_{m}}}},

with equality if and only if
\fracdeg g_{i}=\fracdeg g_{m}
;

(v)

for all distinct
i,j\in[m]
and all
\eta>0
, we have

g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}})}(N)\bigl|g_{m}^{(d_{g_{m}})}(N)\bigr|^{-\frac{d_{g_{i}}}{d_{g_{m}}}}N^{\eta d_{g_{i}}}\not\asymp g_{j}^{(d_{g_{j}})}(N)\bigl|g_{m}^{(d_{g_{m}})}(N)\bigr|^{-\frac{d_{g_{j}}}{d_{g_{m}}}}N^{\eta d_{g_{j}}}.

Proof.

Throughout the proof, we will make use of Lemma
A.4
without citations.
We may write
g_{i}(t)=t^{c_{i}}b_{i}(t)
, where
b_{i}(t)\in{\mathcal{H}}
is a function of fractional degree
0
.
We partition the set
[m]
into disjoint, consecutive intervals
B_{1},\dots,B_{\ell}
, where
g_{i}
and
g_{j}
belong to the same set
B_{r}
if and only if
c_{i}=c_{j}
.
For
r\in[\ell]
, we shall denote by
C_{r}
the common fractional power corresponding to the set
B_{r}
.
Let
D_{1},\ldots,D_{\ell-1}
and
D_{\ell}
be the integers
d_{\theta_{1}},\ldots,d_{\theta_{\ell}}
and
d_{u}
provided by Lemma
B.1
. Then we set

\displaystyle d_{g_{i}}=D_{r}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad i\in B_{r}.

Namely, the choice of
d_{g_{i}}
is the one provided by Lemma
B.1
if we took
g_{i}
to be fractional powers.
For reference, we will rewrite the main bounds from Lemma
B.1
that
D_{1},\ldots,D_{\ell}
obey:

(i)

we have

(127)

\bigcap_{i=1}^{\ell}\Big(\frac{C_{i}}{D_{i}+1},\frac{C_{i}}{D_{i}}\Big]\neq\emptyset;

(ii)

for any
1\leq i\leq\ell-1
, we have

(128)

\frac{C_{\ell}}{D_{\ell}}<\frac{C_{i}}{D_{i}};

(iii)

for any
1\leq i<j\leq\ell-1
we have

(129)

\frac{C_{\ell}}{D_{\ell}}<\frac{C_{i}-C_{j}}{D_{i}-D_{j}}.

Firstly, we observe that Lemma
B.1
allows us to choose all the numbers
d_{g_{i}}
to be larger than
q
. Thus, condition (
i
) is satisfied. Secondly, condition (
ii
) follows by construction. Noting that

\displaystyle\fracdeg\bigl|g_{i}^{(d)}\bigr|^{-\frac{1}{d}}=1-\frac{c_{i}}{d}

for any
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
, condition (
iii
) is equivalent to the inequality
\frac{c_{j}}{d_{g_{j}}+1}<\frac{c_{i}}{d_{g_{i}}}
; when
c_{i}=c_{j}
, this is immediate from the positivity of fractional degrees, otherwise it follows from (
127
). Similarly, condition (
iv
) is equivalent to
\frac{c_{m}}{d_{g_{m}}}\leq\frac{c_{i}}{d_{g_{i}}}
, with equality only if and only if
c_{i}=c_{m}
, and this is an immediate consequence of (
128
). To prove the last condition, we split into the cases
c_{i}=c_{j}
and
c_{i}\neq c_{j}
. If
c_{i}=c_{j}
, then
d_{g_{i}}=d_{g_{j}}
as well, and then result follows since
g_{i},g_{j}
have distinct growth. For the case
c_{i}\neq c_{j}
, we have

\displaystyle g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}})}(N)\bigl|g_{m}^{(d_{g_{m}})}(N)\bigr|^{-\frac{d_{g_{i}}}{d_{g_{m}}}}N^{\eta d_{g_{i}}}\asymp g_{j}^{(d_{g_{j}})}(N)\bigl|g_{m}^{(d_{g_{m}})}(N)\bigr|^{-\frac{d_{g_{j}}}{d_{g_{m}}}}N^{\eta d_{g_{j}}}

only if both sides have the same fractional degrees. We will show that this is not the case. Since

\displaystyle\fracdeg\mathopen{}\left|g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}})}(N)\bigl|g_{m}^{(d_{g_{m}})}(N)\bigr|^{-\frac{d_{g_{i}}}{d_{g_{m}}}}N^{\eta d_{g_{i}}}\mathclose{}\right|=\frac{c_{i}d_{g_{m}}-c_{m}d_{g_{i}}+\eta d_{g_{i}}d_{g_{m}}}{d_{g_{m}}},

both functions above have the same fractional degrees if and only if

(130)

\displaystyle\frac{c_{i}-c_{j}}{d_{g_{i}}-d_{g_{j}}}=\frac{c_{m}}{d_{g_{m}}}-\eta.

Since

\displaystyle\frac{c_{m}}{d_{g_{m}}}-\eta<\frac{c_{m}}{d_{g_{m}}}<\frac{c_{i}-c_{j}}{d_{g_{i}}-d_{g_{j}}}

for every
\eta>0
by (
129
), the equality (
130
) will fail for all
\eta
in this range, giving the result.
∎

We are ready to conclude the proof of Proposition
10.3
.

Proof of Proposition
10.3
.

We use Proposition
B.3
for functions
g_{m_{2}+1},\ldots,g_{m}
to find degrees
d_{g_{m_{2}+1}},\ldots,d_{g_{m}}
satisfying the conditions of Proposition
B.3
, and we keep using the notation
c_{i}=\fracdeg g_{i}
. If
d_{g_{m}}=0
, i.e. all
g_{1},\ldots,g_{m}
are subfractional, we let
\eta_{0}=1
, otherwise take any
\eta_{0}
satisfying

(131)

\displaystyle 0<\eta_{0}<\frac{c_{m}}{d_{g_{m}}}-\max_{i\in[m_{2}+1,m]}\frac{c_{i}}{d_{g_{i}}+1}<c_{m}.

By (
125
) applied with
i=m,j=i
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{c_{m}}{d_{g_{m}}}>\max_{i\in[m_{2}+1,m]}\frac{c_{i}}{d_{g_{i}}+1},

and so the range of
\eta_{0}
is nonempty.
We then set
H(N)=N^{\eta}
if
d_{g_{m}}=0
and

H(N)=|g_{m}^{(d_{g_{m}})}(N)|^{-1/d_{g_{m}}}N^{\eta}

otherwise for an arbitrary choice of
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0})
. By (
131
) and Proposition
B.3
(
iv
), we have

\displaystyle\bigl|g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}})}(N)\bigr|^{-\frac{1}{d_{g_{i}}}}\lll H(N)\lll\bigl|g_{j}^{(d_{g_{j}}+1)}(N)\bigr|^{-\frac{1}{d_{g_{j}}+1}}

for all
i,j\in[m_{2}+1,m]
,
proving the first part of (
68
) (this holds vacuously if
d_{g_{m}}=0
). We can then take
L
to be any positive Hardy function satisfying

\displaystyle H(N)\lll L(N)\lll\bigl|g_{j}^{(d_{g_{j}}+1)}(N)\bigr|^{-\frac{1}{d_{g_{j}}+1}}

for all
j\in[m_{2}+1,m]
(if
d_{g_{m}}=0
, then this condition again holds vacuously, so we can take
L(N)=N^{\eta^{\prime}}
for
\eta<\eta^{\prime}<1
). This completes the proof of (
68
), and hence the condition (
iii
) of Proposition
10.3
. So chosen functions
H,L
have fractional degrees between 0 and 1 because the functions
\bigl|g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}})}\bigr|^{-\frac{1}{d_{g_{i}}}},\bigl|g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}}+1)}\bigr|^{-\frac{1}{d_{g_{i}}+1}}
do. It follows from this and the argument above Proposition
10.3
that the degree of the Taylor expansions of
g_{1},\ldots,g_{m_{2}}
is 0. Together with Proposition
B.3
, this completes the proof of the conditions (
i
) and (
ii
) of Proposition
10.3
. Property (
iv
) follows immediately from the analogous property in Proposition
B.3
, and so it remains to show the property (
v
). For
i\in[m_{2}]
, the fact that
d_{g_{i}}=0
implies that
g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}})}(N)H(N)^{d_{i}}=g_{i}(N)
, and so the functions
g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}})}(N)H(N)^{d_{i}},g_{j}^{(d_{g_{j}})}(N)H(N)^{d_{j}}
have distinct growth for
i,j\in[m_{2}]
because
g_{i},g_{j}
have distinct growth. For
i,j\in[m_{2}+1,m]
, property (
v
) follows from Proposition
B.3
(
v
). Lastly, we have
\fracdeg g_{i}^{(d_{g_{i}})}(N)H(N)^{d_{i}}=0
iff
i\in[m_{2}]
, and so property (
v
) clearly holds when exactly one
i,j
is in
[m_{2}]
.
∎
